Men cannot begin without pretending a beginning. Even science, the most precise of all disciplines, must invent a starting point—fixing its clock at zero as if time itself had a clean edge. Poetry, less rigid, is said to begin in the middle, but upon closer thought, this is no different from science’s own method: both look backward and forward, dividing their units into infinitesimal parts, setting off not from nothing, but from a chosen moment—a moment already shaped by what came before. No memory can reach the true origin; whether we start in heaven or on earth, our story begins with a fragment, a slice of something vast and unspoken.

Was she beautiful? Or was it not beauty that held Daniel Deronda’s gaze, but something else—the quiet power in her eyes, the way they seemed to move beyond mere sight? Was it the good spirit or the dark one that shone through them? Likely the latter—else why did her presence stir unease rather than peace? Why did the desire to look again feel like obligation, not longing? Why did the soul resist being drawn to her, even as the body could not turn away?

The woman who stirred these thoughts in Daniel Deronda was not gambling in the open air beneath a sun-warmed sky, nor tossing coins among beggars on crumbling walls. She was in one of those grand resorts built by centuries of wealth and indulgence—places where pleasure is wrapped in gold leaf, deep shadows, and statues of half-naked gods, all designed to concentrate the breath of the fashionable few. The air here was thick, brewed into a soft haze by late September heat. Silence reigned, broken only by faint rattles, the delicate clink of chips, a low sweeping sound, and occasional murmurs in French—words that might have come from a machine made to mimic human speech.

Two long tables stood at the center of the room, crowded with players. All faces were turned toward the games—except one. A small boy, bare-kneed and dressed in an absurd costume, stood motionless near the doorway, staring blankly ahead like a puppet placed there for display. Behind him, a woman leaned over a roulette table, deeply absorbed. Around her, fifty or sixty people gathered—some playing, others watching, some merely passing through. Among them, a woman would occasionally place a five-franc note with a smile, just to taste the thrill of gambling, though she never meant to stay long.

The players themselves were a curious mix: Livonian nobility, Spanish merchants, Italian aristocrats, German scholars, English lords and commoners—all united by a single passion. An English countess, her fingers gleaming with jewels, sat beside a gaunt, yellow-skinned hand reaching for coins—its owner a woman whose face looked like a vulture’s, grizzled and sharp-eyed. Nearby, a London tradesman, neat and well-groomed, sat quietly, not driven by greed but by habit—his life a rhythm of earning, spending, and now, playing, all with the same detached calm. He believed Providence had never condemned his pastimes, and so he continued, willing to stop when the joy of winning faded into the pain of losing.

Near him stood a handsome Italian, poised and still, placing a pile of napoleons across the table. His hand moved with precision, as if he followed a system too perfect to fail. Beside him, another man—thin, pale, wearing a monocle—trembled slightly as he asked for change. His play was not governed by logic, but by superstition: perhaps the eighth of the month was lucky, or maybe he dreamed of white crows. Yet each player, though different in appearance and background, shared a strange uniformity—a dullness in expression, as if they had all eaten from the same root that dulled the mind and narrowed the soul.

Deronda watched them and felt a pang of pity. Compared to these people, lost in artificial pleasures, he remembered how much more alive the Spanish shepherd-boys seemed—free, simple, untouched by the poison of excess. Rousseau might have been right after all: civilization had not improved mankind, but trapped it in gilded cages.

Then—suddenly—the scene shifted. His attention was drawn to a young woman standing nearby, speaking softly in English to an older lady seated beside her. In the next instant, she returned to her game, revealing a figure of striking grace. Her face was not one that demanded admiration—but one that refused to be ignored.

A quiet storm began within Deronda. His gaze lingered, not out of desire, but curiosity. He watched her hands—delicate, gloved in pale gray—as she placed her stake with firm confidence. Then he returned to her face, steady and focused, untouched by the crowd’s noise. She won. As she adjusted the coins pushed toward her, she glanced around—her eyes cold, almost aloof, as if masking a secret triumph. But then, her gaze met his.

She froze.

Not because she was seen, but because she *felt* seen—measured, judged, dismissed. She sensed that he saw her not as a person, but as a specimen—something inferior, something to be observed from above. The idea sparked a sudden, fierce resentment. It didn’t flush her cheeks—it drained the color from her lips. Yet she controlled herself, turning back to the game with no outward sign of emotion except that paleness.

But the moment had changed. Her luck turned. Her stake was swept away. Again. And again. Each loss doubled. She played not to win, but to defy. To prove she was not afraid. To show that she could endure.

Many watched her now. But only one mattered—Deronda. She never looked at him, yet she knew he hadn’t moved.

“Faites votre jeu,” the croupier called.

Her arm stretched out—once more. The last of her coins went down.

“Le jeu ne va plus.”

Five seconds later, she turned—and faced him.

A flicker of irony danced in his eyes. But it was better than indifference. At least he had noticed her. And despite his coolness, she couldn’t help wondering—did he admire her courage? Her spirit? She was young, beautiful, bold—not like the others. Not like the dull men who passed by, scowling as if gambling were a sin. He was different. And that difference made her want to know him.

That evening, the room was hotter, brighter. Gaslight sparkled off silks and feathers. Gwendolen Harleth, now dressed in sea-green robes and silver ornaments, with a feather trailing behind her like a serpent’s tail, walked beside her chaperone and a stiff, German-looking gentleman. They moved through the crowd, and Gwendolen was noticed.

“She’s striking,” someone murmured. “Unlike the others.”
“Yes, she’s become a serpent tonight—green and silver, winding her neck like one.”
“Oh, she always does something extraordinary.”
“Do you find her pretty, Mr. Vandernoodt?”
“Very. A man might risk hanging for her—I mean, a fool might.”
“You like a retroussé nose and narrow eyes?”
“When they go with such an *ensemble*.”
“The *ensemble du serpent*?”
“If you insist. Woman was tempted by a serpent—why not man?”

Gwendolen listened, but her thoughts were elsewhere. She was searching—not for compliments, but for him.

At last, she found her chance.

“Mr. Vandernoodt,” she said, her voice light, almost bored, “you know everyone. Who is that man near the door?”

“There are six near the door. Do you mean the old man with the George IV wig?”

“No. The dark-haired young man on the right. With that… dreadful expression.”

“Dreadful?” he laughed. “I think he’s quite remarkable.”

“But who is he?”

“He’s recently arrived with Sir Hugo Mallinger.”

“Sir Hugo Mallinger?”

“Yes. Have you heard of him?”

“No.” (She blushed faintly.)

“He has a house nearby, but never visits it. What was the name of that gentleman?”

“Deronda. Mr. Deronda.”

“What a lovely name! Is he English?”

“Yes. He’s said to be closely related to the baronet. You’re interested in him?”

“Yes. He’s not like other young men.”

“And you don’t admire young men in general?”

“Not at all. I always know what they’ll say. But I can’t guess what this Mr. Deronda would say. What *does* he say?”

“Nothing, mostly. I sat with his party last night. He didn’t speak. Didn’t smoke. Looked bored.”

“Another reason why I’d like to meet him. I’m always bored.”

“I should think he’d welcome an introduction. Shall I arrange it? Will you allow it, baroness?”

“Why not? Since he’s connected to Sir Hugo Mallinger. Though, Gwendolen, this is new for you—to be bored.”

“Exactly. That’s why I need excitement. If I stop gambling, I must break something—my arm, my collarbone. I must make something happen. Unless you’ll take me up the Matterhorn.”

“Perhaps meeting Mr. Deronda will do instead.”

“Perhaps.”

But the meeting never happened. Mr. Vandernoodt failed to bring them together. And when Gwendolen returned to her room, she found a letter—calling her home.

The drama ended not with a kiss, nor a confession, but with silence and distance. And yet, somewhere between the spinning wheel and the flicker of a glance, something had begun.

This man contrives a secret ’twixt us two,
That he may quell me with his meeting eyes—
Like one who quells a lioness at bay.

The letter lay on Gwendolen’s table when she returned from the springs, its edges crisp and unopened, yet already carrying within it the weight of a world turned upside down.

*DEAREST CHILD,* it began, *I have been expecting to hear from you for a week. In your last you said the Langens thought of leaving Leubronn and going to Baden. How could you be so thoughtless as to leave me in uncertainty about your address? I am in the greatest anxiety lest this should not reach you.*

Her breath caught. The words were not merely urgent—they were desperate.

*I must now entreat you to return as quickly as possible,* the letter continued. *If you spent all your money, it would be out of my power to send you any more, and you must not borrow from the Langens—for I could not repay them.*

She read on, her fingers tightening around the paper.

*This is the sad truth, my child—I wish I could prepare you for it better—but a dreadful calamity has befallen us all. Grapnell & Co. have failed for a million. We are totally ruined—your aunt Gascoigne as well as I. Only your uncle has his benefice, so that by putting down their carriage and getting interest for the boys, the family can go on. All the property our poor father saved for us goes to pay the liabilities. There is nothing I can call my own.*

Gwendolen stood frozen. The certainty of ease—the quiet assumption that life would always provide—had been her foundation. Now it was gone, like smoke. She had never known want; she had never even imagined it. And yet here it was, written plainly before her: poverty, dependence, the end of everything she had taken for granted.

She looked at herself in the mirror. Her hair was still coiled neatly, her face untouched by sleep or sorrow. But inside, something had cracked. Not grief—not yet—but a cold fury.

She sat down heavily on the red velvet sofa, picked up the letter again, and read it twice. Then let it fall to the floor. Her hands rested in her lap, clasped tightly. No tears came.

She did not weep for her mother. Fanny Davilow had lived a life of quiet constraint, of careful appearances and unspoken burdens. Gwendolen had never felt the need to pity her. If anything, she pitied herself. For wasn’t she the one meant for grandeur? Wasn’t she the one whose destiny should have been carved in gold and silk?

But instead—this.

A sharp anger flared. Not just at fate, but at chance. At the roulette table where she had lost her gains yesterday. If only luck had held—just one more spin—she might have won enough to carry them all through. She still had four napoleons. And jewelry—her Etruscan necklace, which she hadn’t worn since arriving. It could be sold. Easily. The Germans did it all the time. It was no shame.

She pictured it: ten louis in hand, a few more days at the table, a return of fortune. A triumph.

But then came the memory of Deronda—standing beside her, watching with those calm, knowing eyes. Not mocking, not laughing, but seeing. Seeing her pride, her recklessness, her fragile hope. And now, if she sold the necklace, the Langens would notice. They would ask questions. They would offer pity. She would be indebted. She could not bear it.

So she made her decision: sell the necklace early, tell them only that her mother wanted her home—no explanation—and leave that evening. No maid. No delays. Just a train to Brussels, and then straight to Offendene.

She didn’t go to bed. Instead, she lit every candle she could find and began to pack. Her movements were swift, precise. But in her mind, scenes unfolded: the farewell, the journey, the changed house, the silence of a home now stripped of comfort. And then—another scene: the roulette table again, the wheel spinning, the ball falling… and Deronda standing there, watching.

His presence haunted her. Not because he was handsome or clever—but because he seemed to know her too well. To see through her bravado. And now, if she stayed, she might lose everything again.

At midnight, she finished. By dawn, the room was empty except for a single pile of belongings. She dressed in her gray traveling gown, adjusted her felt hat, and stepped into the morning light.

She paused before the mirror. Her reflection stared back—pale, but composed. Her lips curved slightly, not in sadness, but in defiance. She took off her hat, leaned forward, and kissed the glass.

How could she believe in sorrow? She had strength. She had will. She would not break.

Madame von Langen never left her room before breakfast, so Gwendolen walked early, through the Obere Strasse, toward Mr. Wiener’s shop. She passed the *Czarina*, where Deronda stayed. She saw no one. But she knew—knew with a strange certainty—that someone watched.

She entered the shop with cool grace, offering the necklace without a word. Mr. Wiener examined it, nodded. “Fine stones,” he said. “Very good.”

She received nine louis. Only nine. The rest was lost to the dealer’s greed. But it was enough. Thirteen louis total—more than enough for the journey.

She turned homeward, entering the *salon* to wait for breakfast. She was tired. Hungry. But her mind was set. She would tell them she had heard from her mother. That was all.

Then—knock. A servant entered, holding a small packet addressed to Miss Harleth.

Gwendolen took it. Her pulse quickened. Before opening it, she *knew*.

Inside, wrapped in cambric, was her necklace. Beneath it, a scrap of torn paper:

> _A stranger who has found Miss Harleth’s necklace returns it to her with the hope that she will not again risk the loss of it._

She flushed. Not with gratitude—but with outrage.

It was him. Deronda. He had seen her. He had followed her. He had bought back the necklace and sent it back to her—like some moralizing guardian, pretending to do her a kindness while stripping her of dignity.

He had humiliated her. He had made her feel small.

Tears burned behind her eyes. But she blinked them away. She could not cry. Not now. Not in front of anyone.

She shoved the necklace, the note, the handkerchief—all—into her *nécessaire*. Pressed her handkerchief to her face. Waited. Breathed.

Then she walked into the *salon*, her composure restored.

“Mother wrote,” she said lightly. “She wants me home.”

They protested. Of course they did. But she refused help. Refused companionship. Would travel alone.

“I’ll be fine,” she said. “I’ve traveled before.”

And so, on Thursday evening, she left Leubronn.

On Saturday morning, she arrived at Offendene—home, for now. But soon, she would leave it too.

For the first time in her life, Gwendolen Harleth was not certain of what came next.

“Let no flower of the spring pass by us; let us crown ourselves with rosebuds before they be withered.”—BOOK OF WISDOM.

It was a pity Offendene had not been the home of Miss Harleth’s childhood, or cherished in her heart by family memories. A human life, I believe, ought to take root somewhere—in a corner of native soil where one can grow fond of the earth’s face, the labors men pursue, the voices and accents that linger in the air, and all those small things that make a place unmistakably one’s own amid the widening world. There should be a spot where early impressions are woven into affection, where familiarity with neighbors—even the dogs and donkeys—comes not from sentimentality, but as a quiet habit of the blood. At five years old, children aren’t ready to be citizens of the world, nor stirred by abstract ideals. That blind preference for milk we begin with is, in truth, a sign of how body and soul must first be nourished. The best way to learn astronomy, perhaps, is to think of the night sky as just a little cluster of stars belonging to your own backyard.

But such rootedness had never come to Gwendolen. It was only a year before her return from Leubronn that Offendene had been chosen as her mother’s home—not for its charm or history, but simply because it lay near Pennicote Rectory. And so Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen, and her four half-sisters (with governess and maid following behind) had arrived on a late October afternoon, when rooks cawed overhead and yellow elm leaves swirled through the air.

The season matched the house’s mood: an old, long red-brick building, too eagerly adorned with stone along every line—from the narrow windows to the large square portico. The stone nurtured a greenish lichen, the brick a dusty gray, so though the structure was rigidly rectangular, its expression softened with age. It stood behind three avenues cutting through a hundred yards of ancient plantation, yet one could wish it lifted upon a knoll, so it might look beyond its modest domain to distant thatched roofs, church towers, scattered homesteads, and the gentle rise of woods and parkland that made the landscape so lovely in this part of Wessex. But even tucked away in flat pastures, it offered one view—the grand sweep of chalk downs, their lofty curves rising like steadfast sentinels beneath shifting skies.

The house was barely large enough to be called a mansion, moderately rented, without a manor attached, and difficult to let due to its somber furnishings and faded upholstery. Yet inside and out, it bore none of the marks of retired tradespeople—no one would suspect such inhabitants. That certainty alone was worth much to tenants who valued tradition over new finery and lived on the edge of rank, where status mattered deeply. To settle in a house once occupied by dowager countesses gave Mrs. Davilow a quiet satisfaction, a sense of legitimacy she had long lacked. This, though mysterious to Gwendolen, became possible only after the death of her stepfather, Captain Davilow, who for nine years had visited his family only briefly and sporadically—just enough to keep them reconciled to his absences. But Gwendolen cared little for the reason; she welcomed the change with open arms. Her prospects had brightened dramatically. She had disliked their former life—drifting from foreign spas to Parisian apartments, always encountering unfamiliar furniture and strangers who treated her as inconsequential. Even two years at a fashionable school, where she was placed front and center during every performance, had only deepened her conviction that someone as exceptional as herself deserved more than ordinary circumstances or a social standing less than distinguished. Any fear of falling short was now gone, for she knew her mother would soon have an establishment of her own. On matters of birth, Gwendolen felt secure. She didn’t know how her maternal grandfather had earned the fortune passed to his daughters—he’d been a West Indian, which seemed to end the inquiry—but she did know her father’s family was so high-born they ignored her mother entirely. Still, her mother proudly kept a miniature of Lady Molly, a relic of that connection. Gwendolen might have learned more about her father had it not been for a moment when she was twelve.

Mrs. Davilow had brought out, as she rarely did, memorials of her first husband. While showing Gwendolen his portrait, she spoke with fervor, clearly expecting a special bond of sympathy: “Dear papa died when his little daughter was still in long clothes.”

Gwendolen, thinking instantly of the unlovable stepfather who had shaped most of her life while her dresses were still short, asked quietly, “Why did you marry again, mamma? It would have been nicer if you hadn’t.”

Mrs. Davilow flushed deeply. A tremor passed across her face. Without hesitation, she shut away the relics and said, in a tone rare for her, “You have no feeling, child!”

Gwendolen, who adored her mother, felt both hurt and ashamed. From then on, she dared not ask another question about her father.

This was not the only time she had drawn guilt upon herself. Usually, when possible, she slept in a small bed beside her mother’s. Mrs. Davilow’s affection clung most fiercely to her eldest daughter, born during her happier years. One night, suffering from pain, she realized the medicine meant to be by her bedside had been forgotten. She begged Gwendolen to get up and fetch it. But the young woman, snug and warm in her tiny bed like a contented infant, refused. She lay perfectly still, grumbling her refusal. Mrs. Davilow went without the remedy and said nothing. But the next day, Gwendolen sensed what her mother must be thinking—and tried to make amends with tender gestures, though they cost her nothing. Always the pet of the household, waited on by mother, sisters, governess, and maids as if she were a princess in exile, she found it hard to see others’ needs as more important than her own. When thwarted, she reacted with startled anger—once, in a fit of rage, strangling her sister’s canary for its relentless singing. She later bought a white mouse as recompense, and though she justified herself by claiming a heightened sensitivity—a mark of her superiority—the memory of that act still made her flinch. Gwendolen was not cruel by nature; she simply preferred easy penance. Now, at twenty, some of her raw energy had hardened into self-control, shielding her from public shame. Her fire remained, but beneath it now ran calculation.

On the day of their arrival at Offendene—unknown even to Mrs. Davilow, who had left its arrangements to her brother-in-law, Mr. Gascoigne—the party stepped from the carriage and gathered under the porch. The door stood open, revealing a stone hall and staircase draped in dark paintings, warmed by a glowing hearth. No one spoke. Mamma, the four sisters, and the governess all turned to Gwendolen, as if their hopes rested entirely on her judgment. The girls—Alice, sixteen, to Isabel, ten—were plain, their black dresses worn. Miss Merry was elderly, expressionless. Mrs. Davilow’s beauty, though faded, looked more poignant for the appeal she cast toward her daughter, who surveyed the house, grounds, and interior with swift, critical eyes. Picture a young racehorse in a paddock among untrimmed ponies and weary hacks.

“Well, dear,” Mrs. Davilow finally said, gently, “what do you think of the place?”

“I think it’s charming,” Gwendolen replied quickly. “Romantic. Anything delightful might happen here. It would make a perfect backdrop for any story. No one need be ashamed to live here.”

“There’s certainly nothing common about it.”

“Oh, it would suit fallen royalty—or any kind of noble poverty. We should properly have lived in splendor and descended to this. It would be wonderfully romantic. But I thought Uncle and Aunt Gascoigne would be here to meet us—and my cousin Anna,” she added, her tone shifting to surprise.

“We’re early,” Mrs. Davilow said, stepping into the hall. “And you expect Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne?” she asked the housekeeper.

“Yes, madam. They were here yesterday, giving orders about the fires and dinner. As for fires, I’ve had them lit in every room for a week. Everything’s aired. I wish the furniture paid better for all the cleaning it’s received, but I *think* the brasses have been well attended to. I *think* when Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne arrive, they’ll say nothing has been neglected. They’ll be here at five, for certain.”

This reassured Gwendolen, who could not bear indifference to their arrival. After glancing up the matted stone stairs, she returned, followed by the girls, and inspected each room off the hall—the dining room, dark oak and worn red satin damask, with a painting of snarling dogs above the sideboard and Christ breaking bread over the mantel; the library, thick with the scent of old leather; and finally, the drawing room, entered through a small antechamber crowded with antique curiosities.

“Mamma, mamma, come here!” Gwendolen called, Mrs. Davilow still speaking with the housekeeper. “There’s an organ! I’ll be Saint Cecilia. Someone should paint me as Saint Cecilia. Jocosa—let down my hair. See, mamma?”

She had removed hat and gloves, seated herself before the organ in a striking pose, gazing upward. Jocosa, submissive and melancholy, untied the comb holding her hair and released the cascade of light-brown locks, which fell far below her waist.

Mrs. Davilow smiled. “A charming picture, my dear!”

Gwendolen rose, laughing with delight. All seemed fitting in a new home so rich in potential.

“What a curious, quaint, picturesque room!” she exclaimed, looking around. “I love these embroidered chairs, the garlands on the wainscot, the pictures—any of them could be anything. That one with the ribs—nothing but ribs and darkness—I think it’s Spanish, mamma.”

“Oh, Gwendolen!” cried little Isabel, opening a hinged panel at the far end of the room.

Everyone rushed to see. Behind the panel was a painting of a dead face turned upward, from which a shadowy figure fled with outstretched arms.

“How horrible!” said Mrs. Davilow, recoiling.

Gwendolen shuddered silently. Isabel, plain and oddly perceptive, whispered, “You’ll never stay in this room alone, Gwendolen.”

“How dare you open things meant to be shut, you perverse child?” Gwendolen snapped, snatching the panel and slamming it shut. “There’s a lock—where’s the key? Find it, or have one made. No one opens it again. Or rather—bring the key to me.”

Her voice rang with command. Turning, flushed from the chill of the moment, she said, “Let’s go to our room, mamma.”

The housekeeper found the key in a nearby cabinet drawer and handed it to Bugle, the lady’s-maid, murmuring, “For Her Royal Highness.”

“I don’t understand you, Mrs. Startin,” Bugle replied, offended. She’d been busy upstairs during the scene.

“I mean the young lady who will rule us all—and well deserves it, for looks and bearing,” Mrs. Startin said, softening. “She’ll know which key it is.”

“Go check on the others, Bugle,” Gwendolen said as they entered their black-and-yellow bedroom, where a delicate white couch sat beside the ornate bed known as the best. “I’ll help mamma.”

Her first move was to the tall mirror between the windows, which reflected her and the room in full. Her mother sat nearby, also studying the image.

“That’s a flattering glass, Gwendolen,” she said. “Or is it the black and gold that suits you?”

Gwendolen stood obliquely, brushing back her hair with one hand. “I’d make a decent Saint Cecilia with white roses in my hair—only, what about my nose, mamma? Saints’ noses never turn up. I wish you’d given me yours—straight, perfect, good for any role. Mine’s just a happy nose. Not suited for tragedy.”

“Oh, my dear,” Mrs. Davilow sighed, throwing her bonnet onto the table. “Any nose will do for sorrow in this world.”

“Now, mamma,” Gwendolen said, turning away from the mirror, vexed. “Don’t start being dull here. It ruins everything. Life could be so happy now. What’s troubling you?”

“Nothing, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, beginning to undress. “Just seeing you happy is enough for me.”

“But you should be happy too,” Gwendolen insisted, helping her with gentle touches. “Can no one be happy after youth? You’ve made me feel sometimes that nothing matters. With the girls so troublesome, Jocosa so stiff and ugly, everything makeshift—and you so tired—what was the point of my being anything? But now you *could* be happy.”

“So I shall, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, patting her daughter’s cheek.

“Yes, really,” Gwendolen pressed. “Not just pretending. Look at your hands—so beautiful. Anyone can see you were more beautiful than I am.”

“No, no,” Mrs. Davilow said. “I was heavier. Never half as charming.”

“But why does charm matter if it ends in dullness and indifference? Is that what marriage always becomes?”

“No, child,” she said. “Marriage is the happiest state for a woman—as I trust you’ll prove.”

“I won’t accept it if it isn’t happy,” Gwendolen declared. “I’m determined to be happy. At least, not to waste my life like others do—doing nothing remarkable. I’ve decided not to let anyone control me anymore. Here’s warm water for you, mamma,” she added, removing her own dress and waiting for her mother to wind her hair.

Silence fell for a moment. Then Mrs. Davilow, coiling her daughter’s hair, said softly, “I’ve never crossed you, Gwendolen.”

“You often want me to do things I dislike.”

“You mean teaching Alice?”

“Yes. I’ve done it because you asked. But why should I? It bores me. She’s slow—no ear for music, language, anything. It would be better for her to be ignorant, mamma. It’s her *role*. She’d play it well.”

“That’s harsh, Gwendolen,” Mrs. Davilow said. “She’s so kind to you, waits on you hand and foot.”

“I don’t see why it’s wrong to name things rightly. The hardship is mine—wasting my time.”

“Then let me fix your hair, mamma.”

“We must hurry. Your uncle and aunt will be here soon. For heaven’s sake, don’t be scornful to them, my dear. Or to Anna. Promise me, Gwendolen. You know—you can’t expect Anna to match you.”

“I don’t want her to,” Gwendolen said, tossing her head and smiling. The conversation ended there.

When Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne and their daughter arrived, Gwendolen was anything but scornful. She greeted them with grace and charm. She was reintroducing herself to relatives she hadn’t seen since sixteen, and she was resolved—yes, resolved—that they would admire her.

Mrs. Gascoigne resembled her sister, but was darker, slimmer, her face untouched by grief, her movements sharper, her expression more alert—fit for a rector’s wife accustomed to quiet authority. Their deepest similarity was a tendency to defer, to imitate, to obey. But circumstance had led them to different lives. The younger sister had been reckless in marriage; the elder believed herself blessed, and her pliability had shaped her into surprising firmness. Many of her opinions—on church governance, Archbishop Laud—seemed too fixed to have formed naturally, suggesting instead a wifely receptiveness. And indeed, her husband’s influence was strong. He had virtues: agreeable, capable, admired. His flaws leaned toward success, not failure.

One of his strengths was his presence—perhaps even more striking at fifty-seven than earlier. His face bore no clerical stiffness, no affected ease. In his Inverness cape, he looked merely a gentleman—handsome, dark-eyed, with a nose that began aquiline but straightened abruptly, iron-gray hair. Perhaps his freedom from professional pretense came from having once been Captain Gaskin—only recently taking holy orders and a diphthong before marrying Miss Armyn. If criticized for inadequate preparation, friends might ask: who made a better figure in clerical robes? Who preached with greater authority? He had a natural gift for administration—tolerant of opinions and conduct because he felt able to manage them. He smiled at tastes he didn’t share—floriculture, antiquarianism—while preferring military campaigns or political speculation. His thinking had settled into an ecclesiastical tone, not modern Anglican, but what he called “sound English”—clear, free of nonsense. He saw religion in daylight, balanced against other truths. No magistrate carried more weight at sessions, nor more practical wisdom in worldly affairs. The worst charge against him was worldliness—though he never abandoned the poor, his friendships favored those likely to benefit his six sons and two daughters. Some bitter souls in Wessex once remarked his views shifted with his interests. But cheerful, successful worldliness appears more selfish than the acrid, failed kind—whose secret is summed up in the words: “Sold, but not paid for.”

Gwendolen wondered why she hadn’t remembered how impressive her uncle was. At sixteen, she’d been too careless to notice. Now, it thrilled her to have a dignified male relative close by—to break the insipid femininity of her life. She didn’t intend to be controlled by him, but she relished the idea of him being proud to introduce her as his niece. And already, signs pointed to pride. He looked at her with admiration as he said, “You’ve outgrown Anna, my dear,” placing his arm gently around his daughter, whose shy face was a miniature of his own. “She’s not quite a year younger, but her growth is finished. I hope you’ll be excellent companions.”

He glanced between them, and though he might have seen Anna’s limitations, he also saw that her timidity and smallness appealed to a different taste—one not drawn to Gwendolen’s boldness. The girls wouldn’t be rivals. Gwendolen understood this, and kissed Anna warmly, saying, “A companion is exactly what I want. I’m so glad we’re here. And mamma will be much happier now she’s near you, Aunt.”

CHAPTER IV. “_Gorgibus._— * * * Je te dis que le mariage est une chose sainte et sacrée: et que c’est faire en honnêtes gens, que de débuter par là.”
“_Madelon._—Mon Dieu! que si tout le monde vous ressemblait, un roman serait bientôt fini! La belle chose que ce serait, si d’abord Cyrus épousait Mandane, et qu’Aronce de plain-pied fût marié à Clélie! * * * Laissez-nous faire à loisir le tissu de notre roman, et n’en pressez pas tant la conclusion.”
MOLIÈRE. _Les Précieuses Ridicules._

It was not surprising that the rector of Pennicote, in his thoughtful reflections on life and character, should see in Gwendolen Harleth a girl destined for something extraordinary—a brilliant marriage, perhaps, such as would bring honor to her family and grace to any social circle. Why should he be expected to think otherwise? In an age where a woman’s future was often measured by the rank and fortune of her husband, it was natural enough for him to hope that his niece would rise above the ordinary, especially when she already stood out so clearly from those around her. And yet, though he entertained such expectations, his feelings were rooted in kindness rather than ambition. He did not wish her to suffer or sacrifice; he simply wanted her to shine where she belonged—among the best company, in the most favorable light.

His intentions matched perfectly with Gwendolen’s own desires. But let no one imagine that she saw marriage as her ultimate goal, the grand climax of her existence. While she accepted that she would marry someday, she had no particular longing for it. The idea of becoming a wife, bound by duties and children and domestic routine, held little appeal. To her, marriage seemed more like a quiet surrender—something necessary, yes, but hardly glorious. She had watched others settle into it, and what she saw was not enchantment, but weariness. Women became pale, preoccupied, their spirits dulled by repetition and constraint. Marriage might elevate one socially, but it could never fulfill the deeper hunger within her—the need to lead, to command attention, to shape her world rather than merely inhabit it.

She meant to be admired, certainly, and to be desired—not for herself alone, but as a symbol of power. To be pursued, to be envied, to be spoken of with breathless admiration—that was a kind of triumph. But to be confined by a man’s will, to lose her independence behind closed doors, that was another matter entirely. She would not become a mere ornament in someone else’s home. No, she intended to live vividly, to act decisively, to make every moment count. Her ambitions were not grand in the way of political influence or intellectual reform, but they were no less fierce. She wanted to do what pleased her—and to do it in a way that made others feel the force of her presence.

“Gwendolen will not rest until she has the world at her feet,” said Miss Merry, the gentle governess, using words that had long since lost their edge through overuse. Yet even in their exaggeration, there was truth. For who among us does not speak of having “the world at one’s feet” when meaning only a few flattering attentions, a place at a fashionable dinner, or a nod from a titled neighbor? Still, for Gwendolen, such phrases carried real weight. They hinted at a destiny beyond the ordinary, a life lived on a higher plane.

Others drifted through life, shaped by circumstance, pulled this way and that by forces they could not control. Not she. She refused to be sacrificed to lesser people, to be treated as a passive object of affection or duty. She would seize her chances, use her talents, and master the world around her—not through force, but through charm, intelligence, and sheer self-assurance.

Her situation at Offendene offered little in the way of dramatic opportunity. Lady Brackenshaw’s notice, the archery club, dinners with the Arrowpoints—these were modest pleasures, nothing more. Yet Gwendolen did not rely on her surroundings. Her confidence came from within. She believed herself well prepared for whatever life might bring. Her education had been thorough enough, though not profound. In school, her quick mind absorbed facts and rules without question, shielding her from any sense of ignorance. Beyond that, novels, plays, and poetry had filled in the gaps, giving her a broad understanding of human nature and society.

Her French and music were polished, her manners impeccable. And then there was that rare gift—what some call instinct, others intuition—the ability to judge any subject with ease and authority, simply because she chose to engage with it. It was this innate certainty, this feeling of being naturally equipped, that made her believe she could handle anything.

Of course, there were many things she found dull—subjects too dry, too slow, too irrelevant to her spirit. But she wouldn’t have felt helpless if they arose in conversation. She knew how to listen, how to respond, how to turn even the most tedious topic into a moment of elegance.

No one had ever challenged her superiority. From the moment she arrived at Offendene, everyone asked, *What will Gwendolen think?* If the footman clumped loudly down the hall, it was because “This will never do for Miss Harleth.” If the laundry was imperfect, the maid fretted: “This won’t suit Miss Harleth.” Even when the fireplace smoked, Mrs. Davilow apologized, fearing Gwendolen’s discomfort.

And when she finally appeared at breakfast—late, always late, her golden-brown hair tumbling freely over her shoulders, her eyes bright and sharp beneath long lashes—no one dared complain. Instead, the staff scrambled to serve her coffee piping hot, toast crisp, everything perfect. She was the princess, the guest of honor, the one whose comfort mattered above all.

Why was she treated so? Was it only her beauty? Her grace? Her position as the eldest daughter, born into a family burdened by misfortune, whose mother still bore guilt over the harshness of her second husband?

Perhaps. But consider this: I have seen similar deference paid to men and women far less attractive, far less remarkable in manner or birth. Some were common, even unremarkable. Yet they commanded the same attention—because they demanded it. Because they had a quiet, relentless will, a refusal to accept anything less than what they wanted. And if they didn’t get it? Then they threatened consequences—real or imagined. Fear, after all, is a powerful motivator.

So maybe Gwendolen’s power wasn’t just in her looks or her lineage. Maybe it was in her spirit—the unyielding belief in her own worth, the courage to assert it, and the subtle ability to inspire both fear and affection. People loved her, yes—but they also respected her, even feared her. And that fear was not cold or cruel, but tinged with admiration. For she was not simply one thing. She could be tender and fierce, playful and commanding, generous and demanding—all at once. Like light refracted through a prism, her character shimmered with contradictions.

Macbeth once declared that no man could be both loyal and treacherous in the same instant. But that was about action, not emotion. A moment can hold many truths. One can desire loyalty while secretly planning betrayal. One can feel remorse even as the knife rises. And so, too, Gwendolen could be beloved and feared, admired and challenged, all at once—because she was not merely a woman, but a force.

CHAPTER V. “Her wit Values itself so highly, that to her All matter else seems weak.” —_Much Ado About Nothing._

Gwendolen’s arrival in the neighborhood had quickly lived up to her uncle’s expectations. From Brackenshaw Castle to the Firs at Wanchester—where Mr. Quallon, the banker, maintained a grand and generous household—she was greeted with open admiration. Even those ladies who didn’t quite warm to her found comfort in having such a striking young woman among their guests. After all, hostesses who entertain often must assemble their parties like ministers forming cabinets: not always by personal affection, but by necessity and balance. And since Gwendolen came with Mrs. Davilow, whose quiet grace made an ideal chaperone, and Mr. Gascoigne, whose charm and reputation were universally welcome, no one hesitated to invite her.

One of her earliest invitations came from Quetcham Hall—a house where she was not entirely beloved, yet still welcomed. The dinner party there served as something of a formal introduction to the local society. With thirty guests carefully chosen across generations, few prominent families could be left out. As Gwendolen moved through the long succession of rooms—each glowing with light and adorned with flowers—her presence stood out. Dressed in white, she seemed to float through the halls, first a slender silhouette, then gradually emerging into full view under the soft illumination. It was a scene she’d never experienced before, and she felt a quiet triumph in it: anyone seeing her for the first time might have assumed she belonged to this world of elegance and ceremony. Her cousin Anna, more accustomed to such settings, looked almost flustered, like a timid creature suddenly placed under bright lights.

“Who is that with Gascoigne?” asked the archdeacon, pausing mid-discussion on military tactics—an unusual interruption from a man of the cloth.

On the other side of the room, his son—already known for his scholarly emendations of Greek texts—muttered, “By George! Who’s that girl with the astonishingly well-set head and jolly figure?”

To those with kinder eyes, however, it was frustrating to watch how Gwendolen overshadowed others. Miss Lawe, daughter of Lady Lawe and generally considered beautiful, suddenly appeared broad and dull beside her. Miss Arrowpoint, also in white, looked instantly like a poorly framed photograph—one where only the skirt seemed worth charging extra for.

Miss Arrowpoint was well-liked for her gentle demeanor and modest way of bearing her wealth, which softened the eccentricities of her mother. Yet now, standing next to Gwendolen, she seemed diminished. There was an awkwardness in the contrast: Gwendolen, though not classically beautiful upon closer inspection, carried herself with a style that commanded attention—and then faded just as quickly once the first impression wore off.

Later, Mrs. Arrowpoint confided to Mrs. Vulcany, “She’s not really so handsome when you look closely. It’s a certain manner she has—striking at first, but afterward… less agreeable.”

Indeed, Gwendolen had unintentionally offended her hostess, though not from malice. Mrs. Arrowpoint, though not vindictive, had sensitivities. She was a woman shaped by several contradictions that the townsfolk liked to link together: once a city heiress from a business empire (wet or dry, they didn’t care), she carried a squat frame, a sharp, parrot-like voice, and a towering hairstyle that seemed designed to defy gravity. These traits made her seem ridiculous to many, and so it was natural, they thought, that she should dabble in literature.

But such assumptions were flawed. Wealthy daughters weren’t always ungraceful; pretty women could have harsh voices; and literary ambition knew no bounds of body type. Still, Gwendolen, who had a keen eye for absurdity in others but was generally kind to those who made life pleasant for her, meant to win Mrs. Arrowpoint over. She believed that showing interest in someone’s passions would earn goodwill.

Yet self-assurance often misjudges others. Like the rich speaking softly to the poor, or the young raising their voices to elders, assuming them deaf or dim-witted, Gwendolen unconsciously assumed that because Mrs. Arrowpoint was comical, she must also lack depth. So she played her part without realizing every nuance was being noted.

“You’re fond of books, music, riding, and archery, I hear,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said, approaching her after dinner for a private moment. “Catherine will be delighted to have such a sympathetic neighbor.”

The words were polite, delivered in a low, melodic tone—but with a sudden twang that gave Gwendolen a fleeting sense of superiority as she replied gracefully, “It’s I who am fortunate. Miss Arrowpoint will teach me what real music is. I shall be entirely a learner. I’ve heard she’s a thorough musician.”

“Catherine has had every advantage,” Mrs. Arrowpoint continued. “We have Herr Klesmer here now—perhaps you know his work. I’ll introduce you. You sing, I believe. Catherine plays three instruments, but doesn’t sing. We hope you’ll let us hear you. I understand you’re accomplished.”

“Oh, no!” Gwendolen laughed. “‘Die Kraft ist schwach, allein die Lust ist gross,’ as Mephistopheles says.”

Ah, you study Goethe,” Mrs. Arrowpoint smiled. “Young ladies are so advanced these days. I suppose you’ve read everything.”

“No, truly,” Gwendolen said, earnestly. “I’d love to know what to read. I’ve been looking through the library at Offendene, but it’s all musty—leaves stick together. I wish I could write books to amuse myself, like you do! How wonderful it must be to create your own stories instead of reading others’—home-made books must be so delightful.”

For a moment, Mrs. Arrowpoint’s gaze sharpened—but then Gwendolen added, “I’d give anything to write a book!”

“And why shouldn’t you?” Mrs. Arrowpoint encouraged. “Just begin as I did. Pen, ink, paper—everyone has them. I’ll send you all my writings.”

“Thank you. I’d love to read them. Knowing authors must give you a special understanding of their work—you’d know which parts were funny and which serious. I often laugh in the wrong place.”

Gwendolen paused, sensing danger, and quickly added, “In Shakespeare, of course—great writers we can never see. But I always want to know more than the books tell.”

“If you’re interested in any of my subjects, I’ll lend you manuscripts,” Mrs. Arrowpoint offered—while Gwendolen felt herself in the position of the young lady who claimed to adore potted sprats.

“These are things I may publish someday,” she went on. “Friends have urged me. One doesn’t like to be stubborn. My Tasso, for example—I could have doubled its size.”

“I adore Tasso,” Gwendolen declared.

“Well, you shall have all my papers. So many have written about him—but they’re all wrong. About his madness, his feelings for Leonora, the real reason he was imprisoned, Leonora’s character—she was cold-hearted, I say. Otherwise, she’d have married him despite her brother. I differ from everyone.”

“How fascinating!” Gwendolen said. “I love differing from everyone. I think it’s stupid to agree. That’s the worst thing about writing opinions—you make people agree with you.”

Mrs. Arrowpoint’s suspicion flickered again. But Gwendolen looked utterly innocent, continuing with a docile air: “I know nothing of Tasso except _Gerusalemme Liberata_, which we memorized at school.”

“Ah, his life is more interesting than his poetry,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said. “I’ve reconstructed the early part as a romance. His father Bernardo—there’s much that must be true.”

“Imagination is often truer than fact,” Gwendolen declared decisively—though she couldn’t have explained the words if asked.

“I’d love to learn about Tasso—and especially his madness. I suppose poets are always a little mad.”

“To be sure—‘the poet’s eye in a fine frenzy rolling.’ Someone said of Marlowe, ‘For that fine madness still he did maintain, Which always should possess the poet’s brain.’”

“But it wasn’t always noticed, was it?” Gwendolen asked innocently. “Some rolled their eyes in private. Mad people are often very clever.”

A shadow crossed Mrs. Arrowpoint’s face—but the men entered, halting any further tension.

“Ah, here comes Herr Klesmer,” she said, rising, and soon introduced him to Gwendolen.

Herr Klesmer was a striking figure—Germanic, Slavic, Semitic all at once—grand features, flowing brown hair, dark eyes behind spectacles. His English was fluent, nearly flawless, but now softened by a faint silliness, perhaps born of desire to please a beauty. Music began soon after.

Miss Arrowpoint and Herr Klesmer performed a four-handed piece on two pianos—long enough to test patience, and convincing enough to show Miss Arrowpoint’s mastery. Gwendolen, though aware of her own limitations, remained undeterred. Everyone wanted to hear her sing—especially Mr. Arrowpoint, a gentleman of impeccable manners and cigars, who led her to the piano with ease.

Herr Klesmer closed the instrument, smiled, and stepped back slightly to observe.

Gwendolen sang without hesitation. Her voice was a strong soprano—some said like Jenny Lind’s—good ear, steady pitch. She sang a Belini aria she knew well. The audience murmured approval. Mr. Arrowpoint called it “charming”—a sentiment echoed around the room.

But Herr Klesmer stood frozen—like a statue wearing spectacles. Not a word.

She was urged to sing again. She hesitated, then approached him, smiling, “It would be cruel to a great musician. You can’t possibly enjoy amateur singing.”

“No, truly,” Herr Klesmer said suddenly, his voice clipped, Germanic, abrupt—unlike before. “That makes nothing. It is always acceptable to see you sing.”

An unexpected assertion of superiority—especially in the wake of Teutonic influence. Gwendolen flushed, but kept her composure.

Miss Arrowpoint, nearby and observant, stepped in kindly. “Imagine what I endure with this professor! He barely tolerates our English music. We suffer his severity—but use it to learn what’s worst about ourselves. It’s comforting to know that. And when everyone else admires, one can bear it.”

“I’d be grateful if he told me the worst,” Gwendolen said, recovering. “I dare say I’ve been ill taught—and have no talent—only a liking for music.”

“That’s true,” Herr Klesmer said quietly. “You haven’t been well taught. But you’re not without gifts. You sing in tune. You have a fair voice. But you produce notes poorly. And the music you sing is beneath you. It’s a form of melody expressing a childish culture—dawdling, cloying, repetitive. It lacks passion, conflict, depth. No cries of deep emotion. No sense of the universal. It shrinks men as they listen. Sing something larger. Then I shall see.”

“Oh, not now—later,” Gwendolen said, heart sinking. For a woman aiming to lead, this first encounter was unsettling.

But Miss Arrowpoint helped, saying, “Yes, later. I always need half an hour to gather courage after criticism from Herr Klesmer. Let’s ask him to play now—he must show us what good music sounds like.”

Herr Klesmer obliged with a fantasia of his own—_Freudvoll, Leidvoll, Gedankenvoll_—a vast exploration of subtle melodies. His fingers danced with imperious magic, sending shivers through keys and hammers, coaxing trembling, lingering tones from the piano. Gwendolen, though wounded, felt the power. Her mortification turned into excitement, lifting her above her own performance. Her eyes brightened, cheeks flushed, tongue ready for mischief.

“I wish you’d sing again, Miss Harleth,” said young Clintock, the archdeacon’s son, who had escorted her to dinner. “That’s the kind of music for me. I can’t follow this high-flown playing. It’s like a jar of leeches—no beginning, no end. I could listen to your singing all day.”

“Yes, let’s have another song,” Mrs. Arrowpoint added politely. “Something lighter.”

“That must be because you’re in a childish state of culture,” Gwendolen said, ignoring her, smiling up at Clintock. “I’ve just learned that. I’ve been taught how bad my taste is—and I’m feeling growing pains. They’re never pleasant.”

Mrs. Arrowpoint didn’t miss the rudeness—but merely said, “Well, we won’t press anything disagreeable.”

As conversation burst forth, she sat back, relieved to be spared further duty.

“I’m glad you like this neighborhood,” Clintock said, pleased to stand near her.

“Exceedingly. There seems to be a little of everything—and not much of anything.”

“That’s rather ambiguous praise.”

“Not with me. I like a little of everything. A little absurdity, for instance, is amusing. I’m thankful for a few odd people—but too many would be a bore.”

Mrs. Arrowpoint, listening, sensed a new edge in Gwendolen’s voice—and renewed doubt about her interest in Tasso’s madness.

“I think there should be more croquet,” Clintock said. “I’m usually away, but if I were here, I’d start a croquet club. You’re one of the archers, I think. But croquet is the game of the future. It needs writing up. One of our best men wrote a poem on it—four cantos—as good as Pope. I want him to publish it. You’ve never read anything better.”

“I’ll study croquet tomorrow. I’ll take it up instead of singing.”

“No, no—don’t give up singing. But do try croquet. I’ll send you Jenning’s poem if you like. I have a manuscript copy.”

“Is he a great friend of yours?”

“Well, rather.”

“Oh—if he’s only *rather*, I think I’ll decline. Or if you send it, will you promise not to quiz me on it? Because it’s not so easy to judge a poem without reading it—like judging a sermon without hearing it.”

Mrs. Arrowpoint thought firmly: “This girl is double-tongued and satirical. I shall be on my guard.”

Still, Gwendolen continued to receive polite attentions at Quetcham—not just because hospitality depends on more than personal preference, but because the brief piano scene had stirred a quiet sympathy in Miss Arrowpoint. Though her mother was otherwise occupied, Miss Arrowpoint managed the social arrangements—and now, gently, began to include Gwendolen in their circle.

“Do you think you have humbled me by teaching me that the earth revolves around the sun? I swear I esteem myself no less.” —FONTENELLE: _Pluralité des Mondes_.

Gwendolen felt a new kind of ache in her heart after reading those words. She would never have admitted how deeply she regretted not having had Miss Arrowpoint’s musical training—how much she envied the ease with which that quiet, unassuming girl could question Herr Klesmer’s judgment with the confidence of true mastery. And even more, she couldn’t confess—least of all to herself—that each time they met, Miss Arrowpoint stirred something unfamiliar within her: not jealousy over wealth or beauty, but irritation at the very idea that such an ordinary-looking woman—slight, average height, small features, pale eyes, sallow skin—could possess such a rare depth of understanding, such refined taste and precision in music, such unwavering standards that made admiration impossible to force, and respect hard to avoid.

This twenty-four-year-old young lady, who might have passed unnoticed in any crowd if not for her name, seemed to carry a silent conviction that Gwendolen’s talents were merely conventional—something that, though always masked by kindness, only added to the sting. Yet Gwendolen refused to dwell on anything that cast doubt on her own worth.

Herr Klesmer, the great musician who had so unexpectedly expanded her world, was not always near. His frequent trips between London and Quetcham soon became a hope rather than a fact—a chance, perhaps, to win his deeper admiration. In the meantime, the pleasure she brought to gatherings at Brackenshaw Castle, the Firs, and other places helped restore her composure. She found comfort in approval, not criticism, and unlike those few souls who crave perfection beyond what others demand, she was content with recognition as it came. It might have been rash to claim she was inwardly exceptional—but then again, wasn’t she? Her grace, her boldness, the sharp edge she gave to common ambition—all these set her apart. Still, she had no grand theories about life or society. She mocked reformers and intellectual women alike, distancing herself from them with irony, yet she craved to be seen as different.

She longed to feel herself extraordinary—yet her vision of greatness was shaped by genteel romance: a heroine whose journal brims with vague rebellion and originality, while her real life dances within the limits of fashion. If she ever stumbled into danger, the tragedy would lie partly in her satin shoes—because nature and society had woven invisible nets around her dreams. A soul burning with longing for meaning, ready to consume the world as fuel, remained trapped by the delicate wires of propriety, doing nothing truly daring.

And now, even in the fresh winter at Offendene, she sensed this fate closing in. She knew she didn’t want the same life as most young ladies. But she had no clear plan for what else she might do. What acts would prove her freedom? Offendene offered a lovely backdrop—if only something remarkable happened there. But mostly, the neighborhood failed her.

Beyond the initial dazzle of her beauty, her early social outings offered little excitement. She returned home from brief forays into wit and satire—like the ones that had offended Mrs. Arrowpoint—and filled the days with childish pastimes. The most defiant thing she could do was skip Alice’s lessons (reasoning that Alice would likely thrive on ignorance), and instead employ her with Miss Merry and the maid to help prepare elaborate costumes for future charades or plays—costumes she insisted on having ready, even if she had to make them happen by will alone.

She’d never acted before—only posed in *tableaux vivants* at school—but she was convinced she could perform with power. Having seen Rachel at the Théâtre Français and heard her mother speak of her, Gwendolen often dreamed of becoming an actress like her—especially since she believed herself more beautiful than the thin Jewish star. For now, the rainy days before Christmas were spent crafting Greek, Oriental, and hybrid costumes, in which she dramatically posed and declaimed lines before a domestic audience, including even the housekeeper, whom she once pressed into service to swell the applause. But when the housekeeper dared say, “Miss Harleth looks far more like a queen in her own dress than in that baggy thing with her arms bare,” she was never invited back.

One day, after showing off her Greek costume to Anna and acting out fragments of tragic scenes, Gwendolen asked, “Do I look as well as Rachel, mamma?”

“You have better arms than Rachel,” replied Mrs. Davilow. “Your arms would suit any role, Gwen. But your voice isn’t as deep or tragic.”

“I can make it deeper if I choose,” said Gwendolen, half-serious. Then, decisively: “I believe a higher voice is more tragic—it’s more feminine. And the more feminine a woman is, the more tragic it seems when she does desperate things.”

“There may be something in that,” Mrs. Davilow murmured, unconvinced. “But I don’t see the point in making one’s blood creep. And if anything terrible must be done, I’d rather leave it to men.”

“Oh, mamma, you’re dreadfully dull! As if all the great poetic criminals weren’t women! Men are just cautious creatures.”

“Well, dear,” her mother said gently, “and you—who are afraid to be alone at night—I don’t think you’d be very bold in crime, thank God.”

“I’m not talking about reality, mamma,” Gwendolen snapped. Then, as her mother left the room, she turned quickly to Anna. “Ask Uncle to let us put on some charades at the rectory. Mr. Middleton and Warham could join us—just for practice. Mamma says it wouldn’t do to have Mr. Middleton rehearsing here. He’s stiff, but we could give him good parts. Do ask—or I’ll do it myself.”

“Oh, not until Rex comes,” Anna replied. “He’s so clever, and such a dear old thing—he could play Napoleon looking across the sea. He really looks like Napoleon. Rex can do anything.”

“I don’t believe a word of it,” Gwendolen laughed. “He’ll turn out like those awful blue-and-yellow watercolors you hang in your room and worship.”

“Very well, you’ll see,” Anna said firmly. “It’s not that I know what clever means, but he already has a scholarship, and papa says he’ll get a fellowship. Nobody’s better at games. He’s smarter than Mr. Middleton, and everyone but you thinks Mr. Middleton is clever.”

“So he may be in a dark-lantern sort of way,” Gwendolen said. “But he *is* a stick. If he had to say, ‘Perdition catch my soul, but I do love her,’ he’d say it exactly as if he were reciting, ‘Here endeth the second lesson.’”

“Oh, Gwendolen!” Anna gasped, shocked. “And it’s unkind to talk like that—especially since he admires you so much. I heard Warham tell mamma once, ‘Middleton is regularly spooney upon Gwendolen.’ She was furious, but I know what it means—it’s what they say at college for being in love.”

“How can I help it?” Gwendolen said, dismissive. “Perdition catch my soul if I love *him*.”

“No, of course not,” Anna said softly. “Papa wouldn’t want it. And he’s leaving soon. But it makes me sad when you make fun of him.”

“What would you do if I made fun of Rex?” Gwendolen teased.

“Oh, Gwendolen, dear—you *won’t*, will you?” Anna pleaded, tears welling. “I couldn’t bear it. There’s nothing in him to mock. But you might find things out. No one ever laughed at Mr. Middleton before you. Everyone said he was handsome, polite, proper. I’ve always been afraid of him because of his learning, his square coat, and his being the bishop’s nephew. But you won’t ridicule Rex—promise me.” Her eyes begged.

“You’re a dear little coz,” Gwendolen said, gently touching Anna’s chin. “I never want to hurt you. Especially if Rex helps bring everything to life—charades and all.”

When Rex finally arrived, he brought light and energy to Offendene and the rectory. His cheerful cooperation with Gwendolen’s plans left no room for mockery—only playful teasing, which he enjoyed. He was a fine, open-hearted young man, with a face like his father’s and Anna’s, but softer and broader. Full of healthy joy, he found vice boring, not tempting. To him, bad habits were “what some fellows did”—stupid stuff best avoided. He loved Anna deeply, though his pleasures outside her were greater than hers. And he had never known a stronger affection.

The cousins were inseparable—mostly at Offendene, where Gwendolen reigned freely. Whatever she wanted became their shared purpose. The charades took place as planned, along with impromptu scenes she hadn’t imagined. Rehearsals and performances happened at Offendene, Mrs. Davilow now allowing Mr. Middleton to join—especially since Rex was there, and Middleton’s presence was essential. Warham, busy preparing for the Indian Civil Service exams under a strict tutor, had no time and was too depressed to participate.

Mr. Middleton played solemn roles, flattered by Gwendolen’s praise of his stillness. At first uneasy about her closeness to Rex, he soon reassured himself that such friendship meant no serious attachment. In fact, he began to think her formal treatment of him was a sign of favor—encouraging him to act before leaving Pennicote, though he’d planned to wait until his position was secure. Gwendolen, aware of his devotion, cared little—she simply liked being admired. She looked at him with calm indifference, feeding his hopes by avoiding dramatic interaction, knowing that meaning depends entirely on interpretation.

Some might have thought it odd that a reserved Anglican clergyman, sensitive to sacred things, rarely laughing, and uncomfortable with plain language, would consider marrying a girl so bold in mockery and lacking the quiet grace expected of a clergyman’s wife. Or that a man steeped in theology would expect harmony with a restless spirit like Gwendolen’s. But why must we explain why people behave differently from what others predict? The fault lies with those who assume too much.

As for Rex, who might have pitied poor Middleton if he’d known his inner struggle, he was too lost in first love to notice anything else. He didn’t observe Gwendolen—he felt her presence. Her words, her movements, even her absence—he registered them all. Within two weeks, he was utterly devoted, seeing no obstacles. His love was proof enough of hers, as natural as breathing. She sang for him whenever he wished, rode with him gladly—even if his borrowed horses were absurd—and appreciated Anna. Nothing seemed missing. That she should make a grand match had never crossed his mind. He had no vanity—only the certainty that Gwendolen was perfect, and that her perfection was part of the goodness he’d always known.

One moment during their rehearsals revealed a side of her he hadn’t expected—a depth of feeling hidden beneath her usual courage and high spirits.

After many practices, they decided to invite a select group to Offendene to watch their performance. Anna surprised everyone with her charm—her simple roles were perfectly played, hinting at quiet intelligence beneath her innocence. Mr. Middleton, though awkward, succeeded by not trying to be funny. The main challenge was Gwendolen’s wish to wear her Greek costume. No charade theme came to mind that allowed her to strike a statue-like pose. Racine was out—no one could speak French. Improvisation risked burlesque. And Mr. Gascoigne forbade acting from actual plays, though he permitted charades in his sister-in-law’s home.

Everyone wanted to please her. Rex suggested ending with a tableau where her dignity wouldn’t be disrupted by speech. Gwendolen agreed—now only the choice remained.

“Something pleasant, children, I beg of you,” Mrs. Davilow said. “No Greek wickedness.”

“It’s no worse than Christian wickedness, mamma,” Gwendolen replied, recalling her earlier mention of Rachel-like heroines.

“And less scandalous,” Rex added. “Besides, it’s all history. How about Briseis being taken away? I’ll be Achilles, and you’ll look back at me—after the print at the rectory.”

“That would suit me well,” Gwendolen said, accepting.

But later, she changed her mind. “No. It won’t do. We need three men in proper costume, or it’ll be ridiculous.”

“I’ve got it,” Rex said, thinking. “Hermione as the statue in *The Winter’s Tale*? I’ll be Leontes, Miss Merry Paulina—each on one side. Our clothes won’t matter,” he laughed. “It’ll be more romantic if Leontes looks like Napoleon and Paulina like a modern spinster.”

They chose Hermione. All agreed age didn’t matter. Gwendolen insisted, however, that instead of just a tableau, there should be a brief scene—enough to signal the music, then she would step down and advance. Leontes, instead of embracing her, would kneel and kiss the hem of her robe—then the curtain would fall. The antechamber with folding doors made an ideal stage, and the whole household, plus Jarrett the carpenter, worked to prepare for what promised to be a success—after all, imitation often exceeds the original.

Gwendolen was thrilled by the prospect. She knew Herr Klesmer was back at Quetcham, and she had made sure he was invited.

Klesmer arrived. Calm and silent, he sat quietly, responding to questions with gentle, indistinct sounds—as if carrying his cross patiently in a world overrun with amateurs, or careful not to crush a noisy mouse with his lion’s paws.

Everything went smoothly—until the moment that revealed Gwendolen’s hidden vulnerability. At first, no one understood what had happened.

The tableau of Hermione stood out sharply from the rest. It worked perfectly, and a soft murmur of approval grew quiet as Paulina (Mrs. Davilow, in a white burnous) said, “Music, awake her, strike!”

Herr Klesmer, seated at the piano, struck a thunderous chord.

At the same instant, before Hermione could move, the panel beside the stage—aligned with the piano—flew open, revealing a chilling image: a dead face and a fleeing figure, starkly lit by wax candles.

Everyone jumped—but all eyes turned back to Gwendolen, who stood motionless, yet transformed. Her expression was terrifying: pallid lips parted, eyes wide and fixed. She looked like a statue possessed by fear. Her mother rushed forward, and Rex followed instinctively. But her mother’s touch sent her collapsing to her knees, hands covering her face. She trembled, silent, yet seemed aware enough to try to control herself. She was gently lifted and led away while the guests exchanged hushed comments.

“A magnificent bit of *plastik*!” Klesmer said to Miss Arrowpoint. A ripple of whispered questions spread.

“Was that part of the play?”

“Oh, no. Miss Harleth was too affected. A sensitive creature!”

“Dear me! I didn’t know there was a painting behind that panel.”

“Neither did I. Probably some oddity from the Earl’s family.”

“How painful! Please shut it.”

“Was the door locked? It’s mysterious. Must be spirits.”

“But there’s no medium.”

“How do you know? When such things happen, we must conclude there is.”

“Oh, the door wasn’t locked. Likely the piano’s vibration opened it.”

Mr. Gascoigne spoke up, asking Miss Merry to fetch the key. But Mrs. Vulcany thought it unseemly for a clergyman to rush to rationalize such things. Still, the key was found, and the rector locked the panel with a firm click—“so it won’t start again,” he said, pocketing the key.

Soon, Gwendolen returned, her usual spirit restored, determined to ignore the incident.

But when Klesmer said, “We must thank you for the perfect climax—you couldn’t have chosen a finer moment of *plastik*,” a flush of pleasure warmed her cheeks. She liked believing it was real talent, not fear. He saw through her mortification and meant to comfort her—by praising her performance. Gwendolen clung to the idea that he now admired her skill as much as her beauty. Her unease melted into pride.

Yet only Klesmer tried to soothe her. Others knew what had been rehearsed and what hadn’t—and no one else bothered. The event was quietly dropped.

But the memory lingered—not just in the minds of those present, but in the quiet corners of Gwendolen’s soul, where the line between art and terror had blurred, and the truth of her own nature had, for a moment, shown through.

“_Perigot_. As the bonny lass passed by, _Willie_. Hey, ho, bonny lass! _P_. She roode at me with glauncing eye, _W_. As clear as the crystal glasse. _P_. All as the sunny beame so bright, _W_. Hey, ho, the sunnebeame! _P_. Glaunceth from Phoebus’ face forthright, _W_. So love into thy heart did streame.” —SPENSER: _Shepard’s Calendar_.

“The kindliest symptom, yet the most alarming crisis in the ticklish state of youth; the nourisher and destroyer of hopeful wits; * * * the servitude above freedom; the gentle mind’s religion; the liberal superstition.”—CHARLES LAMB.

The first hint of the unexpected snowstorm was like a translucent white cloud that seemed to enhance the depth of the blue sky. Anna knew the truth of Rex’s feelings—though for the first time in their lives, he had said nothing about what mattered most to him, assuming she already understood. And for the first time, Anna could not speak her own thoughts aloud. Perhaps it would have hurt her to think that he might soon care for someone else more than for her—but such pain was softened by doubt and worry over his well-being. Anna admired her cousin deeply—she would have said, simply and sincerely, “Gwendolen is always very good to me,” and felt it natural to place herself entirely under her cousin’s influence. Yet she watched her with a mixture of awe and unease, as if gazing upon a creature both wondrous and mysterious—a beautiful animal whose nature remained hidden, perhaps even capable of devouring all the small, cherished things that belonged to Anna alone. And now, a heavy certainty settled within her: Gwendolen would never return Rex’s affection. What Anna held sacred—what she loved and honored—seemed indifferent to Gwendolen. It was easier to imagine her scorn than tenderness. And then there was the way Gwendolen always longed to be extraordinary. Poor Rex! Papa would be furious if he knew. And of course, Rex was far too young for such feelings. Anna had imagined that years would pass before anything like this came along—that she would be his housekeeper for a long time still. But what kind of heart must be so cold as to ignore such devotion? In the shadow of his possible suffering, Anna began to resent her dazzling cousin.

To both of them, the weeks had felt full of quiet excitement, though outwardly calm. If asked, Rex would have said he had no reason to hide what he hoped would become an engagement—one he meant to tell his father immediately. Yet for the first time, he kept his emotions close, and even his actions were guarded—something especially noticeable to Anna. She, too, grew anxious whenever her parents spoke to her privately, fearing they might mention Rex and Gwendolen. But the adults remained oblivious to the unfolding drama, which unfolded more through silent gestures than words—clear enough to those involved, but easily missed by others who were absorbed in reading the _Guardian_ or the _Clerical Gazette_, seeing the younger generation’s stirrings much as one might watch ants scurrying across a page.

“Where are you going, Rex?” Anna asked one gray morning after their father had left for the sessions in his carriage, Mrs. Gascoigne beside him. She noticed her brother had on his antigropelos—the closest thing he owned to hunting gear.

“Going to see the hounds throw off at the Three Barns.”

“Are you taking Gwendolen?” Anna asked, timidly.

“She told you, did she?”

“No—but I thought—Does Papa know you’re going?”

“Not that I’m aware of. I don’t suppose he’d care.”

“You’re using his horse?”

“He knows I do when I can.”

“Don’t let Gwendolen ride after the hounds, Rex,” Anna pleaded, her fears sharpening her intuition.

“Why not?” Rex asked, smiling with a teasing edge.

“Papa, Mama, and Aunt Davilow all wish her not to. They think it’s improper.”

“Why should you assume she’ll do something wrong?”

“Gwendolen doesn’t always listen to anyone,” Anna said, gaining courage in her irritation.

“Then she won’t listen to me,” Rex replied, turning her anxiety into jest.

“Oh, Rex, I can’t bear it. You’ll make yourself miserable,” she whispered, breaking down in tears.

“Nannie, Nannie, what’s wrong?” Rex asked, impatient now, hat on, whip in hand.

“She won’t care for you at all—I know she never will!” the child sobbed, losing all composure.

Rex flushed and hurried out of the hall, leaving Anna to suffer the bitter knowledge that she had only made herself unwelcome.

He thought of her words as he rode. They carried the sting of prophecy—even when laughed off. But he quickly dismissed them as born of Anna’s affection, feeling sorry he couldn’t stay to comfort her. Yet another emotion surged forward: a firm belief that she was wrong, accompanied by a new resolve to prove it. This certainty, tinged with doubt, pushed him toward confession faster than pure confidence ever could.

Gwendolen was already mounted, riding up and down the avenue when Rex arrived at the gate. She had arranged for the groom to wait nearby, just in case he didn’t show—she wouldn’t have waited longer than necessary. Now, the groom was sent away, and the two rode off together, free and unbound. Gwendolen was radiant, and Rex found her more beautiful than ever. Her figure, her long white throat, the graceful curve of her cheek and chin—all were perfectly framed by the simple elegance of her riding attire. He couldn’t imagine a more perfect girl. To a young lover like Rex, the harmony of goodness, truth, and beauty seemed already present in the object of his devotion. Most people would likely feel the same about Rex—his handsome face lacked the faint, unsettling trace of darkness that sometimes made observers hesitate before admiring Gwendolen.

It was a lovely January morning—no threat of rain, just a soft gray sky that deepened the charm of the winter landscape: grassy lanes, hedgerows dotted with red berries and filled with bird song, bare elms in purple shadow, rich brown furrows. The horses’ hooves chimed like music, echoing their young voices. She laughed at his modest outfit—he was no dandy—and he relished her laughter. The freshness of the air blended with the freshness of youth. Every sound from their clear throats, every glance exchanged, felt like water bubbling from a spring of joy. For them, it was all morning—inside and out. And to witness them in these moments, one cannot help wishing—if only things had been slightly different then, so that everything afterward might have been profoundly different—if only these two radiant young souls could have pledged themselves to each other right then, and never once turned away from that vow! Some of the goodness Rex believed in was truly present. Goodness is a vast word, often looking ahead—like a harvest buried beneath the soil, uncertain whether the seed will grow. At one stage, it’s invisible, promising life; at another, green shoots appear, and soon delicate flowers tremble, vulnerable to wind or rain. Each phase carries its own risk, and life may be choked by the poisoned ground around it—or by corruption from afar.

“Anna thought you’d want to ride after the hounds today,” Rex said, his mind still haunted by her earlier words.

“Did she?” Gwendolen laughed. “What a little clairvoyant!”

“Will you?” Rex asked, unsure if she’d defy her family’s wishes, but trusting she had reasons.

“I don’t know. I can’t say until we get there. Clairvoyants are often wrong—they predict what’s likely. I don’t like what’s likely—it’s always dull. I do what’s unlikely.”

“Ah, there’s your secret. Once I knew what most people would do, I’d know you’d do the opposite. So you’d follow your own likelihood. I could count on you. You couldn’t surprise me.”

“Yes, I could. I’d turn around and do what’s likely for everyone else,” Gwendolen replied, laughing musically.

“You see—you can’t escape some sort of likelihood. Contradiction makes the strongest likelihood of all. You must give up your plan.”

“No. My plan is to do what pleases me.” (Let any young woman who wishes to imitate Gwendolen consider her posture: had her head been set differently, her chin jutting, her neck curved too sharply, her words might have sounded harsh to sweet-natured Rex. But in her speech, even the oddness was charm, playful banter he longed to draw toward one purpose.)

“Can you really feel only what pleases you?” he asked.

“Of course not—that comes from what others do. But if the world were kinder, we’d only feel pleasure. Girls’ lives are so boring—they never do what they want.”

“I thought men suffered more. They’re forced into hard work, bored, broken down. And when we love a girl deeply, we want to please her—so in the end, you get your way.”

“I don’t believe it. I’ve never seen a married woman who had her own way.”
