“What would you like to do?” Rex asked, genuinely curious.

“Oh, I don’t know—go to the North Pole, ride steeplechases, or become a queen in the East like Lady Hester Stanhope,” Gwendolen said lightly. Her words came swiftly, but she couldn’t explain their deeper meaning.

“You don’t mean you’d never marry?”

“No, I didn’t say that. Only that if I did, I wouldn’t live like other women.”

“You could do whatever you liked if you married a man who loved you more than anything,” Rex said, unknowingly stepping beyond the limits of his usual conversation. “I know one who does.”

“Don’t talk about Mr. Middleton, for heaven’s sake,” Gwendolen said quickly, her cheeks flushing. “That’s Anna’s favorite topic. I hear the hounds. Let’s go.”

She urged her chestnut into a canter, and Rex had no choice but to follow. Still, he felt encouraged. Gwendolen knew Rex was in love with her—but she saw it as a harmless game, a passing fancy. She had never known true sorrow in love. She wanted the little romance of his devotion to fill his time at Pennicote, avoiding explanations that might end it too soon. And she recoiled, almost physically, from being directly courted. Though she delighted in being adored, there was a fierce pride in her maidenhood.

But soon, all else faded for her in the thrill of the scene at the Three Barns. Several gentlemen of the hunt knew her, and she exchanged cheerful greetings. Rex couldn’t get near her. The color, the movement, the energy of the field took hold of Gwendolen with a force not born of habit—she had never ridden with the hounds before, only said she wished to, sparking a prohibition. Her mother feared danger, her uncle declared such violent exercise unsuitable for women, and insisted no respectable lady followed the Wessex hunt—except Mrs. Gadsby, wife of the yeomanry captain, who had once been a kitchenmaid and still spoke like one. That argument stirred Gwendolen, making her hesitate between desire and dread of being compared to Mrs. Gadsby.

Some of the neighborhood’s most respected women occasionally watched the hounds set off, but none were present this morning. Mrs. Gadsby was also absent. Thus, Gwendolen felt no restraint. The animal excitement—the baying of the hounds, the pawing of horses, the shifting voices, the flashes of color against the still green and gray—overwhelmed her. It was the ultimate anticipation of the chase: the feeling of becoming part dog, part horse, with the added rush of social vanity and the sensation of centaur-like power.

Rex would have enjoyed it more had he stayed closer to Gwendolen, but he saw her constantly engaged with acquaintances, or drawn by others on lively horses that swirled around her like a revolving lever.

“Delighted to see you here this fine morning, Miss Harleth,” said Lord Brackenshaw, a middle-aged peer in stained pink, with easy manners that made disaster seem inconsequential. “We’re in for a splendid run. A shame you didn’t join us. Have you ever tried your little chestnut over a ditch? You wouldn’t be afraid, eh?”

“Not at all,” Gwendolen replied. And it was true—she never feared action or companionship. “I’ve taken him over rails and ditches near—”

“Ah, by Jove!” his lordship murmured, signaling that something had interrupted their talk. As he pulled back, Rex brought his steady hackney alongside Gwendolen—then the hounds bayed, and the entire field surged forward as if the earth itself were spinning. Gwendolen went with the tide—no warning to Rex, who without hesitation followed. Could he let her go alone? Under other circumstances, he might have relished the chase. But now, he was troubled by the sudden halt in his longing to declare his love and receive an answer. His pursuit had been abruptly redirected—not into a romantic chase, but into a real one, atop his father’s old gray nag—a good horse, but slow, dignified, and thoroughly clerical.

Gwendolen, astride her spirited chestnut, raced with the best, feeling as secure as an immortal goddess. Had she considered danger, she’d have trusted her luck—she believed nothing ill would happen to her. But she didn’t think of danger, nor of her cousin. If she had, she might have found amusement in imagining him slowly falling behind, searching for gates—this lean, eager youth, his heart pounding like a beagle’s, trapped on a stiff, old-fashioned horse, frozen by a wizard’s spell. That image would have made her laugh, her sense of fun too strong to pity his embarrassment. But Gwendolen usually thought of those who watched her, not those she couldn’t see. Soon, Rex was so far behind that even if she’d looked, she wouldn’t have seen him. And alas, on a recently repaired lane, Primrose stumbled, broke his knees, and threw Rex over his head.

Fortunately, a blacksmith’s son—also following the hunt, though on foot, a practice some deemed immoral—was nearby and witnessed the fall. He ran to help. Rex was badly stunned, and when sensation returned, it came with pain. Joel Dagge proved himself invaluable—his knowledge suited the moment. He not only diagnosed the horse’s injury, knew their distance from the nearest inn and Pennicote Rectory, and confirmed Rex’s shoulder was merely dislocated, but offered skilled aid.

“Lord, sir, let me pop it back in! I’ve seen Nash, the bone-setter, do it—and done it myself twice for our little Sally. Same thing, shoulders are. Just trust me and tighten your mind a bit, and I’ll fix it in no time.”

“Come on, old fellow,” Rex said, able to focus better than sit still. Joel performed the procedure—painfully, but effectively. Rex turned pale, struggling to endure it, and Joel remarked, “Ah, sir, you aren’t used to it. I’ve seen plenty of joints out. Once saw a man with his eye pushed out—now that was a proper mess. But it went back in. I’ve swallowed three teeth myself, as sure as I’m alive. Now, sirrey” (to Primrose), “come along—you can’t pretend you can’t move.”

Joel, clearly of humble means, need not be further described for the refined reader—save that he helped Rex home as quickly as possible. There was no choice but to return, though Rex was consumed with worry for Gwendolen, more distressed by the thought that she might have been hurt than by his own pain or the trouble he’d cause his father. He comforted himself by believing others would protect her, and someone would surely escort her home.

Mr. Gascoigne was already home, writing letters in his study, when Rex entered with a face pale and strained, yet still strikingly handsome. Secretly, Rex was his favorite son—his living portrait. But Mr. Gascoigne treated him with strict fairness, even rigor. Having questioned Anna, he already knew Rex had gone to the meet with Gwendolen.

“What’s happened?” he asked sharply, not putting down his pen.

“I’m sorry, sir. Primrose fell and broke his knees.”

“Where did you take him?”

“To the Three Barns to see the hounds set off.”

“And you were foolish enough to follow?”

“Yes, sir. I didn’t jump any fences, but the horse stepped into a hole.”

“And you injured yourself, I suppose?”

“My shoulder’s dislocated, but a blacksmith put it back. I’m just bruised.”

“Well, sit down.”

“I’m sorry about the horse, sir. I knew it would upset you.”

“And what of Gwendolen?” Mr. Gascoigne asked abruptly. Rex, who hadn’t expected inquiries about himself, blushed—more remarkable given his paleness. Then, nervously, he said:

“I’m worried—I’d like to go or send to Offendene. But she rides well—I think she’ll be fine. There’ll be others around her.”

“I suppose it was she who led you on?” Mr. Gascoigne asked, laying down his pen, leaning back, and studying his son closely.

“It was natural for her to want to go—she didn’t plan it. She was swept up by the moment. And of course, I followed.”

A silence followed. Then Mr. Gascoigne spoke quietly, with irony:

“But now you see, young gentleman, you’re not equipped with a horse fit to chaperone your cousin. You must give up this pastime. You’ve spoiled my nag, and that’s enough mischief for one vacation. I’ll ask you to leave tomorrow for Southampton, to join Stilfox until you go up to Oxford with him. It will help your bruises—and your studies.”

Poor Rex felt his heart swell as if it were no stronger than a girl’s.

“I hope you won longer insist on my leaving now, sir.”

“Do you feel too ill?”

“No—not that—but—” He bit his lip, feeling tears rise, to his great frustration. Then he rallied, trying to speak firmly: “I want to go to Offendene, but I can go tonight.”

“I’m going there myself. I can bring news of Gwendolen, if that’s what you want.”

Rex broke down. He thought he saw a fate worse than death—fatal to his happiness, perhaps his life. He trusted his father’s insight and expected firmness. “Father, I can’t leave without telling her I love her—and knowing she loves me.”

Mr. Gascoigne inwardly reproached himself for not being more cautious. He truly pitied the boy, but duty demanded wisdom. Calmly, he replied:

“My dear boy, you’re too young for such decisive steps. This is a fleeting fancy, born in idle days. You must apply yourself to something serious and forget it. There are many reasons against it. An engagement at your age would be reckless and unjustifiable. And alliances between cousins are undesirable. Accept this disappointment. Life is full of them. We all must be shaped. This is a mild beginning for you.”

“No—mild? I can’t bear it. I’ll be useless. I wouldn’t mind anything if it were settled between us. I could do anything then,” Rex said, impetuously. “But pretending I’ll obey you is pointless. I can’t. If I said I would, I’d break my word. I’d see Gwendolen again.”

“Wait until tomorrow morning—we’ll discuss it again. You’ll promise me that,” Mr. Gascoigne said quietly. And Rex, unable to refuse, nodded.

CHAPTER VIII. What name doth Joy most borrow When life is fair? “To-morrow.” What name doth best fit Sorrow In young despair? “To-morrow.”

At the rectory, a deeper sorrow had taken root than any fleeting disappointment could bring. Rex arrived home not with the usual sparkle of youth, but collapsed upon his bed in a silence so complete it seemed as if his spirit had fled. For days, he lay wrapped in stillness, responding only with quiet murmurs when anyone approached—“Let me alone,” he would say, each time like a small refusal of the world.

His mother and Anna watched helplessly, their hearts aching for the boy who had once been their brightest light. Where once laughter had filled the house, now there was only a hush, broken only by the soft rustle of curtains or the distant call of birds. The thought of sending him to Southampton slipped away entirely; instead, they focused on tending him, though he resisted even kindness, turning his face from them as if afraid of being touched.

His father, Mr. Gascoigne, stood apart, observing with a calm that masked his own distress. He saw beyond the pain—the fragile moment of crisis—and believed it might be the only way out of an unfortunate entanglement. Still, he grieved for what his son endured. Now and then, he would sit quietly by Rex’s bedside, placing a hand gently on his forehead, whispering, “God bless you, my boy.” And when he left, he did so with a slow, solemn nod, as if bidding farewell to something precious yet lost.

The younger children, Warham and the others, crept near the door sometimes, peering in at the brother they knew so well—once full of energy and mischief—now pale and still. But fingers would wave them back sharply, warning them not to disturb the fragile peace.

Anna was the constant presence, never leaving Rex’s side. Her small hand rested in his, though he never squeezed it in return. Inside her heart, two feelings warred: one for her brother’s suffering, the other for Gwendolen—the girl whose name had become a shadow over their household. “Perhaps it is wicked of me,” she repeated to herself, “but I think I never _can_ love her again.” It was a refrain that echoed in her silent thoughts, a quiet lament born of grief and confusion.

Even Mrs. Gascoigne could not hide her irritation toward her niece. One evening, she spoke softly to her husband, almost apologetically: “I know it’s better this way, Henry. We should be thankful Gwendolen isn’t truly in love with the poor boy. But really—she seems so cold. She has the heart of a coquette. I can’t help thinking she gave him hope, or else he wouldn’t have fallen so hard. And Fanny—poor Fanny—she sees nothing wrong in that girl.”

Mr. Gascoigne responded firmly, “The less said about it, Nancy, the better. I should have seen it sooner myself. As for Rex, let us be grateful nothing worse comes of it. Let this pass quickly. Especially with regard to Gwendolen—let it be as if it never happened.”

Yet beneath his calm exterior, the rector felt relief. Had Gwendolen returned Rex’s affection, the situation would have been far more complicated—one that might have escaped his control. As it was, he could only watch and wait.

Then, one bright morning, something shifted. Rex asked for his bath. He dressed as he always did, with careful precision. Anna, watching from the hallway, trembled with hope. When she heard his footsteps descending, she rushed to meet him at the stairs.

For the first time, he smiled—a faint, fragile thing that made her eyes fill with tears. “Nannie,” he said gently, taking her hand and leading her slowly into the drawing-room. His mother was already there, and when she came forward to kiss him, he whispered, “What a plague I am!”

He sat down by the bow-window, staring out at the garden blanketed in hoarfrost. The sun cast pale glimmers across the frozen grass, and Anna thought it looked like the sadness on Rex’s face—quiet, enduring, beautiful in its sorrow.

He felt as if he had awakened in a new world, stripped of everything familiar. The old dreams were gone. He didn’t know how to live anymore.

Anna sat beside him, pretending to knit, but her eyes never left his. Beyond the hedge, a wagon rumbled along the road, heavy with timber. The horses strained, muscles taut, and the driver cracked his whip, running alongside to guide the lead horse. Rex stirred, rose, and watched until the last log vanished from sight. Then he walked slowly back and forth, as if searching for words.

Mrs. Gascoigne had left. When Rex sat again, Anna, sensing a change in him, brought a stool and placed it close to his knee. She looked up, her expression pleading: *Speak to me.*

And he did.

“I’ll tell you what I’m thinking of, Nannie,” he said. “I want to go to Canada—or somewhere like that.”

Anna gasped. “Oh, Rex, not forever?”

“Yes,” he said quietly. “To earn my living. I’d build a hut, clear land, live among the wilds. A great wide quiet.”

“And not take me?” she whispered, tears falling fast.

“How could I?” he said.

“But I would love it,” she insisted. “Settlers take their families. I’d rather go than stay here. I could make fires, mend clothes, cook food. I’d learn to bake before we left. It would be like playing at life again—like our tent under the drugget, with our little plates and dishes.”

“Father and mother wouldn’t let you go,” Rex said.

“I think they would,” Anna replied, “if I explained. It would save money. Papa would have more to raise the boys. And I wouldn’t cost anything. I’d be happy.”

They talked like this—practical, earnest—until Rex finally agreed, reluctantly, that Anna could go with him—if he could speak to their father.

It was late afternoon when they entered the study together. Mr. Gascoigne looked up, surprised but pleased to see Rex moving again. “Well, my children!” he said cheerfully. “May we sit with you, papa?” Anna asked. “Rex has something to say.”

With all his heart, the rector welcomed them.

They formed a striking trio—father, son, daughter—all bearing the same strong features: high brows, straight noses, short upper lips, firm chins. The same gray tone in their skin, the same steady gaze. Mr. Gascoigne, with his silver hair and deep-set eyes, carried himself with authority, a man used to guiding others. Rex, pale but present, seemed a ghost of his father’s youth—had it not been for the sorrow in his eyes and the absence of purpose. Anna, tiny and serious, mirrored him in every expression, as if they shared a single soul.

“You know what has upset me, father,” Rex began.

Mr. Gascoigne nodded.

“I’m done for in this world. I can’t go back to Oxford. I couldn’t study. I’d fail. I’d waste your money. I want your permission to take another path.”

The rector’s brow furrowed. “You mean—you wish to leave England?”

“To go to the colonies. To work the land.”

Anna jumped in. “And me, papa! Rex needs someone to care for him. Someone to keep house. We won’t marry. I won’t cost anything. I’d be so happy. It would be hard to leave you and mamma—but there are the others to raise. We’d be no burden.”

She stepped closer, clinging to her father’s arm.

He did not smile, but drew her onto his lap, holding her gently—almost as if to remove her from the conversation while he addressed Rex.

“You will admit,” he said, “that my experience gives me some right to judge for you? That I may guide you better than you can guide yourself?”

Rex had no choice but to say, “Yes, sir.”

“And perhaps you will admit—though I don’t press it—that you owe me duty, and should consider my wishes?”

“I’ve never opposed you before, sir.”

But inwardly, Rex rebelled. He did not feel bound to return to Oxford. He wanted only to escape—to vanish into the wilds where no one would ask him to pretend.

“But suppose,” the rector continued, “I believe—on solid grounds—that this feeling is temporary? That if you leave now, you’ll regret it later, having thrown away years of education? Can you not trust me to know what is best? Can you not try, at least, to follow my advice—for a time?”

Rex hesitated. “I can’t study,” he said. “Not now.”

“Not just now,” his father corrected. “You’ll miss a term. But I’ve arranged for you—how you’ll spend the next two months. I confess, I’m disappointed in you, Rex. I thought you had more sense. You’re not the first young man to suffer a blow. But that doesn’t mean you’re free to abandon duty—as if your mind had weakened and you were no longer responsible.”

Rex stood up, as if the meeting were over.

“You accept my plan?” the rector asked, his voice firm, unyielding.

A pause. Then Rex said, “I’ll try, sir. I can’t promise.”

Inside, he knew trying would be useless.

Mr. Gascoigne kept Anna on his lap, holding her tightly as Rex left the room.

“Oh, papa,” she whispered, tears streaming. “It’s so hard for him. Doesn’t he look ill?”

“He will be better,” he said. “This will pass. And now, Anna—be quiet. Never mention this again when he’s gone.”

“No, papa,” she promised. “But I would never be like Gwendolen. To have people fall in love with me so—it’s dreadful.”

She didn’t dare say it aloud, but inside, she was crushed—not by her brother’s sorrow, but by the fact that she couldn’t go with him.

Later, she often replayed the scene in her mind, imagining a different life: no parties, no gloves, no crinolines, no dinners where she had to talk and smile. “I’d rather be a settler,” she told herself. “I’d rather live in a hut, make fires, and be real.”

And in that moment, she felt a strange kind of freedom—born not of joy, but of longing for a world untouched by the rules of society.

It was a time when fashion demanded wider crinolines, and whispers grew louder about expanding churches, ballrooms, and carriages to match. But Anna Gascoigne, slight and modest, would never need such grandeur. Her future, she dreamed, lay beyond the fields—where the wind blew freely, and the sky stretched endlessly.

CHAPTER IX. I’ll tell thee, Berthold, what men’s hopes are like: A silly child that, quivering with joy, Would cast its little mimic fishing-line Baited with loadstone for a bowl of toys In the salt ocean.

Eight months after the family’s arrival at Offendene—by the end of June—the quiet countryside around Wanchester stirred with news that sent ripples through every household, from the village smithy to the grand estates. It was not a matter of war or politics, nor of trade or harvests, but something far more intimate and thrilling: the old manor of Diplow Hall, long silent and shuttered under Sir Hugo Mallinger’s neglect, was being readied once more for life. The stables were being cleaned, the lawns trimmed, and the great house prepared to welcome a new tenant—for the summer and through the hunting season, no less. But it would not be Sir Hugo himself returning; instead, his nephew, Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt, was coming to take up residence. As the presumptive heir to the baronetcy—Sir Hugo’s marriage having produced only daughters—this was a momentous shift in the social landscape of the region.

And it wasn’t just the title that made Mr. Grandcourt a figure of interest. His mother’s lineage carried noble blood, and should certain distant relatives pass on, he might even become a baron—a peer of England. Such possibilities, while uncertain, were enough to spark quiet speculation across the county. For those who lived by the land, this meant hope: the blacksmith dreamed of busy days; laborers’ wives imagined their sons finding work as grooms; farmers saw renewed chances to sell hay or straw at fair prices. And among the gentry, the talk was quieter, yet no less fervent—marriage, after all, was both a personal joy and a strategic alliance.

It is natural, when a man of wealth and standing arrives in a community, that thoughts turn to whom he might choose as a wife. Just as people dream of knighthood when royalty visits, so too did whispers of romance stir beneath polite conversation. The idea that Mr. Grandcourt might settle in the neighborhood—and perhaps seek a bride—was enough to set imaginations spinning. Who could he marry? What sort of woman would be worthy of such a future? And what if she were already among them?

The Arrowpoints of Quetcham, though rich beyond measure and free from financial anxiety, found themselves wondering about the young man. Their daughter, Catherine, had refused Lord Slogan—an impeccable Irish peer—because, despite his estate, he lacked charm. Now, with Grandcourt’s arrival, they mused quietly: Was he handsome? Sound in health? Virtuous—or at least reformed? Did he hold views compatible with their own? They didn’t wish harm upon anyone, but they couldn’t help hoping he might inherit the title and bring stability to the region.

Mr. Gascoigne, the rector, was not immune to these currents. Though bound by duty and decorum, he too felt the weight of responsibility for the young people under his care—especially Gwendolen, whose spirit seemed increasingly difficult to guide. He knew, as many did, that some young men carried reputations that followed them like shadows. Yet he chose not to inquire too closely into Grandcourt’s past. After all, a man born to privilege and leisure could afford indulgences others could not. If he had gambled, so what? If he had erred, but now stood upright, then perhaps reform was genuine. It was not the same as judging a servant who claimed redemption. When one can afford to live without scandal, virtue often follows naturally.

Still, no one spoke openly. People kept their thoughts private, not out of malice, but because such matters were delicate. To confess your hopes aloud was to risk having them mocked—or worse, to have them realized before you were ready. Mrs. Gascoigne, though she said nothing to her husband, likely thought deeply about the possibility of Grandcourt meeting Gwendolen at the upcoming Archery Meeting. She had murmured to her sister, “It would be a mercy, Fanny, if that girl were well married!”—a remark that struck Mrs. Davilow as both hopeful and critical. She replied inwardly, “You will not get her to marry for your pleasure,” feeling a quiet defiance rise within her, as though her daughter’s heart belonged to no one but herself.

Mrs. Davilow, too, could not ignore the stir Grandcourt’s arrival caused. She pictured him—not as a mere suitor, but as a perfect match: handsome, accomplished, refined. But then came the harder question: Would Gwendolen love him? That was the mystery. Her daughter’s tastes were unpredictable. Beauty alone might not win her favor. Perhaps charm, wit, or even a touch of danger would do. The image of the ideal husband flickered and shifted in her mind, never settling.

She feared to speak of it, knowing that any hint of expectation might only make Gwendolen resist. Since the painful aftermath of Rex’s departure, the mother had learned caution. To suggest a path for her daughter’s heart was to risk reopening wounds. Marriage was not merely a social duty—it was a gamble, and Gwendolen had already shown she would not play by anyone else’s rules.

Yet, when Gwendolen tried on her hat for the Archery Meeting, a sudden flash of humor broke through. She tossed her head back, miming the draw of a bow, and laughed: “How I pity all the other girls at the Archery Meeting—all thinking of Mr. Grandcourt! And they have not a shadow of a chance.”

Mrs. Davilow froze. Then, with mischievous eyes, Gwendolen turned and said, “Now you know they have not, mamma. You and Uncle Henry and Aunt Lydia—you all intend him to fall in love with me.”

The mother, piqued, played along. “Oh, my dear, that is not so certain. Miss Arrowpoint has charms which you have not.”

“Of course,” Gwendolen replied, “but hers require thought. My arrow will pierce him before he has time to think. He will declare himself my slave—I shall send him round the world to fetch me back the wedding ring of a happy woman. Meanwhile, all the men between him and the title will die of strange diseases—he’ll return as Lord Grandcourt… but without the ring—and fall at my feet. I shall laugh at him—he will rise in anger—I shall laugh more—he will summon his horse and ride to Quetcham, where he finds Miss Arrowpoint just married to a poor musician, her father stunned, her mother tearing off her cap. Exit Lord Grandcourt, who returns to Diplow and, like M. Jabot, _change de linge_.”

Was ever a young woman so sharp, so full of fire? She saw through secrets as easily as birds sense storms. One could try to hide things from her—but she would know, just by the glance, the hesitation, the slight twitch of an eyebrow. It was as if she had already glimpsed more of Grandcourt than anyone else dared imagine.

So Mrs. Davilow asked, tentatively, “What kind of man do you imagine him to be, Gwendolen?”

“Let me see,” said Gwendolen, finger to lips, then pointing decisively. “Short—just above my shoulder—trying to look taller by curling his mustache and letting his beard grow long. A glass in his eye to give him an air of distinction. Very particular about his waistcoat, but unsure about the weather—will try to draw me out on that. He’ll stare at me constantly, and the glass will make him look grotesque, especially when he smiles. I’ll lower my eyes, and he’ll think I’m captivated. That night, I’ll dream of a giant insect—and the next morning, he’ll propose. The rest, as before.”

“That sounds like someone you’ve already seen,” said Mrs. Davilow gently.

“Oh, yes,” Gwendolen replied, twirling her best hat in her hands. “I wonder what a delightful young man would behave like? I know he’d have hunters and racehorses, a London house, two country homes—one with battlements, another with a veranda. And I’m sure, with a little murder here and there, he could get a title.”

Her tone was teasing, but beneath it lay a kernel of truth. Poor Mrs. Davilow felt uneasy. She had always meant her words literally, innocently. “Don’t talk like that, child,” she pleaded. “You read too many books—they fill your head with wild ideas. When your aunt and I were your age, we knew nothing of wickedness. I think it was better so.”

“Why didn’t you raise me that way, mamma?” Gwendolen asked.

The question struck deep. Mrs. Davilow’s face crumpled, tears rising. Gwendolen dropped the hat, knelt beside her, and whispered, “Mamma, mamma! I was only joking. I meant nothing.”

“How could I, Gwendolen?” the mother sobbed, unable to hear the apology. “Your will was always too strong for me—if everything else had been different.”

The daughter understood. “Dear mamma, I don’t blame you—I love you. How can you help what I am? Besides, I am very charming. Come now.” And with her handkerchief, she gently wiped away the tears.

Such tenderness calmed her mother, as it had done before. But the silence that followed was thick with unspoken fears. From then on, Mr. Grandcourt was avoided in conversation. Even when Mr. Gascoigne mentioned him, Mrs. Davilow braced herself—afraid Gwendolen might reveal too much of her insight into her uncle’s thoughts. But Gwendolen, ever careful, remained silent.

She understood people as birds understand wind and sky. She knew her uncle’s expectations, and she knew how to avoid provoking them. Their shared passion for archery helped bind them—Mr. Gascoigne delighted in her skill, and Gwendolen valued his approval. Since the rift with Rex, Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna had grown distant from her, sensing a coldness she could not explain. Anna, still wounded by Rex’s loss, found Gwendolen’s vivacity painful. She tried to hide it, but sorrow cannot imitate joy. This quiet resentment hardened Gwendolen, pushing her toward defiance.

One day, she said, “Mamma, I see now why girls are glad to be married—to escape being expected to please everyone except themselves.”

Happily, Mr. Middleton had left without declaring his feelings. Though Gwendolen was admired—her beauty and wit known across thirty miles—no one had formally proposed. Not even the most attentive curate had stepped forward. Some say it is nature’s kindness that not every admirer becomes a lover, and not every lover dares to speak.

Gwendolen, of course, was not universally adored. And eight months was not long enough for affection to bloom fully. So why, then, did people believe Mr. Grandcourt might succeed where others had failed?

Perhaps because he seemed more eligible. Or perhaps because hope, in the absence of proof, becomes belief. The Arrowpoints, for instance, had no need to worry about their daughter’s prospects. Their vision of Grandcourt was shaped not by desire, but by practicality.

But in the quiet corners of hearts, dreams still stirred—unspoken, unfulfilled, waiting for the right moment to unfold.

CHAPTER X. _1st Gent._ What woman should be? Sir, consult the taste of marriageable men. This planet’s store—iron, cotton, wool, or chemicals—all matter shaped by our skill—takes form only in response to demand. The market’s pulse sets high or low, by rule sublime. Our daughters must become wives, and wives must embody what men desire. Men’s taste is woman’s test. You mark the phrase? ’Tis good, I think—the sense well-winged and poised with t’s and s’s. _2nd Gent._ Nay, but turn it round: give us the test of taste. A fine _menu_—is it today what Roman epicures insisted a gentleman must eat to earn the dignity of dining well?

Brackenshaw Park, where the archery meeting was held, rose gently from its heights, overlooking the neighboring valley, the distant eastern downs, and the slow swell of cultivated land stretching westward like a vast curtain. The castle perched atop the highest plateau of the clustered hills, built of rough-hewn limestone streaked with shadows and dust from lichens, washed by rain. Beech and fir trees sheltered it on the north, spreading down the green slopes like flocks drawn to water glinting below.

The archery ground lay at the far end of the park—a carefully tended enclosure on a flat stretch of land, shielded from the southwest by tall elms and a thick screen of hollies. These cast a pleasant afternoon shade over the gravel walk and the freshly mown turf where the targets stood. To the north, the Archery Hall, with its arcade front, gleamed white against the greenery, like a temple among trees. It made a perfect backdrop for the flower-like groups of ladies who moved, bowed, and turned their necks with languid grace—just as lilies might if they could walk.

Even when the military band from Wanchester fell silent, the air remained alive with sound: musical laughter in every tone, friendly chatter rising now to gentle excitement, then sinking into soft murmurs. No outdoor event could feel more free from the crush and noise that ruin most modern pleasures. This archery gathering was select—only invited guests accompanying members, tickets strictly limited so that the evening dinner and ball within the castle would not overflow. Outside the enclosure, no common spectators were allowed except Lord Brackenshaw’s tenants and their families. Of these, it was mostly the women who came, bringing children, little brothers and sisters. The men amused themselves with fanciful bets—"anything you like"—on their favorite archers. But the young girls had a different measure: they dreamed of becoming like the elegantly dressed ladies before them. Perhaps the ideal form they’d longed for most was not Gwendolen’s—someone with rosy cheeks and golden hair, fashionably bright—but among the male eyes near her, there was rare agreement: she was the finest girl present.

No wonder she thrived that July day. Pre-eminence is sweet to those who love it—even under ordinary circumstances. It may not be mythical that a slave once took pride in being bought first; nor impossible that a barnyard fowl, though unaware it was "the best of a bad lot," might still strut with quiet self-knowledge. But true joy requires harmony between outward and inward. And for Gwendolen, that harmony was complete.

Who can deny that bows and arrows are among the most graceful weapons for feminine hands? They invite stances of elegance and strength, where the focus of marksmanship is freed from bloodshed. The old British habit of “killing something” is no longer practiced with bow and quiver; armies defend their passes under different skies than clouds of arrows. Poisoned darts survive only in rhetoric or faraway lands. Archery has no foul scent of brimstone; it harms no shins, breeds no grotesque athletes. Its only risk is failure—and for generous spirits, that is enough to shape mastery.

At Brackenshaw, prizes were noble and symbolic—not parcels of gain, but gold and silver arrows, stars worn proudly for months, then passed on to the next worthy. These tokens honored achievement without the burden of vanity. They were like wreaths without the sweat, avoiding the melancholy of overheated ballrooms. Altogether, the Brackenshaw Archery Club was an institution of refined taste—free by design from absurdity.

And today, fortune favored every detail. The sun warmed gently; no wind stirred hair, dress, or arrow. Skill had fair play. When the crowd marched together to retrieve arrows, it became a joyful procession—light-hearted, laughing, moving in unison toward a shared purpose. A sight worth watching. Here, Gwendolen seemed a Calypso among nymphs. Her movements, her posture—everyone acknowledged her charm.

“That girl is like a high-mettled racer,” Lord Brackenshaw remarked to young Clintock, one of the guests. “First chop! Tremendously pretty too,” said the elegant Grecian, who had been attentive to her. “I never saw her look better.”

Perhaps she had never looked so radiant. Her face glowed with youthful delight, untouched by envy or discontent. Satisfied with her own place, she felt kindness toward all and contentment with the world. Not to hold the highest rank, not to be marked as an heiress like Miss Arrowpoint—this gave her triumph a sharper edge. For personal affection, she wouldn’t trade her family group for any other: her mother looked fit for an amiable duchess; her uncle and aunt Gascoigne, along with Anna, formed equally pleasing figures. Gwendolen, full of belief in herself, felt no jealousy—even though Miss Arrowpoint was a skilled archer.

Even the unexpected return of Herr Klesmer, which startled others, seemed to suit Gwendolen’s mood perfectly. Short of Apollo himself, what great musician could make such a figure at an archery meet? She watched the Arrowpoint party enter with a wry smile, the contrast between Klesmer and the typical English country folk at its peak—especially near his hosts—or patrons, as Mrs. Arrowpoint preferred to call them, so she could claim she no longer patronized genius, whose royalty was universally acknowledged.

The contrast might have amused a more solemn observer. We English are a mixed people—fifty random faces show many forms of structure and expression. Yet our general look lacks the liveliness of a passionate race, preoccupied with ideals and treating reality as mere weight. The English gentleman’s strength lies in ease—his figure, his clothes. He resists sharp contrasts in costume, and he avoids appearing inspired. Imagine a gathering of such men, seeing Herr Klesmer arrive: his mane of hair flying backward, defying the stiff chimney-pot hat perched above his strong, well-shaped features and powerful jaw. His tall, lean frame wore clothing that, while not strictly English, drew attention precisely because it seemed so deliberately foreign. Draped in a loose garment with a Florentine _berretta_, he might have stood beside Leonardo da Vinci. But in trousers ill-suited to English taste—knees awkwardly emphasized—and with fire in his glance and movement, reduced to comedy by a hat demanding cropped hair and calm demeanor—like Mr. Arrowsmith’s, whose blank face and flawless tailoring passed unnoticed everywhere—Klesmer became a spectacle.

One understands why greatness often feels better dead, stripped of its physical form. Many knew Klesmer, or had heard of him, but only seen him in candlelit concerts, where he was merely a musician. He hadn’t yet reached the global fame that makes an artist revered even by the uninitiated through sheer cost and reputation. Now, unexpectedly, here he was—on a July afternoon, in exclusive society. Some laughed. Others frowned at the poor judgment of the Arrowpoints in inviting him.

“What extreme guys those artistic types usually are!” young Clintock whispered to Gwendolen. “Look at the figure he cuts—bowing with his hand over his heart to Lady Brackenshaw, and Mrs. Arrowpoint’s feather just brushing his shoulder.”

“You’re one of the profane,” Gwendolen replied. “Blind to the majesty of genius. Herr Klesmer strikes me with awe. I feel crushed in his presence. My courage leaks away.”

“Oh, you understand his music?”

“No indeed,” she laughed lightly. “It’s he who understands mine—and thinks it pitiable.” Klesmer’s critique of her singing had become easier to bear since he’d admired her _plastik_. “It’s not meant for future ears, I suppose. I’m glad. It suits mine.”

“Oh, you’re very kind. But how remarkably well Miss Arrowpoint looks today! She’d make quite a fine painting in that gold dress.”

“Too splendid, don’t you think?”

“Well, perhaps a little too symbolic—too much like Wealth in an allegory.”

Gwendolen’s remark carried a hint of mischief, but it wasn’t malice. She didn’t wish anyone out of the way. She believed in her own luck more than in her skill. As the shooting continued, that belief grew stronger. She promised a top score—astonishing everyone as a newcomer. For Gwendolen, one success led to another. She walked on air. Everything seemed possible. The moment was enough. She didn’t need to plan what came next.

“How does the scoring stand?” asked Lady Brackenshaw, seated gracefully with two daughters and a sturdy son, presiding as lady paramount. Her husband approached during a break.

“It seems Miss Harleth may win the gold arrow,” he replied.

“Gad, I think she will—if she keeps this up! She’s running Juliet Fenn close. Remarkable for a first-year archer. Catherine isn’t at her usual level,” he added, turning to Mrs. Arrowpoint nearby. “But she won last time. And there’s luck even in games of skill. That’s better—it gives the rest a chance.”

“Catherine will be delighted for others to win,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said. “She’s so generous. It was entirely her thought to bring Herr Klesmer instead of Canon Stopley, who wanted to come. For her own pleasure, I’m sure she’d have chosen the canon. But she always thinks of others. I told her it wasn’t quite _en règle_ to invite someone so outside our circle. She said, ‘Genius itself is not _en règle_; it comes to make new rules.’ And one must admit it.”

“Ay, to be sure,” Lord Brackenshaw replied, dismissively. “For my part, I’m not magnanimous—I’d like to win. But confound it! I never get the chance now. I’m growing old and idle. The young ones beat me. As old Nestor says—the gods don’t give us everything at once. I was young once. Now I’m getting wise. Old, at least—which is a gift everyone gets if they live long enough. So it raises no jealousy.”

He smiled comfortably at his wife.

“Oh, my lord,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said, “neighbors for twenty years shouldn’t talk about age.” She paused. “Years, as the Tuscans say, are made for renting houses. But where is our new neighbor? I thought Mr. Grandcourt was coming today.”

“Ah, yes—he was supposed to. Time’s slipping,” Lord Brackenshaw glanced at his watch. “But he only reached Diplow the other day. Came to us Tuesday and said he’d been delayed. Might have been pulled elsewhere. Why, Gascoigne!”—the rector was walking past with Gwendolen on his arm, turning at the call—“you’ve not only beaten us yourself, but brought your niece to beat all the archeresses!”

“It _is_ rather scandalous,” Mr. Gascoigne said, inwardly pleased, curling his upper lip. “But it’s not my doing, my lord. I only meant her to make a decent figure—nothing more.”

“It’s not my fault either,” Gwendolen replied, with playful defiance. “If I aim, I can’t help hitting.”

“Ay, ay,” Lord Brackenshaw chuckled. “That may be fatal for some.” Then, checking his watch again, he turned to Mrs. Arrowpoint. “Time’s slipping, as you say. But Grandcourt’s always late. In town, he’s always late. And he’s no bowman—doesn’t understand a thing. But I told him he must come. He’d see the cream of the neighborhood. He asked about you—had seen Arrowpoint’s card. I think you hadn’t met him in town. He’s been abroad a lot. People don’t know him well.”

“No,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said. “We’re strangers. But that’s not what one might expect. His uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, and I are great friends when we meet.”

“I don’t know,” Lord Brackenshaw said, smiling toward the rector. “Uncles and nephews aren’t as likely to be seen together as uncles and nieces.” Then, turning to Gascoigne: “Just come with me a moment, will you? I want to speak about the clout-shooting.”

Gwendolen chose to go too, joining her mother and aunt until her next turn. The possibility that Mr. Grandcourt might not appear had begun to dim her joy. Beneath her sharp wit—sparked by her sense that others saw him as a match for her—lay a deeper feeling. She cared deeply about the impression she made on him. True, he had no power over her (though Gwendolen hadn’t grasped that desire to conquer is a kind of surrender). She’d decided he’d be another of those admiring, complimentary men—like several she’d already met, with different beards and styles. The knowledge that her friends expected her to find him delightful made her resist thinking him charming. But that didn’t mean she could afford to miss his presence. Even a fleeting thought of rejecting him caused no wish for him to avoid her altogether. The image of Mr. Grandcourt ignoring her and soon marrying Miss Arrowpoint was not one her imagination welcomed.

So Gwendolen listened closely to Lord Brackenshaw’s explanation of Grandcourt’s delay. And when he finally arrived, no consciousness—Mrs. Arrowpoint’s, Mr. Gascoigne’s, or even her own—was more alert than hers. Though she refused to look in his direction, she avoided shifting her gaze, determined not to betray that his presence mattered. She returned to the shooting, resisting the urge to scan the crowd. Even if he stood prominently, she would pretend not to notice. Yet all the while, the certainty of his being there threaded through her awareness.

Perhaps her aim improved for it. At any rate, her shots grew sharper. She ended with three consecutive hits in the gold—an achievement that sent waves of applause through the field. Among Brackenshaw’s archers, this earned not a shilling, but a special gold star to wear on the breast.

That moment was not only her own triumph—it was exactly what her mother and uncle would have wished. The crowd parted to let her advance, and her graceful form moved with perfect poise in the clear afternoon light, shadows long and still. She was the center of the scene—every eye upon her. Enough. She refused to fix her gaze on anyone, focusing only on Lady Brackenshaw. Yet her thoughts wandered elsewhere.

A small part of her pleasure came from knowing Herr Klesmer watched her—when music was irrelevant, when his superiority faded into the background. Vanity is uneasy under indifference, as tenderness is under unrequited love. Even unconquered, Klesmer cast a shadow across her awareness: Mr. Grandcourt was seeing her at her best, likely admiring her without criticism. She didn’t expect to admire him—but that wasn’t necessary for peace.

Gwendolen met Lady Brackenshaw’s smile without blushing (a trait reserved for surprise), but with radiant warmth. She bent gracefully as the star was pinned near her shoulder.

The ceremony over, she exchanged light words and received congratulations as she moved among the groups discussing scores. Then, standing apart, examining the tip of an arrow with a distracted air, Lord Brackenshaw approached.

“Miss Harleth,” he said, “here is a gentleman who refuses to wait any longer for an introduction. He’s had Mrs. Davilow send me to escort him. Will you allow me to introduce Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt?”

The beginning of any acquaintance—whether with people or things—is the moment when our ignorance takes on a shape, however faint.

Gwendolen had long anticipated Mr. Grandcourt’s introduction; yet when Lord Brackenshaw stepped aside to make way for him, and she found herself face to face with the real man, a sudden tremor passed through her. Her cheeks flushed, and the flush deepened as she became aware of it—an unwelcome warmth that seemed to betray her. The surprise came not from his presence, but from how utterly he defied her expectations. Grandcourt was slightly taller than she, their eyes meeting on equal ground. There was no smile on his face, no trace of self-consciousness in his posture. When he lifted his hat, his broad forehead revealed a wide expanse of baldness bordered by a thin fringe of reddish-blond hair—but his hand, raised in greeting, was perfectly formed. His features, clean-shaven from brow to chin, were strikingly regular, with only slight deviations from symmetry. Even his narrow whiskers followed a straight line. His expression carried no hint of grimace or nervous tension—indeed, it was almost unnervingly still. He looked neither animated nor bored, merely… present. Unlike the stiff Englishman who stands rigid after a bow, radiating suppressed energy, Grandcourt seemed drained of motion. His complexion was pale, like an actress stripped of makeup—faded, translucent. His long, narrow gray eyes held no emotion, only a quiet indifference.

To describe a person fully at first glance is impossible. We glimpse the outline, not the whole. We recognize the alphabet, but not yet the language. Gwendolen saw only this much: *He is not ridiculous.* That was enough for now.

Lord Brackenshaw vanished, and conversation began—though it felt more like an exchange of glances. Grandcourt fixed his gaze on her steadily, unchanging, while she glanced back only occasionally, her observations softened by a touch of coquetry. After each of her responses, silence fell—longer or shorter—before he spoke again.

“I used to think archery was a bore,” he said, voice smooth but broken by a slow drawl, as if speaking through a chest cold.

“Are you converted today?” Gwendolen asked.

(Pause. She imagined all manner of thoughts he might be forming about her.)

“Yes,” he replied. “Since I saw you shoot. In such matters, one usually sees people missing—and simpering.”

“I suppose you’re a fine shot with a rifle?”

(Pause. Gwendolen quickly sketched him in her mind for some invisible listener.)

“I’ve stopped shooting.”

“Oh,” she said, “then you’re formidable. People who do something once and then abandon it make others feel trivial—as though we’re wearing outdated fashions. I hope you haven’t given up all your follies, because I indulge in many.”

(Pause. She mentally reviewed her own words, wondering if they sounded too bold.)

“What do you call follies?”

“Well,” she answered, “in general, anything agreeable is considered a folly. But you haven’t given up hunting, I hear.”

(Pause. She recalled what she’d heard of Grandcourt’s standing and decided—he was the most aristocratic-looking man she’d ever seen.)

“One must do something.”

“And do you care for the turf? Or has that too been abandoned?”

(Pause. She mused: perhaps a man so cool and reserved would make a better husband than others—less likely to interfere with her choices.)

“I run a horse now and then. But I don’t get involved in it the way some men do. Do you enjoy horses?”

“Yes, indeed. Nothing makes me feel more alive than galloping across open land. I forget everything. I just feel strong and free.”

(Pause. She wondered whether he approved of her honesty, but resolved not to hide her nature.)

“Do you like danger?”

“I don’t know,” she admitted. “When I’m riding, I don’t think of danger. It feels as though, even if I broke my bones, I wouldn’t feel it. I’d charge at anything that stood in my path.”

(Pause. In her mind, she rode through an entire hunting season with two favorite steeds.)

“You might enjoy tiger-hunting or pig-sticking. I tried both for a few seasons in the East. Everything here pales in comparison.”

“So *you* enjoy danger?”

(Pause. She reflected: perhaps those who seem coldest are actually the most daring. And she felt a flicker of pride in her own insight.)

“One must have something,” he said. “But one grows accustomed to it.”

“I begin to think I’m lucky,” she said. “Everything is new to me. That’s the only thing I can’t get enough of. I’m not used to anything—except boredom. And I’d like to leave that behind, just as you left off shooting.”

(Pause. A thought crossed her mind: perhaps such a man would be dull company. But then she reminded herself—most people were dull. Husbands weren’t companions, really. And anyway, she wasn’t going to accept Grandcourt.)

“Why are you bored?”

“This place is dreadful. There’s nothing to do. That’s why I took up archery.”

(Pause. She realized that an unmarried woman with no freedom and no resources would inevitably grow dull, no matter how high her station.)

“You’ve made yourself queen of it,” he said. “I imagine you’ll win first prize.”

“I don’t know. I have strong rivals. Didn’t you notice how well Miss Arrowpoint shot?”

(Pause. She thought: men have chosen others—not the woman they admired most. She recalled several such stories from novels.)

“Miss Arrowpoint. No—that is, yes.”

“Shall we go see the scores now? Everyone’s moving toward the other end. Shall we join them? I think my uncle is looking at me. He may want me.”

She turned the moment into action—more for herself than for anyone else. The tête-à-tête hadn’t been unpleasant, but it had stirred something unfamiliar in her: a lingering flush, a sense of being unsettled. She didn’t want Grandcourt to assume she valued him highly, or that she was waiting for his attention. Others might speculate about him as a match, but she wouldn’t let him believe she was at his mercy.

Later, Mr. Gascoigne told her, “You just missed the gold arrow, Gwendolen. Miss Juliet Fenn scored eight points higher.”

“I’m glad to hear it,” she replied lightly. “I’d have felt too selfish—taking the best of everything.”

It was impossible to envy Juliet Fenn. She was ordinary in every way except for her archery—and her plainness, which echoed her father’s: underhung jaw, receding brow, like a fish of greater intelligence. (One might wonder: if men truly cared about women’s appearance, should they not examine themselves honestly in the mirror? For a man’s own ugliness could easily outweigh a woman’s beauty, even if she were prettier than he.)

Now the crowd stirred, voices mingling in lively exchanges. Gwendolen watched—curious now about Grandcourt’s interactions with others. He was being introduced to a middle-aged man with a dark, full face and thick hands—Klesmer, whom she recognized vaguely. Klesmer seemed at ease with both men, speaking animatedly—gesturing widely, stretching fingers out, pointing downward, folding arms, tossing his mane. Grandcourt listened impassively, eyes narrowed, left hand in his waistcoat pocket, right finger brushing his thin whisker.

*Which style does Miss Arrowpoint prefer?* Gwendolen wondered, a mocking twist playing on her lips. But she refused to dwell on it, instead turning her full attention to those around her. She was determined not to care whether Grandcourt approached her again.

Yet he did. At the moment he offered to escort Mrs. Davilow to her carriage, he paused and said, “Shall we meet again in the ballroom?”

She nodded. “Yes.” His reply came with the same slow drawl, the same calm gravity.

“You were wrong this time, Gwendolen,” Mrs. Davilow said during their short ride back to the castle.

“In what, mamma?”

“About Mr. Grandcourt’s looks and manners. You can’t find anything ridiculous in him.”

“I suppose I could if I tried,” Gwendolen retorted, a little sharply. “But I don’t want to.”

Her mother wisely said no more.

At dinner, the ladies and gentlemen dined separately—a tradition meant to offer relief and ease. The gentlemen traded tales of the ladies’ supposed taste for venison, especially the fat, which they claimed proved the alarming speed at which corruption might spread among women, unchecked by society. Every year, Lord Brackenshaw—himself a connoisseur—would quote Byron: “A woman should never be seen eating,” adding, confidentially, “The fact is…” as if confessing a long-held belief.

In the ladies’ dining room, Gwendolen was not universally liked. No close friendships formed between her and the other girls. Conversations flowed around her rather than to her. Perhaps it was because she showed little interest in them—when left alone, she felt the emptiness of unoccupied seats. Mrs. Vulcany once remarked that Miss Harleth was too fond of the gentlemen. But Gwendolen wasn’t fond of them—she craved their admiration. And women rarely gave her that.

The exception was Miss Arrowpoint, who often positioned herself near Gwendolen, speaking quietly and kindly.

“She knows,” Gwendolen thought, “as I do, that our friends will fight over a husband. And she refuses to enter the fray.”

“I think Miss Arrowpoint has the best manners I’ve ever seen,” Mrs. Davilow said later, in the dressing room, where Gwendolen, Anna, and Mrs. Gascoigne were preparing for the ball.

“I wish I were like her,” Gwendolen said.

“Why? Are you becoming discontented with yourself, Gwen?”

“No. But I’m discontented with things. She seems content.”

“I’m sure you ought to be satisfied today. You enjoyed the archery, didn’t you?”

“Oh, that’s over,” Gwendolen sighed, stretching her arms upward. “And I don’t know what comes next.”

She was bare-armed now—dancing in her archery dress, jacket discarded. Her white cashmere gown, trimmed with pale green, framed her figure beautifully. A thin gold chain circled her neck, a star pinned to her breast. Her soft hair, piled high in a regal crown, outlined her brow clearly. Sir Joshua Reynolds would have loved to paint her—though he’d have had an easier task than history, needing only to capture one perfect moment, not the shifting truth of a life.

“The dancing comes next,” Mrs. Davilow said. “You’ll enjoy it.”

“I’ll only dance in the quadrille,” Gwendolen replied. “I told Mr. Clintock so. I won’t waltz or polka with anyone.”

“Why suddenly say that?”

“I can’t bear having ugly people so close.”

“Who do you mean?”

“Oh, plenty.”

“Mr. Clintock, for instance, isn’t ugly.” Mrs. Davilow dared not mention Grandcourt.

“Well, I hate woolen cloth touching me.”

“Fancy!” Mrs. Davilow whispered to her sister, who had joined them. “Gwendolen says she won’t waltz or polka.”

“She’s rather whimsical,” Mrs. Gascoigne observed gravely. “It would be more proper for her to behave like other young ladies on such an occasion—especially since she’s had excellent dancing lessons.”

“Why should I dance if I don’t like it, aunt? It’s not in the catechism.”

“My dear!” Mrs. Gascoigne said sharply. Anna looked alarmed. But no one pressed further.

Something had shifted in Gwendolen since the archery field. Yet she looked radiant under the chandeliers of the ballroom, where soft light and floral scents calmed the air. She was clearly the center of attention—every eligible man eager to partner her. Each she accepted was left in mild despair that she refused to waltz or polka.

“Are you under a vow, Miss Harleth?”
“Why are you so cruel to us all?”
“You waltzed with me in February.”
“And you who dance so perfectly!”

These remarks stung with irony. The women who danced assumed she was simply being difficult. But her uncle, overhearing her refusal, defended her: “Gwendolen usually has good reasons.” He admired her distinction—and wished her to remain distinct. The archery ball was meant to be subdued, respectful of all ranks. Dancing, in his view, was too wild, too frivolous.

Among the disappointed suitors, Grandcourt was silent. After dancing a quadrille with Miss Arrowpoint, he appeared content to stay with her. Gwendolen watched him often, but he never approached her. Mr. Gascoigne spoke with him occasionally—but Mr. Gascogne was everywhere.

Still, Gwendolen noticed something: whenever she danced, Grandcourt subtly repositioned himself so he could see her. If he didn’t admire her—so much the worse for him.

This movement became more obvious late in the evening, when she danced with Klesmer. As they walked, Klesmer said, “Mr. Grandcourt is a man of taste. He likes to watch you dance.”

“Perhaps he likes to look at what goes against his taste,” she laughed lightly. “Maybe he’s tired of admiration and enjoys disgust for variety.”

“Those words are unsuitable for your lips,” Klesmer said sternly, frowning. He waved his hand as if dismissing the discord.

“Are you as critical of words as of music?”

“Certainly. I demand your words match your face and form—always part of noble music.”

“That’s both a compliment and a correction. Thank you. But do you know—I’d like to correct *you*, and ask you to understand a joke?”

“One may understand jokes without liking them,” Klesmer replied. “I’ve received opera books full of them. I understood them—so I disliked them. Comic people challenge me: ‘You don’t see the wit, sir?’ ‘No, sir—but I see what you meant.’ Then I’m labeled ‘without esprit.’ But truly,” he added, dropping into reflection, “I am very sensitive to wit and humor.”

“I’m glad you told me that,” Gwendolen said, mischief in her tone. But Klesmer was already lost in thought. She kept her amusement to herself.

“Who’s that near the card-room door?” she asked, spotting the same stranger who’d spoken with Klesmer earlier.

“A friend of mine? No. An amateur I’ve met in town—Lush, Mr. Lush. Too fond of Meyerbeer and Scribe—too fond of the mechanical-dramatic.”

“Thanks. Was his face and form worthy of noble music in speech?”

Klesmer smiled—delightfully, unexpectedly. They were friendly until she asked to be returned to her mother.

Three minutes later, her assumptions were shattered. Turning to speak to her mother, she saw Grandcourt beside her.

“May I ask if you’re tired of dancing, Miss Harleth?” he began, his gaze steady, expression unchanged.

“Not at all.”

“Would you do me the honor—the next quadrille—or another?”

“I should have been happy,” she said, glancing at her card, “but I’m engaged to Mr. Clintock for the next—and in fact, I’m booked for every quadrille. I have none left to give.”

She wasn’t sorry to punish his delay—but she also wanted to dance with him. She smiled warmly as she spoke, and he remained still, staring down at her, no smile in return.

“I’m unfortunate to be too late,” he said after a pause.

“It seemed you didn’t care for dancing,” she said. “I thought it was another thing you’d left off.”

“Yes,” he replied. “But I haven’t begun dancing with you. You make dancing new—as you made archery.”

“Is novelty always pleasant?”

“No. Not always.”

“Then I don’t know whether to feel flattered or not. Once you’ve danced with me, there’ll be no more novelty.”

“On the contrary,” he said. “There would probably be more.”

“That’s deep. I don’t understand.”

“It’s difficult to make Miss Harleth understand her power?” Grandcourt turned to Mrs. Davilow, who smiled gently at her daughter.

“I think she’s rarely seen as slow to grasp.”

“Mamma,” Gwendolen said, teasingly, “I’m adorably stupid. I need everything explained—when the meaning is pleasing.”

“If you’re stupid,” Grandcourt replied, after a pause, “then stupidity is adorable.” He spoke without inflection, but clearly, he knew exactly what to say.

“I begin to think my cavalier has forgotten me,” Gwendolen said after a while, seeing the quadrille forming.

“He deserves to be abandoned,” Grandcourt said.

“I think he’s quite pardonable,” she replied.

“There must have been a misunderstanding,” Mrs. Davilow said. “Mr. Clintock was too concerned about the engagement to forget it.”

But Lady Brackenshaw arrived. “Miss Harleth,” she said, “Mr. Clintock sent me to express his deep regret that he had to leave without dancing with you again. An urgent message came from his father, the archdeacon. Something important. He was to depart immediately. He was _au désespoir_.”

“Oh,” Gwendolen said, “he was kind to remember the engagement. I’m sorry he had to go.”

She was polite, even joyful—on such a favorable occasion.

“Then I may profit from Mr. Clintock’s misfortune?” Grandcourt asked. “May I hope you’ll allow me to take his place?”

“I shall be very happy to dance the next quadrille with you.”

CHAPTER XII. “O gentlemen, the time of life is short; To spend that shortness basely were too long, If life did ride upon a dial’s point, Still ending at the arrival of an hour.” —SHAKESPEARE: _Henry IV_.

On the morning after the archery gathering, Mr. Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt sat at breakfast with his companion, Mr. Lush. The room was bathed in summer light, the open windows framing a view of sun-dappled grass and the quiet sweep of parkland beyond, where trees stood like silent sentinels. Inside, every detail spoke of refined comfort—antique furniture, polished wood, and a hushed elegance that seemed to breathe with restraint. Yet the air between the two men carried a different tone: not warmth, but a careful distance.

Grandcourt had drawn his chair away from the table, one leg draped over another, his cigar held loosely as he gazed out at the lawn. His posture suggested ease, but there was something rigid beneath it—the stillness of someone who expects deference without effort. Lush, meanwhile, continued eating, though his attention flickered between his plate and the man across from him. Between them, six dogs wandered in lazy circles—some sniffing at crumbs, others lounging on rugs or pressing their heads against their master’s knees. All except Fetch, the sleek liver-colored water-spaniel, who sat motionless, her dark eyes fixed on Grandcourt with unwavering devotion.

In his lap rested a tiny Maltese, wrapped in soft fur and adorned with a silver collar and bell. Whenever Grandcourt freed a hand from cigar or cup, it would gently stroke the small dog, murmuring nothing, yet radiating a quiet affection that seemed to exclude all else.

Fetch watched. And waited.

At last, she could endure no more. With a slow, deliberate movement, she placed her large, silky paw upon Grandcourt’s knee.

He looked down—not with kindness, nor anger, but with detached observation. For a moment, silence stretched. Then, with visible effort, he set aside his cigar and lifted the Maltese close to his chin, patting its head with practiced care. Throughout this, his gaze never left Fetch. She whimpered—a soft, pleading sound, barely audible—then lowered her head beside her paw, her eyes wide with appeal.

It was a moment any dog lover might have read as heartbreak. But Grandcourt did not respond with tenderness. When the whimper rose into a full howl, he pushed her away sharply, without a word.

The Maltese was dropped onto the table, where its black nose nearly touched a salt cellar. Grandcourt turned back to his cigar, only to find it had gone out. He frowned—not at the extinguished ember, but at the dog whose cry had disturbed the rhythm of his morning.

“Turn out that brute,” he said quietly, not looking at Lush.

Lush rose immediately. Though Fetch weighed more than he liked, he lifted her without complaint and carried her outside, tending to her in some way that took several minutes. When he returned, he lit his own cigar, settled into a position where he could see Grandcourt’s face, and asked, “Shall you ride or drive to Quetcham today?”

“I am not going to Quetcham.”

“You didn’t go yesterday.”

A pause. Grandcourt exhaled slowly. “I suppose you sent my card and inquiries.”

“I went myself at four. Said you’d be there shortly. They’ll assume something prevented you. Especially if you go today.”

Another silence. Then, “Who else is invited here with their wives?”

Lush pulled out a notebook. “Captain and Mrs. Torrington next week. Then Mr. Hollis and Lady Flora. The Cushats. The Gogoffs.”

“A ragged lot,” Grandcourt remarked after a while.

“Why did you ask the Gogoffs?” Lush replied. “When you write invitations in my name, please give me a list. I didn’t know about the giantess until now. She spoils the look of the room.”

“You invited them yourself when you met them in Paris.”

“What has that to do with it? I told you—I want a list.”

Grandcourt’s voice had changed. Earlier, it had been low, languid, almost bored. Now it was steady, precise—cutting through idle talk like a blade. It was the voice of command, and Lush knew it well.

“Are there other couples you’d like to invite?” he asked cautiously.

“Yes. Think of some decent people—perhaps with daughters. And bring one of your musicians. Not a comic fellow.”

Lush hesitated. “Do you think Klesmer would come once he leaves Quetcham? Only first-class music will satisfy Miss Arrowpoint.”

He said it lightly, but his eyes were fixed on Grandcourt, watching for a reaction. For the first time, Grandcourt turned his gaze toward him—slowly, deliberately. After two long puffs from his cigar, he spoke, lower than before, edged with disdain.

“What in the name of nonsense have I to do with Miss Arrowpoint and her music?”

“Well,” Lush replied, half-smiling, “something. You needn’t trouble much, perhaps. But certain forms must be observed before a man can marry a million.”

“Very likely,” Grandcourt said. “But I’m not marrying a million.”

“That’s a pity,” Lush countered. “To throw away such an opportunity—and undermine your own plans.”

“_Your_ plans,” Grandcourt corrected coldly.

“You have debts. Things may turn out inconveniently. The heirship isn’t absolutely certain.”

Grandcourt said nothing. Lush pressed on.

“It really is a fine chance. Her parents couldn’t ask for better. The daughter—she doesn’t need allowances. She’s not beautiful, but she carries herself well. She could hold any rank. And she wouldn’t refuse what you offer.”

“Perhaps not.”

“And her parents would let you do anything with them.”

“But I wouldn’t want to.”

Lush paused. Then, in a tone thick with warning, “Good God, Grandcourt! After everything you’ve seen—will you let a whim ruin your future?”

“Save your speeches,” Grandcourt said. “I know what I’m doing.”

“What?”

Lush put down his cigar, tucked his hands into his pockets, and leaned back. He looked irritated—but controlled.

“I’m going to marry the other girl.”

“Have you fallen in love?” The question dripped with sarcasm.

“I’m going to marry her.”

“You’ve made her an offer already?”

“No.”

“She’s a young woman with a mind of her own,” Lush said. “She’d make a scene. She knows what she wants.”

“She doesn’t like you,” Grandcourt replied, a ghost of a smile touching his lips.

“True enough,” Lush admitted, then added, again with mockery, “But if you two are devoted, that’ll be enough.”

Grandcourt ignored him. He sipped his coffee, stood up, and stepped out onto the lawn, the dogs trailing behind. Lush watched him go, then resumed his seat. He lit his cigar slowly, stroked his beard thoughtfully, and finally said, softly, “Check, old boy.”

Lush was no fool. Over fifteen years, he had learned what worked—and what didn’t—with Grandcourt. Once a scholar, nearly ordained, he had instead become a traveling companion to a marquess, then to young Grandcourt, who had lost his father early. Grandcourt found Lush indispensable—efficient, discreet, always ready. In return, Lush enjoyed a life of leisure, unburdened by labor, thanks to the arrangements he managed for his employer.

Their relationship had grown into something neither honored nor questioned. Grandcourt relied on Lush, but never respected him. He saw him as useful—like a tool—but also as something that could be dismissed with a word, even kicked, if necessary. Yet Grandcourt never actually kicked anyone. That act was too personal, too undignified. A gentleman’s dogs should be kicked for him. So instead, he spoke in ways that hinted at violence—words sharp enough to sting, but safe because Lush lacked the spirit to retaliate.

And why should he? A man born into modest means, raised to sacrifice comfort for education, now lived in luxury—dining well, riding fine horses, indulging every desire—all without lifting a finger. He had once been known for scholarship, but now that knowledge was merely a scent, a remnant of a past he rarely revisited. His sinecure required nothing more than the appearance of learning. And he was content with that.

He was aware, too, that Grandcourt considered him expendable. But he accepted it—as just another quirk in the master’s unpredictable nature. Since he believed himself blameless, he saw no need to resist temptation, especially when it came wrapped in ease.

So today, despite the tension, Lush retreated to his private room and played the violoncello for an hour—his music rising like a quiet defiance, or perhaps simply a reward for enduring another morning with the man who ruled his world without ever needing to raise his voice.

“Philistia, be thou glad of me!”

Grandcourt had made up his mind—firmly, decisively—to marry Miss Harleth. And once that decision was fixed, he moved with a quiet precision that spoke of a man accustomed to shaping the world around him. Over the next fortnight, he ensured that their paths crossed daily, not by chance but by design. He saw her, or at least made it known that she occupied his thoughts, in ways both visible and subtle. His cousin, Mrs. Torrington, now presided over the household at Diplow, allowing Grandcourt to host a grand gathering where Gwendolen and her mother could be guests of honor. The event was carefully orchestrated: Grandcourt’s attentions were lavished upon Gwendolen—the dowerless beauty—while the heiress remained conspicuously ignored. The effect was unmistakable. The local society, from Mr. Gascoigne down to the most distant neighbor within visiting distance of Pennicote, took the matter as settled. Even the rector, Mr. Gascoigne, found himself compelled to act—he resolved to ensure the settlements would be generous enough for his niece. Indeed, the only mystery left was why Grandcourt hadn’t already proposed. The delay baffled everyone, including Grandcourt himself.

He had confided his intentions to Lush, expecting swift resolution. But time passed, and though he repeatedly told himself in the morning that today would be the day, each evening came without the words spoken. It was odd, even disconcerting. He had never allowed himself to consider that Gwendolen might refuse him. Yet something held him back—not doubt, exactly, but an invisible barrier, like a shadow cast by a fear he couldn’t name. He was certain of her acceptance, yet the certainty felt fragile. We are often paralyzed not by uncertainty, but by the horror of what might happen if we’re wrong. Like a blind worm, harmless in theory, yet terrifying in its potential bite—we avoid it, not because we think it dangerous, but because we can't bear the idea of being hurt.

He had asked permission to bring one of his finest horses for Gwendolen to ride. Mrs. Davilow would accompany them in the carriage, and they would go to Diplow for lunch, Grandcourt riding beside them. It was a perfect autumn afternoon—harvest season, golden and still, with poppies glowing along field edges, a gentle breeze stirring the wheat, and clouds casting fleeting shadows across the hills. Sheaves stood in neat rows; horses strained under heavy loads on stubbled fields. Pastures stretched wide and green, cattle resting beneath broad trees. The road wound through a countryside unchanged for generations—dairy farms nestled quietly, untouched by the rush of progress, while far off, the train’s whistle echoed like a faint reminder of modernity.

But peace did not settle easily in Mrs. Davilow’s heart. As she watched Gwendolen and Grandcourt ride ahead, then slow to a walk so they could talk, she felt the familiar ache of anxiety. This was the moment—a lover’s opportunity to speak plainly, to end all suspense. Yet no proposal came. All she could do was pray, trembling, that her daughter would choose wisely. If Rex had been the suitor, his passion might have repelled her. But Grandcourt? He was different—calm, composed, dignified. And Gwendolen had fallen silent around him, which was unusual. She rarely refrained from sharp remarks, especially about men. Now she said nothing. Mrs. Davilow dared not break the silence, afraid to disturb whatever lay beneath.

Was he the kind of man who could make her happy? That was the question. “Perhaps as happy as any other woman,” she told herself, trying to find comfort. She couldn’t imagine Gwendolen content in modest circumstances. She needed grandeur, freedom, distinction. And Grandcourt offered all that.

Grandcourt, too, felt the weight of hesitation. He longed to speak, to rid himself of this lingering uncertainty. As for whether she would accept him—well, that was a different matter. Not because he doubted her, but because he refused to allow himself to be placed in a position of vulnerability. He wouldn’t propose in a way that invited refusal. No formal letter, no dramatic declaration. He would wait until the moment was right—and when it came, it would be clear that she had already accepted, simply by accepting his attention.

Gwendolen enjoyed the ride. But her joy didn’t spill into laughter or spontaneous chatter as it had with Rex. She smiled, spoke lightly, but there was a distance in her tone, like echoes fading in a hollow valley. She was aware of something—some unseen tension in the air—but it wasn’t submission. Far from it. She meant to remain in control. She wanted everyone, including Grandcourt, to know that she would do as she pleased. If she chose him, it would be on her terms. She would not surrender her independence. She would not “do as other women do.”

Her conversation with Grandcourt was brief, measured—typical of a man who speaks little but carries weight. After entering the park, he asked, “How do you like Criterion’s paces?”

“He is delightful,” she replied. “I’d love to take a leap with him—if it wouldn’t frighten mamma. There was a wide channel we passed earlier. I’d like to gallop back and jump it.”

“Pray do. We can take it together.”

“No, thank you. Mamma is so timid—she might fall ill if she saw me.”

“I’ll go explain. Criterion would clear it perfectly.”

“No—indeed, you’re kind—but it would alarm her too much. I dare take risks when she’s not watching. I just don’t tell her.”

“We can let the carriage pass first.”

“No, no. Don’t think of it again—I only spoke out of impulse,” she said, suddenly retreating from her own suggestion.

“But Mrs. Davilow knows I’ll take care of you.”

“Yes, but she’d worry about my broken neck.”

A pause. Then Grandcourt, looking at her steadily, said, “I should like to have the right always to take care of you.”

Gwendolen didn’t meet his gaze. Her cheeks flushed, then paled. To her, it seemed like a long moment before she answered—lightly, almost flippantly—“Oh, I’m not sure I want to be taken care of. If I chose to risk breaking my neck, I’d like to be free to do it.”

She checked her horse, turned in the saddle, and glanced toward the approaching carriage. Her eyes brushed over Grandcourt, but there was no warmth, no meaning—only a cool detachment. In that instant, she realized she was risking more than her neck—she was risking the chance to stop him entirely. And she wasn’t at all sure she liked that possibility.

“Damn her,” Grandcourt thought, pulling his horse to a halt. He wasn’t given to internal monologue, but that single word carried a storm—frustration, irritation, a sense of being toyed with. Did she expect him to grovel? To declare his love in a melodramatic speech? No. That was not how power worked. He wouldn’t offer himself on a platter. He would wait. And if she rejected him, well—that would be her loss. But he was certain she had already chosen him. Her silence, her teasing, it was all coquetry. She played games, but he would not be fooled.

The carriage arrived, ending the private moment. At Diplow, Gwendolen became the center of attention—riding dress, hat off, surrounded by admiration. Without Lush present to leer, the attention felt flattering rather than oppressive. For a time, the inner conflict faded. She felt lighter, freer. But she also sensed Grandcourt’s presence—unseen, unchanging, unnerving.

After luncheon, Lady Flora Hollis suggested a tour of the grounds. As guests dispersed, Grandcourt had several opportunities to speak privately. But he didn’t. He stayed close, but his words remained polite, impersonal. He looked at her as before, and she returned his gaze—defiantly, unafraid—even if his eyes held no emotion.

Then, near the end of the tour, by a pool where Fetch, the spaniel, attempted to retrieve a water lily, Grandcourt turned and said, deliberately, “This is boring. Shall we go up there?” He gestured to a knoll covered in American shrubs, with a narrow winding path.

“Oh, certainly—since we’re exploring,” Gwendolen replied. She was both intrigued and wary.

The path was too narrow for an arm. They walked in silence. At the top, Grandcourt said, “There’s nothing here. Not worth climbing.”

Gwendolen didn’t laugh. She stood still, holding her robe like a statue, gripping her whip tightly—having grabbed it reflexively when they started.

“What sort of place do you prefer?” he asked.

“Different places have their charm. On the whole, I like open, cheerful ones. I don’t care for anything sombre.”

“Your home at Offendene is rather sombre…”

“It is.”

“You won’t stay there long, I hope.”

“Oh, yes, I think so. Mamma likes being near her sister.”

Silence.

“It’s not expected that *you* will live there forever, though Mrs. Davilow may.”
