“I don’t know. Women can’t go searching for adventures—no North-West Passage, no Nile source, no tiger hunts. We’re planted where we grow, or transplanted by gardeners. We’re raised like flowers—to look pretty, to be dull, and not complain. That’s my view. Flowers get bored. Some become poisonous. What do you think?” She spoke quickly, nervously, tapping her whip against a rhododendron.

“I agree. Most things are bores,” Grandcourt said, his thoughts drifting. Then, after a pause, he added, in his usual broken drawl, “But a woman can be married.”

“Some women can.”

“You, certainly—unless you’re stubbornly cruel.”

“I’m not sure I’m not both.” She turned sharply to face him, sensing his gaze. She wondered what seeing him would do to her—more than to him.

He stood still, half a yard away. A strange stillness settled over her—like a dream. Then he said, “Are you as uncertain about yourself as you make others feel?”

“I’m quite uncertain about myself. I don’t know how uncertain others may be.”

“And you wish them to understand you don’t care?” he asked, his voice harder now.

“I didn’t say that,” she replied, hesitating, turning her eyes away—and whipping the bush again.

“You do care,” he said softly, not faster, but with a new depth.

“Ha! My whip!” she exclaimed, startled. It had slipped from her hand—natural in a moment of agitation—and snapped forward, catching in the branches of an azalea halfway down the slope.

Now she could run down, laughing, and Grandcourt had to follow. But she reached it first, retrieved the whip, and continued down, pausing only to glance back at him—her eyes bright, her cheeks flushed—as if she’d won a small victory. Mrs. Davilow noticed the glow when they rejoined the group.

“It’s all coquetting,” Grandcourt thought. “Next time I call, she’ll come.”

He believed it might happen soon—the picnic archery meeting at Cardell Chase, planned after the ball. That night, Gwendolen found herself torn between two futures, two versions of herself. One was the woman who would accept Grandcourt, the other who would resist. She didn’t know which she would become. The very idea terrified her. Her life motto—“do as I like”—seemed to fail her. She couldn’t predict what she might desire next.

And yet… the prospect of marrying Grandcourt felt increasingly tempting. The luxuries, the dignity, the power to live freely—these were real now, within reach. She could have it all. And Grandcourt? He was flawless in appearance, calm, refined. He had seen the world, traveled everywhere. That alone was appealing. He didn’t seem to enjoy much—perhaps that was a good thing. The less he desired, the more freedom she would have. She imagined managing him effortlessly.

So why did he unsettle her? Why was she quieter, more reserved, around him than with anyone else? His stillness was attractive—charming even—but also slightly numbing. He was formidable. A handsome lizard, perhaps—quiet, deliberate, unlike the darting, flashy creatures she knew. But she knew little of lizards. Maybe this one was gentle. Maybe it would make a fine pet.

She knew nothing about his past. Had he ever loved? Made love? It seemed impossible. He appeared untouched by passion. And yet, here he was, making her question her own resolve. She wished to marry him. He suited her purpose. Her intention, deep down, was to accept.

But could she?

She began to fear herself. Her defiance had gone too far. She wondered what she would do when he approached her again.

On the drive home, seated with her back to the horses as usual, she was watched closely by her mother. The changes in her expression—restless eyes, silence, absent-mindedness—were signs to Mrs. Davilow that something profound had happened.

“Something has happened, dear?” she asked gently.

Gwendolen turned, removed her gloves and hat, letting the breeze touch her skin. They were on a secluded stretch of road, shadows long, no one near. Her eyes met her mother’s, but she said nothing.

“Has Mr. Grandcourt said something? Tell me, dear,” Mrs. Davilow urged, pleading.

“What am I to tell you, mamma?” Gwendolen replied, evasive.

“I’m sure you’re agitated. You ought to confide in me. Don’t leave me in doubt.”

“Mamma, please don’t be miserable,” Gwendolen said, pettishly. “It only makes me worse. I’m in doubt myself.”

“About Mr. Grandcourt’s intentions?”

“No. Not at all.”

“Then about whether you’ll accept him?”

“Precisely.”

“Have you given him a doubtful answer?”

“I’ve given him no answer at all.”

“He’s spoken clearly enough for you to understand?”

“As far as I let him.”

“You expect him to keep pursuing you?”

No reply.

“You haven’t discouraged him?”

“I dare say not.”

“I thought you liked him,” Mrs. Davilow whispered.

“So I do, mamma, as liking goes. There’s less to dislike about him than most men. He’s quiet and *distingué*.” She spoke with a pout, then smiled mischievously. “Indeed, he has all the qualities of a tolerable husband—battlement, veranda, stable, etc.—no grins, no glass in his eye.”

“Be serious, dear. Do you mean to accept him?”

“Oh, pray, mamma, leave me to myself,” Gwendolen said, voice strained.

Mrs. Davilow said nothing more.

At home, Gwendolen announced she wouldn’t dine. She was tired and would come down later. She didn’t mind if her uncle heard—she was convinced he’d side with Grandcourt. And she wanted to accept him, if she could. At that moment, she would gladly have chained her own caprice.

Mr. Gascoigne did hear—not the exact words, but a softened version. Mrs. Davilow conveyed that Gwendolen was uncertain, but leaning toward acceptance. The rector, firm and decisive, felt duty-bound to intervene. Mrs. Davilow hesitated—perhaps it was better to say nothing. Gwendolen was sensitive, perhaps even willful. But the rector wouldn’t be swayed. Once he formed a judgment, he acted. Shadows of doubt were meaningless to him. He would step in.

I will not clothe myself in wreckage—wear jewels carved from the bones of drowned women; feel ghostly hands, cold and vaporous, clutch at my necklace; deceive my maiden heart with an orphan’s inheritance. Let your dead love wed its own silence.

Gwendolen awoke the next morning radiant, like a tall lily newly unfurled—full of life, strength, and quiet confidence. The lingering doubt of yesterday had vanished, swept away by a surge of youthful energy. The archery match in Cardell Chase now seemed a joyful prospect, not just for the sport, but for the chance to be seen, admired, and perhaps courted. She imagined herself gliding beneath the ancient beeches, graceful as a wood-nymph, while Grandcourt watched her with that slow, thoughtful gaze he reserved only for her. He was no passionate shepherd, no romantic Daphnis—but then, why should he be? His quiet intensity was more compelling than any lyric declaration.

She foresaw him making cautious, deliberate overtures—conversation leading to confession—and she saw herself responding, gently, wisely, as she had told her uncle she would: not rushing into vows, but accepting them when they came naturally, as part of a plan already shaped in her mind.

At breakfast, after everyone else had left, Gwendolen found letters on her plate. One brought a smile that deepened as she read it. She passed it to her mother, who returned it with a warm glance. Her daughter’s cheerfulness since waking had lifted her spirits too.

“You don’t feel like running off a thousand miles?” Mrs. Davilow asked.

“Not quite that far.”

“It was careless not to write sooner. Can’t you do it now, before we go?”

“It’s not urgent,” Gwendolen said lightly. “Tomorrow will be fine. They’re leaving town today. I’ll write from Dover. They’ll be there until Monday.”

“Shall I write for you, dear—if it troubles you?”

Gwendolen hesitated, sipping her coffee. Then, sharply, “No, let it wait. I’ll write tomorrow.” A moment later, she looked up with soft playfulness. “Dear, old, beautiful mamma!”

“Old, child, truly.”

“Please don’t say that! I meant ‘old’ as in ‘darling.’ You’re barely twenty-five years older than I am. When you talk like that, my whole future seems to shrink.”

“One can have a great deal of happiness in twenty-five years, my dear.”

“I mustn’t delay,” Gwendolen said, laughing. “The sooner I get my palaces and carriages, the better.”

“And a good husband who adores you, Gwen,” Mrs. Davilow added, encouragingly.

Gwendolen pouted, but said nothing.

There was one small disappointment: the rector was detained by magistrate’s business and might not make it to the Chase at all. Gwendolen didn’t care much that Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna chose not to come without him, but her uncle’s absence felt like a missing anchor. His presence would have made the decision feel inevitable, almost natural. But now, with the choice looming so clearly, she began to feel uneasy. She had nearly accepted Grandcourt. And now, this sudden fullness of possibility—the idea of marriage, wealth, status—felt less like freedom and more like a cage. Yet still, she clung to the thought that marriage might open doors, not close them.

They gathered at Green Arbor, a grassy hollow sheltered by overhanging trees, where the servants had set up the picnic. The warden would guide the archers, keeping them within bounds marked by landmarks—Double Oak, Whispering Stones, High Cross. The plan was to take a light stroll before lunch, saving the real roving for the golden hour. The gathering was swift, and soon the company scattered through dappled sunlight and shadow, a scene so lovely it could have been painted. Roving archery was more elegant than stationary shooting, though less precise. Hits were scattered unevenly among the volunteers. Gwendolen did not stand out in skill, but she carried her failure with charm, laughter, and grace.

She wore white and green again—just as she had the day she first met Grandcourt. He stayed close beside her, yet their interaction remained outwardly unchanged. No one could tell from their expressions or manner that anything had shifted between them. Still, most assumed they were either engaged or on the verge of it. And Gwendolen believed it too.

As they walked back toward Green Arbor in loose groups, chattering and unthinking, a conversation unfolded that felt like the start of something final. Grandcourt asked, “Do you know how long it’s been since I first saw you in this dress?”

“The archery meeting was on the 25th,” Gwendolen replied, smiling. “This is the 13th. I’m not good at math, but I’d guess nearly three weeks.”

A pause. Then he said, “That’s a great loss of time.”

“That your knowing me has caused you? Please don’t be unkind—I don’t like it.”

Another pause. “It’s because of the gain that I feel the loss.”

Gwendolen held her breath. She thought: *He’s clever. Never says a foolish thing.* Her silence, rare and deep, seemed like assent. He continued:

“The joy of knowing you makes me ache for the time I’ve lost in uncertainty. Do you like uncertainty?”

“I think I do,” she said suddenly, her eyes brightening. “There’s more in it.”

Grandcourt met her gaze with a slow, steady look—like seeing beyond the surface into the soul. Then he asked, “More torment for me?”

Something stirred inside Gwendolen—something strange and unsettling. She blushed, looked away. “No—that would make me sorry.”

He would have pressed on, sensing victory in her change of demeanor. But he remembered they were in sight of others, descending a steep slope. It was polite to offer his hand. She took it, and they moved down in silence—watched by Mrs. Arrowpoint, standing near Mrs. Davilow.

Mrs. Arrowpoint frowned. “Mr. Grandcourt isn’t equal to his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger—too languid. True, he’s younger, but I wouldn’t be surprised if Sir Hugo outlived him. Successions are tricky.”

“It is indeed,” Mrs. Davilow agreed, her usual melancholy replaced by quiet contentment. She was pleased with the course of things.

But the story wasn’t about food or scenery. It was about a young woman whose world narrowed to one question, one man, one decision. The champagne flowed, the laughter rang, the sun dipped low. Gentlemen strolled, some lighting cigars. Among them was Grandcourt—but not Mr. Lush, who, unusually, was busy helping everyone, ordering provisions, attending to details. To Gwendolen, he was a blot on the landscape, though he kept his distance, never looking at her directly.

When it was time to depart, the bows had been entrusted to Lord Brackenshaw’s valet. Mr. Lush offered to fetch them for the ladies. Gwendolen, fearing he might bring hers, hurried to retrieve it herself. The valet met her with the bow—and slipped a letter into her hand.

She glanced at it. The handwriting was delicate, feminine—old-fashioned, like a relic from another age. Without asking, she turned away, bow in hand, and walked toward the carriage line. Mr. Lush approached to collect other bows. She stepped aside, back toward the stands, and opened the letter.

It read:

*If Miss Harleth is uncertain whether to accept Mr. Grandcourt, let her break from her party after passing the Whispering Stones and return to that spot. There she will hear something that will decide her—but only if she keeps this letter secret. If she does not act, she will repent, as I have repented. The secrecy she will feel bound to honor.*

Gwendolen froze. Then, with a jolt, she thought: *It’s come in time.* Her youth made her believe in revelations, not tricks. She resolved to go to the Whispering Stones alone. She tucked the letter into her pocket and rejoined the group—her pulse quick, her spirit sharpened by the weight of a secret.

To everyone’s surprise, Grandcourt was absent when the others prepared to leave. “We’ll find him,” Lord Brackenshaw said. “He can’t have gone far.” No one waited. His absence could be interpreted as distraction—lover’s preoccupation. The Earl gave Gwendolen a teasing hint, which she received calmly. But her mind whispered: *Could he be fleeing the decision too?* It was unsettling—but close to the truth.

Yet “fleeing” wasn’t right. Grandcourt wasn’t running. He was simply paralyzed—a numbness taking hold when success seemed near. To withhold now, when all expected commitment, was its own kind of triumph—will over desire, independence over obligation. He lit a second cigar, half-blind, stubborn. Had anyone interrupted him, he might have muttered, “You’ll kindly go to hell.”

But no one did. The party moved on, cheerful, unaware of the storm brewing behind the calm surface.

The forest grew more beautiful with each step. Shadows lengthened. The air thickened with warmth and memory. Someone joked that they were playing *As You Like It*, and when Gwendolen was praised for being Rosalind, she felt compelled to shine—though not from pride, but from a restless need to act, to control, to escape.

An hour passed. They reached the Whispering Stones—two towering, conical blocks leaning toward each other like ancient sentinels. They passed quickly, remarking how they’d make good ghosts under moonlight. But sunlight gilded them now. Gwendolen felt bold.

“How far to Green Arbor?” she asked the warden.

“Half a mile along the avenue we’re crossing. But I’ll take a roundabout route—past the High Cross.”

She fell back. Then, as Mr. Lush guided the group forward, she lingered—her chance. She slipped away, unseen. She walked swiftly, not running, yet feeling as if she flew. Time dissolved. She reached the stones again.

They faced her blankly. What lay beyond? Would there be nothing? That was her only fear—to return empty-handed, confused. She circled the right stone. And there, a few feet ahead, stood a woman.

Her eyes were large, dark, haunted. Gwendolen gasped—then stepped back, instinctively. But she saw: a lady, once very beautiful. And nearby, two children sat on the grass.

“Miss Harleth?” the woman said.

“Yes.”

“Have you accepted Mr. Grandcourt?”

“No.”

“I promised to tell you something. And you must promise to keep my secret. Whatever you decide, you will not tell him—or anyone—that you’ve seen me?”

“I promise.”

“My name is Lydia Glasher. Mr. Grandcourt should not marry anyone but me. Nine years ago, I left my husband and child for him. These two children are his. We have two girls, older. My husband is dead. Mr. Grandcourt ought to marry me. He should make that boy his heir.”

She looked at the boy. Gwendolen followed her gaze. The child was blowing a tiny trumpet—useless, silent. His hat hung backward, curls catching the light. A cherub.

Their eyes met again. Gwendolen said, proudly, “I will not interfere with your wishes.” Her voice trembled. Her lips were pale.

“You are very attractive, Miss Harleth. But when he first knew me, I was young too. Since then, my life has broken apart. It’s not fair—he should be happy, and I miserable, my son cast aside for another.”

Her words were sharp, controlled. Gwendolen watched her face—felt a terror, as if a vision of ruin had stepped from a dream and said, “I am a woman’s life.”

“Is there more you wish to say?” Gwendolen asked, low, proud, cold.

“Nothing. You know what I wanted you to know. You may inquire about me. My husband was Colonel Glasher.”

Then Gwendolen turned and walked away, offering a formal nod. The woman returned it with equal dignity.

Minutes later, she was back in the beech grove. Her party was gone. She walked the avenue back to Green Arbor, moving fast—anything to keep thoughts at bay. She had decided.

Mrs. Davilow was stunned to see her daughter return alone. “Oh, you’ve been silly,” Gwendolen said. “I lingered at the stones. The others rushed on. I took the short way—just as the warden told me. Honestly, I’m glad. I’ve had enough walking.”

“Did you not meet Mr. Grandcourt?” Mrs. Arrowpoint asked, pointedly.

“No,” Gwendolen replied, with a flash of defiance and a laugh. “And we didn’t see any carvings on the trees. Where can he be? I suppose he’s fallen into the pool—or had an apoplectic fit.”

Her voice was high, brittle. Mrs. Davilow sensed trouble.

Mrs. Arrowpoint thought Gwendolen was offended—perhaps Grandcourt had changed his mind.

“If you don’t mind, mamma,” Gwendolen said, “I’ll order the carriage. I’m tired. Everyone will be leaving soon.”

Mrs. Davilow agreed. By the time the carriage arrived, the rovers returned—with Grandcourt.

“Ah, there you are!” Lord Brackenshaw said, stepping forward. “We thought you’d been picked up by Grandcourt. Lush said so. But then we found him. Still, we didn’t think you were in danger. The warden said he’d told you a shortcut.”

“You’re going?” Grandcourt asked, approaching with his usual composure.

“Yes,” Gwendolen said, adjusting her shawl. “We’re leaving.”

“May I call at Offendene tomorrow?”

“Oh yes,” she said, sweeping him with her lashes. Her voice was sharp, frost-like.

Mrs. Davilow accepted his arm. But Gwendolen moved swiftly ahead and leapt into the carriage.

“I got in first, mamma,” she said, apologetically. “I wanted this side.” She had avoided his touch. He merely raised his hat and walked away—satisfied she was offended.

For five minutes, they drove in silence. Then Gwendolen spoke.

“I intend to join the Langens at Dover, mamma. I’ll pack immediately and catch the early train. I’ll arrive almost as soon as they do—we can telegraph.”

“Good heavens, child! Why say such a thing?”

“My reason for saying it is that I mean to do it.”

“But why?”

“I want to go away.”

“Because you’re angry at Grandcourt’s odd behavior today?”

“It’s useless to discuss it. I won *not* marry Mr. Grandcourt. Don’t concern yourself further.”

“What shall I tell your uncle, Gwendolen? You led him to believe last night you’d decided in favor of Grandcourt.”

“I’m sorry to cause you distress, dear mamma, but I can’t help it.” Her tone hardened. “Whatever you or Uncle think, I won’t change my mind. I won’t tell you why. I don’t care what happens. I don’t care if I never marry. Nothing matters. I believe all men are bad. I hate them.”

“But must you leave like this, Gwendolen?” Mrs. Davilow whispered, broken.

“Don’t interfere, mamma. If you’ve ever suffered, remember it. If I’m to be unhappy, let it be by my own choice.”

The mother sank into trembling silence. She began to understand: sometimes, the best way to stop a storm is to let the person walk away.

CHAPTER XV. “_Festina lente_—celerity should be tempered with caution.”—SIR THOMAS BROWNE.

Gwendolen had spent her time abroad caught in the heady rush of gambling, wrapped in the illusion that she was a queen of fortune, carrying with her from her recent experiences a vague belief that in this tangled world, nothing truly mattered—so long as one found pleasure. She had returned to England not by choice, but because circumstances had forced her hand, returning with a necklace she had pawned and which someone else had redeemed—a small, heavy token of her recklessness.

As she made her way home, Grandcourt was on his way to find her—but not with the urgency one might expect. He did not race from Diplow to Leubronn where she was believed to be. Instead, he moved slowly, drawn into the charm of idle pleasures, lingering at Baden-Baden to meet Russian acquaintances, making appointments that he would later break when the pull of reaching Leubronn became stronger. His passions were never fierce or sustained—they flickered like candle flames in a draft. Yet even without fire, life goes on: cravats are tied, dinners attended, speeches delivered with practiced ease, all without the spark of deep desire. A man can rise in society, appear cultivated, speak with authority on politics and science, and yet do so with minimal effort. He may seem stubborn, or sudden in action, but these impulses often stem not from strength, but from a lack of direction—like water without channels, spilling out in bursts or puddles.

Grandcourt had not been displeased by Gwendolen’s departure. In fact, it stirred something in him—something faintly thrilling. Her defiance pleased him. He liked to think it was born of anger over his cold behavior at Cardell Chase, an act that, upon reflection, seemed somewhat careless. To have brought her close to confessing her feelings, only to walk away himself, was enough to provoke any spirited young woman. And for her to resist him meant she had spirit—and spirit, he believed, was worth conquering.

He knew she expected him to pursue her. And he meant to. But for a full week, he took no steps toward leaving, nor did he ask where Miss Harleth had gone. Mr. Lush watched this silence with a mixture of triumph and unease. Grandcourt said nothing about her, his expression unreadable—like an alligator floating still beneath the surface. One could never predict what might stir in his slow-moving mind.

Yet the delay cost energy. The guests at Diplow grew more curious than their host. What had become of Miss Harleth? Could it be true that she had refused Mr. Grandcourt? Lady Flora Hollis, a vivacious woman with a sharp eye for gossip, decided to make a round of calls with Mrs. Torrington—visiting the rectory, Offendene, and Quetcham. She gathered not only news but also rumors: Gwendolen had gone to Leubronn with old friends, the Baron and Baroness von Langen. The initial shock and disappointment of Mrs. Davilow and the Gascoignes had settled into a quiet wish that Gwendolen’s absence not be seen as odd or shameful.

The rector, too, entertained hope—though not openly—that the marriage was merely delayed. Mrs. Davilow had not dared tell him how fiercely Gwendolen had spoken. Still, the rector clung to old maxims: Amaryllis fled, but wished her hiding place known; love, they say, will find its way over mountain and wave—even in this age of steam. Gwendolen, he thought, was an Amaryllis of sound sense and daring charm. The question was whether she had dared too far.

Lady Flora, returning full of news, saw no reason not to try to stir Grandcourt. At dinner, she mentioned the growing talk that he was now a disappointed suitor. Grandcourt listened quietly, but attentively. The next day, he ordered Lush to arrange a reasonable excuse to end the gathering at Diplow within a week—he intended to go yachting in the Baltic or elsewhere. It was impossible, he said, to remain at Diplow like a prisoner, surrounded by people he never wanted.

Lush understood immediately: Grandcourt was going to Leubronn. But he might move like a billiard ball rolling slowly across the table—hesitant, indirect. Lush’s plan was to make himself indispensable so he could accompany him. And he succeeded. Grandcourt found Gwendolen’s dislike of Lush amusing—only adding to his desire to keep him close.

Thus it was that Grandcourt arrived at the _Czarina_ five days after Gwendolen had left Leubronn, finding there his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, with his family—including Deronda.

It is rarely pleasant when two men of influence, each occupied with their own affairs—one plagued by gout, the other by willfulness—find themselves unexpectedly in the same place. Sir Hugo was a man of easy temper, tolerant of others’ flaws, though certain differences grated on him more than others. The way Grandcourt viewed the family estate settlement troubled him deeply—more than church doctrine or political reform. Faults were less forgivable when they belonged to someone whose presence was inconvenient.

Grandcourt had never been the nephew Sir Hugo desired. But as presumptive heir to the Mallinger lands, he embodied the baronet’s deepest regret—the lack of a son to inherit. In a poorly planned inheritance arranged by Sir Francis, even Diplow, with its modest acreage, had been placed under the same conditions as the vast ancestral lands of the Toppings. Diplow had once been Sir Hugo’s home, where he hunted and lived through many seasons. It was meant to be a retreat for his wife and daughters after his death.

But years passed. Lady Mallinger had three daughters in quick succession, then eight years of silence—no son. Sir Hugo, nearly twenty years older, reached an age where hope began to fade, despite the fashionable delays of modern life. He had begun to accept that no son would come. And Grandcourt’s interest in the estates only made his presence more unwelcome. Yet, paradoxically, it also gave Sir Hugo a reason to maintain peace—because it opened the possibility of securing Diplow for his own family.

He had long considered offering Grandcourt a sum of money in exchange for relinquishing his claim to Diplow. If, against all odds, a son were born, the money would be wasted. But Sir Hugo saw this risk as negligible. He had managed his wealth wisely—through mines and leases—and was ready to spend.

So he avoided conflict. Years earlier, when improving the Abbey and needing Grandcourt’s approval to cut down timber, he had been relieved to find no real animosity in his nephew’s nature. Nothing since had worsened their relationship beyond polite distance.

Grandcourt, meanwhile, saw his uncle as a burden—a man who ought to be erased from existence. But through Lush, he had learned of Sir Hugo’s plans regarding Diplow—and this knowledge pleased him. Even if he never accepted the offer, the mere possibility of refusing it flattered his sense of power.

This understanding had influenced his request for a year’s tenancy of Diplow—an arrangement Sir Hugo reluctantly granted. He feared Grandcourt might grow attached to the land, especially with its excellent hunting. A man with two properties naturally seeks a third where the sport is better.

Lush had also hinted to Sir Hugo that Grandcourt might marry Miss Arrowpoint. If so, money might lose its appeal. So when they met unexpectedly at Leubronn, Sir Hugo was eager to learn what had transpired at Diplow. He intended to be civil, and hoped for a private word with Lush.

Between Deronda and Grandcourt existed a subtle, unspoken connection—its roots yet to be revealed. But on first meeting at the _table d’hôte_, no sign of tension emerged. Later, the four men walked together through the rooms. As they entered the large hall, Sir Hugo asked, “Did you play much at Baden, Grandcourt?”

“No,” replied Grandcourt. “I watched and betted a little with some Russians.”

“Had you luck?”

“What did I win, Lush?” Grandcourt asked.

“About two hundred,” said Lush.

“You’re not here for the gambling, then?” Sir Hugo inquired.

“No,” Grandcourt replied, brushing his whisker lightly. “I don’t care for it now. It’s a confounded strain.”

His diamond ring and calm demeanor drew glances from foreigners fascinated by the new English lord.

“The truth is,” said Sir Hugo, “someone should invent a machine to do amusements for us—like the Tartars get their prayers done. But I agree: gambling bores me. It knits the brain into tight meshes. Watching it knocks me out. I suppose one gets poisoned by the air. I never stay longer than ten minutes.”

Then he turned to Deronda. “Where’s your gambling beauty? Have you seen her lately?”

“She’s gone,” Deronda answered shortly.

“An extraordinary girl,” said Sir Hugo, turning back to Grandcourt. “A perfect Diana. Worth straining to look at. I saw her winning—she took it as coolly as if she’d known it all along. The same day, Deronda saw her losing wildly, and she bore it with great courage. I suppose she stopped in time—or was cleaned out. How do you know she’s gone?”

“Oh, by the Visitor-list,” said Deronda, barely shrugging. “Vandernoodt told me her name was Harleth, with the Baron and Baroness von Langen. I checked the list—Miss Harleth is no longer listed.”

Lush heard nothing new—except confirmation that Gwendolen had gambled. He had already noted her absence. But he had no intention of revealing it to Grandcourt until the moment was right. He waited—knowing the object of his search would eventually surface.

Now, however, Grandcourt had heard something intriguing. Not a word about Miss Harleth had escaped him.

After a pause, he asked Deronda, “Do you know those people—the Langens?”

“I’ve spoken with them a little since Miss Harleth left. I didn’t know them before.”

“Where has she gone? Do you know?”

“She’s gone home,” Deronda replied, coldly—then, suddenly, turned to face Grandcourt directly. “Though perhaps you know her. Her home is near Diplow—Offendene, near Winchester.”

Deronda’s gaze held Grandcourt’s—sharp, intense, alive. There was a richness in his features that startled on first glance, so vivid that people sometimes assumed he had spoken when he had not. Grandcourt felt a flicker of irritation—shown only in a slight twitch of his eyelid—at being looked at so directly without invitation.

But he answered, as usual, with a slow drawl: “Yes, I know her.”

Then he turned his shoulder, stepping toward the gaming tables.

“What of her?” Sir Hugo asked Lush, moving on with the others.

“She must be new to Offendene,” said Lush. “Old Blenny lived there after the dowager died.”

“A little too much of her,” Lush added, lowering his voice. “He was on the verge of marrying her. But I hope it’s off now. She’s a niece of the clergyman at Pennicote—Gascoigne. Her mother is a widow with several daughters. This girl has nothing—yet she’s dangerous as gunpowder. A foolish match. But she’s taken a sudden fancy against him—ran off here without notice, when he’d agreed to call the next day. The truth is, he’s here after her—but he wasn’t in a hurry. Between their whims, they may never reunite. Of course, he’s lost his chance with the heiress.”

Grandcourt joined them. “What a wretched place this is!” he muttered. “Worse than Baden. I’m going back to the hotel.”

When Sir Hugo and Deronda were alone, the baronet remarked, “Rather a fine tale. That girl has fire. She’s worth chasing—has _de l’imprévu_. I believe her appearance has improved my chances of getting Diplow, whether the marriage happens or not.”

“I hope such a marriage doesn’t happen,” Deronda said, with clear disdain.

“What! Are you touched by the sublime lash?” Sir Hugo asked, adjusting his glasses. “Are you tempted to run after her?”

“On the contrary,” said Deronda. “I’d rather run away.”

“You’d easily outshine Grandcourt,” Sir Hugo replied. “A spirited girl like her would see you as the finer match.”

“I suppose pedigree and land count for a fine match,” Deronda said, coldly.

“The best horse wins regardless of pedigree,” said Sir Hugo. “You remember Napoleon’s saying: _Je suis un ancêtre._”

“I’m not sure I want to be an ancestor,” Deronda replied. “It doesn’t seem the rarest kind of origin.”

“You won’t chase the pretty gambler, then?” Sir Hugo asked, putting down his glasses.

“Decidedly not.”

The answer was honest. Yet somewhere in Deronda’s mind, a quieter voice admitted that under different circumstances, he might have followed her curiosity. But his past had shaped him otherwise. He felt bound—not free.

Men, like planets, carry within them a hidden history as vast and intricate as the visible paths they trace across the sky. The astronomer maps their orbits with precision, tracing every arc through darkness and light; but the storyteller of human lives must journey deeper—into the silent corridors of feeling, where moments of sorrow and revelation shape the soul more powerfully than any deed. It is in these unseen currents that true character is born, just as Prometheus’s chained cry was not merely pain, but a force greater than the heavens he defied.

Daniel Deronda’s life had been shaped by circumstances both rare and tender. One moment stood out, etched into his memory like a sunlit dream: a July afternoon dappled with sunlight and pink roses falling softly onto a grassy courtyard, enclosed on three sides by the ancient arches of a Gothic cloister. There he lay, a boy of thirteen, stretched on the cool grass beneath the shade, his curly head resting on his arms as he read Sismondi’s *History of the Italian Republics*. His tutor, Mr. Fraser, sat nearby on a camp-stool, absorbed in a book of political economy. Daniel, always drawn to history, longed to know how time unfolded after the flood, how civilizations endured through ages of quiet and turmoil.

Then, without warning, Daniel lowered his arm and looked up at Mr. Fraser, his voice clear and innocent as a bell:

“Mr. Fraser, why did the popes and cardinals always have so many nephews?”

The tutor, a sharp-witted young Scot who served as Sir Hugo Mallinger’s secretary, stirred reluctantly from his reading and answered with the crisp clarity that made truth sound certain in Scottish speech:

“Their own children were called nephews.”

“Why?” Daniel asked.

“It was simply for propriety,” Mr. Fraser replied, slightly impatient. “Priests don’t marry, and the children were illegitimate.”

He turned back to his book, lips pursed in emphasis, but Daniel sat upright abruptly, his back now turned to the man. A sudden chill passed through him, as if something invisible had pierced his innocence.

He had always called Sir Hugo his uncle. When he once asked about his parents, the baronet had said gently, “You lost your father and mother when you were very small—that’s why I care for you.” Daniel remembered only fragments: dim warmth, soft scents, a kiss that lingered, then a sharp pain when his fingers caught on something hard—and then tears. Everything else belonged to the world he knew: the grand estate, the cheerful company of Sir Hugo, the beauty of the abbey-turned-home, with its gallery of ancestors watching silently from the walls.

There were men in armor, women in ruffs and hoops, politicians in powdered wigs, ladies painted with rosy lips and wide eyes—each generation a portrait of lineage and pride. The family tree stretched back centuries, rooted in Hugues le Malingre, who came with the Conqueror. The Mallingers had held Monk’s Topping since Henry VIII, and their bloodline bore the mark of refinement, even if their noses sometimes varied in length. Sir Hugo, whose portrait showed a bright-eyed youth with a slightly elongated nose (a liberty taken by Sir Thomas Lawrence), had passed on his charm to his younger brother Henleigh, who had married Miss Grandcourt and merged two noble lines—three Saracens’ heads and three bezants joined with a tower and falcons argent.

But Daniel, though handsome beyond the others, did not resemble any of them. He was more striking than all—his face seemed carved for legend, capable of making one believe in the nobility of mankind. To see him was to feel the weight of what might be possible in the world, and the sorrow of what had gone wrong.

Yet now, on this peaceful afternoon among fallen petals, Daniel felt something shift inside him. The idea—unbidden—had come: *What if the man I call uncle is really my father?* And if so, what had become of my mother? Had she died? Was she alive somewhere, unknown to me?

It was not the kind of thought a child usually carries. Most boys imagine their parents as unshakeable figures, able to provide anything. But Daniel, already sensitive and thoughtful, had never questioned his place in the world—until now. The knowledge he had gathered from Shakespeare and history suddenly turned inward. He had read of bastards rising to greatness, of men born in shame who became heroes. But those stories had never touched him—until today.

Now, the past felt different. The affection he had for Sir Hugo deepened into something heavier, tinged with suspicion. Could it be that his uncle kept secrets? That he had been lied to? That his birth was not what it seemed? The thought brought no comfort, only a quiet dread. To speak of it felt like stepping into fire. Children raised in love often fear the idea of their parents’ imperfections—not because they are cruel, but because such truths threaten the sacredness of childhood.

Tears welled up slowly, silently, before he heard Mr. Fraser’s voice:

“Daniel, do you see you’re sitting on your book?”

He moved it without turning, then rose and walked away, into the open grounds, where he could weep unseen. The shock passed, but the question remained: What if everything he believed was false?

He reminded himself that he had no proof—only guesses, like the stories he’d made up about Pericles or Columbus. Yet certain things clung stubbornly: the way people looked at him, the words spoken in whispers, the odd glances. Once, years ago, while drinking whey at Mrs. Banks’s cottage, the bailiff had winked and said, “He features the mother, eh?” At the time, Daniel had thought it foolishness. Now, it echoed like a clue.

Could he look like his mother and not his father? If Sir Hugo was his uncle, then his mother must have been a Mallinger. But what if his father was Sir Hugo’s brother—Henleigh’s brother? But then why had Sir Hugo never mentioned a brother named Deronda? Why did no one speak of it?

He remembered the illuminated parchment in the library—the family tree. He had seen it once, as a child, but hadn’t understood it. Now, he wished he could examine it. But he dared not. To touch it would be like touching a wound.

In such moments—when the world seems stable and yet trembles beneath—character is forged. Daniel was not bitter. He loved deeply, and that love refused to let resentment take root. He adored Sir Hugo—everything about him: the way he smoked, wrote, laughed with his dogs. The books he had written—on travel, politics, philosophy—were sacred to Daniel, not because they were wise, but because they were *his*.

And now, the thought that this beloved man might be hiding something—something shameful—was agony. Children demand perfection from their heroes. To discover otherwise is like watching the sky fall.

But soon, a new turn came. Sir Hugo summoned Daniel to the library. He sat back in his chair, smiling.

“Ah, Dan,” he said, pulling over an embroidered stool. “Come sit here.”

Daniel obeyed. Sir Hugo placed a hand on his shoulder.

“What’s wrong, my boy? Have you heard something troubling?”

Daniel couldn’t speak. Tears threatened.

“All change is painful,” Sir Hugo said gently, stroking his curls. “You can’t be educated exactly as I wish without our parting. But I think you’ll find much to like at school.”

Daniel looked up, surprised.

“Am I to go to school?”

“Yes,” Sir Hugo said. “To Eton. You’ll need a gentleman’s education. Cambridge afterward—my own university.”

Color flushed and faded in Daniel’s cheeks.

“I should like to be a gentleman,” he said firmly. “And go to school—if that’s what a gentleman’s son must do.”

Sir Hugo smiled. “And you won’t mind leaving your old Nunc?”

“I shall,” Daniel whispered, clutching his uncle’s arm. “But will I come home in holidays?”

“Oh yes,” Sir Hugo said. “But first, you’ll go to a new tutor—to ease the transition.”

After that, Daniel’s spirit lifted. He was meant to be a gentleman. Perhaps his fears were unfounded. The unknown still loomed, but hope returned. He sang, danced, gave gifts to the servants, and begged the groom to care for his black pony.

“Do you think I’ll know less than the other boys, Mr. Fraser?” he asked.

“There are fools everywhere,” Fraser replied. “You won’t be the biggest. But you’re no Porson or Leibnitz.”

“I don’t want to be,” Daniel said. “I’d rather be a leader—like Pericles or Washington.”

“Ay,” Fraser chuckled. “They didn’t need much grammar.”

But he knew—this boy was extraordinary. Whatever he set his mind to, he mastered.

At school, Daniel settled well. Quick, good-natured, gifted at both study and sport, he earned respect. But when another boy began talking freely about his family, Daniel pulled back. He couldn’t share. He wouldn’t.

Soon, everyone saw him as reserved—but not cold. His silence was not hostility, but a shield. And though his lips stayed closed, his heart remained open, waiting—for truth, for love, for the day when the shadows would lift.

*"This is true the poet sings,
That a sorrow’s crown of sorrow
Is remembering happier things."* —TENNYSON: _In Memoriam_

On a quiet evening toward the end of July, Daniel Deronda rowed alone along the Thames. It had been over a year since he returned to England, ostensibly finished with his education and expected to take his place in society. Though he had begun studying law—mostly out of respect for Sir Hugo’s wishes and to avoid idle days—this choice had done little more than deepen his uncertainty about what life meant for him. He was no longer the boy who dreamed in grand gestures; yet he still carried within him a restlessness that refused to settle.

Boating had always drawn him, but now it held an even deeper pull. In London, surrounded by the Mallingers, the river offered something rare: peace, solitude, and silence. At Putney, he kept a boat of his own, and whenever Sir Hugo didn’t need him, he would row until dusk, returning under the stars. He wasn’t lost in sentimentality, not exactly—but he was caught in a mood common among young men of his class: questioning whether it was worth joining the world’s relentless struggle. Not that he lacked means—he had capital, inherited ease, and time enough to wonder. But wonder, without purpose, can become a kind of paralysis.

He wore a dark blue shirt and skullcap, his curls neatly trimmed, a soft beard framing his mouth. The seraphic boy he once was had long faded into a man of quiet strength—still handsome, but grounded, thoughtful. His face bore no trace of childishness now, though there remained a certain depth in his gaze—something Gwendolen had once called “dreadful,” though it was really only a calm, searching look. His voice, when it slipped into song, was not a tenor but a high baritone—pleasant, yes, but not striking. And his hands—long, strong, flexible—were made for work, not for delicate gestures. They spoke of power tempered by refinement, like those painted by Titian in some noble portrait where force and grace met.

As he rowed, he hummed softly—a fragment from *Otello*, Rossini’s setting of Dante’s lines:
*"Nessun maggior dolore
Che ricordarsi del tempo felice
Nella miseria."*
(“No greater pain than to remember happy times in misery.”)

It was a melody that haunted him, one he often sang without meaning to. That evening, as he neared Kew Bridge, the words came again, low and mournful, drifting on the water like a whisper.

The river was not empty. A few people strolled along the towpath, and a barge approached slowly. Deronda steered aside, letting his oar rest as he waited. And then, near the bank, he saw her.

A girl—barely eighteen, slight and pale, wrapped in a woolen cloak. Her dark hair was tucked beneath a large black hat, her face small and fragile, her eyes fixed on the water with a stillness that seemed carved from stone. She stood motionless, as if she had forgotten how to breathe. Something about her stirred Deronda deeply—not just her beauty, but the unbearable weight of sorrow pressing down on her.

He stopped singing. And as he did, she turned her head slightly, startled. Their eyes met.

For a moment, nothing moved. No word passed between them. She looked at him with the wide-eyed fear of a fawn—no blush, no panic, just a quiet trembling, as though she were half awake, half dreaming. Then she turned away and walked slowly to a bench beneath a tree.

Deronda could not stay. He had no right to follow, to intrude. Poor women in distress were not uncommon. But this one—her delicate features, the way the light fell on her—made her seem different. Unforgettable.

He rowed on, but the image stayed with him. Who was she? What had brought her here? He imagined stories—abandoned daughters, broken families, love gone wrong. He smiled at himself for believing that beautiful faces must have tragic lives. Yet he couldn’t deny that such sorrow felt sharper when it touched someone so tender, so young.

“I wouldn’t have remembered her look if she’d been plain,” he thought. “But the beauty makes it harder to forget.”

By the time he reached Richmond Bridge, the sun was sinking. The sky blazed gold and crimson, and the trees cast long shadows across the water. This was his favorite hour—the moment when day slipped into night, and silence deepened. He found a secluded spot opposite Kew Gardens, where the river curved gently, and moored his boat. Lying back on the cushions, he watched the sky fade, the stars begin to blink into existence, like answers to a silent call.

He was lost in thought—half-wondering if one could ever dissolve oneself into the world around, until the self became no more than another part of the landscape—when he noticed movement on the opposite bank.

A figure stepped forward from behind the willows.

His breath caught. It was her.

She looked up, then glanced around, reassured by the emptiness. She pulled off her hat and buried it in the bushes. Then, slowly, she removed her cloak and dipped it into the river. She held it there, letting it soak, before lifting it with effort and standing.

Deronda understood instantly. She meant to drown herself.

He rose without hesitation, seized his oar, and rowed swiftly toward her. She froze, terrified, sank to the edge of the bank, clutching the wet cloth as if it were a shield. She covered her face, hoping he hadn’t seen her.

But he was close now. He steadied the boat against the shore and spoke softly, gently:
“Don’t be afraid. You’re unhappy. I know. Please trust me. Let me help you.”

She raised her head. The light caught her face. She recognized him.

After a pause, she whispered, in a voice so sweet it broke through the silence:
“I saw you before… *nella miseria*.”

Deronda frowned. Was she losing her mind?
“You were singing?” she asked, hesitating. “*Nessun maggior dolore*…”

Her voice gave the melody life. He understood now.
“Yes,” he said quietly. “I sing it often. But please—don’t stay here. Let me take you somewhere safe. And that cloak—let me carry it.”

She clutched it tighter, trembling. But then she looked at him, her eyes full of questions.
“You look good,” she murmured. “Perhaps it’s God’s command.”

He reached out his hand.
“Do trust me. I’ll protect you. I’d rather die than let harm come to you.”

She stepped forward. Slowly, she laid the soaked cloak on the ground. Then, with delicate hands folded at her waist, she leaned forward, as if afraid she might lose sight of him.
“Great God,” Deronda breathed, the words escaping like a prayer.

Something stirred deep within him—a memory, a fear, a longing. *Could my mother have been like this?* The thought came with sudden force, mingling grief and awe.

She stepped into the boat.
“You had a covering for your head,” he said.
“My hat,” she replied, touching her head. “It’s hidden in the bush.”

He jumped out, retrieved it, wrung out the cloak, and threw it into the boat.
“We must take it with us,” he said cheerfully. “So no one thinks you’ve drowned.”

He offered his coat.
“It’s cold. Will you mind wearing it?”

She accepted it with a faint, melancholy smile, wrapping it around her shoulders.

“Do you have any food?” he asked.
“No,” she said. “I still had money for bread.”

They rowed in silence. The twilight thickened. Stars blinked one by one. The moon climbed, half-hidden by trees.

She stared at the oar, leaning forward, as if feeling warmth return to her body.
“I like to listen to the oar,” she said softly.
“So do I,” he replied.

“If you hadn’t come,” she whispered, “I’d be dead now.”

“I can’t bear you saying that,” he said. “I hope you’ll never regret I came.”

“But how can I be glad to live?” she asked. “The *maggior dolore* and the *miseria* have lasted longer than the *tempo felice*.”

She paused, then added dreamily:
“*Dolore—miseria*... I think those words are alive.”

Deronda said nothing. To question her felt intrusive. He didn’t want to act as a savior, or assume authority. He simply wanted her to speak, when she was ready.

At last she did.
“I thought it wasn’t wicked. Death and life are one before the Eternal. Our fathers slew their children and themselves to keep their souls pure. That’s what I meant. But now—I am commanded to live. I don’t know how.”

“You’ll find friends,” he said. “I’ll help you.”

She shook her head.
“Not my mother. Not my brother. I cannot find them.”

“You’re English?” he asked gently. “You speak perfectly.”

She looked at him, straining to see in the dim light.
“You want to know if I’m English?”

He flushed under her gaze.
“I only want to know what you wish to tell me.”

She paused, then said quietly:
“I am English-born. But I am a Jewess.”

Deronda was silent. He had not guessed. Yet now, seeing her—delicate, dark-haired, with a quiet dignity—he wondered why he hadn’t seen it before.

“Do you despise me for it?” she asked, her voice breaking.

“Why should I?” he said. “I’m not foolish.”

“I know many Jews are bad,” she said.

“So are many Christians,” he replied. “But I wouldn’t judge you because of that.”

“My mother and brother were good,” she whispered. “But I shall never find them. I came from abroad. I ran away. I cannot speak of it. I thought God would guide me to my mother. But I despaired. This morning, when the light came, I heard one word: *Never!* But now…” Her voice cracked. “Now I think—I am commanded to live. Perhaps we are going to her.”

And she wept, burying her face in her knees.

Deronda hoped the tears would bring relief. But he also felt a growing unease. Where could he take her? Lady Mallinger’s home? Too formal, too bright, too likely to frighten her. He thought of Mrs. Meyrick’s house in Chelsea—small, quiet, warm. Hans Meyrick was away in Italy, but his wife and daughters lived there. Kind-hearted, gentle, inclined to believe in innocence. They would welcome a wounded bird.

He decided quickly. The cab ride felt endless after the stillness of the river. She slept fitfully, her head lolling, her breathing shallow. He watched her, wondering what had brought her to this brink.

Then, suddenly, a memory surfaced—Plutarch’s tale of the Delphic women. When the Maenads, exhausted from their rites, fell asleep in the marketplace, the townswomen gathered silently around them, guarding their sleep. When they woke, they were cared for, guided home.

Deronda felt a quiet certainty. These women would do the same. They had hearts like that.

He was no longer just a young man rowing the river. He was becoming someone else—someone who had found a life, not by choice, but by chance. And perhaps, in saving her, he was being saved too.

For in that moment, as he carried her into the unknown, he realized: to rescue someone from death is not always to save them. But to offer them a chance—to say, *live*—is the beginning of everything.

CHAPTER XVIII. Life is a many-sided mother: sometimes she wears her finest robes and climbs the marble steps with head held high, never glancing at those who serve her; at other times, she walks in tattered clothes through shadowed lanes, breathing in the fumes of cheap gin, shouting amid the chaos of poverty. But to the Meyricks, she came as a modest housewife—neat, thoughtful, and full of quiet joy, finding richness in simplicity. Mrs. Meyrick’s home was quiet, nestled beside the river on one side and gardens on the other. The front parlor faced the water, the back opened onto greenery, so that even while reading aloud to her daughters, she could leave the window open to let in fresh air. Inside, two candles burned on a small table for Kate, who was sketching illustrations for a publisher, while a lamp lit the rest of the room—just enough for Amy and Mab, who were embroidering satin cushions meant for “the great world.” Outside, the house looked narrow and worn, its windows framed in old-fashioned wood, the light from within glowing softly through a holland blind. Yet it was comforting to know that such modest dwellings in foggy London often sheltered lives marked by a culture refined not by wealth, but by choice—where display was seen as meaningless, and the grand spectacles of society merely distractions, provoking envy or false ambition rather than true desire.

The Meyrick household was one such haven. They cherished their little home not just for its comfort, but for its history—each object had its place, each piece holding memory. For the mother, the furnishings reminded her of her marriage days; for the girls, they felt as natural and unchangeable as the stars above. Mrs. Meyrick had sacrificed much so that her husband’s favorite engravings might remain in their home, and the walls, though narrow, held scenes and portraits of world history that the children knew by heart. The chairs and tables were old friends, preferred over anything new. In these two small rooms, where only the prints and piano would have drawn interest from a dealer, there lived a mind wide-reaching and carefully chosen—a life enriched by music, poetry, and art.

It may be that during leaner times, before Kate earned her living through drawing, the family had no servant to tend fires or clean floors. Yet they remained fastidious, especially about manners. They refused to believe that fashionable society was as coarse, quarrelsome, or vulgar as it was often portrayed in literature. The Meyricks had quirks—oddities passed down from both parents—minds like ancient houses with hidden chambers, unexpected passages, sudden views. But what bound them together was stronger than any eccentricity: love for one another, reverence for noble deeds and fine art, and a steady habit of work.

Hans, their brother, had once wanted to spend his money improving their lives—making them more comfortable, more luxurious. But they had all resisted, not out of pride, but because they valued their freedom and integrity. They understood that if Hans gave in to his passion for art, he might eventually lose his fellowship. So they kept their simple ways, laughing at his Gavarni caricatures and honoring his choices without guilt. Their greatest treat was opera—always in the gallery—when Hans returned home.

That evening, the scene was peaceful, almost sacred. All four women were small, perfectly proportioned to their tiny rooms. Mrs. Meyrick read aloud from a French novel—Erckmann-Chatrian’s *Historie d’un Conscrit*. She was lively, half-French, half-Scotch, with speech so clear and vivid it seemed to bring sunlight into the listener’s mind. Though not yet fifty, her hair, covered by a plain net cap, was mostly gray, but her eyebrows and eyes remained dark and bright. Her black dress, buttoned tightly like a priest’s cassock, suited her neat, petite frame. Her daughters matched her in size and spirit—except Mab, who bore her father’s fair hair and complexion, along with a bold brow and features that echoed him. Everything about them was compact: their braided hair tied back *à la Chinoise*, their gray skirts modestly defying fashion, which demanded space and volume. Together, they might have fit into a single lady’s traveling trunk. Their faces seemed alive with thought—like horse-chestnuts cracked open, revealing their inner light.

Only one large creature in the room was Hafiz, the Persian cat, perched calmly on a leather chair, opening his eyes now and then to watch over the humans below.

Mrs. Meyrick had just finished reading. Mab, who had leaned forward, captivated, exclaimed, “That’s the finest story in the world!”
“Of course,” said Amy, teasingly. “It’s the last thing you’ve heard. Everything pleases you in turn.”
“It’s hardly a story,” said Kate. “It’s history brought close—seen through a strong telescope. We see the soldiers’ faces… no, we hear them—almost feel their hearts beating.”
“I don’t care what you call it,” Mab replied, tossing aside her thimble. “Call it a chapter from Revelation. It makes me want to do something good—something grand. I feel sorry for everyone. I love Schiller. I want to take the whole world in my arms and kiss it. I’ll kiss you instead, little mother!” She threw her arms around her mother’s neck.
“Whenever you’re like this, Mab,” Amy said, “your work goes flying.”
“It would be doing something good to finish your cushion without ruining it,” she added.
“Oh—oh—oh!” groaned Mab, bending to pick up her embroidery. “I wish I had three wounded conscripts to nurse.”
“You’d spill their beef tea while talking,” Amy teased.
“Poor Mab! Don’t be hard on her,” said their mother. “Give me your embroidery, child. You go on with your feelings, and I’ll continue with the pink and white poppy.”
“Well, ma,” said Kate, looking at her drawing, “you’re more sharp-tongued than Amy.”
“Oh—oh—oh!” Mab cried again, rising and stretching. “I wish something wonderful would happen. I feel like the flood—waters breaking forth, heavens opening. I must sit down and play scales.”

As Mab opened the piano, laughter filled the room—until a cab stopped outside, and a sharp knock rang at the door.
“Dear me!” Mrs. Meyrick said, jumping up. “It’s after ten, and Phœbe’s asleep.” She hurried out, leaving the parlor door open.
“Mr. Deronda!” the girls heard her say.
Mab clasped her hands. “There! Something *is* happening.”
Kate and Amy dropped their work in surprise. But Deronda’s voice was too low for them to catch his words, and soon the door closed behind Mrs. Meyrick.

“I know I’m asking much,” Deronda said, once inside. “But you can imagine how helpless I am with a young girl like this. I couldn’t take her among strangers, and in her state, I feared bringing her into a house full of servants. I’ve trusted you—with hope you won’t think me reckless.”
“On the contrary,” Mrs. Meyrick replied. “You’ve honored me by trusting me. I understand your difficulty. Bring her in. I’ll tell the girls.”

As Deronda returned to the cab, Mrs. Meyrick stepped back into the parlor.
“Here’s someone to care for instead of your conscripts, Mab—a poor girl who tried to drown herself in despair. Mr. Deronda found her just in time. He brought her in his boat and didn’t know what else to do—so he brought her here. She’s a Jewess, but very refined, he says—speaks Italian, understands music.”

The three girls stood together, silent, expectant, united in compassion. Mab looked awestruck—as if her longing had summoned something divine.

Deronda approached the cab. The girl inside, pale and wide-eyed, stared out.
“I’ve brought you to some of the kindest people in the world,” he said gently. “They have daughters like you. It’s a happy home. Will you let me take you to them?”

She stepped out, hand in his, hat forgotten. When Deronda led her into the warm glow of the parlor, where the four women waited, she became a vision that stirred even the most guarded hearts. At first, the sudden light made her blink. Then, slowly, she focused—and Deronda placed her hand into his mother’s.

He was glad the Meyricks were small—the girl’s dark curls reached higher than anyone else’s. She wouldn’t fear these gentle faces so near. She looked at each in turn, and Mrs. Meyrick said, “You must be tired, poor child.”
“We will take care of you—we will comfort you—we will love you,” Mab burst out, taking the girl’s hand between both of hers.

A warmth spread through the stranger. She stepped back slightly, studying the four faces—seeing kindness reflected in her own eyes, not in a smile, but in a quiet shift: anxiety fading into peace. For a moment, she looked up at Deronda, as if giving thanks to him for all this mercy. Then, turning to Mrs. Meyrick, she spoke softly, with more composure than he had ever heard:
“I am a stranger. A Jewess. You might have thought me wicked.”
“No,” Mab insisted. “We know you are good. We think no evil of you, poor child. You shall be safe here.”
Mrs. Meyrick added, “Come now. Sit down. You must eat, then rest.”

The girl looked up at Deronda again.
“You will have no more fears? You will rest tonight?”
“Oh, I should not fear. I should rest. These—these are the ministering angels.”

Mrs. Meyrick offered her a seat, but the girl hesitated, as if uncertain whether she deserved such welcome without explanation.
“My name is Mirah Lapidoth,” she said. “I came all the way from Prague alone. I escaped—ran from terrible things. I came to find my mother and brother in London. I was taken from my mother when I was small, but I believed I could find her again. I searched—houses were gone, I couldn’t find her. It has been long. I had little money. That is why I am in distress.”

“Our mother will be kind to you,” Mab whispered. “See what a nice little mother she is!”
“Do sit down now,” Kate said, moving a chair forward. Amy rushed to prepare tea.

Mirah sat quietly, gracefully crossing her feet, placing her hands one over the other in her lap. She gazed at her hosts with calm reverence. And then, Hafiz—the Persian cat—came forward, tail upright, rubbing against her ankles.

Deronda knew it was time to leave.
“May I come again tomorrow—at five?” he asked Mrs. Meyrick.
“Yes, please. We’ll have time to get to know her.”
“Good-bye,” he said, looking down at Mirah and extending his hand.

She rose, took it—then lifted her eyes to his, filled with deep gratitude.
“The God of our fathers bless you and deliver you from all evil,” she said. “You saved me when I was poor and broken. You saw worth in me when no one else did. You found me lost, and gave me the best.”

Deronda could not speak. He bowed silently to the Meyricks, then hurried away.

CHAPTER XIX. “I pity the man who can travel from Dan to Beersheba, and say, ‘’Tis all barren’: and so it is: and so is all the world to him who will not cultivate the fruits it offers.”—STERNE: _Sentimental Journey_.

To call Deronda romantic would be a misjudgment of his nature. He was not given to flights of fancy or theatrical emotion; yet beneath his quiet demeanor, beneath the stillness that often masked his thoughts, there burned a deep sensitivity—a capacity to find wonder in the ordinary, beauty in the fleeting moment. Poetry, for him, was not confined to verse or legend; it lived in the ripple of water on a riverbank, in the glance of a stranger’s eyes, in the quiet ache of recognition. And romance? It wasn’t something he sought—it found him, unbidden, in the turning of a leaf, the fall of light, the sudden clarity of a soul revealed.

He sat awake long into the night, replaying the moments since he had first seen Mirah by the river’s edge. The memory was alive, vivid as if it were unfolding again—the way she stood, trembling but resolute, her voice like a thread of music carried on the wind. He tried to read, hoping the words might dull the intensity of what he felt, but the printed page blurred before him. Instead, he saw her clearly—her face, her hands, the look in her eyes when she spoke of her mother and brother. And beyond the facts, he saw possibilities: hopes that stirred like embers, fears that flickered into shadowed visions.

Mirah’s search for her family stirred something deep within him—something personal, almost painful. He too had longed to know his mother, though that longing had always been laced with dread. What if she were cruel? What if she were lost to him forever? And now, seeing Mirah’s yearning, he could not help but imagine the same tragedy for her. She said her mother and brother were good—kind, loving people—but how could she know? Ten years had passed since she last saw them. Time changes everything. The woman she remembered might no longer exist. Or worse—she might have become someone Mirah could never recognize, someone whose presence would wound rather than heal.

Deronda had never truly known Jews—not as people, not as individuals. His knowledge of them came through stereotypes, through stories told in whispers or sneers. He had assumed they were either assimilated, eager to shed their past, or hidden in poverty, clinging to ancient customs. He had no sympathy for prejudice, and yet—he had never cared deeply enough to learn otherwise. Now, however, Mirah’s innocence made him question everything. Her grace, her purity, the gentle strength in her voice—they seemed to belong to a world apart, untouched by the harshness he feared might await her.

And so he imagined what he might find. A narrow street, dimly lit. A door opening onto a cramped room where a woman, sharp-eyed and weary, bartered away the last remnants of a daughter’s dignity. Or perhaps a more polished quarter, where a young man, dressed in borrowed elegance, spoke with easy familiarity, offering favors to gentlemen while hiding a life built on secrets and shame. These images were not born of malice, but of fear—fear that Mirah’s reunion might bring not comfort, but sorrow.

He did not wish to think this way. He despised the kind of prejudice that reduced entire peoples to caricatures. Yet here he was, caught in the web of assumptions, shaped by history and hearsay. But he refused to act without knowing. He would wait—wait to hear from Mirah herself, perhaps through Mrs. Meyrick, to gather clues from her speech, her manner, her memories. Only then could he judge.

Yet even as he hesitated, he knew what must be done. Mirah needed shelter, protection—someone to stand beside her, not just in thought, but in action. He felt a chivalric duty rise within him, stronger than reason, fiercer than caution. He wanted to shield her, to make sure no one could ever claim she owed him anything. Not out of pride, but because he believed she deserved freedom—freedom to choose her own path, to live without being bound by the kindness of others.

And so he wrestled with another truth: secrecy. He hated it. Not only because it bred lies, but because it twisted relationships, turned love into obligation, trust into suspicion. He had vowed to himself that truth should never be used as a weapon—to never hide the facts of his life in a way that would shame another. He had learned, painfully, that some truths are not ours to control. They come from the past, from forces beyond our will. And to conceal them was to betray the very soul of honesty.

At one point, he decided he would tell Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger everything at breakfast the next morning. Let them know the story, the whole of it. But then he paused. What if something new emerged? What if Mirah revealed more about her past, or if Mrs. Meyrick offered insight he hadn’t anticipated? Better to wait. To listen. To see.

And so, finally, he closed his eyes. The night was still heavy with thought, but the decision was clear: he would wait. He would go to Mrs. Meyrick, speak with Mirah, learn what he could—and then act. Not out of impulse, but from understanding. For Mirah, and for himself, the truth mattered. And he would not let it be buried.

“It will hardly be denied that even in this frail and corrupted world, we sometimes meet persons who, in their very mien and aspect, as well as in the whole habit of life, manifest such a signature and stamp of virtue, as to make our judgment of them a matter of intuition rather than the result of continued examination.”—ALEXANDER KNOX: quoted in Southey’s Life of Wesley.

Mirah said she had slept well that night. When she came down wearing Mab’s black dress, her dark hair curling softly as it dried from its long bath, she looked like someone slowly emerging from sorrow into quiet comfort. The pale cheeks and shadows beneath her eyes still spoke of recent grief, but now there was a new light in her presence—gentle, steady, as though hope had begun to settle within her.

It was Mab who brought her breakfast and led her downstairs, proud of the tiny felt slippers she’d rushed out to buy—there were no shoes small enough for Mirah’s feet. The borrowed dress ended just above her ankles, revealing plain, humble footwear that clung close to her feet like tender buds wrapped in their own casing. To Mab, they were treasures.

“Oh, if you please, mamma?” she exclaimed, clasping her hands and bending toward Mirah’s feet as they entered the parlor. “Look at the slippers—how beautifully they fit! I declare, she looks just like Queen Budoor—‘two delicate feet, the work of the protecting and all-recompensing Creator, support her; and I wonder how they can sustain what is above them.’”

Mirah glanced down at her feet with innocent curiosity, then smiled up at Mrs. Meyrick, who stood silently, her heart stirred. *One could hardly imagine this creature harboring an evil thought,* she reflected. *But wise people would warn me to be cautious.* Still, she returned Mirah’s smile warmly. “I fear those little feet have carried more than their share lately,” she said gently. “But today, they may rest—and you’ll be my companion.”

“And she’ll tell you so many things, and I won’t hear a word,” Mab grumbled, already missing the unfolding story like a child left behind at the start of a fairy tale. She had duties to attend to—pupils to teach, lessons to prepare.

Kate had already gone sketching by the river, and Amy was out on errands. It was precisely as Mrs. Meyrick had wished—to be alone with this quiet stranger whose story must be sorrowful, yet one that needed to be told.

The small front parlor glowed that morning like a sanctuary. Sunlight spilled across the river and drifted through the open window, filling the room with warmth and softness. On the walls hung silent witnesses in black-and-white prints—the Virgin ascending among cherubs, Melancholia with her vast, brooding sky, prophets and sibyls, the School of Athens, the Last Supper, mystic gatherings where time dissolved into unity, solemn portraits by Holbein and Rembrandt, the Tragic Muse, children of past centuries lost in thought or play, Italian poets—each image speaking without sound.

Mrs. Meyrick, neat and composed after years of hardship, sat sorting colored wools for embroidery. Hafiz purred contentedly on the windowsill. The mantel clock ticked steadily, unhurried. Outside, the occasional rumble of wheels seemed distant, almost forgotten, beneath the calm that settled over the room.

Mrs. Meyrick believed this quiet might be the best invitation to speech. So she stayed silent, letting the peace speak for her.

Mirah sat opposite, hands folded in her lap, ankles crossed, eyes at first wandering slowly around the room—taking in every detail—then resting quietly upon Mrs. Meyrick, as though seeing not just a woman, but a vessel of kindness and safety. At last, she began to speak, her voice low and tender.

“I remember my mother’s face better than anything else. Yet I was not seven when I was taken from her. Now I am nineteen.”

“I can understand that,” Mrs. Meyrick murmured. “Some earliest memories last the longest.”

“Oh, yes—it was the earliest thing. I think my life truly began when I woke and saw her face. It was so near to me, her arms around me, and she sang to me. One hymn she sang again and again—so often that I learned it by heart. It was the first song I ever knew. They were always Hebrew hymns, and though I didn’t understand the words, they felt full of love and joy—just ours. When I lay in my little bed, everything white above me, she would lean over me, between me and the ceiling, and sing in a sweet, low voice. I can dream myself back into that time, even when I’m awake. Sometimes in my dreams, my hand—so small—is raised to her face, and she kisses it. Then I begin to tremble, thinking we are both dead. But I wake, and my hand lies here—still, and I don’t know who I am for a moment. But if I could see her again, I would know her instantly.”

“You must expect some change after twelve years,” Mrs. Meyrick said gently. “See my grey hair—ten years ago it was bright brown. Time moves over us like restless birds, leaving footprints forward and backward. Especially when they’re heavy-hearted birds—they tread hard.”

“Ah, I know her heart has been heavy, longing for me. But to feel her joy if we could meet again—if I could show her I love her, give her comfort after all her mourning—that would be worth every trial. If that could happen, I would gladly bear all I’ve suffered. I did despair once. The world seemed cruel and unjust. No one helped me. I couldn’t bear their looks or words. I thought my mother was dead, and death was the only way back to her. But then—yesterday, when I stood by the water, wishing it would close over me, thinking death was the kindest mercy—something changed. Goodness came alive to me. I felt trust in life again. And strangely, I began to believe she might still live. And now—here, with you, this morning—peace has flooded into me. I want nothing. I can wait. Because I hope. I believe. And I am so grateful—oh, so deeply grateful. You haven’t judged me. You haven’t despised me.”

Mirah spoke with quiet fervor, sitting perfectly still, like a painting come to life.

“Many others would feel as we do, my dear,” Mrs. Meyrick whispered, mist rising in her eyes as she stared at her embroidery.

“But I never met them. They never came to me.”

“How did you come to be taken from your mother?”

Mirah paused, then sighed. “Ah, it takes me a long while to reach that. It’s terrible to speak of—but I must. My father—he took me away. I thought we were going on a short journey. I was pleased. There was a box with all my little things inside. But we boarded a ship, and sailed farther and farther from land. Then I became ill—my first real misery, and it seemed endless. But finally, we landed. I knew nothing. I believed what my father said. He comforted me, promised I’d return to my mother. But we were in America, and it was years before we came back to Europe. At first, I kept asking when we’d go home. I tried to learn writing quickly so I could write to her. But one day, when he found me writing a letter, he took me on his knee and told me my mother and brother were dead. That was why we couldn’t return.”

She remembered her brother vaguely—he had once carried her, but he wasn’t always at home. She believed her father. She saw them buried under the earth, their eyes forever closed. She never doubted it. For a long time, she wept every night. But then, she began to dream of her mother often. In those dreams, she felt her presence—alive, near, though unseen. That comforted her. She wasn’t afraid in the dark anymore. Often during the day, she would shut her eyes, bury her face, and try to see her, hear her singing. Eventually, she could do it without closing her eyes.

Mirah paused, her face softened by memory, gazing out toward the river as if she saw her mother there.

“Still, your father was kind to you, I hope?” Mrs. Meyrick asked, needing to draw her back.

“No—he petted me, taught me carefully. He was an actor—I later learned that ‘Coburg’ was a theatre. But he did more than act. He’d been a teacher, knew many languages. His acting wasn’t strong, but he managed the stage, wrote and translated plays. An Italian singer lived with us for years—Signora. She and my father taught me. I had a tutor too, who made me memorize and recite. I worked hard, even as a child. I was nine when I first stepped on stage. I learned easily and wasn’t afraid. But from that moment on, I hated our life. My father had money, and we lived in disorderly finery. Always men and women came and went—loud laughter, arguments, strutting, snapping fingers, jeering faces—many petted me, but I disliked them all. I remembered my mother. Even then, when I understood little, I pulled away from all that noise, retreating into thoughts unlike theirs. I read constantly—plays, poetry, Shakespeare, Schiller—learning about good and evil. My father thought I might become a great singer. My voice was considered extraordinary for a child. He gave me the best teachers. But it hurt that he boasted of me, making me sing at any moment—as if I were a musical box.”

Once, at nine, she played a girl abandoned without knowing it, singing while playing with flowers. She performed effortlessly, but the applause, the chaos of the theatre, repelled her. She didn’t enjoy the praise—it felt cold, unloving. She missed the warmth of her early life, the trust she’d been born into.

She built a world within herself—different from everything around her. She chose beauty from the plays, shaped her own inner life from it. But it was like a knife cutting her—a constant clash between two lives: women gentle and noble on stage, saying kind words as if they meant them, then stepping off to behave crudely. My father noticed my withdrawal. Once, after rehearsal, Signora said, “She’ll never be an artist. She has no idea of being anyone but herself. That’s fine now, but soon you’ll see—she’ll have no more expression than a singing bird.” My father grew angry, and they quarreled. I sat alone and wept—because her words revealed a future I feared. I didn’t want to be an artist, but that was what he expected. Afterward, Signora left. A governess came to teach me other subjects, because my father feared I’d sing too much. But I still acted occasionally.

Rebellion grew stronger. I wanted to escape—but I didn’t know where to go, nor did I dare. I feared the world. And I dreaded leaving my father. I feared doing wrong. I thought if I turned wicked, I’d lose the world of happy thoughts where my mother lived. That belief held me through all those years. Oh, how long they were!”

Mirah fell into silence again.

“Did you have no teaching about duty?” Mrs. Meyrick asked, hesitating to say “religion”—uncertain how Jewish faith might have evolved.

“No—only that I should do what my father wished. He didn’t follow our religion in New York. I think he didn’t want me to know much about it. But because my mother used to take me to the synagogue, and I remembered sitting on her knee, looking through the railing, hearing the chanting and songs—I longed to go. Once, when I was very young, I slipped away and tried to find it. I got lost for hours until a peddler found me and took me home. My father, frantic, was furious. I was so terrified I didn’t dare go out again for a long time. But after Signora left, we moved into rooms where our landlady was a Jewess who practiced her faith. I asked her to take me to the synagogue. I read her prayer books and Bible. When I had money, I bought my own books.
