These texts felt like a deeper connection to my mother—I knew she had read these same words, spoken them aloud. That way, I came to understand a little of our religion, our history. I pieced together what I read in plays and stories about Jews and Jewish women—because I was sure my mother followed her faith. I stopped asking my father about her. It’s dreadful to say, but I began to doubt him. I realized he didn’t always tell the truth, made promises he didn’t keep. That made me suspect my mother and brother were still alive. As I grew older, I saw clearly that my mother had been deceived—she expected us back soon. My father’s words on my knee, telling me they were dead—now it seemed like a performance, meant to calm me. The cruelty of that lie sank deep. I came to hate falsehood because of it.”

She had written to her mother secretly—knowing the street, Colman Street, where they’d lived. She knew their name was Cohen, though my father called us Lapidoth, claiming it was a name from his Polish ancestors. She sent the letter in secret—but no reply came. I thought there was no hope.

Our life in America didn’t last long. Suddenly, my father announced we were packing for Hamburg. I was glad. I hoped for different people. I spoke German well—knew many plays by heart. My father spoke it better than English. I was thirteen then, and I felt old—knowing so much, yet so little. I often wished I’d drowned on the boat away from my mother. But I resolved to obey and endure. What else could I do?

One day, on our voyage, a new thought struck me. I wasn’t very ill, and I spent much time on deck. My father performed and joked to entertain passengers. I heard remarks about him. One man said, “Oh, he’s one of those clever Jews—a rascal, I shouldn’t wonder. There’s no race like them for cunning in men and beauty in women. I wonder what market he means for that daughter.”

When I heard that, it dawned on me: my suffering came from being a Jewess. The world would always look down on me because of my name. I would be judged by that. But it comforted me to believe my pain was part of a greater affliction—my share in the ancient song of mourning that has echoed through ages. For if many of our people were wicked and revelled in sin—was that not part of the burden borne by the just among them? Those who were despised for the sins of others?

“But you have not rejected me,” Mirah said softly, suddenly aware of her gratitude instead of sorrow.

“And we will try to save you from being judged unfairly, my poor child,” Mrs. Meyrick replied, having long since put down her work. She sat with folded hands, listening intently—her face as eager as Mab’s might have been. “Go on. Tell me all.”

After that, we lived in various towns—Hamburg, Vienna, mostly. I began studying singing again. My father earned money through theatres. I think he brought a fortune from America—I never knew why we left. For a time, he was excited about my voice. He made me rehearse constantly, preparing me for opera. But gradually, my voice failed to strengthen. My master in Vienna said, “Don’t strain it further. It won’t serve the public. It’s gold, but a thread of gold dust.” My father was crushed. We weren’t as well off.

I hadn’t fully told you about my feelings toward him. I knew he loved me, wanted to indulge me. That made me afraid of hurting him. But he never understood what would bring me joy. He took everything lightly. I stopped asking him about things I cared for, because he’d brush them aside with jokes. He even mocked our people. Once, after imitating their prayers just to make others laugh, I couldn’t hold back. I’d always carried anger about my mother. Alone, I said, “Father, you shouldn’t mock our people before Christians who already scorn them. Would it be right if I mocked you, so they could ridicule you?” He shrugged, laughed, pinched my chin. “You couldn’t do it, my dear.”

That dismissiveness created a wall between us. Whatever I felt most, I hid from him. Some things—when ridiculed—I couldn’t bear. The world seemed like hell. Is life only a farce, a vaudeville? Why then are there tragedies and grand operas, where men choose to suffer, do difficult things? To call all of it a joke feels silly. I saw that his desire for me to sing great music was only about profit—what would fetch the highest price. That limited my gratitude for his affection. The deepest feeling I had for him was pity. Yes, I pitied him. He aged, changed. He wasn’t lively anymore. I thought he grew worse—less kind to others than to me. Occasionally, his cheer vanished suddenly. He’d sit silently at home, gloomy. Or come in, throw himself down, and cry—as I have done in sorrow. If I touched his knee and asked, “What’s wrong, father?” he wouldn’t answer. Instead, he’d pull me close, wrap his arm around me, and weep. No real confidence passed between us. But oh, I was sorry for him. At those moments, I knew he felt his life was bitter. I pressed my cheek against his head and prayed. Those moments bound me to him most tightly. I often thought of how much my mother once loved him—else she would not have married him.

But then came the terrible time. We had been in Pesth and returned to Vienna. Despite my master Leo’s warning, my father secured me an engagement—not at the opera, but at a suburban theatre. He had nothing to do with the theatre then—I didn’t understand what he did, but I think he spent much time at a gambling house. Though he was careful never to take me there.

I was miserable. The plays I performed were hateful. Men approached me, wanting to talk. Women and men watched me with sneering smiles. It was like a fiery furnace. Perhaps I exaggerate—you don’t know that world. But the glare, the faces, the need to perform what I despised, then see people staring behind the scenes—it was worse than when I was a child. I endured it. I did it. I had decided to obey my father, to work—for I saw no better choice. But I felt my voice weakening. I knew my acting was only good when it wasn’t acting at all—when the role allowed me to be myself, and some inner feeling carried me. That happened rarely.

CHAPTER XXI. It is often said that knowledge is power—but who has truly reckoned the force of ignorance? Knowledge builds slowly, brick by patient brick, over centuries of careful thought and record; while ignorance, hungry and impatient, sets fire to the very records it destroys, turning wisdom into ash with a single reckless spark. Knowledge teaches us to see, to desire more, to shape our lives with skill and artistry; but ignorance, drunk on its own emptiness, comes roaring in on the seventh day with oil and flame, shouting “Let there not be,” and reduces all that was colorful and living to smoldering ruin. Yes, knowledge is power—but it is a power held in check by conscience, aware of what must be and what may not. Ignorance, however, is blind and boundless—a giant without sight, who, once freed, would delight in pulling down the pillars of human progress, leaving behind only darkness as deep as buried Babylon.

And when we look closely at a life—especially one shaped by sudden upheaval—can we not see how ignorance of true cause and effect, how false notions of control and influence, can lead a soul astray? Like a vision blurred by distance, mistaking far-off horizons for things within reach, such blindness drives the heart toward disaster.

It was just past ten in the morning when Gwendolen Harleth arrived at the station near Offendene, after a long and gloomy journey from Leubronn. No carriage waited. No friend met her. Her telegram from Dover had mentioned a later train, and in her eagerness to escape the London station, she had left without considering what it meant to arrive unannounced at a remote stop—situated not near anything, but equally distant from everything.

Left alone with her large trunks, she stood in the waiting room, feeling the weight of every detail: the peeling paint, the dusty decanter of flat water, the bold script on the wall urging repentance. These were not merely surroundings—they were part of the bleak future unfolding before her. She hurried outside, hoping for some relief in the open air, but even the sunlight seemed dimmed. Autumn leaves trembled in the wind, grass bent under a chill breeze, and a lone cock fluffed his feathers, joined by two hens whose croaking voices echoed the loneliness of lost purpose. They, too, had seen their young grow up and leave—and now they wandered aimlessly, uncertain of their place.

The railway official, new and awkward, watched her with an expression of mild confusion, his gaze lingering on her fine shawl and expensive luggage. He did not know her name or rank, and his ignorance made him seem coarse, almost mocking. And then came the carriage—a grimy old barouche being readied by an elderly laborer. It was a small thing, perhaps, but each moment added to the sense of humiliation. These were not grand details, yet they mattered deeply. The smallest impressions accumulate until they form a mood, and that mood becomes a belief, a decision.

Philosophers may claim to rise above such trifles, but even they are shaped by circumstance. To be dropped alone at a desolate spot, with no income, no prospects—how could one help but question the fairness of the world? For Gwendolen, a woman raised in elegance, trained in taste and expectation, this arrival was worse than a dream—it was waking into reality, and it felt like defeat.

She had imagined returning home to find the family already planning another foreign escape—some modest retreat abroad where they might live quietly, if poorly. She had pictured herself among other English exiles, enduring dullness with dignity. But even that prospect paled beside the truth: they had nothing. Not a penny. And she, who had once believed in her own charm and strength, now found herself questioning the point of being clever, beautiful, or resolute at all. What good was any of it, if the world refused to value it?

Men, especially, seemed hateful. But lately, something had shifted. It was one thing to despise stolen goods; quite another to hate them because their theft blocked your own path. Gwendolen’s anger had turned—not toward Grandcourt himself, but toward him as the obstacle that had prevented her marriage, the reason for her downfall. If he had not interfered, she might have won back her losses at the gaming table. If he had not entered her life, she might still be free.

But now the carriage rolled forward, nearing the house. A figure appeared beneath the portico—and suddenly, Gwendolen’s thoughts changed. It was her mother. She saw the familiar face, now marked by sorrow, and without hesitation, threw her arms around her. In that moment, her own pain melted into care for her mother’s grief. All else faded.

Behind them, the four girls stood in quiet sadness—each one, like countless sisters across the land, carrying her own private world, unseen by others. Yet they all looked to Gwendolen as if her presence brought hope. Even her brief dismissal of them—"Now go away, girls"—carried comfort. Authority, even fleeting, was a balm.

Miss Merry, usually so composed, moved silently, tending to the trunks and the driver’s pay. Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen climbed the stairs, shutting themselves into the black-and-yellow bedroom. There, Gwendolen pressed her handkerchief gently against her mother’s tears.

“Never mind, mamma dear,” she whispered. “I don’t mind. I will do something. I will be something. Things will come right. I was away—I didn’t see it clearly. But now I’m here, and I’ll fix it.”

She meant every word. A wave of tenderness surged through her, stirring her resolve. Ideas that had floated vaguely during her journey now took shape. She knew, suddenly, how she could be “something.” It was one of her finest moments. Her mother, overwhelmed, looked at her with something close to awe.

“Bless you, my good, good darling,” she murmured. “If you’re happy, I can be too.”

But later, the tide receded. The old fears returned. Misfortune was no longer news—it was a guest, settled in, demanding obedience. At first, the house had felt like home. The silence, the comfort, the familiar luxuries—all remained. Gwendolen had asked to be alone with her mother, and for a time, they rested together, untouched by the storm ahead.

When she descended, fresh and poised, she sat beside her mother on the settee, ready to face the truth. “What have you decided, exactly, mamma?” she asked, voice steady.

“We must leave this house,” Mrs. Davilow replied. “Mr. Haynes is glad to take it. Lord Brackenshaw’s agent—Bazley—is handling it. We’ve nothing to carry except our clothes.”

“And where are we going?” Gwendolen asked, her tone sharpening slightly. Fear stirred beneath the surface.

“To Sawyer’s Cottage,” her mother said softly.

Gwendolen went pale. She said nothing at first, then spoke with cold pride: “That is impossible. Something better should have been arranged. Uncle should not allow this. I will not submit.”

“Oh, my sweet child,” Mrs. Davilow pleaded. “Your uncle is kind, but he suffers too. He has his own family to support. And remember—we have nothing. Only what he and my sister give us. We must try to earn something. I and the girls are making embroidery for charity—tablecloths, communion cloths. It’s all we can do.”

“But surely somewhere else could be found,” Gwendolen insisted. “Not that cottage—where an exciseman lived!”

“No, dear,” her mother said gently. “Houses are scarce. This one is private. Two parlors, four bedrooms. You can have your own space.”

Gwendolen ignored the reassurance. “I cannot believe all your money vanished so quickly. How can you be certain? It wasn’t even a week since your last letter.”

“The first news came earlier,” Mrs. Davilow explained. “But I didn’t want to spoil your joy until it was unavoidable.”

“Oh, how vexatious!” Gwendolen exclaimed, flushed with anger. “If I’d known, I could have brought home the money I won. I had nearly two hundred pounds. It would have helped us survive—until I could make plans. Instead, I stayed, and lost it all.”

She paused, then added fiercely, “Everything has gone against me. People have come near me only to ruin me.”

Among those people, she included Deronda. Had he not stepped into her life, she might have returned to the tables with a few napoleons and regained her fortune.

“We must accept Providence, my child,” Mrs. Davilow said, startled by the mention of gambling but too afraid to press further.

“But I won’t accept it,” Gwendolen replied. “I’ll fight it. Why call men’s cruelty ‘Providence’? You said in your letter it was Mr. Lassman’s fault. Has he run off with it?”

“No, dear,” her mother said. “There were risky investments—mines and such. He meant to gain, but he risked too much.”

“That’s not Providence,” Gwendolen snapped. “That’s his greed with our money. He should be punished. Can’t we sue? Uncle should act. We must go to law.”

“My dear,” Mrs. Davilow said sadly, “law can’t recover lost money. Your uncle says it’s milk spilled on the ground. And law costs money—money we don’t have. Others have suffered too. We’re not alone.”

“But I won’t accept living in that cottage,” Gwendolen declared. “I won’t let you work for sixpences because of this. I won’t do it. I’ll find something better—something worthy of our rank and education.”

Mrs. Davilow sighed, relieved. “Your uncle and aunt think the same. They’ve heard of two positions—both paying a hundred a year. One is in a bishop’s household with three daughters. The other is in a high-class school. Your French, music, dancing, manners—everything fits. Perhaps you’d consider one? Just for now—to avoid the… the commonness of what we must endure.”

“What?” Gwendolen laughed bitterly. “Be like Miss Graves at Madame Meunier’s? No.”

“I think Dr. Monpert’s would be better,” Mrs. Davilow said. “No hardship in a bishop’s family.”

“Excuse me, mamma,” Gwendolen replied. “Hardship exists everywhere for a governess. And I don’t see why it would be easier to be looked down upon by a bishop than anyone else. Besides—you know I hate teaching. Imagine me trapped with three awkward girls like Alice! I’d rather emigrate than become a governess.”

She didn’t explain what emigration meant. Mrs. Davilow simply stared, helpless, dreading the confrontation with her uncle and aunt.

Yet there was something in Gwendolen’s tone—a defiance that suggested she already had a plan. Despite her ignorance of practical matters, her mother still believed in her strength, her will.

“I have some ornaments,” Gwendolen said. “I could sell them. They’d bring in a sum. Just enough to start. Marshall in Wanchester might buy them—he once showed me bracelets he’d bought from a lady. Jocosa could ask. She’s leaving, of course. But she could do that first.”

“She would do anything,” Mrs. Davilow said softly. “She offered to give me her savings—three hundred pounds. I told her to start a school. It will be hard for her now.”

“Then recommend her for the bishop’s daughters,” Gwendolen said, a sudden smile lighting her face. “She’d do better than I would.”

“Don’t say that to your uncle,” Mrs. Davilow warned. “He’ll be hurt. But perhaps you have something else in mind—one he might approve, if you speak to him.”

“There’s someone else I want to consult first,” Gwendolen said. “Are the Arrowpoints still at Quetcham? Is Herr Klesmer there? I suppose you don’t know—poor, dear mamma. Can Jeffries ride with a note?”

“Oh, my dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, “Jeffries isn’t here. The horses are gone. But someone could go from Leek’s farm. The Arrowpoints are at Quetcham—I know. Miss Arrowpoint left her card. But I don’t know about Herr Klesmer. Do you want to send before tomorrow?”

“Yes,” Gwendolen said firmly. “As soon as possible. I’ll write now.”

She rose and wrote:

> Miss Harleth presents her compliments to Herr Klesmer, and ventures to request the very great favor that he will call upon her, if possible, to-morrow. Her reason for presuming so far on his kindness is of a very serious nature. Unfortunate family circumstances have obliged her to take a course in which she can only turn for advice to the great knowledge and judgment of Herr Klesmer.

“Send this to Quetcham at once, mamma,” she said, sealing the letter. “Tell the man to wait for an answer. No time must be lost.”

For now, the urgent need was to dispatch the letter. But once that was done, a new anxiety gripped her. What if Klesmer weren’t there? What then?

Her faith in fate had been wounded. A brilliant match had crumbled. Roulette had betrayed her. A stranger had disrupted her plans. The world seemed cruelly indifferent—especially to her.

Gwendolen Harleth, beautiful and strong, now faced the threat of humiliation. For the first time, she felt adrift in a world that cared little for her, despite her talents and position. If Klesmer were not at Quetcham—that would fit the pattern. But if he were… then hope might bloom again.

And if someone had asked her whether she was remarkable, she would have been surprised to hear “no.”

We weave dreams of what might be,
But life unfolds in the loom of real time—
Where passion threads the shuttle,
And deeds take shape in patterns we cannot deny.

Gwendolen’s note, arriving just as Klesmer was preparing to leave Quetcham, found its way into his hands at the perfect moment—neither too soon nor too late. To honor her plea for kindness, he stayed the night at Wanchester, despite the inconvenience it caused him. There were reasons why he could not remain at Quetcham any longer.

The grand estate, built with no expense spared, had grown too stifling for him. Its owners, much like certain powerful figures in politics, had been caught off guard by a quiet rebellion against the order they believed unshakable—though afterward, from a distance, one can see how the seeds of resistance had long been sown beneath their very feet.

As usual, the house buzzed with guests, among them a man whom Miss Arrowpoint saw as a new contender for her hand: Mr. Bult, a well-connected politician of noble lineage who confidently expected a peerage and felt, on grounds of public standing, that he required a substantial fortune to match the dignity of his future title. Heiresses are never uniform—those who seek them often find fault with their appearance, temperament, or manner; yet there is always one assumption: she will see herself as an appendage to her wealth, marrying whoever others believe her fortune should join. But nature rarely obeys such tidy plans. It does not favor only children who grow up without siblings—and sometimes, even more surprisingly, it endows the daughter of privilege with a mind of her own, sharp and resolute. The Arrowpoints had already begun to fret over Catherine’s independence. She refused to accept the notion that her duty was to marry a poor nobleman or a commoner climbing the social ladder. Their anxiety grew as she continued to reject suitable matches. As for the idea that she might be in love with Klesmer—such a thought did not trouble them at all. A common blindness, indeed. For people often marvel at effects they themselves have helped create, while failing to notice those they’ve merely wished for. Parents are astonished when their sons are ignorant, though they’ve paid for the most expensive tutors. Husbands and wives are surprised when affection fades, though they’ve done nothing to preserve it. And each of us, in turn, wonders why others don’t admire us—forgetting that admiration must be earned, not assumed. In this way, truth becomes improbable. Truth is not shaped by our desires, but by reality. And now, the Arrowpoints’ moment of shock had arrived.

When an heiress falls for a proud, independent man, understanding between them is rare. Yet if that pride doesn’t harden into constant refusal, the connection may still flourish. Brief meetings after long absences can reveal hidden feelings—but nothing is more revealing than sustained companionship, marked by mutual taste and shared values. Especially when one acts as teacher and the other as eager student, delighted in their ability to learn, and equally delighted in teaching. This dynamic has echoed through history and holds no less charm today than it did in the days of Abelard.

Yet such comparisons had never crossed the Arrowpoints’ minds when they first invited Klesmer to Quetcham. Having a master musician in residence was a mark of distinction; Catherine’s talent demanded the best instruction, and she especially wanted to deepen her studies during quieter country days. Klesmer was not yet a Liszt, whose name stirred the hearts of ladies across Europe (except perhaps in Lapland). Even so, it didn’t follow that he would propose to an heiress. No honorable musician would do such a thing. And certainly, Catherine would never give him reason to think she’d consider it. The large check Mr. Arrowpoint intended to issue in his name made Klesmer seem as harmless as a footman. Where marriage was unthinkable, a young woman’s heart was safe.

Klesmer was unquestionably a man of honor. But marriages rarely begin with formal proposals. And Catherine’s sense of what was possible did not quite align with her mother’s.

Others might have thought Klesmer’s position dangerous—if Miss Arrowpoint had been considered beautiful. But they overlooked the deeper truth: the most compelling beauty is not seen at first glance, but revealed slowly, through sympathy and understanding. There is a magic in the eye and lip that light up with every subtle phrase, showing sensitivity and judgment; in the quiet word or gentle smile that reveals a soul attentive to others. No movement of dress or posture is more moving than one that restores confidence—that assures you someone is present, fully aware, and worthy of your trust. How much meaning gathers in frowns and laughter that never misplace themselves! What grace radiates from a body animated by a mind capable of insight and hands skilled in expression! The kind of beauty that dazzles at first—the kind we call "adorable"—often begins as an excuse for folly, and ends, like all excuses, in boredom. That Klesmer, though deeply drawn to such beauty, should fall passionately for Miss Arrowpoint was no paradox, but rather a triumph of complex connection over shallow attraction. We bristle less at being accused of passionate excess than at lacking broader passion. But if truth be told, our supposed intensity often stems not from depth, but from emptiness—having no better use for ourselves. Tannhäuser, we suspect, was a knight of limited imagination, no more eloquent than a bored guardsman. Merlin, once brilliant, had aged into repetition when he fell captive. And Ulysses himself confessed to boredom under Calypso’s spell—so much so that she helped him leave. There is even a rumor he later left Penelope. But since she spent her days absorbed in knitting, and likely passed on her narrow-mindedness to Telemachus—always anxious about property and meat consumption—we can draw no firm conclusions about the link between companionship and fidelity.

Klesmer, on sufficient acquaintance, was as versatile and captivating as a young Ulysses—one whom nature seemed to bless generously, then gifted with music as a dominant force. Like Mendelssohn, he expressed himself not only in flawless execution, but in the fervor of creation and belief that gave his entire life a purposeful glow. His flaws—arrogance, vanity—were no greater than those found in many English families. And Catherine, though naturally kind, was perhaps too cool and self-contained. Yet she was one of those rare souls whose presence invites discovery—whose honesty and clarity stir curiosity about what she’ll say next, how she’ll perform any task. With her, companionship became a rhythm of anticipation and fulfillment—a dance of joy. Over time, the image in the mind came to mirror the person in flesh. Soon both realized they were deeply drawn to each other—but the question of how far remained uncertain. Klesmer didn’t imagine Miss Arrowpoint could ever see him as a lover. And she, accustomed to believing men admired her only for her fortune, never feared stirring anything beyond friendship. Each accepted a quiet ache, a half-acknowledged longing, for the sake of loving the other’s company too deeply. Thus, for a year, Klesmer’s visits—both in town and country—had gone unchecked. He knew full well that if Catherine were poor, he would have pursued her passionately instead of storming the piano or delivering fiery rants about distant places. And she, in turn, understood that if Klesmer had ever desired her hand, she would have given it willingly. Such was the safety of full cups—no more likely to overflow than empty ones, unless disturbed. But silence, like a clock’s unseen hand, moves forward. On this visit to Quetcham, Klesmer began to feel he would not return. Catherine, meanwhile, noticed his growing sharpness—what she saw as unnecessary attempts to assert dominance in every way except convention.

Then entered Mr. Bult, the anticipated peer. A respected political figure, neutral in private life but fervent in debate, he spoke with authority on the Niger, Brazil, and the South Seas. He polished his speeches with care, exuding the solid pinkness of a healthy Englishman in midlife. Catherine, aware of the unspoken agreement that he was the ideal husband for an heiress, had only one objection: he was profoundly tedious. Mr. Bult, amiably confident, couldn’t imagine his ignorance of musical counterpoint being a flaw. He barely regarded Klesmer as a serious human being worthy of a vote, and cared little for Catherine’s passion for music—or her likely spending on antique lace. So when Klesmer erupted after dinner, condemning English politics for lacking idealism and reducing relations between peoples to mere trade, Mr. Bult was taken aback. The crusades, Klesmer argued, at least had a banner of sentiment around which noble feelings could gather—even if scoundrels joined too. But now, everything rallied around slogans like “Buy cheap, sell dear.” His speech, gesticulatory and vivid, burned like stray fireworks before fading into silence. Mr. Bult wasn8t surprised by Klesmer’s opinions—he expected them to be erratic—but was startled by his command of English and his rhetorical flair. Likely, he mused, due to Klesmer’s Polish or Czech roots, a refugee forced into music. Later, in the drawing-room, Mr. Bult approached Klesmer at the piano—Catherine nearby—and said,

“I never knew you were a political man.”

Klesmer replied only by folding his arms, sticking out his lower lip, and staring.

“You must have experience in public speaking,” Mr. Bult continued. “You speak remarkably well, even if I disagree. From your talk of sentiment, I suspect you’re a Panslavist.”

“No,” Klesmer said, flashing a smile at Catherine. “My name is Elijah. I am the Wandering Jew.” Then, with a sudden burst, he played a mysterious, wind-like passage on the piano. Mr. Bult found it offensive—too Polish, too theatrical—but hesitated to leave, seeing Catherine near.

“Herr Klesmer has cosmopolitan views,” Catherine interjected, trying to smooth things over. “He believes in the fusion of races.”

“With all my heart,” Mr. Bult replied, eager to appear gracious. “I knew he had too much talent to be just a musician.”

“Oh, sir,” Klesmer retorted, rising sharply, “you’re mistaken. No man has too much talent to be a musician. Most men have too little. A true artist is no more ‘just’ a musician than a great statesman is just a politician. We are not puppets, sir, confined to a box and brought out only when the world wants amusement. We help shape nations and define eras, as much as any lawmaker. We stand beside legislators. And one who speaks through music faces a challenge harder than parliamentary rhetoric.”

With that, Klesmer turned and walked away.

Catherine flushed. Mr. Bult observed, as usual, with calm detachment, “Your pianist doesn’t think lightly of himself.”

“Herr Klesmer is more than a pianist,” she said, defending him. “He is a great musician—true in every sense. He will stand with Schubert and Mendelssohn.”

“Ah,” Mr. Bult replied, “you ladies understand these things.” Still, he remained convinced music was frivolous—especially after Klesmer’s display of arrogance.

Catherine, always uneasy when Klesmer behaved proudly, found a chance the next day in the music room to say, “Why were you so harsh with Mr. Bult last night? He meant no harm.”

“You wish me to be polite to him?” Klesmer asked, voice tight.

“I think it’s hardly worth your effort to be otherwise.”

“You tolerate him easily, then? You respect a political drone who feels nothing outside political gain? You think his monumental dullness fits the dignity of the English gentleman?”

“I didn’t say that.”

“You mean I acted without dignity—and you’re offended.”

“Now you’re closer,” Catherine smiled.

“Then I should pack my burial clothes and leave at once.”

“I don’t see why. If I endure your criticism of my operetta, you should bear mine of your impatience.”

“But I can’t,” Klesmer insisted. “You want me to ignore his ignorance, to let him insult music and artists without reply. I’m to hear my gods mocked and myself ridiculed. But I apologize—I know you can’t grasp the artist’s anger. To you, we belong to another caste.”

“That’s true,” Catherine admitted, emotion flickering. “You belong to a caste I admire—one above mine.”

Klesmer, seated at a table reviewing scores, rose abruptly and stepped back, saying, “That’s beautifully said—I’m grateful. But I should go anyway. I’ve decided. You’ll manage perfectly without me—your operetta is already moving forward. And Mr. Bult’s company suits me perfectly. I have engagements. I must go to St. Petersburg.”

No answer.

“You agree I should go?” Klesmer asked, irritation creeping in.

“Certainly—if that’s what your duty and heart dictate. I only wonder why you’ve stayed so long. There must be triple the interest elsewhere. I’ve never seen your visits as anything but sacrifice.”

“Why should I sacrifice?” Klesmer asked, walking to the piano and playing softly, delicately, a melody set to Heine’s “Ich hab’ dich geliebet und liebe dich noch.”

“That’s the mystery,” Catherine murmured, trembling. Her hands tore paper into tiny shreds, as if under a cruel fairy’s spell.

“You can’t guess my motive?” Klesmer asked, arms folded.

“None that seems probable.”

“Then I’ll tell you. You are the chief woman in my world—the queen whose colors I carry between my heart and armor.”

Her hands shook too violently to tear paper. Her lips could not form words.

Klesmer continued, “This would be the ultimate impertinence—if I meant to build anything on it. That’s impossible. I don’t intend such a thing. But you once said it was your fate to suspect every suitor of being an adventurer, and what angered you most was men accusing you of foolishly believing they loved you for yourself. Did you not say so?”

“Very likely,” she whispered.

“A bitter truth. Well, at least one man who has seen women as plentiful as May flowers has lingered near you—for your own sake. And since he is one you can never marry, you’ll believe him. That’s proof enough for some other man. But don’t surrender to a minotaur like Bult. I’ll pack now. I’ll explain to Mrs. Arrowpoint.”

He rose and moved toward the door.

“You must take this manuscript,” Catherine said suddenly, gathering courage. She fetched the pile from another table. Klesmer returned. They stood separated only by the sheets.

“Why shouldn’t I marry the man who loves me, if I love him?” she asked. To her, it felt like leaping from a ship into a lifeboat.

“It would be too hard—impossible. You couldn’t endure it. I’m not worth what you’d face. I won’t accept your sacrifice. It would be a _mésalliance_—and I’d be blamed.”

“Are you afraid of gossip?” she asked. “I fear only that we might miss the chance to live together.”

The decisive word was spoken. There was no doubt about their desire—only the path to reach it. Catherine chose the direct route. She went to her parents in the library and announced she had promised to marry Klesmer.

Mrs. Arrowpoint’s reaction was heartbreaking. Imagine Jean-Jacques Rousseau, after writing about the corrupting influence of art, waking to find himself among primitives who offer raw bone for breakfast with a flint knife. Or Saint Just, after denouncing all hierarchy, receiving praise for his mediocrity. Something similar happened to the author of *Tasso*, when her own daughter enacted what she had once demanded of Leonora. It’s hard to live up to our own ideals while grounded in reality. Literature’s proprieties differ from life’s. Mrs. Arrowpoint wanted the best of both worlds—not only to surpass her peers in literary taste, but also in social standing. While Klesmer was a charming musician, tolerated as an eccentric guest, the thought of him as her son-in-law ignited a fierce dread of what society would say. And she had long portrayed Catherine as a model of virtue.

At first, she forgot everything but fury, seizing any words to wound.

“If Klesmer dared propose to you, your father shall horsewhip him off the premises. Speak, Mr. Arrowpoint!”

The father removed his cigar and responded gravely, “This will never do, Cath.”

“Do!” Mrs. Arrowpoint cried. “Who in their right mind ever thought it would? You might as well say poisoning and strangling are acceptable. This is a farce, Catherine. Or you’re mad.”

“I’m sane and serious, mamma. Herr Klesmer isn’t to blame. He never dreamed I’d marry him. I discovered he loved me—and loving him, I told him I would.”

“Don’t say that,” Mrs. Arrowpoint snapped. “Everyone else will say it for you. You’ll be a public joke. People will say you offered yourself to a man paid to come here—who is nobody knows what—a gypsy, a Jew, a bubble on the earth.”

“Never mind, mamma,” Catherine replied, indignant. “We all know he’s a genius—as Tasso was.”

“Those times were different,” Mrs. Arrowpoint insisted. “And Klesmer is no Tasso.”

“The sarcasm stings only because it’s rooted in disrespect.”

“I’m sorry to hurt you, mamma. But I won’t give up my happiness for beliefs I don’t hold or customs I don’t respect.”

“You’ve lost all sense of duty? You forget you’re our only child—our responsibility to place the family fortune in worthy hands?”

“What are ‘worthy hands’? My grandfather earned this land through trade.”

“Mr. Arrowpoint, will you sit silent while this goes on?”

“I’m a gentleman, Cath. We expect you to marry a gentleman,” the father said, straining.

“And a man tied to the institutions of this country,” added the mother. “A woman in your position has serious duties. When duty and desire clash, she must choose duty.”

“I don’t deny that,” Catherine said, growing colder. “But people misuse the word ‘duty’ to justify what they want others to do.”

“Doesn’t your parents’ desire make no duty for you?”

“Yes, within reason. But before I give up my happiness—”

“Catherine, Catherine, it won’t be happiness,” Mrs. Arrowpoint warned, voice darkening.

“Well, what I believe is happiness—before I give it up, I must see a better reason than wanting me to marry a nobleman or a man who votes to become one. I have the right to marry the man I love and respect—unless a higher duty forbids.”

“And it does forbid, Catherine, though you’re blinded. A woman’s duty is not to lower herself. You are lowering yourself. Mr. Arrowpoint, tell your daughter her duty.”

“You must see, Catherine, that Klesmer is not right for you,” the father said. “He won’t manage estates. He looks foreign. He’s impractical.”

“I don’t see how that matters, papa. England’s land has passed to foreigners before—Dutch soldiers, sons of foreign women of questionable reputation. If our land were sold tomorrow, it might go to a foreign merchant on ’Change. Everyone says successful swindlers could buy half the country. How can I stop that tide?”

Among the heirs of Art, as in the division of a promised land, each must fight hard to claim their share. The gift may be prophetic, but without effort, it remains mere title—no possession at all. Carrying a map of an estate you’ve never owned is little better than a hollow promise. And imagining you can cast your shoe over Eden doesn’t mean you’ll ever set foot on its soil.

The most stubborn beliefs we hold about ourselves often rest on no evidence beyond a constant, unexamined pulse of self-satisfaction—a hidden seed of madness, a quiet certainty that we can move the world, even when we don’t know where we stand or what lever we might use.

“Please go to church, mamma,” Gwendolen said the next morning. “I’d rather be alone with Herr Klesmer.” (He had replied to her note that he would arrive at eleven.)

“That’s hardly proper,” Mrs. Davilow said, uneasy.

“Our situation is too serious to worry about such trivial rules,” Gwendolen answered, dismissive. “They’re both absurd and insulting.”

“You wouldn’t mind if Isabel sat nearby? She could read quietly in a corner.”

“No,” Gwendolen insisted. “She’d fidget and stare. It would only distract me. Trust my judgment, mamma—I must be alone. Take everyone else to church.”

Gwendolen had her way, of course—though Miss Merry and two of the girls stayed behind, seated by the dining-room windows, to keep the house from looking abandoned.

It was a perfect Sunday morning. The soft, fading light of autumn rested gently on the half-mown grass, slanting through the windows in golden bands across the old furniture and the glass panel that reflected it all—the tapestried chairs with their faded flower patterns, the dark, mysterious paintings, the outdated organ where Gwendolen had once delighted in playing Saint Cecilia upon her joyful arrival, and the dusty collection of knick-knacks visible through the open doors of the antechamber, where she had once worn her Greek costume as Hermione. That memory stirred her now—hadn’t Klesmer been struck by her poise and expression then? Whatever he had said, whatever she imagined he thought, mattered deeply to her now. For the first time in her life, she felt truly dependent on another’s opinion, aware of needing validation from someone outside herself. A new flutter stirred within her, a fresh layer of self-awareness replacing the effortless confidence of intuition. Yet still, her inner voice kept repeating: Klesmer has seen so little of me. Any judgment he makes must be based on too narrow a foundation. Still, she believed herself clever enough for anything.

To pass the time, she gathered her music books and scores, placing them neatly atop the piano before beginning to sort them. Then, catching her reflection in the glass panel, she paused, drawn to her own image. Dressed simply in black, without ornament, her warm skin glowing between her light-brown hair and square-cut bodice, she might have inspired an artist to try again the ancient Roman trick of sculpting in black, white, and tawny marble. Watching herself approach slowly, she thought, *I am beautiful*—not with pride, but with quiet certainty. Beauty, after all, was the one thing she needed confirmation for. If anyone criticized the shape of her nose or the line of her neck and chin, she couldn’t imagine how she might prove herself capable of mastering those details.

There wasn’t much time left before the sound of wheels, the sharp ring of the doorbell, and the opening of the front doors confirmed that Klesmer was not going to disappoint her. This only heightened the flutter in her chest. Despite her confidence, she feared him—as part of a world beyond her control, something sharp and unyielding that burned regardless of whether you smiled or frowned. Poor girl! She stood at a higher turning point in her womanhood than she had during her encounter with Grandcourt. Then, her question had been whether to accept a man as husband. Now, the deeper question was whether she even needed a husband at all—whether she could achieve independence, fulfill her ambitions, and find satisfaction without being bound.

Klesmer entered with his most respectful bow in the wide doorway of the antechamber—his fine gray trousers and flawless gloves marking him as one who knew his place among the masters. Gwendolen met him with unusual seriousness, extending her hand. “Thank you so much for coming, Herr Klesmer. I hope I haven’t seemed presumptuous.”

“I took your request as a command—an honor,” he replied, equally grave. He had indeed rearranged his own affairs to give her full attention, though his nerves were still jangled from the events of the previous day, lending his words a sharper edge than usual.

For once, Gwendolen was too emotionally charged to remember etiquette. She remained near the piano, and Klesmer stood at the opposite end, backlit, his piercing eyes fixed on her. No pretense would serve. She began at once.

“I need your advice, Herr Klesmer. We’ve lost everything. We have no fortune. I must earn my own living—and I want to provide for my mother so she won’t suffer. The only path I can see—and the one I’d prefer above all—is acting. To go on the stage. But naturally, I’d like to reach a high position. So I thought—if you thought I could—that I might also study singing. Perhaps become a singer-actress, like Grisi.”

Klesmer placed his hat on the piano and folded his arms, as if gathering his thoughts.

“I know,” Gwendolen continued, her face shifting from pale to pink and back again, “that my singing technique is flawed. I’ve had poor instruction. But I could learn. I could train. And you understand my ambition—to sing and act, as Grisi does, is a far greater role. Of course, I’d aim as high as possible. And I trust your judgment. I’m sure you’ll tell me the truth.”

Somehow, she believed that now she made this earnest appeal, the truth would be favorable.

But Klesmer remained silent. He quickly removed his gloves, tossed them into his hat, placed his hands on his hips, and walked to the other side of the room. Compassion filled him—he wanted to guard his words carefully. When he turned again, he looked at her with a mild frown, speaking gently yet sharply: “You’ve never really seen artists’ lives, have you?—musicians, actors, performers of that kind?”

“Oh, no,” Gwendolen replied, untroubled by the obviousness of the fact—she had lived sheltered, well-provided for.

“You are—excuse me,” Klesmer said, pausing near the piano, “coming to such a decision without considering everything. You’re perhaps twenty?”

“I’m twenty-one,” she said, a flicker of unease rising. “Do you think I’m too old?”

Klesmer pursed his lips and raised his fingers upward in a gesture that meant nothing.

“Many begin later,” Gwendolen said, instinctively drawing on her usual habit of offering information as proof of her worth.

Klesmer ignored her, speaking with even more care: “You’ve probably never seriously considered an artistic life until now. You didn’t dream of it, desire it—what shall I say?—you didn’t long to be an actress until this crisis?”

“Not exactly,” she admitted, “but I’ve always enjoyed acting. I’ve performed—remember? You saw me here in charades, and as Hermione.”

“Yes, yes,” he said quickly, “I remember perfectly.” And again, he walked to the other end of the room. He found it hard to stay still when engaged in any argument, spoken or silent.

Gwendolen felt she was being weighed. The silence was uncomfortable. But she still didn’t believe the scale could tip against her. It seemed graceful to say, “I’ll be grateful for your honest advice, whatever it may be.”

“Miss Harleth,” Klesmer said, turning toward her, his accent slightly stronger, “I will not hide anything from you. I would feel guilty if I presented things falsely—making them too bright or too dark. The gods curse the man who willingly leads another down the wrong path. And if I misled someone so young, so beautiful—who, I hope, will find happiness on the right road—I would consider myself a _Bösewicht_.” His voice dropped to a low whisper on the last word.

Gwendolen felt a sudden pang beneath her chest. She stared at him, transfixed.

“You are a beautiful young lady,” he continued, “brought up in comfort. You’ve done as you pleased. You’ve never told yourself, ‘I must know this exactly,’ ‘I must understand this exactly,’ ‘I must do this exactly.’” As he spoke the three “musts,” he raised three fingers in succession. “In short, you’ve never been called upon to be anything but a charming young lady—someone it’s impolite to criticize.”

He paused. Then, hands on hips, chin thrust forward, he added:

“So now, with this background, you wish to enter the life of an artist—a life of relentless, unending work and uncertain praise. Your acclaim would have to be earned, like your bread; and both would come slowly, sparingly—perhaps not at all.”

This discouraging tone, which Klesmer hoped would suffice, stirred resistance in Gwendolen. With a slight turn of her head and a hint of irritation, she said:

“I thought, as an artist, you’d see this life as noble and fulfilling. And if I can do nothing better—well, I suppose I can endure the same risks as others.”

“Do nothing better?” Klesmer asked, slightly provoked. “No, my dear Miss Harleth—you could do nothing better—neither man nor woman could—*if* they could achieve excellence, if they could do what is best of its kind. I’m not denigrating the artist’s life. I’m exalting it. It belongs only to chosen souls—natures shaped to love perfection and labor for it. Ready, like true lovers, to endure, to wait, to say, ‘I am not yet worthy—but she—Art, my mistress—is worthy, and I will live to deserve her.’ An honorable life? Yes. But the honor comes from inner calling and hard-won achievement. There is no honor in wearing the life like a uniform.”

A spark from yesterday still burned in Klesmer, urging him into speech beyond his immediate purpose. He had wished only to gently awaken Gwendolen to her unpreparedness, but he could not resist condemning false pretensions. He caught himself and paused. But Gwendolen’s main impression was that he hadn’t yet denied her ability to achieve excellence. His passion seemed merely a glow he often cast over things. What she wanted to assure him was that she wasn’t afraid of hardship. The idea that stepping onto the stage would not bring the automatic admiration she’d always known—like shedding a piece of flesh—was painful, but not impossible. She said, with firmness:

“I’m ready to endure hardships at first. No one becomes famous overnight. And not everyone needs to be first-rate—actresses or singers alike. If you’d kindly tell me what steps to take, I’ll have the courage to follow them. I don’t mind climbing uphill. It’s easier than the flat emptiness of being a governess. I’ll take any path you recommend.”

Now Klesmer knew he must speak plainly.

“I’ll tell you the steps—not because I recommend them, but because they’ll be forced upon you. Whether your goal is excellence, fame, or mere mediocrity—it makes no difference. You must go to town under your mother’s care. You must submit to training—musical, dramatic, theatrical. Whatever you wish to do, you must learn.” Here, Gwendolen opened her mouth to speak, but Klesmer held up his hand. “I know. You’ve performed—recited, sung—from the drawing-room perspective. My dear Fräulein, you must unlearn all that. You’ve not grasped what excellence truly means. You must unlearn your mistaken ideals. You must know what you strive for, and then discipline your mind and body completely. Your mind, I say. Because you must stop thinking of fame. Extinguish that candle from your eyes and look only at perfection. You’ll earn nothing at first—no engagement for a long time. You’ll need money for yourself and your family. But that,” he added, frowning and flicking his fingers as if brushing aside a trifle, “could perhaps be arranged.”

Gwendolen flushed and paled through his words. Her pride had been cut with a sharp blade, and the final sentence only deepened the wound. She felt herself showing emotion and tried to regain composure by walking to a chair and gesturing to one for Klesmer. He refused, turning slightly to face her, leaning against the piano. At that moment, she regretted summoning him. This first experience of being judged not by her beauty or rank, but by her capacity—was becoming bitter.

Klesmer, focused on his purpose, continued without changing tone.

“And what might reasonably be expected from all this sacrifice? You’d ask that. It’s right that you see clearly. I’ll tell you honestly. The outcome would be uncertain—and likely, not worth much.”

At these words, Klesmer pursed his lips and looked through his spectacles with an expression that seemed almost monstrous, impenetrable by charm.

Gwendolen’s eyes began to sting, but fear of appearing weak drove her to tighten her control. She forced herself to speak, coldly:

“You think I lack talent—or that I’m too old to begin.”

Klesmer hummed softly, then declared emphatically: “Yes! The desire and training should have begun seven years ago—or much earlier. A child born to a mountebank, helping her father earn shillings at six, a girl inheriting a singing voice from generations of choristers, learning to sing as she learns to walk—such a start is far likelier. Every great accomplishment in music or acting grows with growth itself. Whenever an artist can say, ‘I came, I saw, I conquered,’ it’s after years of patient practice. Genius, at first, is little more than a vast capacity for discipline. Singing and acting—like the juggler’s dexterity with cups and balls—require shaping the body toward finer and finer precision. Your muscles, your entire frame, must function like a watch—true, true to a hair. That takes springtime, before habits harden.”

“I never claimed genius,” Gwendolen said, still clinging to the idea that she might achieve what he deemed impossible. “I only thought I might have some talent—enough to improve.”

“I don’t deny that,” Klesmer said. “If you’d been trained properly years ago and worked diligently, you might now have become a public singer—though I doubt your voice would carry much weight. On stage, your charm and intelligence might have helped, but now you lack experience, discipline, instruction.”

Klesmer seemed cruel, but his heart was not cruel. Even our most sincere speech carries echoes of other impulses. His words were shaped by compassion—for Gwendolen’s ignorance, her eagerness to leap into a world he saw in stark detail, with suffering and humiliation laid bare, yet unable to convey it fully to her.

But Gwendolen was not convinced. Her self-image rallied. Since the advisor she had summoned delivered such harsh judgment, she began to suspect his view was not only flawed, but biased. It occurred to her that a simpler, wiser step would have been to write directly to a London theatre manager, asking for an audition. She would no longer mention singing—Klesmer had already opposed it. But she felt confident she could argue her case for acting. She replied, defiantly:

“I understood, of course, that no one becomes a finished actress overnight. It may be impossible to predict success—but that seems reason enough to try. I thought I might secure an engagement while studying, earning money and learning simultaneously.”

“It can’t be done,” Klesmer said firmly. “I speak plainly—it can’t be done. I must clear your mind of these illusions, which resemble reality no more than a pantomime. People think that once they’ve dressed and put on gloves, they’re fit for the stage as they are for a drawing-room. No manager believes that. With all your grace, if you appeared as an applicant, a manager would either charge you as an amateur or send you away to be trained—taught to carry yourself on stage, like a horse must be trained for the circus. Not to mention the deeper study needed to embody a character convincingly, to animate it with natural expression, gesture, and voice. An immediate engagement suitable for you? Impossible.”

“I can’t understand that,” Gwendolen said, slightly haughty—then, correcting herself, softer: “Please explain how such poor actresses get hired. I’ve seen plays, and I’m certain there were performers who acted poorly and were plain.”

“Ah, my dear Miss Harleth,” Klesmer said, “that’s the easy criticism of the spectator. We who buy shoes discard pairs as clumsy, but each pair required apprenticeship. Excuse me—you couldn’t teach one of those actresses now. But she could teach you much. For example, she can project her voice so it reaches the back row. Ten times out of ten, you couldn’t do that without many trials. Merely standing and moving on stage is an art—it requires practice. We’re not discussing a minor role in a small theatre earning a needlewoman’s wages. That’s unthinkable for you.”

“Of course I must earn more than that,” Gwendolen said, wincing rather than refuted. “But I think I could soon master all these things you mention. I’m not so stupid. And even in Paris, I saw two actresses playing noblewomen who weren’t noble at all—ugly, even. I suppose I have no special talent, but surely being a lady, rather than a fright, is an advantage—even on stage.”

“Ah, let us clarify,” Klesmer said, a new intensity in his voice. “I’ve been speaking of what you’d face if you aimed at becoming a true artist—if you treated music and drama as a higher calling, striving for excellence. In that case, what I’ve said stands. After twenty-one years of doing things loosely, you’d face immense difficulty in study. You’d endure humiliations when presenting yourself as skilled. You’d undergo tests—people would no longer pretend not to see your flaws. You’d start on trial. You’d bear what I call glaring insignificance. Success would require the utmost patience. You’d have to hold your place in a crowd—and likely lose it, fade into obscurity. If you still choose this path, you’ll have the dignity of a noble purpose, even if you’ve made a mistake. You’ll have merit, even if you win no prize. You asked for my judgment on your chances. I don’t claim absolute knowledge, but judging probabilities: you’ll likely achieve no more than mediocrity.”

Klesmer spoke with forceful speed, then paused. Gwendolen sat motionless, staring at her hands folded in her lap, until the deep, lingering “_But_” startled her, making her look up.

“But—there are other ways, other dispositions, by which a young lady might enter the arts and appear before the public. She might rely on her beauty as a passport. She might seek admiration that demands no skill. That works, to a degree, on stage—but not in music. On stage, beauty is accepted when nothing stronger is available. But not without training: technical mastery is always required. Beyond that, we’re not dealing with art. Such a woman is not an artist—usually one seeking a luxurious life through a quick, easy route—perhaps marriage, her brightest chance, and rarest. Still, her path won’t be luxurious at first. She can barely earn her own livelihood immediately, and the indignities she faces… I won’t name them.”

“I want to be independent,” Gwendolen said, wounded and confused by what sounded like scorn in Klesmer’s tone. “That’s why I asked if I could get an immediate engagement. I don’t know how theatres work. But I thought I could make myself independent. I have no money, and I won’t accept help from anyone.”

Her pride demanded this declaration. It was unbearable to think Klesmer might have imagined she expected anything more from him than advice.

“I question things but do not find
One that will answer to my mind:
And all the world appears unkind.” —WORDSWORTH.

Gwendolen was relieved to have finished her meeting with Klesmer before facing her uncle and aunt. She had made up her mind—there would be no comfort in what lay ahead, only discomforts. Yet she resolved to stand firm, to keep her composure no matter how humbling the situation might become.

The meeting didn’t take place until Monday, when Gwendolen went to the rectory with her mother. On the way, they stopped at Sawyer’s Cottage, where they walked through every corner of the cramped rooms, lit by harsh midday sun unsoftened by curtains or blinds. The furnishings were still incomplete—meant to be filled from the rectory’s own meager surplus.

“How can you possibly bear it, mamma?” Gwendolen asked as they left, unable to speak while standing inside the bare, dusty space—the tiny garden with its cabbage stalks, the yew arbor covered in dust and cobwebs. “You and the four girls crammed into that room, with those green-and-yellow walls pressing in on you? And without me?”

“It will be some comfort that you won’t have to endure it too, dear,” her mother replied gently.

“If it weren’t for needing money, I’d rather live there than become a governess,” Gwendolen said, her voice sharp with resentment.

“Don’t decide against it yet,” her mother urged. “If you go to the bishop’s house, you’ll have every luxury. You’ve always cared about such things. It won’t be nearly as hard as climbing those narrow stairs, hearing dishes clatter, and listening to the girls talk.”

“It feels like a nightmare,” Gwendolen burst out. “I can’t believe Uncle Henry would let you go to such a place. He should have found another way.”

“Don’t be unreasonable, child. What could he have done?”

“That was his responsibility,” Gwendolen insisted. “It seems absurd that people in our position must fall so suddenly. The world feels unjust.” Her usual sense of order—the idea that life should unfold with fairness and grace—had been shattered.

She spoke with a temper sharpened by sudden hardship, though she might have handled others’ misfortunes more thoughtfully. Still, she wasn’t seeking virtue in her words—only cleverness. That mattered now, even if it made her sound worse than she felt.

Yet despite her inner turmoil, she couldn’t help being moved when her uncle and aunt greeted her with kindness far greater than ever before. Their quiet dignity, their calm acceptance of necessity, struck her deeply. Mr. Gascoigne, whose character had once been dimmed by worldly comforts—like a woman’s beauty obscured by fashion—now shone clearly under this new burden. He had already sold his carriage, rethought his wardrobe, given up meat at breakfast, canceled subscriptions, pulled Edwy from school, and arranged study hours for the boys himself. He managed the household with frugal precision, and his spirit had infected everyone around him.

Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna, who always modeled themselves after their father, scarcely missed anything for themselves. They truly believed the hardest blow was for Mrs. Davilow and her children. Anna, for the first time, set aside her anger over Rex to feel compassion for Gwendolen. Mrs. Gascoigne hoped trouble might shape her niece for good—without feeling the need to add further pain for that purpose. Together, they had quietly planned ways to furnish the cottage using household items, but they kept those plans quiet, focusing instead on Gwendolen’s return and the comfort it brought her mother.

There was no reason for Gwendolen to resent them. Instead, she found herself alert, almost startled, when her uncle began speaking in a steady, kind tone about the efforts he’d made to secure her a suitable position.

“I knew we couldn’t wait, Gwendolen,” he said. “A good post isn’t found overnight. And even if we waited longer, I doubt we’d find one better than Bishop Mompert’s. I know both him and his wife well—that helps. Our correspondence has gone well, but Mrs. Mompert wants to meet you before making a final decision. She may arrange for you to see her in Wanchester on her way to London. I know it might be difficult, but you’ll have time to prepare.”

“Do you know why she wants to see me, Uncle?” Gwendolen asked, already imagining the reasons—judgmental glances, hidden expectations, disapproval.

Her uncle smiled. “Don’t worry. She wants a clearer picture than I can give. A mother is careful about choosing a companion for her daughters. I told her you’re young, but she supervises her girls closely, so age isn’t much of an issue. She’s refined and principled—she doesn’t want a French governess. I’m confident she’ll find your manners and talents excellent. And both she and the bishop will oversee the moral and religious aspects of your work.”

Gwendolen said nothing, but the flush rising on her cheeks betrayed her deep unease. Anna, worried, slipped her hand into Gwendolen’s. Mr. Gascoigne, kind-hearted as he was, sensed the weight of this change upon her. Still determined to present things cheerfully, he added, “I value this position so highly I’d have tried to get it for Anna—if she fit the role. It’s not just a job—it’s a home, with real education continuing. ‘Governess’ is really a misnomer. The bishop’s views are more Low Church than mine—he’s close to Lord Grampian—but he’s not narrow-minded. In fact, he’s one of the most respected bishops in the diocese. We once debated the Bible Society, but he’s always remained respectful toward me.”

To him, these words carried reassurance. To Gwendolen, they were like snakes slithering across her path—each phrase a threat. “Continuation of education,” “bishop’s views,” “privately strict,” “Bible Society”—they echoed like warnings. She saw not opportunity, but confinement. Already repelled by the prospect, these words intensified her dread. She began to search desperately for another way.

“There was another position, wasn’t there, mamma?” she asked, forcing control.

“Yes,” the rector replied, slightly hesitant. “But that’s in a school. I wouldn’t feel as satisfied with that choice. It’s harder work, less rewarding, and you’d have less chance of getting it.”

“Oh, dear no,” Mrs. Gascoigne said quickly. “It would be much harder for you, my dear—less suitable. You might not even have your own room.” Memories of school flooded back—crowded dormitories, harsh rules—and Gwendolen realized this alternative offered no relief.

She turned back to her uncle. “When might Mrs. Mompert send for me?”

“It’s uncertain,” he said, “but she promised not to consider anyone else until she meets you. She understands your situation deeply. Likely within two weeks. But I must go—I’m letting part of my glebe land.”

He left cheerfully, convinced he’d helped Gwendolen accept her fate. Having spoken wisely, he assumed wisdom would produce results. As a man used to guiding others, he expected obedience to follow speech.

“What a support Henry is to us all,” Mrs. Gascoigne said after he left.

“He truly is,” Mrs. Davilow agreed. “I wish I had that kind of cheerfulness. It’s like having a fortune.”

“And Rex is just like him,” Mrs. Gascoigne added. “I must tell you about a letter he sent. Let me read you a bit,” she said, pulling it from her pocket. Anna looked nervous—she hadn’t been allowed to mention Rex in front of Gwendolen.

Mrs. Gascoigne scanned the letter, searching for passages to share. But she paused, folded it, and said, “Still, he says our troubles have made him a man. He sees meaning in hard work. He wants a fellowship, to teach pupils, to help his brothers—everything remarkable. The letter is full of humor—just like him. He writes, ‘Tell mother she’s advertised for a jolly good hard-working son, just in time to stop me from shipping off; I offer myself for the role.’ It came Friday. I’ve never seen Henry so moved since Rex was born. It felt like a gain to balance our loss.”

That letter had softened both Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna toward Gwendolen. Gwendolen, in turn, smiled at Anna, pinching her chin playfully—“Everything’s fine now, isn’t it?” She wasn’t cruel or petty. She simply couldn’t bear being made miserable by others.

But when conversation turned to furnishing the cottage, Gwendolen withdrew entirely. She felt she’d done enough for the day. Inside, a fierce struggle raged. The thought of presenting herself to Mrs. Mompert—being judged, approved or rejected—felt like adding insult to injury. Even as a governess, she’d be tested. Once accepted, she’d still be watched, expected to perform tasks she loathed. The bishop might quiz her on serious matters. This wasn’t a career—it was imprisonment.

Wild thoughts flared: running away to become an actress, free from all restraint. But Klesmer’s words lingered—his warning of vulgar men, crude familiarity, coarse laughter behind polite masks. She wasn’t reckless. She demanded respect. She dreamed of being a heroine—not at a gaming table, but in a world where she’d still be treated with dignity. To be cast alone among strangers, relying on their uncertain kindness, felt unbearable. Even the episcopal household, though rigid, seemed preferable. At least there, she wouldn’t be mocked.

Yet here, too, she’d be unseen, unpetted, her feelings ignored. The injustice of it grew heavier each hour. Why her? She’d been shaped for something else—something grander. Now, everything felt ruined. Hope felt foolish. Her talents meant nothing. No direction promised fulfillment. Some girls might imagine romantic futures—governesses becoming beloved, sought-after figures. But Gwendolen couldn’t dream such dreams. Her heart was crushed by what was real, not what might be. She felt sick of life. Nothing seemed worth living for. Religion offered no solace—her suffering felt caused by others’ cruelty, not divine design. The world, in her eyes, had nothing pleasant to offer. All talk of courage, duty, or joy in labor sounded hollow. For her, becoming a governess meant falling—descending, never rising. She’d never separated happiness from distinction, from being admired.

No wonder she felt life unworthy of her breath. When personal glory fades, what remains? Perhaps we all feel this way, in moments of despair—when our pride is wounded, when the world seems indifferent. A young girl facing life’s maze with no map, suddenly stripped of confidence, deserves pity, not judgment.

Despite her strength, her despair affected her body. She felt numb, unable to act. Meals became burdens. Others’ conversations seemed irrelevant, ignorant of her pain. She didn’t entertain thoughts of suicide—those were for weak minds. Her torment was simpler: the certainty that she must live a life she hated.

She avoided the rectory. Pretending compliance felt like a betrayal. She refused to care about the cottage’s furniture. Miss Merry stayed to help—people like Jocosa loved such tasks. Her mother had to make excuses when Anna visited. The calm Gwendolen had vowed to keep had turned into helpless emptiness. “I suppose I’ll pretend soon,” she thought. “But why start now?”

Her mother watched her, silent with sorrow. She fell back into old habits of tenderness, trying to guess what Gwendolen was thinking, wishing she could ease her daughter’s pain.

One afternoon, in the black-and-yellow bedroom, as her mother lingered over her clothes, Gwendolen suddenly sat up.

“Mamma,” she said, lifting the lid of the casket. “I forgot these. Why didn’t you remind me? Please sell them. You won’t miss them. You gave them all to me long ago.”

She lifted the tray, looking beneath.

“If we don’t need them, darling, I’d rather keep them for you,” Mrs. Davilow said, sitting beside her. “Why did you put that handkerchief in here?”

It was the torn one—stuffed with the turquoise necklace.

“It was with the necklace. I was in a hurry,” Gwendolen said, pulling it out and tucking it in her pocket. “Don’t sell the necklace, mamma,” she added, a new emotion stirring within her.

“No, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said softly. “It was made from your father’s chain. I’d rather not sell the rest—they’re not valuable. My best jewelry was taken years ago.”

She blushed, avoiding the painful truth of her husband’s theft.

“These haven’t been counted for expenses. Take them with you.”

“That’s useless, mamma,” Gwendolen said coldly. “Governesses don’t wear jewels. Get me a gray frieze dress and a straw bonnet—like Aunt’s charity children.”

“No, dear. I’m sure the Momperts will appreciate elegance.”

“I’m not sure what they’ll expect,” Gwendolen snapped. “All I know is that I must be what they want.”

“If there’s anything you’d prefer—anything else we could do instead—tell me, Gwendolen. Say what’s in your heart. I’ll try anything for you.”

“Oh, mamma, there’s nothing. I can’t do better. I’ll be lucky if they’ll have me. I’ll bring you eighty pounds. That’s all I care about. I won’t spend anything this year—you’ll have it all. I don’t know how far it’ll go, but you won’t have to sew yourself blind or strain your eyes anymore.”

She didn’t touch her mother. Her eyes were fixed on the necklace, turning it slowly in her fingers.

“Bless you, my dear,” Mrs. Davilow whispered, tears forming. “Don’t despair. You’re young. There may still be great happiness.”

“I don’t see any reason to hope, mamma,” Gwendolen said, her voice hard.

Mrs. Davilow said nothing. She wondered again—what happened between her daughter and Grandcourt?

“I’ll keep this necklace, mamma,” Gwendolen said, placing it aside and closing the casket. “But sell the rest—even if they fetch little. Ask Uncle Henry what to do. I’ll never wear them again. I’m going to take the veil. I wonder if all the poor souls who’ve done it felt this way.”

“Don’t exaggerate, dear.”

“How can anyone know I’m exaggerating when I’m speaking of my own pain? I didn’t say what others feel.”

She took the handkerchief from her pocket, wrapped it carefully around the necklace, and placed it inside the casket.

Mrs. Davilow watched, puzzled. But Gwendolen’s tone discouraged questions.

CHAPTER XXV. How does one trace the origin of a mind reduced to the barrenness of refined self-absorption, where desires have dimmed into mere expectations, and impulses flicker unpredictably through a landscape of emotional neglect? Such a state often arises when life is unshaped by duty or constraint—when freedom becomes its own prison. _Nam deteriores omnes sumus licentiæ_, or, in simpler terms, “As you like” is no guidepost to virtue.

Potentates convey their will with few words, and so it was with Grandcourt. Upon learning that Gwendolen had left Leubronn, he casually declared the resort a "beastly hole," worse than Baden—a remark Mr. Lush instantly interpreted as an order to return to Diplow. Though the execution lagged behind the intent, Grandcourt did not rush; he lingered over his morning rituals, descending at last with an air of detached elegance that made vitality seem crude. He wandered aimlessly—the terrace, the gaming rooms, the reading room—showing no interest in anyone or anything, yet somehow commanding attention.

When Lady Mallinger approached, he paused, raised his hat, and listened to her praise of the mineral waters. "Yes," he replied, voice flat, "I heard someone say how providential it is that springs always happen to be near gambling places."

"Oh, that was a joke!" she exclaimed, misunderstanding his deadpan tone. "Like the old one about towns and rivers."

"Ah, perhaps," he murmured, without shifting expression.

Lady Mallinger later confided this exchange to Sir Hugo, who chuckled. "He’s not a fool. Don’t imagine he doesn’t see humor. He plays his cards as well as any man."

"But he never seemed very sensible to me," she confessed, half-apologetically. Her real unease stemmed from Grandcourt’s presence—he reminded her of her own failure: the absence of a son, which haunted her sense of worth. She feared Sir Hugo might regret his choice, and though he was kind, she suspected he could grow distant if disappointment lingered too long.

Deronda also encountered Grandcourt, receiving a brief nod. He returned it courteously, though inwardly resentful. A deep-seated belief remained: that his birth had been marred by injustice, and his father—Grandcourt’s own father—was responsible. He knew that, had circumstances been different, Grandcourt might have inherited what was now his. Yet Deronda refused to let envy fester. He saw the danger of petty triumph in another’s downfall and turned away from it, clinging instead to compassion and dignity. Pride helped him—pride in refusing to acknowledge poverty as a mark of shame. He would not allow Grandcourt to believe himself envied.

But interpretation is beyond control. Grandcourt imagined Deronda felt the sting of comparison, and this fantasy pleased him. The illusion that others envy us is a familiar companion to egoism. Like his dogs, whom he enjoyed dominating, Grandcourt took pleasure in feeling superior—even in making others jealous. So, on the terrace, he spoke to Deronda about the hunting season at Diplow, even inviting him: “You’d better come over for a run or two when it begins.”

Meanwhile, Lush found time to amuse himself—chatting with Sir Hugo, sharing gossip about Grandcourt’s affairs, especially those involving Diplow. The baronet already knew enough about his nephew’s entanglements to allow Lush to speak freely. On a sunlit autumn day, such talk was both pleasant and safe—scandal lightly dressed as _traits de mœurs_. Sir Hugo enjoyed these glimpses into high society’s secrets, but kept them carefully confined. He never discussed his knowledge of Grandcourt’s private life with Deronda, who viewed Grandcourt as a pale, lifeless figure—far removed from the blood and fire of passion.

Lush, ever eager to please, was content with the baronet’s favor. He was not quite a gentleman, but he served well—like a second-best coat, worn with comfort and ease. “Well,” said Sir Hugo, “let me know if things change—whether the marriage seems likely, or if money troubles become urgent. My plan would be better for him than burdening Ryelands.”

“That’s true,” Lush agreed. “But we mustn’t push it. Just let it drift into his thoughts—like a scent that tickles the nose. Grandcourt isn’t a man driven by what’s best for him—especially if he sees it benefiting you too. I’ve stayed by him for fifteen years, given up everything. He wouldn’t find another like me. He’s peculiar—has grown more so lately. But I’m constant. I’ve been a sort of guardian since he was twenty. He was fascinating then—and could be now, if he chose. I’m attached to him. And it would be worse for him if I were gone.”

Sir Hugo nodded, needing no response. Lush, too, didn’t expect one. Who can avoid speaking of themselves? Even in silence, men betray their inner lives. And so Lush offered this portrait—not as truth, but as performance, as the polite dressing of his soul.

But the next morning, Grandcourt cut short Lush’s easy delay. He asked sharply, “Are you preparing for our departure by the Paris train?”

“I didn’t know you meant to leave,” Lush replied, not truly surprised.

“You might have known,” Grandcourt muttered, staring at his cigar. “See to everything. And make sure no brute shares our carriage. Leave my P.P.C. at the Mallingers’.”

They reached Paris the following day. There, Grandcourt instructed Lush to proceed directly to Diplow, while he stayed behind with his valet. Only days later did Lush receive a telegram summoning the carriage to Wanchester station.

In the interim, Lush worked diligently—overseeing the stud, managing the household, and gathering news of Gwendolen and Offendene. He couldn’t predict how the news of her family’s ruin might affect Grandcourt’s erratic nature. On one hand, her poverty might remove the fear of rejection—making her more accessible. On the other, certainty of acceptance might only make Grandcourt indecisive, drifting like a moth caught in a web.

Lush had observed Grandcourt closely for years. He understood him better than most—but understanding Grandcourt meant knowing that prediction was impossible. Could he suddenly act nobly, like a French drama hero rising from moral decay? Perhaps. But Lush doubted it. Generosity was not among Grandcourt’s habits.

Yet Lush had firm beliefs: Grandcourt would never willingly choose a woman who brought nothing but hardship. He preferred wealth—or at least gratitude. An heiress would bring immediate security. Mrs. Glasher, whom Lush had long supported, would offer lasting loyalty. Neither outcome troubled him socially. He wouldn’t mind no marriage at all. But he *did* want to prevent a union with Gwendolen—one that might bring chaos to Grandcourt and distress to himself.

He wanted peace, not harm. Except, perhaps, a little discomfort for Gwendolen—unconvenient, impertinent, and now, possibly, a threat.

Still, even the most agreeable servant has limits. Lush had long been treated as useful, pleasant, reliable. He hadn’t prepared himself for insult—only for conflict.

On the first day back at Diplow, Grandcourt focused on the stables. He ordered a groom to saddle Criterion with a side-saddle, and rode the horse slowly, inspecting its gait. This small gesture signaled purpose. Lush hesitated—should he speak first, before any step was taken?

The next morning, he almost decided to reveal what he knew about Gwendolen. But Grandcourt remained silent, answering even personal questions with the stillness of a fish under water. After reading his letters, he gave orders to Lush, then turned slightly, muttering, “Oh.”

“What is it?” Lush asked, unbothered by formality.

“Shut the door,” Grandcourt said. “I can’t speak into the corridor.”

Lush closed the door and sat down.

After a pause, Grandcourt asked, “Is Miss Harleth at Offendene?”

He knew Lush had inquired. He liked that Lush didn’t need *him* to ask.

“Well, I don’t know,” Lush replied carelessly. “The family’s ruined. Lost everything in some banking scandal. The mother has nothing. They’re living in a cottage—like laborers.”

“Don’t lie to me,” Grandcourt whispered, low and sharp. “It’s not amusing. It serves no purpose.”
