“What do you mean?” Lush asked, irritated. “I’m not making this up. Bazley told me. Brackenshaw’s man too. He’s finding a new tenant for Offendene.”

“I don’t mean that,” Grandcourt said, regaining his calm. “Is she there? Or isn’t she?”

“I can’t tell,” Lush snapped. “She may have left yesterday. I heard she took a governess position. Might already be there. But if you want to see her, the mother would send for her.”

That sneer slipped out before he could stop it.

“Send Hutchins to inquire if she’ll be there tomorrow.”

Lush didn’t move.

He had rehearsed arguments, and now, provoked, he delivered them. “You’re entering a serious situation now, Grandcourt. No more flirting—no games. You must decide: do you want to be accepted? And what if you’re refused? One or the other. You can’t dally for six weeks.”

Grandcourt said nothing. He pressed the newspaper onto his knees and lit a new cigar.

Lush took this as a sign he was listening—and seized the moment. “Everything’s changed. Her family needs support. You can’t marry her and let her mother live in poverty. Marriage will tie you down. You’re not used to restraint. And financially, you’re not exactly free. What will you gain? You control your estates—why burden them for a whim you may regret in a year? I’d hate to see you ruin your life like this. If there were real benefit, it would be different.”

His tone grew increasingly smooth, almost honeyed. He nearly forgot he was gambling.

Then Grandcourt spoke. “I knew you objected to my marrying Miss Harleth.”

A pause. Then: “But I never thought that mattered.”

“I never thought you did,” Lush replied, dryly. “But consider this: after all you’ve seen, are you really acting like a ballad hero? Making a fool of yourself—for what? You couldn’t make up your mind before. You can’t care much for her. And the tricks she’ll play—judge from what you heard in Leubronn. But the point is—there’s no more hesitation now.”

“Perfectly,” Grandcourt said, turning to look at him with narrowed eyes. “I don’t intend there to be. It may be disagreeable to you. But if you think I care a damn about that—you’re profoundly mistaken.”

“Oh, well,” Lush said, hands in pockets, “if you’ve made up your mind—though there’s another angle. I assumed she’d accept. But I’m not so sure. She’s tricky. And she had reasons to run before.”

He stepped forward, standing near Grandcourt, though not close.

“She had her reasons,” he repeated, more pointedly.

“I came to that conclusion,” Grandcourt said, coldly ironic.

“Yes, but do you know what they were?”

“You do,” Grandcourt replied, not blinking.

“And you should know—so you can judge whether she’ll swallow her reasons and accept you. I’d wager against it. She saw Lydia in Cardell Chase. She heard the whole story.”

Grandcourt smoked in silence.

Lush waited, restless, watching the windows. He expected Grandcourt to accuse him of engineering the affair—Mrs. Glasher had been at Gadsmere, a hundred miles away. He was ready to admit it. But he wanted Grandcourt to realize: she knew the truth. She was frightened.

At last, Grandcourt looked up. “What follows?”

It was a checkmate.

Lush felt fury rise—stronger than ever before. But to fight now would be foolish. He shrugged, turned to leave.

Then Grandcourt said, calmly, “Oblige me by pushing that pen and paper here.”

No command could have been more absolute. Lush obeyed. Grandcourt wrote quickly, addressed the note, and pushed it across the table. “Let Hutchins take it at once.”

It was addressed to Miss Harleth, Offendene.

Later, when Lush cooled, he was glad there had been no explosion. But he knew a rift had formed. He had lost ground. And Grandcourt’s resolve had hardened—not softened.

As for how it happened—what shifted within that baffling mind—Lush could only shrug in despair.

He brings white asses laden with the freight
Of Tyrian vessels, purple, gold and balm,
To bribe my will: I’ll bid them chase him forth,
Nor let him breathe the taint of his surmise
On my secure resolve. Ay, ’tis secure:
And therefore let him come to spread his freight.
For firmness hath its appetite and craves
The stronger lure, more strongly to resist;
Would know the touch of gold to fling it off;
Scent wine to feel its lip the soberer;
Behold soft byssus, ivory, and plumes
To say, “They’re fair, but I will none of them,”
And flout Enticement in the very face.

Mr. Gascoigne arrived at Offendene one day bearing news he believed would bring some comfort—Mrs. Mompert had set Tuesday of the coming week for her meeting with Gwendolen at Wanchester. He said nothing of having overheard that Mr. Grandcourt had returned to Diplow, unaware that Leubronn had been the destination of his niece’s admirer. He thought it kinder not to stir up memories of a future once bright and now dimmed. In truth, he regretted Gwendolen’s sudden refusal of Grandcourt, yet justified her in his mind by recalling how oddly Grandcourt himself had behaved—vanishing abruptly when the moment seemed ripe for declaration. The rector’s practical nature told him that his duty now was to help Gwendolen meet her new circumstances with courage, for no sign suggested a reversal of fate.

“You will find meaning in change, my dear,” he said gently. “I have no doubt you’ll grow into a stronger woman through this.”

“I cannot pretend to like it,” Gwendolen replied, a flicker of impatience in her voice. “But I understand I must endure it.” She recalled another time when she’d accepted his counsel about a prospect she’d never truly desired.

“And your good sense will guide your behavior,” he added, his tone a little graver. “Mrs. Mompert will surely approve of you. You’ll know how to act before someone who holds such authority over you in every way. This trial comes early, yes—but that makes it easier in some respects. And all suffering has value if we learn to accept it.”

That was precisely what Gwendolen could not do. After her uncle left, tears—rarely shed during this ordeal—rose slowly, then fell, as she sat alone. Her heart refused the idea that youth made hardship easier. When would joy come, if not now? Not that her dreams were untouched by sorrow anymore—not that she still imagined life’s pleasures without knowing their cost. But this growing disillusionment—with the world, even with herself—only deepened her loneliness. It felt as though a barren plain stretched ahead, empty and uninviting, and she had no strength to walk it.

She stood at the edge of that first fierce rebellion of youth—the rage against a world that denies joy rather than inflicting pain. We who’ve grown older often forget this storm, remembering only the calm after, and so judge it harshly as childish, irrational, even blasphemous. What seems absurd, once we stand outside it, is the furious grief at being the one chosen for sorrow—why not someone else? Yet perhaps those who later shielded others, who bore their own wounds in silence, who achieved greatness—perhaps they too began here: stunned by their own hurt, raging at the universe for denying their dreams, as if stung by invisible wasps that turned the whole world into an unjust wound.

This was nearly Gwendolen’s state. True, many girls had faced reversals like hers. But what mattered was that it was *hers*. It was what she felt under Klesmer’s cold assessment that she lacked the power to command fortune by will or merit. It was what she would feel beneath Mrs. Mompert’s constant expectations, under the dull demand to be cheerful with three Miss Momperts, under the need to hide her thoughts, to submit completely. To lose a crown is painful—but worse, perhaps, is losing the belief in one’s own divinity. Imagine a man raised to think himself godlike, suddenly stripped of all worship, unable to perform the miracle that would restore faith. Something like that had happened to Gwendolen—the spoiled child with the radiant eyes and noble form, now seeming powerless, drained of magic.

She rose from the low ottoman where she’d sat aimlessly, pacing the drawing-room, resting her elbow on one hand while her cheek leaned on the other. A slow tear slipped down.

“I’ve always known,” she thought, “since I was small, that mamma wasn’t happy. And now—I may be even unhappier than she was.”

Her mind lingered on the image of herself growing old, losing her beauty, no longer caring what she did. But that vision inevitably brought back her mother. “Poor mamma—it will be worse for her now.” She could earn a little money for her—that was all she cared about now. Then, with a sudden shift of imagination, she saw both of them aged: her mother white-haired, herself faded, their faces meeting across years, filled with memory and love. And she understood what her mother might think: *“Poor Gwen too is sad and faded now.”* For the first time, she sobbed—not in anger, but with quiet sorrow.

She turned toward the door just as her mother entered. She barely registered it—her eyes were wide with tears, and she pressed her handkerchief hard against them. Before she removed it, she felt her mother’s arms around her. That touch, echoing her inner vision, broke her resolve to stay silent. She sobbed again, helplessly, as their cheeks met.

Mrs. Davilow had come with something that already troubled her deeply. She dared not speak until her daughter calmed. But Gwendolen, for whom tears were always a struggle, pressed her handkerchief to her eyes once more, took a deep breath, and drew back her head to look at her mother—pale and trembling.

“It was nothing, mamma,” she said softly. “It’s all over now.”

But Mrs. Davilow had stepped back, and Gwendolen saw a letter in her hand.

“What is that?” she asked sharply. “More bad news?”

“I don’t know what you’ll think of it, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, holding the letter tightly. “You’ll hardly guess where it comes from.”

“Don’t ask me to guess,” Gwendolen replied, irritated—like a bruise being pressed.

“It’s addressed to you,” her mother whispered.

Gwendolen gave the faintest shake of her head.

“It’s from Diplow,” Mrs. Davilow said, handing it over.

She recognized Grandcourt’s handwriting at once—and flushed deeply. Her mother watched her read, wondering what the message held. Then the color drained from Gwendolen’s face. Even her lips turned pale as she turned the open note toward her mother.

The words were few and formal:

> Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene tomorrow after two o’clock to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.

Mrs. Davilow read it, then looked at her daughter questioningly, leaving the note in her hand.

Gwendolen dropped it to the floor and turned away.

“It must be answered, darling,” Mrs. Davilow said timidly. “The man waits.”

Gwendolen sank onto the settee, clasped her hands, and stared straight ahead—not at her mother. Her expression was that of one startled by a sound, listening for what would follow. The world had shifted in minutes. Just moments ago, she had seen only a path of endless monotony, her spirit rebelling against a fate that offered no escape. Now—choice had returned.

Was it triumph she felt—or terror? Impossible not to feel some triumph, at being valued when she tasted the bitterness of insignificance. Again, she felt a fleeting sense of power over her own life. But how to use it? That was the fear.

Like pages turning rapidly in a book, fragments of memory flooded back—Grandcourt’s allurements, her vacillations, her near surrender, the final rejection; the sharp face of the dark-eyed woman with the beautiful boy; her own promise—was it a vow not to marry him?—and the new disbelief in men, in love, in everything, that scene having become a symbol. That unchangeable past formed a vision that, in the first wild moment before reason could temper it, made her instinctive fear recoil.

What good was choice now? What did she want? Anything different? No. And yet, deep within the shadows of her mind, a new wish stirred: *I wish I had never known him.*

Anything—anything—that would spare her the dread of letting Grandcourt come.

It was not long—yet to Mrs. Davilow, it felt endless—before she ventured, softly, “You’ll need to write, dear. Or shall I write for you? You can dictate.”

“No, mamma,” Gwendolen said, taking a deep breath. “But please lay out the pen and paper.”

It was a way to buy time. Should she refuse him? Close the door? Refuse even to look? Though she knew she would remain exactly where she was. Yet the young fire within her stirred—warm current through her fear—yearning for action, for an event, for a chance to speak and look with the force she once had. Tomorrow was no longer frozen.

“There’s really no reason to be so alarmed,” Gwendolen said, remonstrating gently, as her mother prepared the writing materials and waited. “Servants expect to wait. It’s not as if I must reply instantly.”

“No, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, her tone softened by correction, turning to pick up her embroidery. “He can wait another quarter-hour, if you like.”

It was simple—almost unconscious—but it was a subtle move. Gwendolen felt a conflicting urge to hurry: haste would save her from deliberate decision.

“I didn’t mean him to wait long enough for that needlework to finish,” she said, lifting her hands to smooth her hair, rising and standing still.

“But if you don’t feel able to decide?” Mrs. Davilow asked sympathetically.

“I *must* decide,” Gwendolen said, walking to the writing table and sitting.

All the while, a restless current ran beneath her thoughts—like a man keeping up conversation while planning his escape. Why not let him come? It bound her to nothing. He’d gone to Leubronn after her—he meant to renew his suit plainly now. So what? She could reject him. Why deny herself the freedom to do so?

“If Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn,” Mrs. Davilow observed, noticing Gwendolen lean back in her chair after picking up the pen—“I wonder if he knows about our troubles?”

“That wouldn’t matter to a man like him,” Gwendolen said, slightly scornful.

“It would to some,” Mrs. Davilow replied. “Many men wouldn’t want a wife whose family is nearly beggarly. Here we are at Offendene, safe in our shell. But imagine him finding us at Sawyer’s Cottage. Most men fear being bored or burdened by their wives’ families. If Grandcourt does know… I think it proves his attachment.”

She spoke with unusual emphasis. It was the first time she’d mentioned Grandcourt in a way that hinted at approval—her usual belief being that such talk would only make matters worse.

But her words struck deeper than she knew. They opened new possibilities—a vision of what Grandcourt might do for her mother, if only Gwendolen chose to—what she *would not* do.

Overwhelmed by a rush of ideas, she felt the urgency of the moment: the letter must be written now, or it might never be.

In the end, she acted quickly—just as she’d wished. To act fast was to avoid full decision, to leave doors open.

She wrote:

> Miss Harleth presents her compliments to Mr. Grandcourt. She will be at home after two o’clock tomorrow.

Before sealing it, she said, “Please ring the bell, mamma, if anyone needs to answer.” She didn’t know who managed the household.

Only after the letter was taken away, and Gwendolen had risen again, stretching one arm, then resting her hand on her head with a low moan that sounded like relief, did Mrs. Davilow dare ask, “What did you say, Gwen?”

“I said I’d be at home,” Gwendolen replied, with quiet dignity.

Then, after a pause, “Don’t expect anything to happen just because Mr. Grandcourt is coming, mamma.”

“I don’t expect anything, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said. “I only want you to follow your own feeling. You’ve never told me what that is.”

“What’s the use?” Gwendolen said, hearing reproach in the truth. “When I have something pleasant to tell, you’ll hear it.”

“But Mr. Grandcourt will think you’ve accepted him,” Mrs. Davilow said. “His note makes clear he’s coming to propose.”

“Very well,” Gwendolen answered. “Then I’ll have the pleasure of refusing him.”

Mrs. Davilow looked up, astonished. But Gwendolen turned away, saying, “Put down that hateful needlework. Let’s walk in the avenue. I’m suffocating.”

CHAPTER XXVII. Desire has trimmed the sails, and Circumstance brings but the breeze to fill them.

As Grandcourt rode his sleek black Yarico toward Offendene, with Criterion following behind under the groom’s steady hand, Gwendolen sat before her mirror while her mother gently gathered her long, light-brown hair into a loose coil. “Just tie it up simply, mamma,” she said softly, her voice calm but edged with quiet resolve.

“Let me bring you some earrings, Gwen,” Mrs. Davilow offered, stepping back to admire the reflection in the glass.

They both paused—unconsciously drawn by the change in Gwendolen’s face. Her eyes sparkled with a brightness they hadn’t held in days, and the faint shadow that had clouded her features seemed lifted, restoring her youth and composure. A warmth stirred in Mrs. Davilow’s heart. “You do want your earrings?”

“No, mamma,” Gwendolen replied, smiling faintly. “I won forgo any ornaments. I’ll wear my black silk. Black is the only color when one is going to refuse an offer.”

She rose, slipping off her dressing gown with a grace that carried a new strength. The way she moved—head held high, body poised with renewed energy—was like a plant rehydrated after drought. Her mother watched, thinking, *She’s herself again. It must be pleasure at his coming. But can she truly mean to turn him down?*

Gwendolen would have been furious if such thoughts had been spoken aloud. Yet over the past twenty hours—broken only by brief sleep—her mind had swirled with images and arguments, pulling her between desire and duty, pride and fear. She had resolved not to accept Grandcourt from the moment she read his letter. But now, that decision felt hollow—a shell stripped of life, like a prayer whispered without belief. She still meant to refuse him. But the very act of resisting had forced her to confront what she’d once avoided: the weight of his past, the woman he had left behind.

At first, her hesitation had been vague—just a flicker of unease. But then came the day at Cardell Chase. That was when everything shifted. Not with reason, but with instinct. She had reacted not just out of maidenly pride or jealousy, but from a deeper dread—an unspoken horror of wrong, of complicity in something hidden, something dishonorable. She didn’t know exactly what had happened between Grandcourt and Mrs. Glasher. All she knew was that she had promised not to interfere. And yet—what did that promise really mean? If another woman married him, would it truly harm Mrs. Glasher or her children? Or might it even help them?

Her mind spun through possibilities. Marriage, to Gwendolen, was still a mystery—no more real to her than the invisible currents of magnetism or the laws of storms. She saw her mother’s marriage as poorly managed, and vowed she would do better. But she couldn’t speak of marriage’s trials—not with this daughter, whose spirit was already too tangled in silence and suspicion.

*I wonder what mamma or Uncle would say if they knew about Mrs. Glasher,* she thought. Not that she could ever tell them. Nor could she imagine what others might think of Grandcourt marrying someone else—and having other children. What would people say? What would they say about *her*, if she accepted him?

The thought of public opinion brought a strange relief. At least it gave her a measure. She collected her memories: yes, society judged illegitimate children far more harshly than illegitimate fathers. So perhaps, in their eyes, she had no duty to defend Mrs. Glasher. But that didn’t erase the feeling—the deep, almost angry shame—that she should be expected to join a man whose life was steeped in secrets, in broken promises. He had never loved her, not truly. His advances had been calculated, deliberate. And now, knowing what she knew, she felt a kind of retrospective disgust—not just at him, but at the idea of being wooed by someone who laughed at her ignorance.

Yet love had never been her concern. For her, marriage had always been about security, status, escape. To be loved was the man’s role. But now, the idea of being chosen by Grandcourt—this man of power and mystery—felt like a trap. Still, she refused to give in to fear. She had made up her mind. She would not accept him.

And yet—there was a thrill in the thought of refusing him. She had the reins in her hands again. A current ran through her veins, lifting her from the despair of Klesmer’s visit. She wasn’t asking for approval anymore. She was claiming power.

Then came the sound of hooves on gravel. Miss Merry appeared at the door. “Mr. Grandcourt is here. In the drawing-room.”

Gwendolen’s breath caught. The hours of preparation, the triumph of her resolve—it all vanished. It was as if he had burst in upon her in the midst of her deepest sorrow.

She walked into the room with every ounce of willpower focused on control. She met his gaze with calm dignity, offering her hand as he greeted her with a low, languid voice: “I hope you are quite well.”

She answered just as softly. “Yes.”

They sat—she upright, eyes lowered; he relaxed, leaning on the chair back, hat in hand. From the outside, they looked like lovers suspended in a moment of quiet tension. And indeed, the game had begun. He was wooing her—not with words, but with presence, with attention so intense it seemed to breathe around her. And she, though she tried to resist, found herself drawn in.

“I was disappointed not to find you at Leubronn,” he began, his voice slow, tinged with a hint of longing. “It was unbearable without you. A mere kennel of a place. Don’t you agree?”

“I can’t judge what it would be without myself,” she replied, lifting her eyes with a flicker of mischief. “But with me, I liked it well enough to stay longer—if I could. Though I had to return for family troubles.”

“Very cruel of you,” he said, ignoring her mention of hardship. “You knew you were the heart of everything. Are you so reckless with me?”

She couldn’t answer yes—too obvious. No—too final. So she turned her eyes down, flushing crimson.

Grandcourt saw it as a sign of interest. But he wanted more. “Is there someone else? Some engagement? A man standing between us?”

Inwardly, she answered: *No—but there is a woman.* But how could she say it? Even if she hadn’t sworn silence, the subject was forbidden ground. And how could she stop his courtship with formalities? Like a fish trapped in a net, she had no clear path.

After a pause, he asked, “Am I to understand that someone else is preferred?”

That was it. The moment of truth. Gwendolen, tired of hesitation, rose abruptly. “No,” she said, her voice clear and firm. “Not that. But I may not be ready to take *you*.”

He understood instantly. Such things touched his pride deeply. “I would never importune you,” he said, his tone cool, composed. “I wouldn’t ask you to endure me. If there’s no hope, I’d ask only that you tell me so, so I may ride away.”

At those words, something inside her stirred. A sudden fear—what if he left? What then? The old emptiness would return. She needed him there. She clung to the next topic, desperate for delay. “You don’t know what’s happened. Mamma has lost everything. We’re leaving this house. I beg you to forgive my distraction.”

With that, she regained some composure. She spoke with dignity, meeting his gaze. His eyes—long, narrow, unreadable—held hers, creating a silent drama neither could name. Love, like thought, cannot be captured in words. The exchange between them was deeper than speech, charged with unspoken forces.

Then, slowly, he said, “You will tell me now, I hope, that Mrs. Davilow’s loss will not trouble you further. You will trust me to protect her. You will let me provide for her future.”

His voice was measured, deliberate. Each pause allowed time for her imagination to wander. As the words sank in, they felt like wine—warm, soothing, making the world seem lighter. Suddenly, the pain of poverty receded. She imagined herself springing to her mother, laughing again. The repugnance she once felt faded into memory. Here, in this moment, was rescue.

Yet when he finished speaking, she felt the pivot point. “You are very generous,” she murmured, not moving her eyes.

“You accept what will make such things a matter of course?” he asked, no urgency in his voice. “You consent to become my wife?”

This time, she remained pale. Without meaning to, she rose and stepped back, folding her hands before her. Silence stretched.

Grandcourt followed, placing his hat on the chair but holding onto it. The hesitation of this destitute girl—so close to accepting his wealth—sparked a fierce interest in him. He believed it was because of Mrs. Glasher.

“Do you command me to go?” he asked, his voice low, almost intimate.

“No,” she whispered. She couldn’t let him leave. That single word was a grip.

“You accept my devotion?” he asked, holding his hat at his side, eyes locked on hers.

Their gaze held—long, still, full of meaning. How could she contradict herself? What had she waited for? He had closed the door on explanations.

“Yes,” she said, gravely—like a verdict pronounced in court.

He received it quietly. They stood, staring, unchanged in posture.

Was there ever a more solemn way to say “yes”? Grandcourt preferred the distance, the ritual, the unspoken boundary she imposed. But at last, he placed his hat down and stepped forward, taking her hand, pressing his lips lightly against it—then letting go.

She thought his manner perfect. And in that moment, she felt free—almost ready to tease again. Her “yes” meant so little now. The darkness of her future dissolved. She saw herself freed from the Momperts, her mother from Sawyer’s Cottage.

A smile curled her lips. “Will you see mamma? I’ll fetch her.”

“Wait a moment,” he said, falling into his familiar pose—thumb and forefinger in his waistcoat pocket, right hand stroking his whiskers. “Have you anything else to say?”

“Only this,” she said, playfully. “Will it bother you to be asked when we can marry?”

“I think it will, today,” she replied, tilting her chin.

“Not today. Tomorrow. Think on it. In a fortnight—or three weeks—as soon as possible.”

“Oh, you think you’ll tire of me?” she teased. “I’ve noticed married husbands aren’t as present as engaged ones. But perhaps I’ll like that better.”

She laughed, bright and charming.

“You shall have whatever you like,” he said.

“And nothing that I don’t like?” she countered. “Please say that. Because I dislike what I don’t like more than I like what I like.”

He paused. These were nuances he knew well. “I don’t know. This world is a brute place. Things happen you don’t like. I can’t always keep you from boredom. If you want to ride Criterion, I can’t stop him from appearing.”

“Oh, my friend Criterion—how is he?”

“He’s outside. I had the groom ride him so you could see. He wore a side-saddle yesterday. Come to the window.”

They watched the horses circle the drive—beautiful, powerful creatures, groomed to perfection. A surge of joy passed through Gwendolen. They were symbols of command, luxury—everything opposite to the ugliness of poverty she had faced.

“Will you ride Criterion tomorrow?” Grandcourt asked.

“If you wish, everything will be arranged.”

“I should like it above all things,” she said. “I want to lose myself in a gallop again. But now I must go fetch mamma.”

“Take my arm to the door,” he said.

She did. Their faces were nearly level, close together. He looked at her. She thought his manners—his ease, his restraint—were more refined than any lover she’d ever imagined. There was no threat of a kiss. Only quiet intimacy.

Then, halfway across the room, she stopped. “Oh—while I think of it. There’s something I dislike that you can save me from. I do *not* like Mr. Lush’s company.”

“You shall not have it. I’ll get rid of him.”

“You’re not fond of him yourself?”

“Not at all. I let him hang on because he’s always been a poor devil. He traveled with me as a boy. Coarse-haired brute—half hog, half dilettante.”

She laughed. It felt natural, kind—even gentle.

When they reached the door, he opened it for her with effortless grace. Truly, she thought, he might be the least disagreeable husband imaginable.

Mrs. Davilow waited anxiously in her bedroom. When Gwendolen entered, she rushed forward, kissed her cheeks, and whispered, “Come down, mamma. See Mr. Grandcourt. I’m engaged to him.”

“My darling child,” Mrs. Davilow breathed, stunned—not joyful, but solemn.

“Yes,” Gwendolen said, calm, decisive. “Everything is settled. You won’t go to Sawyer’s Cottage. I won’t be inspected by Mrs. Mompert. Everything will be as I choose. Come down with me now.”

“It is easier to know man in general than to know a particular man.”—LA ROCHEFOUCAULD.

An hour after Grandcourt had departed, the news of Gwendolen’s engagement spread quickly to the rectory, and Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, along with Anna, spent the evening at Offendene.

“My dear,” said the rector, “I must congratulate you on having inspired such a deep attachment. You look thoughtful—and I can understand why. A lifelong union is no small matter. But from what I’ve seen of Mr. Grandcourt’s behavior and words, I believe we may already glimpse some good coming out of our trials. He has shown a sensitivity and generosity that speaks well of his character.”

He was referring to Grandcourt’s subtle suggestion that he would provide for Mrs. Davilow—a detail Gwendolen had recalled with precision when she told her mother about their conversation.

“But I have no doubt Mr. Grandcourt would have acted just as generously if you hadn’t gone to Germany,” said Mrs. Gascoigne, feeling it her duty to speak plainly. “You might have been engaged to him more than a month ago. Now, there’s no room for hesitation—or desire to hesitate. A woman owes great gratitude to a man who persists in offering her such a future. But I trust you feel this properly.”

“I’m not certain I do, aunt,” Gwendolen replied, her tone both teasing and serious. “I don’t know exactly what one *should* feel when engaged.”

The rector smiled and patted her shoulder, as if amused by a harmless rebellion. His wife took this as a sign that no rebuke was intended. Anna, meanwhile, kissed Gwendolen and whispered, “I hope you’ll be happy,” before retreating into silence, fighting back tears. In recent days, she had imagined a quiet romance: that Rex still loved Gwendolen, and perhaps, through hardship, her heart might soften into love—allowing them to marry someday. That dream had become a prayer: that she, Anna, could rejoice as a faithful sister, content to serve Gwendolen as long as Rex remained without wealth. But now, she needed grace to rejoice in something else entirely.

Miss Merry and the four girls—Alice, with her high shoulders; Bertha and Fanny, the whisperers; and Isabel, always listening—were all present, drawn together by the occasion. Everything seemed to turn toward Gwendolen’s honor and glory, and real life felt as vivid and grand as the pages of *Sir Charles Grandison*. The evening passed mostly in the rector’s firm pronouncements, answering the speculations of the two elder women. He insisted that settlements were unnecessary—everything, he believed, would be safely left to Mr. Grandcourt.

“I’d like to know what Ryelands and Gadsmere are really like,” said Mrs. Davilow.

“Gadsmere is likely a secondary estate,” Mr. Gascoigne replied. “But Ryelands—I know it well—is one of the finest seats in the country. The park is vast, and the woods are of great value. The house was designed by Inigo Jones, with ceilings painted in the Italian manner. The estate is said to bring in twelve thousand pounds a year, and includes two livings—one a rectory—in the Grandcourt family’s gift. There may be encumbrances on the land, but Mr. Grandcourt was an only child.”

“It would be extraordinary,” Mrs. Gascoigne mused, counting off points on her fingers, “if he were to inherit Lord Stannery too. Think of it—the Grandcourt estate, the Mallinger estate, the baronetcy, *and* the peerage!” She paused on the fourth finger. “Though they say no land comes with the peerage.” It seemed a pity there was nothing for the fifth.

“The peerage,” the rector added wisely, “is a distant possibility. Two cousins stand between the current peer and Mr. Grandcourt. Still, it’s sobering how death and chance sometimes concentrate fortunes in one hand. But such excess should be avoided. To be Sir Mallinger Grandcourt Mallinger—that will be his title, I suppose—with such properties is more than enough for any man. Let us hope he uses it well.”

“And what a position for you, Gwendolen!” Mrs. Gascoigne exclaimed. “A great responsibility. But Henry, you must write immediately to Mrs. Mompert. It’s fortunate you have an engagement to offer as an excuse—otherwise, she might take offense. She’s rather proud.”

“I’m free of that horror,” thought Gwendolen, whose name for Mompert had become almost magical in its insignificance. She remained quiet throughout the evening, and that night barely slept at all in her small white bed. It was rare for her strong youth to be restless—but even rarer that she would hide her unease from her mother. Her mind was utterly changed. Once sure of herself, ready to lead others, she had just taken a step she had once believed she wouldn’t take—perhaps even shouldn’t take. There was no turning back now. Much of what lay ahead appealed to her. And if she returned, there would be nothing to gain. Yet beneath her resolve lingered the shadow of her earlier conviction—the one that had once moved her whole being with certainty. As she lay awake, eyes wide in the dark, she was seized by dread: she was doing what she had once fled from with disgust. This was new—this fear of moral consequence. She had never known guilt that couldn’t be erased with gifts or affection. But now, a kind of awakening had begun. She seemed poised on the edge of accepting, for the rest of her life, what she had once said in bitterness, when her discovery drove her to Leubronn: that nothing mattered—she只需 amuse herself. That recklessness, that abandonment of conscience, suddenly terrified her. It came with visions of disaster—disaster that was no longer just a word. All the half-heard teachings of religion, the deeper sense of something terrible and unyielding surrounding her, converged into a vague but powerful idea of judgment.

Before her lay the brilliant life she had longed for—the freedom she imagined marriage would give her—the escape from her dull girlhood. Yet it now tasted tainted, like food touched by sacrilege. She had to snatch it, trembling. Alone in the darkness, her stronger self could not resist the first wave of terror after her irrevocable choice. The face of that unhappy woman and her children—Grandcourt and his past—repeated themselves in her mind like a stain she couldn’t wash away. They eclipsed every other thought, leaving only the knowledge that she had brought those scenes into her own life. Her wakefulness felt like delirium. A faint light crept through the curtain. The cold grew sharper. She could endure it no longer.

“Mamma!”

“Yes, dear,” Mrs. Davilow answered at once, her voice already awake.

“Let me come to you.”

She soon fell asleep against her mother’s shoulder, sleeping deeply until late, when, dreaming of a ballroom aglow with light, she awoke to find her mother standing beside the bed, holding a small parcel.

“I’m sorry to wake you, darling,” Mrs. Davilow said gently, “but I thought it better to give this to you right away. The groom has brought Criterion—he arrived on another horse and says he’s to stay here.”

Gwendolen sat up and opened the package. Inside was a delicate enameled box, containing a magnificent diamond ring and a letter with a folded slip of colored paper and these words:

> Please wear this ring when I arrive at twelve, as a token of our betrothal. I’ve enclosed a check made out to Mr. Gascoigne for immediate expenses. Of course, Mrs. Davilow will remain at Offendene—at least for now. I hope, when I come, you’ll grant me an early date so I may begin to obey your wishes more closely.—Yours devotedly,

The check was for five hundred pounds. Gwendolen held it up to her mother.

“How thoughtful and considerate!” Mrs. Davilow said, deeply moved. “But truly, I’d prefer not to depend on my son-in-law. We could manage quite well on our own.”

“If you say that again, I won’t marry him,” Gwendolen said sharply.

“My dear,” Mrs. Davilow replied, pleading, “I don’t think you’re marrying me alone.”

Gwendolen turned her head away, letting the ring lie untouched. She resented this attempt to strip her motive. Perhaps deeper still was her awareness that she wasn’t marrying solely for her mother’s sake—she was drawn forward by forces stronger than her mother’s sacrifice. She had woken to the reality of her commitment, and now faced the day’s challenge: to meet the fears and doubts of the night under daylight, where they would surely seem less formidable.

“All I want is your happiness, dear,” Mrs. Davilow continued softly. “I won’t say anything to upset you. Will you put on the ring?”

For a moment, Gwendolen didn’t answer. Then, rising with determination—like a rider setting off on horseback, resolved to press on despite inner turmoil—she said, laughing, “I thought the lover was supposed to place the ring himself.”

She slipped it on, tilting her head with a charming gesture. “I know why he sent it,” she added, nodding at her mother.

“Why?”

“He’d rather I put it on than ask me to let him. Aha! He’s very proud. But so am I. We’ll match each other. I’d hate a man who knelt and fawned. Honestly, he’s not repulsive.”

“That’s mild praise, Gwen.”

“No, it’s not—especially for a man,” Gwendolen replied cheerfully. “Now, I must get up and dress. Will you do my hair, mamma?” She drew her mother close, brushing her cheeks against hers. “And please, no more talk of poverty. You must accept comfort—even if you don’t enjoy it. And Mr. Grandcourt behaves perfectly, doesn’t he?”

“Indeed he does,” Mrs. Davilow said, encouraged, believing now that her daughter truly liked her fiancé. She saw him as a man whose attentions could easily influence a young woman’s feelings. Suitors are often judged by their social standing and presence—not much else. And her own concern wasn’t Grandcourt’s character, but whether Gwendolen truly wanted this man.

That morning, Gwendolen’s mood shifted. Even while dressing, she gathered arguments to justify her decision. Most compelling was her plan: once married, she would urge Grandcourt to treat Mrs. Glasher’s children generously.

“What good would it do her if I didn’t marry him? He could have married her if he’d wanted to—but he didn’t. Maybe she’s to blame. There’s much about her I don’t know. And he must have been kind to her in many ways, or she wouldn’t have wanted him.”

Yet even this argument began to falter. Mrs. Glasher naturally wished to exclude other heirs who might divide Grandcourt’s inheritance. Gwendolen’s understanding of this led her to a new justification.

“Perhaps we’ll have no children. I hope we won’t. He might leave the estate to the little boy. Uncle said Grandcourt could do as he pleased with the lands. Only when Sir Hugo dies, there’ll be enough for two.”

This made Mrs. Glasher seem unreasonable in demanding sole inheritance. The dual property offered security—Grandcourt’s marriage would harm no one, especially not when his bride was Gwendolen Harleth, proud and determined not to be accused of wrongdoing. She had always considered herself faultless—others were the ones at fault.

It was striking how firmly this reasoning had taken hold—so much so that her revulsion at Grandcourt’s past had faded into the background. The terror she had felt at night—of crossing into wickedness by doing what she once knew was wrong—had dulled her emotional response to his history. She now saw him not as a man of mystery, but as someone over whom she would wield power. Love had never been a question; whatever charm he possessed was simply an advantage. Poor Gwendolen had no fear of marriage’s uncontrollable forces—she saw it as pure management, something she could control. Regarding his past, she began to wonder if he was really so different from other men. She devised little plans to learn what society expected of men.

Still, one thing remained certain: her hair was perfectly styled for riding. She descended in her riding habit, eager to avoid delay. She craved the rush of youth—the intoxicating courage she once carried through life. Already, a burden had lifted. In daylight and motion, doubts felt lighter than they had in the stillness of night.

“Go back and make yourself look like a duchess, mamma,” she called, turning abruptly on the stairs. “Put your point-lace over your head. I must have you look like a duchess. Don’t be humble.”

When Grandcourt gently raised her left hand and looked at the ring, she said gravely, “It was very kind of you to think of everything and send me that packet.”

“You’ll tell me if I forget anything?” he asked, keeping her hand softly in his. “I’ll do anything you wish.”

“But I’m terribly unreasonable in my wishes,” Gwendolen replied, smiling.

“Yes, I expect that. Women always are.”

“Then I won’t be unreasonable,” she declared, pulling her hand away and tossing her head playfully. “I won’t be told I’m what women always are.”

“I didn’t say that,” Grandcourt said, looking at her with his usual calm. “You are what no other woman is.”

“And what is that, pray?” she asked, stepping back with a hint of challenge.

Grandcourt paused before answering. “You are the woman I love.”

“Oh, how sweet!” Gwendolen laughed. The memory of that love he had once given another woman—under strange circumstances—was becoming familiar.

“Give me a sweet reply. Say when we’ll marry.”

“Not yet. Not until we’ve galloped over the downs. I’m so thirsty for it, I can think of nothing else. Sunday the twentieth… twenty-seventh… Monday… Tuesday…” She counted on her fingers, nodding prettily, then swept one palm over the other triumphantly. “It begins in ten days!”

“Then let’s marry in ten days,” Grandcourt said. “We’ll avoid boredom about the stables.”

“What do women always say in reply?” Gwendolen teased.

“They agree,” he answered, slightly off guard.

“Then I won’t!” she declared, picking up her gauntlets and putting them on, her eyes sparkling with mischief.

The moment was delightful for both. A cruder man might have missed her grace, ruined the mood with clumsy advances. But Grandcourt preferred the drama—and Gwendolen, left at ease, found her spirits rising as she played at ruling. Perhaps if Klesmer had seen more of her in this natural performance—rather than when she tried to act—his opinion of her might have been higher.

After their exhilarating ride, she felt uplifted, eager to hasten the marriage that would make her life a continuous feast of such joy. She refused further debate over a choice she had already made. She agreed to set the wedding in three weeks, despite the challenges of assembling a proper trousseau.

Lush, of course, learned of the engagement through signs, though he was never formally told. Yet he expected some communication—and after a few days, grew impatient. He sensed Grandcourt’s silence meant his own prospects were changing. He didn’t oppose things for their own sake; he might cause annoyance, but it would harm him too. He wouldn’t mind thwarting Miss Gwendolen a little—but still, the future was uncertain. Grandcourt’s odd behavior struck Lush as strangely fatalistic—like a man marked by fate. That someone born to such fortune should mismanage his life seemed tragic. Having protested the marriage, Lush now foresaw its consequences. Grandcourt had taken to writing letters and giving orders personally—no longer relying on Lush. He even chose to breakfast alone in his dressing-room, breaking a long-standing habit.

But a tête-à-tête could not be avoided in an empty house. One evening, after dinner—when Grandcourt had declined to dine at Offendene—Lush seized the moment.

“When is the marriage to take place?”

Grandcourt, who rarely drank wine, had left the table and now lounged in a velvet chair near the hearth, smoking. A fire of oak boughs burned brightly, casting soft ash-glow across the room. The chair framed his pale, finely cut features and elegant hands. Without the cigar, he might have been a Moroni portrait—impenetrable, distinguished. But he answered promptly.

“On the tenth.”

“You intend to stay here?”

“We’ll go to Ryelands briefly, but return for the hunting.”

After this, a low, indistinct sound—common with Grandcourt when he meant to continue—rose from his lips. Lush waited. Nothing followed. He was about to speak again when the sound returned, leading to a quiet suggestion:

“You’d better make new arrangements for yourself.”

“What? I’m to leave?” Lush asked, prepared to be reasonable.

“Something like that.”

“The bride dislikes me. I hope she makes up for my absence.”

“I can’t help that you’re so disagreeable to women,” Grandcourt said, almost apologetically.

“To one woman, if you please.”

“It makes no difference—since she’s the one.”

“I suppose I’m not to be cast aside after fifteen years without support?”

“You must have saved something from me.”

“Very little. I’ve often saved things for you.”

“You can have three hundred a year. But you must live in town and be available when I need you. I’ll be hard up.”

“If you’re not at Ryelands this winter, I could visit and report on Swinton.”

“If you like. I don’t care where you are—just keep out of sight.”

“Much obliged,” Lush replied, taking it easier than expected. He secretly believed he’d be needed again soon.

“Perhaps you’ll pack soon,” Grandcourt said. “The Torringtons are arriving, and Miss Harleth will be riding over.”

“With pleasure. Can I assist with Gadsmere?”

“No. I’m going myself.”

“About being hard up—have you considered that plan—”

“Just leave me alone,” Grandcourt muttered, tossing his cigar into the fire and walking away.

He spent the evening in the smaller drawing-room, surrounded by books—political, comic, cynical, romantic—yet did not read. Instead, he sat meditatively on the sofa, abstaining from literature altogether. Hours passed swiftly, not through thought, but through avoidance of effort. His inner world felt aged—prematurely so—where impulse died before fulfillment, and action became mere memory. This state often comes with gray hair, and sometimes with a stubborn, rigid egoism that grows more prominent as youthful sensibilities fade.

But Grandcourt’s hair, though sparse, was a fine, golden blonde. His mood couldn’t be explained by age alone. Within us mortals, a strange spiritual chemistry unfolds—where laziness or softness may conceal sharp, explosive potential. Like the navvy who wakes and crushes his sleeping comrade without malice, lacking trained motive, so too a gentleman may become unpredictable—raising uncertainty about what he’ll do next, spoiling companionship.

“Surely whoever speaks to me in the right voice, him or her I shall follow. As the water follows the moon, silently, with fluid steps anywhere around the globe.” —WALT WHITMAN.

“Now my cousins are at Diplow,” Grandcourt said, “would you like to go there? Tomorrow? The carriage will come for Mrs. Davilow. You can tell me what you’d like done in the rooms. Things must be arranged properly while we’re away at Ryelands. And tomorrow is the only day.”

He sat sideways on a sofa in the drawing-room at Offendene, one arm draped over the back, the other tucked between his crossed knees—posture of a man deeply absorbed in watching the person beside him. Gwendolen, who had never cared much for needlework, had taken it up since her engagement, now holding a piece of white embroidery that, upon closer inspection, revealed many uneven stitches. For the past week or so, their days had mostly been spent riding together, but always a few hours remained for this quieter companionship—one she found surprisingly agreeable. She was content with Grandcourt. His answers to her eager questions about his travels and experiences were delivered with a calm ease that suited her well. From the start, she’d noticed he knew exactly what to say; and increasingly, she sensed not just that he wasn’t foolish, but that somehow he made her feel that folly belonged elsewhere—among those who did things he didn’t care to do. A man who seems to have had everything within reach possesses a quiet power to belittle others. And Grandcourt’s behavior as a lover had never overstepped the bounds of tender homage—soft as a breath of roses, leaving no trace but a quiet sense of pride. Once, yes, he had kissed not her cheek but the delicate skin just below her ear. Startled, she had jerked upright, heart pounding, making him rise too and murmur, “I beg your pardon—did I disturb you?”
“Oh, nothing,” she replied, trying to sound light, though afraid of herself. “It’s just that I don’t like being kissed there.” She sat again, laughing lightly, but beneath it all, her pulse fluttered with a vague unease. She could no longer treat him as she once had treated poor Rex—flaunting, indifferent. Her reaction had seemed almost flattering to him, and he had not repeated the gesture.

That day, rain kept them from riding. But in compensation, a parcel arrived from London, and Mrs. Davilow had just left after presenting the beautiful items—Grandcourt’s choice—scattered across the tables. Gwendolen, basking in the glow of her present life, let her hands fall into her lap and asked, with a teasing air,

“Why is tomorrow the only day?”

“Because the next day is the first meet with the hounds,” Grandcourt replied.

“And after that?”

“I’ll need to leave for two days—it’s tedious—but I’ll go one day and return the next.” He noticed a shift in her expression, and releasing his hand from under his knees, placed it gently over hers. “You mind my going?”

“It’s no use objecting,” she said, coldly. She fought hard against the urge to confess her suspicion—that he was going to see someone else. To speak plainly, without restraint, would have been both freeing and dangerous.

“Yes, it is,” he said, enfolding her hand. “I’ll delay my trip. And I’ll travel at night—so I’m only gone a single day.” He thought he understood the cause of this small flare-up, and she was especially captivating to him now.

“Then don’t delay,” she said, feeling a sudden surge of control. “Just travel at night.” It was a command, sharp and decisive—a tiny release of irritation.

“So you’ll go to Diplow tomorrow?”

“Oh, yes—if you wish,” she answered, her tone casual, almost dismissive. Her focus was elsewhere; she hadn’t even realized her hand was still in his.

“How you treat us poor men!” Grandcourt murmured, lowering his voice. “We always end up getting the short end.”

“_Are_ you?” she asked, looking at him more honestly than usual. She longed to believe this banter was true—her lover truly suffering. If so, then she, too, was justified. If she knew everything, Mrs. Glasher might seem more to blame than Grandcourt. “_Are_ you always getting the worst?”

“Yes,” he said, gazing into her eyes. “Are you as kind to me as I am to you?”

Gwendolen felt struck. She was aware of how much she had received—how much he had given—and her sense of power faded. She saw now that no matter how she looked, she couldn’t turn back. It was as if she had climbed into a chariot where another held the reins. And it was not in her nature to leap out before the world. She had not entered blindly; any words she spoke now would be an admission she had known better. Her right to question was gone. All she could do was adjust—so that the thorns of conscience wouldn’t wound her. With a quiet shiver, she shifted her inner stance. There had been a brief silence, during which she had not looked away. Then, breaking into a smile, she said:

“If I were as kind to you as you are to me, it would ruin your generosity. It wouldn’t be as great as it is now—and it *is* great.”

“Then I may not ask for one kiss?” Grandcourt asked, pleased to pay such a high price for this new form of affection—this love that led quietly toward marriage.

“Not one?” she teased, nodding at him with defiance.

He lifted her small left hand to his lips, then released it with respect. To say he was not repulsive was faint praise—he was nearly charming. And in that moment, she felt sure she could never love another man as deeply as this one. His reserve stirred something inexplicable, delightful within her.

“Apropos,” she said, picking up her work again, “is anyone besides Captain and Mrs. Torrington at Diplow? Or are they alone? I suppose he talks in cigars, and she replies with her chignon.”

“She has a sister,” Grandcourt said, smiling faintly. “And two men besides—one you know, I think.”

“Oh,” she said, shaking her head. “Then I have a low opinion of him.”

“You saw him at Leubronn—young Deronda. A young fellow with the Mallingers.”

Gwendolen felt her heart give a sudden leap. Her fingers, clutching her embroidery, turned cold.

“I never spoke to him,” she said, careful not to betray herself. “Is he unpleasant?”

“No, not really,” Grandcourt replied, in his usual languid tone. “He thinks too highly of himself. I thought he’d been introduced to you.”

“No. Someone told me his name the evening before I left. That was all. What is he?”

“A sort of ward of Sir Hugo Mallinger’s. Nothing important.”

“Oh, poor thing! How terribly sad for him!” she said, half-joking, without meaning to mock. “I wonder if the rain has stopped,” she added, rising to look out the window.

Fortunately, it had cleared by the next day, and Gwendolen rode to Diplow on Criterion—the same horse she’d ridden when returning with her mother in the carriage. She always felt bolder in riding dress, and liked to believe she looked her best in it—a comforting assurance when facing something daunting. Her anger toward Deronda had softened into a strange dread—perhaps because of the way he had influenced her thoughts—fearing that his first intrusion into her life might foreshadow a deeper influence. Such feelings often birth superstitions: intense emotions about ourselves that make the stars seem to warn us, or a beggar’s blessing feel like prophecy. And superstitions bring consequences that often confirm their fears or hopes.

Before luncheon, Gwendolen toured the rooms with Mrs. Torrington and Mrs. Davilow. She assumed, if she saw Deronda, there would be little more than a polite bow between them. She meant to ignore him completely.

Yet, despite herself, she found she was drawn to him. From the moment they were in the same room, she felt herself doing nothing but notice him—everything else felt automatic, routine.

At lunch, Grandcourt said, “Deronda, Miss Harleth tells me you weren’t introduced to her at Leubronn?”

“Miss Harleth hardly remembers me,” Deronda replied, looking at her simply as they bowed. “She was very absorbed when I saw her.”

Had he known she suspected him of retrieving her necklace?

“On the contrary,” Gwendolen said, nerves prickling but her composure intact. “I remember you well. You disapproved of my playing roulette.”

“How did you come to that conclusion?” he asked, gravely.

“Oh, you cast an evil eye on my game,” she said, turning her head with a smile. “I started losing the moment you came near. Before that, I’d been winning.”

“Roulette in a place like Leubronn is sheer boredom,” Grandcourt remarked.

“_I_ found it boring when I began to lose,” Gwendolen said, glancing at him, then quickly at Deronda. She saw his eyes fixed on her—not with mockery, but with a depth that pierced her more than Klesmer’s criticism ever had. She turned her head sharply as if listening to others, though she was thinking only of him. His face carried that unsettling quality—something that challenged judgment, as if one’s own standards were flawed. (Who hasn’t met someone whose presence subtly undermines belief?) His voice, heard for the first time, contrasted sharply with Grandcourt’s flat drawl—like the deep tones of a cello compared to idle chatter in the sunlit afternoon. Perhaps Grandcourt was right in saying Deronda thought too much of himself—just a way of dismissing a superiority that humbles. But the conversation turned to rinderpest and Jamaica, and the subject of roulette passed.

Grandcourt declared the Jamaican negro a beastly sort of Caliban; Deronda said he always sympathized with Caliban, who naturally had his own view and could sing a good song. Mrs. Davilow mentioned her father had an estate in Barbados, though she’d never been to the West Indies. Mrs. Torrington insisted she’d never sleep among blacks. Her husband corrected her: “They’d be manageable enough if it weren’t for the half-breeds.” Deronda added, “The whites have only themselves to blame for the half-breeds.”

While these polite exchanges flowed, Gwendolen played with her jelly, watching each speaker so she could feel safe looking at Deronda.

“What does he really think of me?” she wondered. “He must have been interested—I mean, why else would he send back my necklace? What does he think of my marriage? What makes him so serious? Why is he here?”

These questions swirled in her mind, born of an uneasy longing to be admired by him—unmixed admiration. This desire had begun with her resentment of his critical gaze. Why did she care so much about a man who was “nothing of consequence”? She had no time to find the answer—she was too caught up in caring.

Later, in the drawing-room, when Grandcourt stepped away, she walked unthinkingly to Deronda, who stood alone at a table, flipping through prints.

“Will you hunt tomorrow, Mr. Deronda?” she asked.

“Yes, I believe so.”

“You don’t mind hunting?”

“I find reasons to enjoy it. It’s a sin I’m prone to—when I can’t get boating or cricket.”

“Do you mind my hunting?” she asked, tilting her chin saucily.

“I have no right to object to anything you choose.”

“You thought you had a right to object to my gambling,” she persisted.

“I was sorry for it. I don’t recall telling you I objected,” he said, his gaze steady, large-eyed and grave—innocent of intent. His eyes had a peculiar power—dark yet gentle, conveying a special interest in whoever he looked at. They could easily inspire demands, as if kindness were a reason for excessive claims. And this effect was beginning to stir in Gwendolen.

“You stopped me from gambling again,” she said. But the moment she spoke, her cheeks flushed. Deronda blushed too, aware of the questionable freedom he’d taken with the necklace.

No further words could be exchanged. She turned to the window, ashamed of having said too much, yet strangely glad she had stepped into this quiet understanding. Deronda, too, welcomed it. Gwendolen seemed more compelling than before. And surely, changes had occurred in her since Leubりnn—the struggle of realizing her mistake had awakened something new, something more complex than her old confidence: a soul stirring, with both nobler and darker possibilities. Among the forces she now feared was something inside herself that disturbed peace.

That evening, Mrs. Davilow asked, “Was it really true, or just a joke, about Mr. Deronda spoiling your play, Gwen?”

Her curiosity was piqued, and she dared ask a question not involving Grandcourt.

“Oh, it just happened that he was watching when I started losing,” Gwendolen said casually. “I noticed him.”

“I wouldn’t be surprised,” Mrs. Davilow said. “He’s a striking young man. Reminds me of Italian paintings. One could guess, without being told, that he has foreign blood.”

“Does he?”

“Mrs. Torrington says so. I asked who he was, and she said his mother was some foreign noblewoman.”

“His mother?” Gwendolen asked sharply. “Then who was his father?”

“Well—everyone says he’s Sir Hugo Mallinger’s son, raised by him. Though he’s called a ward. She adds that if Sir Hugo had his way, he’d have left his estates to Mr. Deronda, since he has no legitimate heir.”

Gwendolen said nothing. But her mother saw the change in her face and regretted speaking. It seemed inappropriate for her daughter, whom she disliked calling worldly. And indeed, she wished she hadn’t known such things herself.

An image formed instantly in Gwendolen’s mind: the unknown mother—no doubt dark-eyed, perhaps sorrowful. Hardly any face could be less like Deronda’s than the portrait of Sir Hugo at Diplow. A dark-eyed woman, no longer young, became a figure in her conscience.

That night, lying in her dimly lit bed, she whispered,
“Mamma, do men usually have children before marriage?”

“No, dear,” Mrs. Davilow replied. “Why do you ask?”

“If it were true, I ought to know,” Gwendolen said, indignantly.

“You’re thinking of what I said about Mr. Deronda and Sir Hugo. That’s very unusual, dear.”

“Does Lady Mallinger know?”

“She knows enough to be satisfied. That’s clear, because Mr. Deronda has lived with them.”

“And people don’t think worse of him?”

“Well, of course he’s at a disadvantage. It’s not as if he were Lady Mallinger’s son. He doesn’t inherit, and isn’t influential. But people don’t need to know. He’s well received.”

“I wonder if he knows. And whether he’s angry with his father?”

“My dear, why would you think that?”

“Why?” Gwendolen asked, sitting up abruptly. “Don’t children have reason to resent their parents? How can they help their parents marrying or not marrying?”

But then a sudden realization struck her—she fell back onto her pillow. It wasn’t just the old fear of reproaching her mother for her second marriage. Now she felt something deeper: that her own marriage seemed tainted, forbidden—by the very story she’d just heard.

There was no more talk. Until sleep claimed her, Gwendolen wrestled with arguments against her marriage—arguments newly urgent, mirrored in the tale of a man whose fleeting connection with her had somehow rooted itself in her deepest feelings. Characteristically, she never questioned whether her marriage was indefensible because she’d chosen Grandcourt not for love, but for convenience—not as a duty-bound partner, but as a means to an end. Gwendolen’s ideas were painfully simple; yet many of life’s greatest dilemmas press upon us in our simplicity. And to judge wisely, perhaps we must understand how things appear to the unwise—because that appearance shapes most of history.

The next morning brought double excitement. She was going to hunt—once threatened by propriety, until Mrs. Torrington proved capable enough to ride with her—her first hunt since her escapade with Rex. And she would see Deronda again. Since the previous night, her interest in him had grown, and she expected to see something new in him—something she’d missed before.

What would he become? What life awaited him—though he was “nothing of consequence”? With only slight differences, he might have been as powerful as Grandcourt—even, her imagination ran, holding the very estates Grandcourt would inherit. Yet now, Deronda might someday see her mistress of Topping Abbey, bearing the title that should have been his wife’s. These futile speculations about what might have been marked a turning point for Gwendolen. She, who had always taken the best as her due, now saw her position differently—as a harsh, unjust exclusion of others. The news about Deronda seemed to group him with Mrs. Glasher and her children—before whom she now felt a need to apologize—she who had once been surrounded by people who owed her apologies.

Perhaps Deronda was thinking of these things too. Could he know of Mrs. Glasher? If he knew she knew, he’d despise her. But he couldn’t possibly. Would he despise her for marrying Grandcourt? His possible judgment weighed on her as heavily as Klesmer’s critique of her talent. Yet she found more resistance to disapproval of her marriage—because it’s easier to justify our actions than to impress others with our ability. “How can I help it?” is not our favorite excuse for failure. But Gwendolen found strength in saying:

“How can I help what others have done? Things wouldn’t be right if I turned back now and refused to marry Mr. Grandcourt.” And turning back was impossible. The horses in the chariot she’d boarded were racing forward.

This mood of youthful desperation returned like a tide. She could face anything ahead rather than retreat into shame. And it was oddly comforting to think that harm lay equally in either path. But the immediate joy was the hunt—where she would see Deronda, and he would see her. Always lurking behind other thoughts was the impression that he was deeply interested in her. Yet today, she resolved not to repeat yesterday’s mistake—no eager words, no seeking contact. The hunt would be absorbing enough.

And so it was—for a long while. Deronda was there, often within sight, but this only heightened the pleasure—something Gwendolen had tasted only once before, and which seemed to promise delight beyond any obstacle except the loss of riding itself. No accident brought them together. The run ended near home, and as the gray November afternoon settled into a soft yellow glow in the west, Gwendolen returned with the party from Diplow, heading toward Offendene. The thrill had passed, and she grew irritable—disappointed she’d had no chance to speak to Deronda, whom she wouldn’t see again, since he was leaving in two days. What would she say? She wasn’t sure. But she needed to speak to him.

Grandcourt rode beside her; Mrs. Torrington, her husband, and another gentleman ahead; and Deronda’s horse could be heard behind. The desire to talk to him—to hear him speak—was becoming overwhelming. Unless she simply asserted her will and defied convention, there would be no chance. Where Miss Gwendolen willed it, order must yield. They had just emerged from a pine and beech wood, where twilight pressed down heavily, increasing her impatience. The hoofbeats behind, growing louder, became an irritation. She reined in her horse and glanced back. Grandcourt paused after a few steps. But she waved her whip, nodded sideways with playful authority, and said:

“Go on! I want to speak to Mr. Deronda.”

No confession, no absolution—no priest to guide or forgive. Yet they sit, burdened by the ashes of years that have burned away all hope.

Picture a sprawling, uneven house, its finest section built from gray stone and topped with red tiles, a round tower jutting out at one corner, its conical roof darkened by time and crowned with a weather-vane that catches the eye whether summer sparkles through the trees or winter’s clouds hang low over snow-laden branches. The ground is shaded by wide-spreading oaks; on one side, a great tree thrives near a broken bank where Scotch firs lean, their roots exposed like pale fingers clutching at the earth, beyond which lies a rookery. On the other side, a pool edged with thick bushes, where ducks flutter and cry. Around it all stretches a vast meadow—almost a park—bordered by an old wood and guarded by stone lodges that resemble small prisons. Beyond the gate, the countryside once peaceful and green now bears the scars of coal mines, blackened and desolate. Here, men walk with candles stuck in their hats, faces grim and sullen, their presence a constant source of unease for the children of Gadsmere—Mrs. Glasher’s four beautiful children, who had lived here for nearly three years.

In November, when flowerbeds are bare, trees stripped of leaves, and the pool shivers under a cold sky, the place seems to mirror the bleakness of the land around it—except when the children play on the gravel, dogs bounding beside them. But Mrs. Glasher, in her present state, found peace in Gadsmere as she might have in any retreat. Its isolation, ensured by the unappealing landscape, suited her perfectly. When she drove her two ponies pulling a waggonet full of children, she met no aristocrats in carriages—only businessmen in gigs. At church, no eyes watched her with suspicion; the curate and his wife either knew nothing of her past or chose to ignore it. To them, she was simply a widow, tenant of Gadsmere. The name Grandcourt meant little here compared to Fletcher or Gawcome, the colliery lessees.

Ten years had passed since the elopement of an Irish officer’s beautiful wife with young Grandcourt, followed by a duel that left only empty air wounded. That affair had stirred some talk, but now most remembered it only vaguely. Where was that Mrs. Glasher? Once famed for her beauty and charm, known abroad as the companion of Grandcourt, she had vanished from memory.

It seemed natural enough that Grandcourt would leave her behind. A man of his rank and taste could not be expected to remain bound to a woman who had abandoned her child along with her husband. It was assumed she had slipped further into obscurity. Grandcourt, after all, was known for his pursuit of women. Now, he was likely seeking a suitable marriage—perhaps with a noble daughter, fair and young. No one spoke of Mrs. Glasher anymore. She was like a ship lost at sea, forgotten, unrecovered. But Grandcourt remained visible—returning to harbor, flags flying, declared seaworthy as ever.

Yet, in truth, Grandcourt had never freed himself from her. His passion for her had been deeper, more enduring than any other. Though now extinguished—like music from a cracked flute—it still left behind a dull ache. Three years earlier, when her husband died, Grandcourt had briefly considered marrying her, echoing a promise made during the early days of their affair. Then, he had been willing to pay for a divorce, but her husband refused—unwilling to remarry and unwilling to expose his private life in court.

Mrs. Glasher, however, had changed. At first, she cared little about marriage. Escape from a harsh husband, the intoxication of love with a young, handsome, passionate man—these were enough. She had been vivacious, craving admiration, worn down by five years of marital neglect. Freedom was sweet, and she didn’t mind the uncertainty of her position. She saw no value in being a conventional wife, overlooked and dull. The only shadow on her joy was the thought of leaving her three-year-old son—though he died two years later. His voice calling “mamma” remained distinct in her memory, different from the sounds of the children who came after.

But time had altered her. The lines of her face deepened, her body softened. And now, the idea of Grandcourt marrying her had become her deepest desire. What once mattered little now weighed heavily: the dignity of her children, the future they deserved. Her love for him had transformed into a fierce need for legitimacy—not for herself alone, but for them. If he married her, they would be secure. Her son could inherit his father’s estate. That dream gave her life purpose. She longed only for this: to see her children raised in respect, her own worth affirmed before them. Marriage to Grandcourt wasn’t just a wish—it was the only happiness she could imagine. For that, she was ready to endure anything quietly, even silence and pain. She understood his temper—how he hardened under pleading, how his moods shifted unpredictably. So she kept her feelings hidden, refusing to beg, to make scenes. Reticence cost her dearly. There was venom growing in her silence, bitter and slow.

She was utterly dependent on him. He had always paid for her upkeep, but never made it permanent. Everything remained voluntary. Still, she would accept nothing less than marriage. He had said he would never settle property until death—by will only. And sometimes, when thinking of the future, she wondered: if he never had a son, perhaps hers could inherit. At that age, no child could yet claim the strength of his father. But becoming his wife was not a fantasy—Lush himself had once believed it possible. In fact, Lush had encouraged Grandcourt’s interest in Miss Arrowpoint, seeing it as a sign that Grandcourt had given up on Mrs. Glasher. But both plans collapsed with Gwendolen’s arrival. And so Mrs. Glasher eagerly embraced Lush’s scheme—to warn Gwendolen against Grandcourt, to build a barrier between them. She entered it with fervor, passion mingled with purpose, letting some of her pent-up bitterness spill forth.

Afterward, she heard from Lush that Gwendolen had left—and that the danger was gone. But she received no word that the threat had returned. She wrote to Grandcourt, as she always did. He took longer than usual to reply. She began to hope—what else is hope but a bold interpretation of another’s thoughts? Perhaps he, too, felt tired of that failed pursuit and would return to the old path.

Grandcourt came to Gadsmere with two purposes: to tell her personally of his impending marriage, making the end final; and to reclaim the diamonds he had entrusted to her years ago, meant for his bride. They were not large stones—small peas and beans for ears, neck, hair—but valuable. He wanted them for Gwendolen. Years before, when he asked her to return them, saying they’d be safer in a bank, she refused. “If you marry another woman,” she said, “I’ll give them to her. Are you going to marry another?” At the time, Grandcourt had no reason to press. He had a certain grace—he rarely imposed his will on her, though others might say it was because she was helpless, a constant reminder of his power. But there was more: a lingering echo of the influence she once held over him. That faint pull toward the familiar, toward what had once brought him joy, had often made him hesitate before new ventures. But now, novelty had seized him. He wanted to move forward.

Mrs. Glasher sat in her favorite room, where mornings passed with her children gathered close. The window faced broad gravel and grass sloping to a brook feeding the pool. An old oak table, tawny leather chairs, a low black cabinet—all littered with toys, books, garden clothes. Above, a portrait of a gentle woman in pastel smiled indulgently down. The children were all there. The three girls, seated near her by the window, were tiny reflections of her—dark-eyed, delicate-faced brunettes with rich color in their cheeks, finely shaped brows and nostrils, each one a miniature woman. The eldest was barely nine. The boy, Henleigh, sat apart on the carpet, bent over animals from a Noah’s ark, commanding them sharply, occasionally licking the spotted ones to test the paint.

Josephine was having her French lesson. The others sat still, dolls in their laps, solemn as images of the Madonna. Mrs. Glasher had dressed carefully—each day she told herself Grandcourt might come. Her head, though thinner, still held a striking beauty: sharp profile, crisp curls, defined eyebrows. She wore bronze silk and velvet, a gold necklace Grandcourt had placed around her neck years ago. Not that she enjoyed dressing—the reflection in the mirror reminded her only of how much she had changed. But she preserved what she could. If her wish were granted, she imagined herself one day looking like a matron fit for high society. The children’s faces, so like her own, seemed to whisper of the bloom she once had, now replaced by pallor. But they kissed her cheeks without hesitation. Their love was now her only goal.

Suddenly, she turned from Josephine’s book and listened. “Hush, dear. I think someone is coming.”

Henleigh jumped up. “Mamma, is it the miller with my donkey?”

He got no answer. He moved to her knee, repeating the question insistently. But the door opened, and the servant announced Mr. Grandcourt.

Mrs. Glasher rose, agitated. Henleigh frowned—disappointed it wasn’t the miller. The girls lifted their dark eyes to him, timid. None of them truly liked this visitor. When Grandcourt took her hand and then touched Henleigh’s head, the boy struck at his arm with his fists. The girls submitted to kisses and pats, but soon were sent outside, dancing and chatting with the dogs on the gravel.

“How far have you come?” she asked as he removed his hat and coat.

“From Diplow,” he replied slowly, seating himself opposite her, gazing at her without focus—though she noticed every glance.

“You’re tired?”

“No. I rested at the Junction—a hideous place. Railways are always a bore. But I had coffee and smoked.”

He pulled out his handkerchief, wiped his face, tucked it back in, glancing at his crossed knee and polished boot as if addressing a stranger. But she trembled, every word, every look a potential key to hope or despair. He was already planning the conversation, calculating what would follow. Imagine the contrast: her, worn by time into sharper longing, more aware of dependence; him, dulled into stubborn neutrality.

“I expected you,” she said. “It’s been so long since I heard from you. Do the weeks seem longer at Gadsmere than at Diplow?”

“Yes,” he drawled. “But you received the money in the bank.”

“Oh, yes,” she answered curtly, tingling with impatience. Always before—she thought—Grandcourt had shown more interest in her, in the children. Today, he seemed distant.

“Yes,” he said, playing with his whisker, not looking at her at first. “Time has flown for me—usually it drags. But a lot has happened, as you know.” Then he looked at her.

“What do I know?” she asked sharply.

He paused. Then, without changing tone: “That I was thinking of marrying. You saw Miss Harleth?”

“She told you that?”

Her cheeks grew paler, perhaps from the fire in her eyes above.

“No,” he said slowly. “Lush told me.” It was like watching hands creep in with thumb-screws and iron boots, within sight of the victim.

“Good God,” she burst out, “say at once that you’re going to marry her!” Her knees shook, her hands clenched tightly.

“Of course, these things happen,” he said, not wanting to prolong the pain. “Lydia, it’s inevitable.”

“You didn’t always see it as such.”

“Perhaps not. I do now.”

In those quiet words, she felt absolute resistance—as if pushing against a locked iron door. She knew her helplessness. She couldn’t plead, couldn’t cry, couldn’t cling—only feel the immovable face, the rigid limbs. She didn’t weep. She didn’t speak. She was overwhelmed by sudden certainty—cold, sickening, heavier than thought or emotion. The collapse of hope left her with a horror. At last, she rose, spasmodically, pressed her forehead against the cold glass of the window. The children, playing outside, ran forward, faces upturned, expecting her attention. She shook her head, waved them away, and sank into the nearest chair, overcome.

Grandcourt had risen too. He was annoyed—both by the scene and by the feeling that nothing he could do would prevent it. But the task remained: to arrange things so that future discomfort was minimized. He leaned against the fireplace. She looked up.

“All this means nothing to you,” she said bitterly. “Me and the children—we’re nuisances. You just want to go back to Miss Harleth.”

“Don’t make this worse than it needs to be,” he said. “Lydia, it’s no use dwelling on what can’t be changed. Of course, it’s unpleasant to see you suffering. I came to tell you what you must accept—you and the children will be provided for as before. That’s all.”

Silence. She dared not respond. This woman, once vibrant, now carried the steel of maternal grief. She could suppress pain better than scream. But beneath the silence, hatred burned. She wished the marriage would bring misery to others—two others, besides herself.
