He continued. “It will be better for you. You can stay here. I’ll settle a good sum on you and the children. You can live wherever you like. Nothing to complain of. Things moved quickly. No time for preparation.”

He stopped. He didn’t expect gratitude. He thought she might accept it. But she showed no change. After a moment, he said:

“You’ve never had reason to fear I’d be stingy. I don’t care about the money.”

“If you did care,” she said, sarcasm slipping out, “you wouldn’t give it to us.”

“That’s unfair,” he replied, voice lower. “And I advise you not to say things like that again.”

“Would you punish me by leaving the children in poverty?” The venom escaped, despite her control.

“There’s no question of that,” he said firmly. “I advise you not to say things you’ll regret.”

“I’m used to regretting,” she said. “Perhaps you’ll regret. You’ve already regretted loving me.”

“This only makes it harder for us to meet again. Who else do you have?”

“Quite true.”

The words came like a low moan. At that moment, she wished he might, after promising a better life, find himself lonely and wretched—driven back to her, searching for memories of youth and joy. But no—he would go untouched. Only she would suffer.

The words ended. Grandcourt had planned to stay until evening, but now he wanted to leave. No earlier train was available. He still needed to speak to her about the second matter—like a second surgery, requiring space. Time dragged on. Meals were eaten. The children came in. Life went on, stiff and constrained, like any household quarrel. For Lydia, the children’s presence was a relief—a savage pride in their beauty, a silent taunt to Grandcourt’s indifference. He handled Antonia gently, promised Henleigh a fine saddle and bridle. Only the older girls remembered him clearly—now shy, intimidated by his demeanor. They exchanged only polite remarks in front of servants. Otherwise, silence. Grandcourt’s mind wandered to the diamonds—why had he given them to her? It was a nuisance. He valued his caste, his possessions. He disliked asking for anything. Yet, he had created a past that now bound him. He must ask for what he had promised.

At last, they were alone, candles lit, facing each other. He glanced at his watch, then said, casually,

“There’s one thing I had to mention, Lydia. My diamonds—you have them.”

“Yes,” she answered, rising, arms folded, fingers laced. She had expected this. She had decided how to handle it. She would not provoke him. During the silence, she had wished she could take back the words that widened the chasm.

“They’re in the house?”

“No. Not in the house.”

“I thought you said you kept them close.”

She stood still. The moment hung.

CHAPTER XXXI. “A wild dedication of yourselves
To unpath’d waters, undreamed shores.” —SHAKESPEARE.

The morning Gwendolen Harleth became Mrs. Grandcourt was sharp and clear, the sun low in the sky, casting a pale gold over the frost-kissed leaves. The bridal party moved through Pennicote like a vision—bright, solemn, and stirring enough to draw every soul from their doors. Half the village lined the path to the church, eyes wide with admiration, breath held for the spectacle. The rector, acting as father-in-law by proxy, added dignity to the procession, while an old friend officiated at the marriage rites. Only two faces carried shadows: Mrs. Davilow’s, soft and pink around the eyes as if tears had been her companion through the night, and Anna’s, whose quiet sorrow puzzled those who saw her in such a radiant bridesmaid’s gown.

Everyone else seemed wrapped in the joy of the occasion—especially the bride. She stood tall and proud, a figure that could have belonged to any noble house. Her carriage was regal, her presence commanding. If her complexion lacked the bloom of youth, few minded—Grandcourt himself was not flushed with health, his pallor matching hers in quiet harmony. As the miller’s wife remarked, “He’s much of her husband’s complexion,” and so the union seemed complete, even inevitable.

“Surely he must love her deeply,” people whispered. “And may he never remind her she once thought of becoming a governess—or that her mother lived at Sawyer’s Cottage.” Such talk had stirred the village, but today it was buried beneath the glitter of silk and satin.

The miller’s daughter, only fourteen, couldn’t imagine how high-born men might mistreat their wives. But her mother, seasoned by years of gossip and experience, leaned in and said, “Oh, child, men are men—gentle or plain, they’re all much the same. I’ve heard my own mother say Squire Pelton used to bring his dogs and a long whip into his wife’s chamber, just to frighten her. And I was there, serving as lady’s-maid.”

“That’s ill luck for a wedding day,” the tailor interjected. “A quarrel starts with words, not whips. And women? They’ve got the tongue.”

“The Lord gave it to them,” Mrs. Girdle replied, “so they should use it. He never meant you to have it all your way.”

“And from what the groomsmen at Offendene tell me,” the tailor added, “Mr. Grandcourt speaks little. Everything seems to happen without him saying a word.”

“Then he’s the more dangerous,” Mrs. Girdle muttered. “She’s got tongue enough, I’ll wager. Look—here they come!”

“See how long her eyelashes are!” the tailor murmured. “She makes you feel odd when she looks at you.”

Indeed, Gwendolen had never looked more alive—her eyes bright, her step light, her spirit electric. It wasn’t pain she felt now, though her conscience had stirred in recent weeks. No, this was something else: a fierce, exhilarating rush, like the moment before losing at cards when Deronda watched her, breathless. She ignored the unease within, treating it like a feverish ache one endures for pleasure. Ambition, vanity, the hunger for luxury—they were too strong to be poisoned by guilt.

This morning, she could not truthfully say she regretted marrying Grandcourt. Fear did not cloud her joy—not yet. The scene around her was dazzling, and she was its center. That she was doing something wrong—that punishment might follow—that Lydia Glasher, the woman she had promised to wait for, was suffering in silence—that Deronda likely despised her for this choice—these thoughts had haunted her during the weeks of engagement. But now? Now they were buried under defiance, swept aside by the intoxication of being the heroine of her own grand story.

She walked into the future not with trembling, but with fire in her veins. She was entering a world of power—marriage, title, wealth—where she would command, not submit. The egoism that had once shaken her in shame had returned, stronger than before, fed by triumph and expectation.

She did not look like a weeping bride. Some had called her too worldly, too forward. But now, with her head high and her steps springing, she walked among illusions—and yet, deep down, she knew she was slightly drunk on her own glory.

“Thank God you bear it so well, my darling,” Mrs. Davilow whispered, helping her remove the bridal veil and slip into her traveling dress.

All the tremors had been hers—the mother’s hands shook as she touched her daughter’s shoulders. Gwendolen, sensing her distress, pushed back the sorrow with laughter and warmth.

“If I’d been going to Mrs. Mompert’s, you dear, sad, incorrigible mamma, you’d have said the same thing!” she teased, brushing her fingers gently over her mother’s cheeks. Then, stepping back with arms outstretched, she declared, “Here I am—Mrs. Grandcourt! What else could I be? You know you nearly died of grief when you thought I wouldn’t become her.”

“Hush, hush, my child,” Mrs. Davilow breathed, almost inaudibly. “How can I help feeling it? I’m parting from you. But if you’re happy, I can bear anything.”

“No, mamma,” Gwendolen smiled, shaking her head. “You’d bear it willingly—but always with sorrow. Sorrow is your spice; you taste nothing without it.”

She clasped her mother’s shoulders, showering kisses on each cheek between words. “And you’ll sorrow over me having everything at my command—everything glorious—houses, horses, diamonds—I shall have diamonds—going to court—being Lady Certainly, Lady Perhaps—grand here, tantivy there—and always loving you better than anyone in the world.”

“My sweet child,” Mrs. Davilow whispered. “But I won’t be jealous if you love your husband more. He will expect to be first.”

Gwendolen pouted playfully. “A ridiculous expectation. But I won’t treat him ill… unless he deserves it.”

They clung together, and then, despite herself, Gwendolen sobbed softly. “I wish you were coming with me, mamma.”

A tear traced her lashes—but only made her more radiant as she offered her hand to Grandcourt, ready to step into the carriage.

The rector came to bid farewell. “Good-bye, God bless you. We’ll see you again soon.” Then, turning to Mrs. Davilow, he said, half smiling, half solemn, “Let us be thankful, Fanny. She is where she belongs—beyond what I ever dared hope. Few women are chosen so entirely for themselves. You should be a happy mother.”

***

The journey to Ryelands took fifty miles by rail. By the time they arrived, twilight had settled over the land. Still, Gwendolen, gazing from the carriage window, caught glimpses of the estate’s grandeur—the winding drive flanked by evergreens, towering oaks, open lawns dotted with dark trees, and finally, the white mansion rising against a forested hillside, its terrace curving like a wave.

She had chattered all the way, ignoring the shift in their roles, as if yesterday’s vows were no more than a dream. Grandcourt remained quiet, content, his hand gently clasping hers—until she turned it into a tight grip, pulling him closer, restless and vibrant, like a kitten refusing to sit still.

Her excitement was growing febrile. The new surroundings, the shifting light, the nearness of fulfillment—it all sent her heart racing. Was it just the thrill of becoming someone important? Of walking through her own corridors, under ceilings high beyond reach, where painted gods and nymphs watched her pass? Of servants bowing to her whims, of being the star of a drama without the effort of acting?

Or was it something deeper—a whisper of dread beneath the triumph? A sense that this moment, so perfect, might also be the beginning of ruin?

Her voice fell silent as the gates loomed. When Grandcourt said, “Here we are at home!” and kissed her lips for the first time, she barely registered it—only absorbed the spectacle of arrival.

After the whirlwind of the day, a numbness settled over her. Yet inside, the world was blazing.

Inside the hall, warmth greeted her—carpets, portraits, statues, servants moving with precision. Not many staff—just a few from Diplow, and her own maid, now being shown to her duties by the housekeeper.

Grandcourt led her through scented corridors into a boudoir glowing with light and color. “These are our dens,” he said. “You may rest here until dinner. We dine early.”

He pressed her hand to his lips, then stepped back—more in love than he had ever believed possible.

Gwendolen removed her hat and cloak, sank into a chair beside the hearth, and stared at her reflection in the glass panels—herself repeated in soft green satin, framed by flickering flames.

The housekeeper lingered, perhaps curious about the new mistress. But Gwendolen, eager for solitude, said sharply, “Tell Hudson to leave everything after she removes my dress. I won’t need her again—unless I ring.”

The housekeeper approached. “Madam, here is a packet. I was instructed to give it only to you, when you were alone. The messenger said it was a gift from Mr. Grandcourt—something he wished you to wear before he knew of it. I felt it right to obey orders.”

Gwendolen took the package and let it rest on her lap until the door closed behind the woman.

A thought struck her—diamonds. Grandcourt had spoken of them, stored away, to be given on their wedding day. This must be it.

She peeled back the layers—paper, box, then a jewel case. Yes—diamonds. But as she opened the case, her breath caught.

Lying atop the gems was a letter.

She recognized the handwriting.

It was as if a snake had coiled itself upon the jewels.

Her heart leapt—then failed. Her hands shook as she unfolded the thin paper.

The words were clear, sharp as ice:

*These diamonds were once given with ardent love to Lydia Glasher. She passes them on to you. You broke your word to her, so you might possess what was hers. Perhaps you think you’ll be happy, as she once was, and have beautiful children who will push hers aside. But God is too just for that. The man you married has a withered heart. His best love was mine. You could not take that from me, even when you took the rest. It is dead. But I am the grave where your chance of happiness lies buried—as mine is. You had your warning. You chose to hurt me and my children. He meant to marry me. He would have, if you had not broken your word. You will be punished. I desire it with all my soul.*

Will you give him this letter to turn him against me? Will you stand before him with these diamonds on your neck, knowing these words haunt both of you? Will he believe you have the right to complain when he makes you miserable? You took him with your eyes open. The wrong you’ve done me will be your curse.

At first, Gwendolen’s eyes were fixed, reading the words over and over—like a sentence of doom. Then, a sudden terror seized her. She lunged forward, reaching toward the fire, desperate to destroy the proof before it could be seen.

The letter flew from her trembling fingers, caught in the draft, and vanished into the flames.

As she moved, the casket slipped from her lap. Diamonds scattered across the floor.

She collapsed back into the chair, motionless, helpless.

She could not see her reflections now—only ghostly images, frozen white. But close up, you might have seen her lips quiver, her hands tremble.

She sat like that—long minutes passing—knowing only that she was ill, and that those words echoed in her mind.

Poisoned gems. And the poison had entered her.

Then—knock at the door.

Grandcourt entered, dressed for dinner.

The sight of him sent another shock through her. She screamed—again and again—hysterical, raw.

He had expected a smiling wife, ready to descend. Instead, he found her pale, shrieking, jewels strewn at her feet.

Was it madness?

In some form or other, the furies had crossed his threshold.

Throughout history, love has often been described as a force beyond reason—a kind of fate that overwhelms the mind, rendering logic and resolve powerless. Think of Daphnis, swept away by passion despite his understanding of Heraclitus’s wisdom; or Tristan, whose heart was undone not by philosophy but by a potion no scholar could resist; or Romeo, who in the moonlight beneath Juliet’s balcony forgot all argument against Ptolemy’s cosmos. Yet not all love is so blind. Even when powerful, it may coexist with thought, imagination, and moral conviction. It can be shaped by dreams of distant stars, stirred by visions of what once was and what might yet be—drawing strength from memory and hope alike.

When Deronda returned to London, he told Sir Hugo he had planted a clear idea in Grandcourt’s mind: that fifty thousand pounds could be gained by relinquishing a future claim, uncertain though it was. But beyond this, there was no sign of Grandcourt’s true feelings—only a continued willingness to keep their acquaintance warm.

“And what did you think of the bride-to-be upon closer inspection?” asked Sir Hugo.

“I liked her better than I did in Leubronn,” Deronda replied. “Roulette brought out something harsh in her—almost a shadow. But at Diplow, she seemed softer, more truly feminine. Her eyes and mouth carried a gentler expression.”

“Don’t flirt too much with her, Dan,” Sir Hugo said, aiming for lightness. “If you make Grandcourt furious when they come to the Abbey at Christmas, it’ll spoil my plans.”

“I can stay in town, sir.”

“No, no,” Sir Hugo insisted. “Lady Mallinger and the children need you. Just don’t cause trouble—unless you can manage a duel and shoot Grandcourt. That might be worth the hassle.”

“I don’t recall ever flirting,” Deronda said, unamused.

“Oh, haven’t I?” Sir Hugo teased. “You always look at women with such tenderness, talk to them like a Jesuit. You’re dangerous—you’re the kind of man who makes Clarissas chase after you instead of the other way around.”

What good came of being irritated by a tasteless joke? Only irritation comes first—the reflection on usefulness follows later. Few compliments are more irritating than being told we seem to do things we never intend. Sir Hugo’s idea of flirting was clearly peculiar. For Deronda’s part, he was certain he had never flirted. Still, he was relieved the baronet knew nothing about his secret purchase of Gwendolen’s necklace—no chance of that becoming fodder for teasing.

He would be cautious moving forward—especially at Mrs. Meyrick’s house, where he was soon to visit for the first time since returning from Leubronn. Mirah was a woman who inspired quiet affection simply through her presence and speech. It was hard not to feel drawn to her.

Mrs. Meyrick had already written him a letter full of warmth about Mirah’s life in their home. “We grow fonder of her every day,” she wrote. “At breakfast, we all turn toward the door, waiting for her to appear. We watch her, listen to her—as if she were a visitor from another world. Not a word she’s spoken has given me cause for doubt. She is content, deeply grateful. My daughters are learning from her, and hope to find her more pupils. She refuses to live idle—she wants to work, just as my girls do. Mab says our life feels like a fairy tale, and all she fears is that Mirah will turn into a nightingale again and fly away. Her voice is perfect—not loud, but deep and tender, like thoughts of what once was. That’s how an old soul like mine feels about beauty in song.”

But Mrs. Meyrick had left out the details that might have made her hesitant. Amy and Mab, who had accompanied Mirah to synagogue, found Jewish customs harder to accept than they had expected—especially compared to Scott’s idealized Rebecca. They kept silent, out of respect for Mirah, whose faith was too personal to question lightly. But eventually, Amy—practical and reform-minded—could no longer hold back.

“Excuse me, Mirah,” she ventured, “but does it seem right to you that women sit apart, behind rails in a gallery?”

“Yes, I’ve never thought otherwise,” Mirah answered, gently surprised.

“And you prefer seeing men wear their hats?” Mab asked cautiously, offering the smallest difference.

“Oh, yes,” Mirah smiled. “I like what I’ve always seen—it brings back feelings I wouldn’t trade for anything.”

After that, any criticism—of belief or practice—would have felt cruel to these gentle souls. Mirah’s religion wasn’t a set of doctrines; it was woven into her very emotions, inseparable from her sense of self.

“She says she’s a poor Jewess,” Amy whispered later, after Mirah had gone to bed. “She doesn’t even know half her people’s traditions. Maybe if she loves us enough, and never finds her mother, she’ll slowly drift into Christianity—like most of the world does. It’s strange to belong to the Jews now.”

“Oh, oh, oh!” Mab exclaimed. “I wish I weren’t such a hideous Christian. How can an ugly Christian, always dropping her knitting, convert a beautiful Jewess who has no flaw?”

“It might be wrong of me,” Kate said shrewdly, “but I can’t help hoping her mother isn’t found. There might be something unpleasant.”

“I don’t think so,” Mrs. Meyrick replied. “I believe Mirah is made in her mother’s image. And imagine the joy if such a daughter were returned! But a mother’s feelings aren’t always rational” (she gave her daughters a playful glance). “And is a dead mother worth more than a living one?”

“Well, perhaps,” Kate said softly, “but we’d rather have you alive, little mother.”

Not only the Meyricks, whose knowledge had come from scattered reading and curious minds, but Deronda himself—well-educated, worldly, and grounded—had been shaken by Mirah’s presence. He realized how little he knew of modern Judaism, of the inner life of the Jewish people. The Chosen People had long been seen as chosen for others’ sake, their beliefs treated as outdated or exotic, fit only for scholars. Deronda, like many of his class, had assumed Judaism was a relic—something to ignore unless studying it academically. But Mirah—fleeing one parent, yearning for the other—had shown him a truth he hadn’t known: Judaism still lived in human hearts, still shaped lives, still offered meaning in a broken world.

On the journey back with Sir Hugo, he began noticing synagogues, searching book titles about Jews. This awakening—from indifference to curiosity—was a cure for boredom, though not one easily prescribed. Deronda carried it with him, making his idle weeks bearable. It was during this trip that he entered a Jewish synagogue for the first time—in Frankfurt, on a Friday evening.

Walking through the Juden-gasse, familiar from earlier visits, he remembered its old houses, but now his eyes lingered on the people—their faces, their bearing, their quiet dignity. His mind raced, connecting them to centuries past, stirring a deep sympathy with history. This sensitivity had shaped parts of his character—traits important to understand if one wished to follow his future.

Though young, well-born, and well-mannered, Deronda was not the kind of man others typically examined closely. He didn’t provoke questions about his beliefs or tastes. He could pass as a pleasant, clever fellow without scrutiny. But later, when he aged and softened, his quirks became clearer—and it was mercy if they weren’t offensive.

To grasp how later events affected him, one must see more than what he showed in conversation. His vivid impressions often made him seem mysterious, even contradictory. From early youth, he had developed a deep sensibility and reflective nature, which grew into a wide-ranging empathy. This empathy threatened to paralyze action—whenever he opposed something, even in thought, he felt he was attacking kin, things he loved. His imagination taught him to see the world through others’ eyes, so strong partisanship—except against injustice—felt like hypocrisy.

His sympathy was vast, flexible, yet entangled with analytical thinking that weakened emotion. Few men avoided vice more than he, yet he disliked it mildly, seeing it not as abstract sin, but as part of complex human lives shaped by history and pain. He understood and pitied.

This balance made him fiercely democratic in spirit, yet deeply conservative in feeling—drawn to tradition, haunted by memory. He hungered for ideas about government and religion, yet clung to ancient forms that held meaning for him, forms no argument could destroy. He leaned toward causes that lost—perhaps because he felt a kinship with those who suffered. Martyrdom changed sides, and he feared he might change with it, repelled by the idea of chasing success, which the world often calls duty.

Yet he also feared blind hatred. He sympathized with privileged heirs, recoiled from bitterness, and disliked the tone of angry reformers. Too much reflection risked paralyzing his moral courage—his anger at injustice, his choice of companionship. In recent years, he had grown acutely aware of this danger. What he craved was either a sudden event or a moment of insight that would pull him into a clear path, channeling his restless energy.

He no longer cared much for knowledge—he had no ambition for achievement—unless both could merge with his emotions. He dreaded the cold, empty culture that reduced everything to analysis, knowing everything *about* things but missing the thing itself—like knowing all about violets except their scent.

How would this turning point come? What force would justify loyalty, make him part of society instead of a wandering spirit, stirred by vague passions but without place or purpose? To make some difference—for the better—was not something he could live without. But how?

He saw part of the fault in his upbringing—no demands placed on him, no fixed role, only a relationship of uncertain nature. But he admitted he had slipped into a meditative numbness, drifting farther from the active, passionate life he believed was best—indeed, the only life worth living.

He wanted to keep emotion alive, strong, in the face of overthinking. To grind sentiment into dust while keeping it vibrant—like making cannon: first shape a round hole, then encase it in iron. Hold fast to your core.

Yet how distinguish what should be preserved whole from what is merely preserved as relic?

Such thoughts ran beneath his surface as he read law or listened politely. He had not pursued any single task with passion or consistency. Not a model life, certainly—but a struggle before dawn, one many young men have faced, bruised but not broken.

Under his calm exterior burned a quiet fire, making him sensitive to poetry in ordinary things. The Juden-gasse stirred in him a sense of connection to the distant past, evoking two poetic elements of history: the fragile beginnings of faiths and institutions, and their slow decay. The dust and remnants covering them only deepened their power—like twin green leaves destined to become a sheltering tree, or a legacy turned to sorrowful memory.

As he stepped out of the Juden-gasse and wandered in the evening air, heading for the synagogue, this reflection softened the sting of petty scenes along the way. At a dusty bookshop, he asked for the service time and was warmly guided by a sharp-eyed Jewish boy who enthusiastically directed him to the old Rabbinical school rather than the new Reformed building. Then, with charming politeness, overcharged him for a rare book—“nicht so leicht zu bekommen,” he explained.

Across the counter, a gruff, deaf shopkeeper glared at cards combining business and ritual, while a haggard man in a tall coat shouted in Yiddish, offering a bag. Moments later, another man of the same sort emerged from the shadows and repeated the cry. Deronda saw several odd-looking Jews—not wholly innocent, but indistinguishable from similarly flawed Christians. Recently, worried about Mirah’s family, he had viewed vulgar Jews with personal alarm. But comparison helped. Seeing similar flaws in others lessened surprise. He paid too much, but not with regret—his interest in Hebrew destiny remained intact.

He reached the Rabbinische Schule at sunset, entering with a modest congregation.

He took a seat near an elderly man with a white beard and felt hat—his profile striking, equally fitting for an Italian nobleman or a Hebrew sage. Their eyes met briefly, awkwardly. Deronda looked away—then felt a prayer book pushed toward him. He bowed thanks.

The service began. The reader climbed the platform, chanting Psalms and Old Testament phrases. Deronda, reading the German translation, let go of words and surrendered to the music—its rhythm, its chant, its shared devotion. Liturgy, at its strongest, transcends language. It becomes a plea not for specific gain, but for escape from weakness, an invitation to Good to enter and abide. Or it becomes pure joy—an exaltation that Good exists. Both emotions, amplified by communion across generations, struck him deeply.

The Hebrew liturgy moved through litanies, hymns, proclamations, blessings. Tonight, for Deronda, it was all one: the reader’s soaring voice, the boys’ sweet chorus, the swaying of worshippers, the humble surroundings—all blended into a single expression of history—tragic, enduring, glorious.

He was overwhelmed. The feeling seemed too intense for the moment—like divine presence in darkness before vision arrives.

Then, as the sounds faded and indifferent faces passed before him, a cold thought crept in: perhaps he was alone in this feeling. Perhaps he was the only one for whom the service meant more than routine.

Just as he bowed to his neighbor and turned to leave, a hand gripped his arm. Startled, he turned to see the white-bearded man close by.

“Pardon me, young gentleman,” the man said in German. “May I ask—what is your parentage? Your mother’s family name? Her maiden name?”

Deronda felt a surge of resistance. He wanted to shake off the touch, but managed to withdraw it calmly. “I am English,” he said coolly.

The man studied him a moment, then lifted his hat slightly and walked away. Whether he regretted the question or felt rebuffed, Deronda couldn’t tell.

On the walk back, he tried to dismiss the incident. He could not have acted differently. How could he reveal a name he didn’t know—to a stranger who had overstepped? The man likely mistook resemblance for kinship—common, meaningless.

The event was trivial, yet his instinctive recoil had been strong. He was glad he had ended it quickly.

It was also a reason not to mention the synagogue to the Mallingers—beyond his usual reluctance to speak of things Sir Hugo might call “Quixotic.” Sir Hugo was kind-hearted, especially to women, and sometimes did romantic deeds. But he never saw them as romantic. He laughed at grand motives, far-off reasons. This was the key difference between him and Deronda, who often spent breakfast lost in silent speculation about how to fill his days as others did.

Their stop in Frankfurt was brief, on the way home. But the impressions lingered—especially because of Mirah. That question about his mother’s family—if he hadn’t dismissed it as insignificant, it might have felt like a threat, not a promise. It deepened his anxiety about finding Mirah’s relatives and reinforced his caution. If he discovered something painful, was he bound to tell her? Might it bring new suffering?

He had written to Mrs. Meyrick to announce his visit at four. When he arrived, Mirah sat quietly working, surrounded by Mrs. Meyrick, Mab, the open piano, and the glowing engravings on the walls. Her hair was neatly done, her dress delicate. A peaceful glow lit her face—so different from the ragged figure by the river. A painter could have used her without changing a line, placing her before a choir singing “peace on earth, goodwill to men.”

Mirah noticed the contrast too. As they greeted each other, she said:

“See how different I am from the wretched creature by the river—because you found me and brought me to the very best.”

“It was luck,” Deronda said. “Any man would have done the same.”

“That’s not how I see it,” Mirah said firmly, shaking her head. “I think of what really happened. It was you—no one else—who found me and were kind to me.”

“No man,” a Rabbi once declared, “may turn the bones of his father and mother into spoons”—a statement meant to stir moral reflection, not practical debate. The idea of such transformation was absurd, not because it lacked utility, but because it offended against something deeper: sentiment. And sentiment, though invisible in the market, holds sway over the heart. It does not command those who do not believe that emotions are among the truest riches of life.

Deronda, meanwhile, had turned from the polished streets of London to quieter corners—those parts where ordinary Jewish families lived their lives. He wandered through neighborhoods thick with shops, synagogues, and faces that spoke of hardship and endurance. He walked without purpose, yet with intent: to feel, to see, to know. Why did he not simply go to a respected Rabbi or community elder and ask about a woman named Cohen, her son Ezra, and a lost daughter called Mirah? He had considered it—but only after Christmas. For now, the weight of reality pressed upon him more than ever before.

Though Deronda often found beauty in the everyday, when personal longing stirred within him, he too felt the cold grip of actuality—the unyielding world that refuses to conform to our dreams. Enthusiasm thrives on ideals; it can endure garlic in medieval halls, admire ancient processions, even find nobility in suffering centuries past. But when ideals become flesh—when they walk beside us in plain clothes, with common features—they lose their glow. We may dream of finding Mirah’s family in Cordova, surrounded by scholars and poets under Moorish skies, but when we stand in St. Mary Axe, where shop signs are grim and faces weary, the romance fades. Yet, paradoxically, the deepest poetry lies not in escaping reality, but in embracing it—seeing the divine in the mundane, the sacred in the squalid.

And so Deronda walked, not expecting much, but hoping for a glimpse of truth. He saw each Jew as a possible echo of Mirah—her mother, perhaps, her brother. He carried with him a quiet dread: what if the woman he found were not noble, not kind, not worthy of Mirah’s memory? And worse—what if the same collision awaited *him*? What if his own origins, hidden beneath layers of silence, held secrets just as painful?

He did not long to meet anyone in particular. When he passed a shop and glanced at the name above the door, he breathed easier when it wasn’t “Ezra Cohen.” In fact, he secretly hoped Ezra Cohen did *not* run a shop. Wishes, people say, carry weight. If you wish hard enough against something, fate may grant it anyway. Or if you desire it, it will never come. Such superstition amused Deronda, but he could not deny the strange pull of hope.

One morning, tired of Holborn’s noise, he stepped into a narrow side street. A hansom cab approached, and he paused to hail it—then stopped short. In the window of a small shop, gleaming in silver, were ornate clasps, finely chased. He thought of Lady Mallinger—how she might love them turned into a bracelet. Then he looked further. The shop was a pawnbroker’s, specializing in jewelry, lace, and other curious things labeled _bric-à-brac_. A sign read: *Watches and Jewelry Exchanged and Repaired.*

Inside, a young man appeared at the door, smiling warmly. “Good day, sir.”

Deronda returned the greeting, but already he was turning away—unwilling to be drawn into sales talk. Across the street, he beckoned the cabman. From there, he saw the name: **Ezra Cohen**.

It could be any one of a hundred Ezra Cohens. But this one was young—about thirty—and stood exactly the age Mirah’s brother might have been when she was still a child. Still, Deronda told himself firmly: no evidence existed. Even if this man *were* Mirah’s brother, and even if the mother were dead, it was not *his* duty to reveal it. Was it fair to impose his own sense of justice on another’s life?

But then came the thought: Mirah would want to know. She would need to know whether her mother lived or died, whether her brother was alive. How could he decide her future based on his own feelings? Wasn’t this the very thing he resented in his own upbringing—the absence of clear truth, the burden of hidden truths that shaped a life without consent?

For now, he decided, he had not discovered anything real. He would return—not to confront, but to learn. He intended to buy the clasps for Lady Mallinger and use the visit as an excuse to observe.

But Sir Hugo delayed him. The baronet, preparing a speech on a controversial legal matter, insisted Deronda help him research. Every day, they argued—passionately, evenly matched. Sir Hugo admired Deronda’s clarity, though he disagreed with him.

“Confound it, Dan! Why don’t you speak up in public? You’re wrong, I know. You won’t win. But you’ve got the power—the heavy artillery of the country is against you. That’s *exactly* why you should step forward. When I was your age, I’d have taken it. It would throw you into relief. Be in opposition to me here and there—it would make you stand out. Seize an opportunity like this, and you could be in Parliament in no time. And you know that would please me.”

“I’m sorry not to do what pleases you, sir,” said Deronda. “But I can’t see politics as a profession.”

“Why not? If you’re not born into public life, you must make your way into it. The country’s business must go on. And it won’t if everyone treats politics like prophecy, demanding divine calling. You can’t wait for a call from heaven or your constituents.”

“I don’t want to live off opinions—especially borrowed ones. Not that I blame others. Many better men than I climb platforms and praise themselves. They give their word of honor to a party.”

“Then let me tell you, Dan—a man who rejects all humbug is a three-cornered, impractical fellow. There’s bad humbug, yes. But there’s good humbug too—the kind that oils the wheels of progress. To rule men, you must rule them through their beliefs. I agree with the Archbishop of Naples who led a procession of St. Januarius during a plague. You can’t fight popular ignorance with orders. Action needs a little acting.”

“One may yield to necessity,” said Deronda. “But there’s a difference between saying, ‘In this case, I must wear foolscap and grin,’ and buying foolscap and practicing grinning. I believe true public service must keep an ideal in sight. Otherwise, success becomes confusion. If I entered politics, I might mistake my fame for justice.”

After this exchange—jarring, yet thoughtful—Deronda set out again for Ezra Cohen’s shop.

This time, he entered from the opposite end of the street. As he walked, he hesitated. The questions that haunted him now were not political, but personal: how far could he justify concealing kinship? Was it wise—or moral—to hide a truth that might bring comfort to Mirah?

He paused before a second-hand bookshop, its window filled with books from Homer to railway novels. Among them lay the autobiography of Salomon Maimon, a Polish Jew whose life had stirred many minds. Deronda took it, intending to pay inside.

Instead of the usual dusty clerk, he saw a man seated on a stool, reading the *Times*. His face was startling—pale, yellowish, almost carved from ivory. His features were sharp, not large, but deeply expressive. His eyes held a distant, searching gaze. It was a face that seemed to belong to another time—perhaps to a prophet in exile, or a poet of old Hebrew days. The man looked worn, as if years of struggle had left their mark, not on the body alone, but on the soul.

When he lowered the paper and met Deronda’s eyes, the stranger’s expression shifted—suddenly alert, questioning.

“You are perhaps of our race?” he whispered, gripping Deronda’s arm tightly.

Deronda flushed. He shook his head gently. “No.”

The hand released. The fire in the man’s eyes dimmed. He retreated into silence, offering the book with distant politeness. “Half-a-crown, sir. Mr. Ram will accept it.”

Deronda paid and left, feeling oddly humbled—as if some high authority had dismissed him.

He entered Ezra Cohen’s shop, where the young man greeted him with a smile. “Mother! Mother!” he called. “Coming, sir—coming directly.”

A woman in her fifties emerged—plain, yes, but not repulsive. Her hair was neatly done, her earrings large, her presence warm. She smiled at Deronda. “How can I serve you, sir?”

“I’d like to see the silver clasps in the window—the larger ones.”

She reached for them, but her son interrupted. “I’ll get them, mother—I’ll reach ’em.” He handed them to Deronda with a grin. “Mother’s too proud. She wants to do everything herself. That’s why I called her. When there’s a gentleman customer, I daren’t do otherwise.”

Deronda examined the clasps carefully.

“They’re only three guineas, sir,” said the mother.

“First-rate workmanship,” added the son. “Worth twice the price. I got them cheap from Cologne.”

At that moment, two new customers arrived. “Addy!” called the son.

From the back came a young woman in a bright blue dress, coral jewelry, her hair piled high. She carried a baby with dark curls, which she placed on the counter. The child stared around with unnerving intelligence. Beside her were a six-year-old boy and a younger girl—both with black eyes, black hair, faces more Semitic than their parents’. The boy, Jacob Alexander Cohen, marched forward, hands in pockets, and studied Deronda with a boldness that surprised him.

“What’s your name, sirrah?” Deronda asked.

“Jacob Alexander Cohen,” he replied, proudly. “Named after my grandfather—he sells knives and razors and scissors. He gave me this knife.” With quick fingers, he opened two blades and a corkscrew.

“Is that safe?” Deronda asked the grandmother.

“He’ll never hurt himself, bless you!” she said, watching him with pride.

“Have *you* got a knife?” Jacob asked, stepping closer.

“Yes,” said Deronda, pulling out a small penknife. “Do you want to see it?”

The boy leaned in, his voice hoarse with the weight of generations—already sounding like a merchant grown old in trade.

Deronda stood there, holding a penknife, facing a child who knew nothing of the world beyond his shop, yet carried within him the echoes of a people whose history was written in silence and survival.

And somewhere, in the quiet hum of the shop, a question stirred—no longer just about Mirah, but about identity, belonging, and the cost of truth.

CHAPTER XXXIV. “He was called Israel. A witch’s spell turned him into a dog. * * * * * But every Friday evening, at twilight, the spell fades, and the dog becomes once more a human being.” —HEINE: _Princess Sabbath_.

When Daniel Deronda arrived at five o’clock, the shop was closed, and the door was opened by the Christian maid. She led him into the room behind the shop, where he was struck by the warmth and charm of the scene. The house was old and spacious at the back—likely gloomy in daylight—but now bathed in soft light from an elegant brass lamp with seven oil flames hanging above a table draped in snow-white linen. The ceiling and walls were dark and smoky, which made the people in the room glow like figures in a Venetian painting.

The grandmother wore a yellowish-brown dress and a thick gold chain instead of a necklace. In this dim, golden light, her pale face, dark eyebrows, and coiled gray hair gave her a dignified, almost regal presence. Young Mrs. Cohen was dressed in red and black, with a string of large artificial pearls wrapped around her neck. The baby slept peacefully in its cradle beneath a scarlet blanket. Adelaide Rebekah stood on a chair in amber-colored braids, and Jacob Alexander wore black velveteen with bright red stockings. All four pairs of dark eyes sparkled with welcome as they looked up at Deronda. He felt a sudden pang of shame for the aloofness he had felt earlier that day when seeing them in their ordinary life.

The greeting was warm and open-hearted. Both mother and grandmother seemed to grow taller and more dignified under the quiet honor of hosting a guest in their private home. Deronda glanced around, noticing the old furniture—an oak bureau, a high side-table—surely kept out of necessity rather than taste. On the side-table sat a large dish of blue and yellow pottery, flanked by two silver vessels, and before them lay a thick book bound in darkened vellum with a deeply ribbed spine. At the far end of the room, an open door revealed another lit space beyond.

Deronda took in these details briefly while Jacob pressed him about the knife. He had gone to some trouble to find one with the proper hook and white handle, and now produced it, asking, “Is this what you wanted, Jacob?”

The boy examined it closely—the hook, the blades—then pulled out his own knife with a corkscrew attachment to compare. “Why do you prefer a hook?” Deronda asked.

“Because I can grab things with a hook,” Jacob replied. “A corkscrew only fits corks. But it’s better for you—you can pull out corks.”

“You agree to trade, then?” Deronda asked, noting how the grandmother watched with delight.

“What else have you got in your pockets?” Jacob asked seriously.

“Hush, hush, Jacob, love,” the grandmother said gently.

Deronda, remembering the rules of decorum, replied, “I think I shouldn’t tell you. Our business is about the knives.”

Jacob studied him for a moment, then nodded slowly. “I’ll shwop,” he said, handing over the corkscrew knife. Deronda accepted it solemnly and slipped it into his pocket.

At once, the youngest son of Shem darted into the next room, chattering rapidly, then returned—only to stop when he saw his father enter. He snatched a small velvet hat from a chair and placed it carefully on his head before approaching. Cohen kept his own hat on, ignoring the visitor. He stood still as the two children rushed to him and clasped his knees. Then he laid his hands on each child in turn and spoke a Hebrew blessing. His wife, who had just taken the baby from its cradle, brought it forward and held it beneath his hands so it could be blessed even in sleep.

For a fleeting moment, Deronda thought that this pawnbroker, proud of his trade, was not entirely without soul.

“Well, sir,” Cohen said, finally setting down his hat and returning to his usual self, “you’ve found your welcome in my family, I see. And you’ve been punctual. Nothing like a little pressure here,” he added, tapping his side pocket as he sat down. “It keeps us all sharp. I’ve felt it myself when I’ve had to make payments. I used to fit every kind of box. It’s good for the mind. Now then—let’s see, let’s see.”

“That’s the ring I mentioned,” Deronda said, removing it from his finger. “I believe it cost a hundred pounds. I think it’s worth fifty as a pledge. I’ll likely redeem it in a month or so.”

Cohen’s eyes gleamed as he looked at the young man—so earnest, so naive, believing redemption was a comfort to a pawnbroker. He took the ring, inspected it, and handed it back. “Good, good. We’ll talk about it after our meal. Perhaps you’d join us? Me and my wife would feel honored—and so would mother, wouldn’t you, mother?”

The invitation was repeated twice, and Deronda gladly accepted.

Everyone gathered around the table. Only one dish was visible, covered with a napkin. Mrs. Cohen had placed a china bowl near her husband so he might wash his hands. After putting his hat back on, Cohen paused and called loudly, “Mordecai!”

Could this be part of the ritual? Deronda wondered, unsure what to expect. Then came a quiet “Yes” from the next room. He turned toward the doorway—and there, to his astonishment, stood the mysterious Jew he had met that morning in the bookstore. Their eyes met. Mordecai looked as surprised as Deronda did—neither acknowledging the other.

But when Mordecai sat at the far end of the table, he bowed slightly to the guest, coldly, as if the disappointment of earlier still lingered between them.

Cohen washed his hands, reciting Hebrew words as he did so. Then he lifted the napkin to reveal two long flat loaves sprinkled with seeds—the bread of manna, a reminder of the wandering ancestors. He broke off pieces and gave one to each member of the family, including Adelaide Rebhak, who stood proudly on her chair in her amber dress, her little Jewish nose pinched tight as she tried to look dignified.

Then Cohen offered another blessing. The men uncovered their heads, and the meal began—ordinary, unremarkable food. Deronda wasn’t sure what he ate; his thoughts were elsewhere—on how to steer the conversation, and on Mordecai, whose gaze occasionally met his, full of quiet intensity.

Mordecai wore no fine Sabbath clothes. Instead of the threadbare black coat he’d worn that morning, he had on a faded drab jacket—once perhaps a fine paletot, now shrunk and dull. Yet this only emphasized his sharp, dark-haired face, so alive and intense it might have belonged to the prophet Ezekiel—unfashionable, certainly, but commanding.

It was noticeable that the thin tails of the fried fish were given to Mordecai—small portions, like those reserved for a poor relative. An ancient custom, perhaps, still observed without question.

Mr. Cohen carried the conversation with lively charm, speaking of the Queen and Royal Family, the Emperor and Empress of France—subjects dear to Jewish pride. The grandmother and younger Mrs. Cohen joined in eagerly. The younger woman remembered birthdays of notable figures precisely; the elder helped her son recount the visit of the French imperial couple to London ten years prior.

“I dare say you know more about it than we do, sir,” Cohen said repeatedly, preface to each story, as though offering a generous lesson.

“Our baby is named Eu_genie Esther,” the young wife announced cheerfully.

“The Emperor looks just like a cousin of mine,” the grandmother declared. “It struck me like lightning when I saw him. I never would have guessed.”

“Mother and I went to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace,” Cohen said. “I had a valuable piece of work to guard—mother might have been crushed flat. Though she was nearly as strong then as she is now. I told her, if I had a hundred mothers, I’d never take one to see the Emperor again. You can’t afford it when you’ve only one—not unless you’ve got a big insurance policy on her.” He chuckled, stroking her shoulder affectionately.

“Your mother has been a widow for many years, I suppose,” Deronda said, seizing the moment. “That makes your care for her all the more important.”

“Ay, ay,” Cohen replied quickly. “It’s been many _yore-zeit_ since I had to manage for her and myself. I started early. That’s what makes you a sharp knife.”

“What does—what makes a sharp knife, father?” Jacob asked, cheeks swollen with sweet cake.

The father winked at Deronda. “Having your nose put on the grindstone.”

Jacob slid from his chair, holding the cake, and approached Mordecai—who had remained silent throughout. “What does that mean—putting my nose on the grindstone?” he asked.

“It means you must endure pain without crying out,” Mordecai said softly, looking down at the child with gentle eyes.

Jacob offered a corner of the cake into Mordecai’s mouth. “I shan’t cry out,” he declared, watching closely as the man took a bite. Mordecai smiled, clearly pleased to please the boy. The simple act made both seem more tender, more real.

Deronda felt a flicker of frustration—he had gained nothing from his question.

“I suppose that’s the right place to learn,” he said, continuing the thread, turning to Mordecai. “You’ve been a great student, I imagine?”

“I have studied,” Mordecai replied quietly.

“And you—your German, from the book you bought…”

“Yes, I’ve studied in Germany. Are you usually involved in bookselling?” Deronda asked.

“No,” Mordecai answered. “I go to Mr. Ram’s shop every day to keep it while he eats.”

Now, he looked at Dernda with renewed interest—as if something in the young man stirred a forgotten hope. After a pause, he asked, “Perhaps you know Hebrew?”

“I’m afraid not at all,” Deronda admitted.

Mordecai’s face fell. He lowered his eyes, stared at his hands folded before him, and said nothing more.

Deronda now noticed more clearly the difficulty in Mordecai’s breathing—a sign, he thought, of consumption.

“I’ve had other things to do than study,” Cohen said. “I’ve had to learn useful things. I know stones well.” He gestured toward Deronda’s ring. “I’m not afraid to take that ring at my own valuation. But now”—his voice dropped, becoming softer, more intimate—“what do you want for it?”

“Fifty or sixty pounds,” Deronda replied, a bit too casually.

Cohen paused, thrust his hands into his pockets, fixed Deronda with glistening eyes—like a guinea-pig with a secret—and said, “Couldn’t do that. Happy to help, but I can’t go that far. Forty pounds—say forty—I’ll give you forty.”

Deronda noticed Mordecai glance up again, watching the exchange. He said, “Very well. I’ll redeem it in a month or so.”

“Good,” Cohen said indifferently. “I’ll make out the ticket later.”

Then he raised a finger—conversation paused. He, Mordecai, and Jacob put on their hats. Cohen began a prayer of thanksgiving, which continued with responses until Mordecai took over, chanting solemnly, chin slightly raised, hands loosely clasped before him. In tone, accent, and ease—free from any need for approval—Mordecai stood in stark contrast to the man at the other end of the table.

It was an odd union: among these prosperous, everyday Jews, a man so frail, so worn, yet so imposing—making Deronda feel uneasy, as if he had failed to meet some unseen expectation.

No sooner had Mordecai finished than he rose, bowed slightly to the stranger, and walked back into his room, closing the door behind him.

“That seems to be a remarkable man,” Deronda said, turning to Cohen.

Cohen immediately straightened, stuck out his tongue slightly, and tapped his temple. Clearly, Mordecai didn’t measure up to Cohen’s standard of sanity.

“Does he belong to your family?” Deronda asked.

The idea amused everyone. They exchanged glances.

“No, no,” Cohen said. “Charity! Charity! He worked for me. When he grew weaker, I took him in. He’s a burden, yes—but he brings blessings. He teaches the boy. And he repairs watches and jewelry.”

Deronda barely suppressed a smile—this mix of kindness and justification, of generosity masked as practicality. But his desire to learn more about Mordecai—whose presence now seemed even more mysterious—was cut short. Cohen shifted the topic back to the loan, a charitable act, and proceeded to write the ticket, hand over forty pounds, and exchange them for the diamond ring.

Deronda, feeling it would be impolite to stay longer than necessary, took his leave—carrying only the money and the pawn ticket in his pocket. He resolved to return after Christmas, when he would try to uncover more about Mordecai—and perhaps, through him, learn why it was forbidden to ask the elder Mrs. Cohen whether she had a daughter.

If guilt were measured by the scale of one’s sins, human history might have taken a far different turn. We would likely find the architects of greed-driven wars and the ruthless predators of finance standing shoulder to shoulder with petty thieves and common murderers—each lacerating their souls in equal penitence. Yet wickedness does not go unrewarded; those who win in this devil’s game must be more base, more cruel, more hardened than most of us can bear to imagine. For while many are born with conscience—a flicker of justice, a whisper of love—that prevents them from embracing untroubled evil, others rise above such scruples, leaving behind the fragile moral compass that binds ordinary humanity.

On December twenty-ninth, Deronda learned that the Grandcourts had arrived at the Abbey, though he had seen nothing of them before dressing for dinner. A heavy snowfall had transformed the grounds into a winter wonderland, delighting the children with snowball fights and snowmen. During the Christmas holidays, the Mallinger girls found no joy unless “cousin”—as they always called Deronda—was part of the fun. After playing outdoors, he had spent some time at billiards, letting the hours slip by without thought of meeting Gwendolen again. Still, the prospect stirred something within him. When he finally retired to his room, still warm from exertion, his mind turned to her—not with longing, but with quiet speculation.

What kind of woman would she become now that she was married? Would the weight of her new life show in her eyes, her voice, her bearing? He remembered her at Diplow, then at Leubronn—how vividly she had been shaped by experience. "Some people," he mused, "you could watch grow or decay day by day. I believe she is one of them—someone who carries every impression like a mark upon her soul. That necklace incident, the idea that gambling was wrong—those things cut deep. But sensitivity can lead either way: it may drive a person to despair as easily as to redemption."

And what of Grandcourt? Could anyone truly feel affection for him? His face seemed carved from stone—emotionless, detached. "One might almost wish to strike him," Deronda thought, "just to see if any passion might stir beneath that cold exterior." He feared Gwendolen had married not for love, but for escape—from poverty, perhaps, or from the crushing pressure of expectation. Why had she once fled from him? The poverty came later. Poor thing—had she been pressured? Was she trapped?

He felt only pity for her—this young woman, full of life yet so recklessly blind, believing herself entitled to rule the world simply because she was beautiful and admired. How swiftly her dreams might curdle into bitter regret! And yet, after all he’d seen of her, how could he not look forward to seeing her again?

Still, three weeks had passed since the wedding. She had already lived at Ryelands, at Diplow—enough time to mask her true feelings. She was not one to expose herself carelessly to curious eyes.

A gathering had been arranged to welcome the newlyweds. Among the guests were Lord and Lady Pentreath, representatives of old aristocracy; young Mr. and Mrs. Fitzadam, of the Worcestershire branch of an ancient gentry family; Mr. Fenn, Member of Parliament for West Orchards, accompanied by his two daughters—his presence symbolizing civic duty through cider interests; Mr. Raymond and his wife, cousins to Lady Mallinger; the distinguished lawyer Mr. Sinker; and Mr. Vandernoodt, a man of Dutch descent whom Sir Hugo had met at Leubronn and deemed worth keeping in England.

The drawing-room buzzed with polite conversation before the couple appeared. Children—little Raymonds, nephews and nieces of Lady Mallinger, along with her own three daughters—were allowed to roam freely, adding warmth to the scene. Portraits lined the cedar-paneled walls, their dark backgrounds framing noble faces. Above, the ceiling glowed with heraldic colors between candle sconces. The fire crackled with oak boughs, casting a golden light across the deep-piled carpet, while voices remained hushed, subdued by the dignity of the setting. Ages mingled—from the white-haired Pentreaths to four-year-old Edgar Raymond—creating a tapestry of life.

Lady Mallinger moved gracefully in black velvet, cradling a tiny white dog like a finishing touch to her elegance. The children clung to the ladies; the men stood apart, speaking softly, awaiting dinner. Deronda stood slightly apart, engaged in conversation with Mr. Vandernoodt—a man of bland charm, impeccably connected, but utterly unremarkable in himself. He was the sort who knew everything about everyone except what truly mattered.

They spoke of the bride and groom. Vandernoodt prided himself on collecting personal details. He hinted that he knew much about Grandcourt since their meeting at Leubronn.

“Men who’ve seen much of life don’t always choose wisely,” he said. “He’s had a colorful past—indulged deeply in pleasures, though lazy at heart. But you know all this, I’m sure.”

“No,” replied Deronda lightly. “I know little more than that he’s Sir Hugo’s nephew.”

At that moment, the door opened.

The room was made for grand entrances—and indeed, when Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt stepped in, no one could deny their presence commanded attention. Grandcourt wore the same flawless suit, the same impassive expression as before his marriage. One sensed he would tolerate nothing less than perfection—in dress, in manner, in wife. And Gwendolen—she was exactly what he might have chosen. “By George,” murmured Vandernoodt, “I think she’s even handsomer now!”

Deronda agreed, though he said nothing. Her white silk gown, adorned with diamonds—on her neck, in her ears, woven into her hair—added to her regal beauty. It struck him with greater certainty than ever before: this was a woman whose grace had deepened, though not necessarily softened. Some beauties are like rare works of art—first met with skepticism, then recognized as masterpieces. At Leubronn, he had glimpsed a tenderness beneath her boldness. Now, at Diplow, he had seen a deeper, more complex woman. Was there change now?

She greeted guests with a proud calm, a smile that touched only her lips. But beneath it, he sensed the same fierce will—the same force that had once held him under her gaze and sent him away defeated at the roulette table. There was no time to reflect further—the dinner bell rang.

Seated near her, Deronda caught fragments of her conversation with Sir Hugo, who was in high spirits. He glanced toward her, intending to bow, but she offered no sign. Finally, Sir Hugo said, “Deronda, you’ll be pleased to hear what Mrs. Grandcourt tells me about your favorite Klesmer.”

Gwendolen’s eyelids lowered. Deronda watched her—saw a brief tremor as she raised them, returned his nod and smile, her own smile stiff, mechanical. It lasted only a second. Sir Hugo continued:

“The Arrowpoints have accepted the marriage. Klesmer is spending Christmas with his bride at Quetcham.”

“I suppose he’s glad for his wife’s sake,” said Deronda. “Otherwise, he might not mind staying away.”

“It’s like a troubadour tale,” said Lady Pentreath, a stately, deep-voiced elder. “Good to see a bit of romance left in our world. Our youth today are too worldly-wise.”

“True,” said Sir Hugo. “But it shows good sense on the Arrowpoints’ part to accept it after the scandal. Disowning your child over a _mésalliance_ is like losing your only eye—everyone knows it belongs to you, and you have no other to show.”

“As for _mésalliance_,” added Lady Pentreath, “there’s no bloodline to speak of. Admiral Arrowpoint was one of Nelson’s men—a doctor’s son. And we all know where the mother’s fortune came from.”

“If anything is a _mésalliance_,” said Deronda, “it’s on Klesmer’s side.”

“Oh?” said Sir Hugo, turning to Gwendolen. “You think it’s the immortal marrying the mortal? What do you say?”

“I have no doubt Herr Klesmer believes himself immortal,” she replied, regaining composure. “But I suspect his wife will burn enough incense to satisfy him.”

“Don’t you approve of a wife honoring her husband?” asked Sir Hugo, grinning.

“Oh, yes,” she said, “if only to make others believe in him.” She paused, then added with a flicker of humor, “When Herr Klesmer admires his own genius, it helps if his wife says Amen.”

“Klesmer isn’t your favorite, then,” said Sir Hugo.

“I hold him in high regard,” she replied, suddenly serious. “His genius is beyond my judgment. And I know him to be generous.”

Her tone carried a hidden edge—bitterness she couldn’t explain, even to herself. Deronda wondered: what would he have thought of her if he hadn’t known her past? Perhaps he’d have seen only a woman masking pain with defiance. But why didn’t she acknowledge him with kindness?

Sir Hugo changed the subject. “Isn’t this room magnificent? It was once the abbey’s refectory. Those pillars and arches divided the space—later built up. Originally, it was twice as large. Benedictine monks sat here. Imagine if the lights dimmed and the ghosts rose behind every chair!”

“Please don’t!” Gwendolen laughed, shivering playfully. “It’s lovely to follow in ancestors’ footsteps—but they should stay underground. I’d be afraid to walk alone in this house. I suppose the old generations are angry we’ve changed so much.”

“Oh, the ghosts are of all political stripes,” said Sir Hugo. “Those who wanted reform in life but failed—they’re on our side now. But if you don’t like walking alone, you’ll enjoy it with company. You and Grandcourt should see the whole place. Let’s ask Deronda to guide us—he knows more about it than I do.”

Gwendolen glanced at Deronda—her eyes fleeting, awkward. He had been serving himself, his face calm, unreadable. The thought of him showing her and Grandcourt the house—this place that might have been his, had things been different—stirred memories she couldn’t control. She felt exposed, ashamed. With her usual quick wit, she said, “You don’t know how afraid I am of Mr. Deronda.”

“Why?” asked Sir Hugo, intrigued. “Because he’s too learned?”

“No,” she said. “Since I first saw him at Leubronn. When he looked at the roulette table, I began to lose. He cast an evil eye on my play. He disapproved. He told me so. Now, whatever I do in front of him, I fear he’ll judge it.”

“Gad!” said Sir Hugo, glancing at Deronda. “I’m afraid of him too when he disapproves.” Then, lowering his voice, he added, “I don’t think ladies usually mind having his eyes upon them.”

“I object to critical eyes,” she said coolly, turning her head. “Are there many old rooms left in the Abbey?”

“Not many,” said Sir Hugo. “There’s a cloistered court with a gallery above. But the finest part—part of the old church—is now stables. I improved the rest, but couldn’t touch the stables. So the horses enjoy the choir.”

“I’d like to see the horses as well as the building,” said Gwendolen.

“Oh, I don’t keep many,” said Sir Hugo. “Grandcourt will scoff at my horses. I’ve given up hunting. I jog along, as becomes an old gentleman with daughters. Truth is, I did too much here. We lived at Diplow for two years during renovations. Do you like Diplow?”

“Not particularly,” she said, indifferent.

“Ah,” said Sir Hugo, pleased. “It won’t do after Ryelands. Grandcourt chose it for the hunting—but found something better.” He lowered his voice. “Something so much better he’d prefer it to any other place in the world.”

“It has one attraction for me,” she said, smiling coldly. “It’s near Offendene.”

“I understand,” said Sir Hugo, and let it drop.

No baronet is immune to desire. Sir Hugo wished Grandcourt disliked Diplow—for reasons of his own. But he also hoped that this preference wouldn’t stem from his marriage to such a charming woman. Gwendolen suited him perfectly, yet, as he later confided to Lady Mallinger, he would never have believed her capable of marrying beyond her expectations.

Deronda had paid little attention to the talk, but the glimpses he caught of Gwendolen confirmed his growing sense: her manner had grown colder, more artificial.

Later, in the drawing-room, Deronda sang at the piano, requested by someone. Afterward, Mrs. Raymond took his place. As he rose, he noticed Gwendolen had moved to the far end of the room, standing with her back to the others, gazing at a finely carved ivory head on a small table. He longed to speak to her—why not? He would have approached any other lady. Yet he hesitated, drawn instead to the graceful curve of her back.

Staring at a woman’s back is dangerous when you wish to speak to her. It only makes you want to see what lies beyond. There may be a smile waiting—soft, inviting. Deronda finally stepped forward, positioning himself at right angles to her. But before he could speak, she turned—not with a smile, but with a look of profound sadness, so different from her earlier chillness that he froze.

For a moment—long enough to feel the depth of understanding between them—they stared. She seemed to offer confession; he responded with silent empathy, absorbing all else.

“Will you join in the music?” he asked, needing to break the silence.

Her expression shifted—just a flicker—as she composed herself. “I join in by listening. I love music.”

“Are you a musician?”

“I’ve spent years studying it. But I lack talent. I shall never sing again.”

“But if you love music, it’s always worth it—private joy. I make it a virtue to accept my mediocrity,” he smiled. “It’s forgivable, as long as I don’t pretend it’s excellence.”

“I can’t imitate you,” she said, returning to her bright tone. “To me, being middling means being dull. And the worst fault I find in the world is that it’s dull. In fact,” she added, “I’m going to defend gambling—despite you. It’s a refuge from boredom.”

“I don’t accept that justification,” he said. “I think what we call ‘dullness’ is really a sickness in ourselves. Otherwise, how could anyone find life intensely meaningful? Many do.”

“Ah,” she smiled, “so the fault I find in the world is actually my own.”

Then, looking up at the ivory carving, she asked, “Do you never find fault with the world—or with others?”

“Oh, yes,” he said. “When I’m in a grumbling mood.”

“And hate people? Admit it—when they stand in your way, when their gain is your loss? That’s your own phrase.”

“We often stand in each other’s way without meaning to,” he replied. “Hating them for that seems foolish.”

“But if they harm you—and could have avoided it?” she pressed, her voice sharp, uncharacteristic.

Deronda was startled. Why this sudden intensity? A pang of unease silenced him for a moment. Then, quietly, he said, “Then I prefer my place to theirs.”

“There,” she said, laughing softly, “I believe you’re right.” And with that, she turned away, joining the group at the piano.

“Nothing weighs so heavily as a secret. Carrying it far is difficult for ladies; and I know, indeed, on this very point, many men who are women.” —LA FONTAINE.

While Deronda was being led away by Mr. Vandernoodt—eager for a brisk walk, a cigar, and a bit of idle talk—the two men stepped out into the cool evening air. There is something about being in another’s company that stirs a man to confide in someone less informed, and soon enough, Mr. Vandernoodt leaned forward with a sly glint in his eye.

“What a faded piece of lace Grandcourt is!” he declared. “Though, if he’s your friend, I’ll retract the remark.”

Deronda shook his head. “Not at all. He’s not my favorite.”

“I suspected as much,” said Vandernoodt. “Still, one wonders how such a fellow could fall so deeply again—and marry like this. Lush, his old pal, hints it was mere obstinacy. By George! That would be a very understandable kind of stubbornness. A man might decide to marry her without needing anyone to provoke him. But you must admit he’s spent a good deal of money on it, eh?”

“I know nothing of his affairs,” Deronda replied.

“What? Not even about the other place he keeps up?”

“Diplow? Of course. He took it over from Sir Hugo. But only for a year.”

“No, no—not Diplow. Gadsmere. Sir Hugo knows, I assure you.”

Deronda said nothing. He felt a flicker of curiosity, but sensed that whatever Vandernoodt had to say would come out sooner or later, whether he asked or not.

“Lush wouldn’t admit it openly, naturally. He’s Grandcourt’s go-between, you see. But I’ve heard it from the best source. The truth is, there’s another woman at Gadsmere—with four children. She’s been holding sway over him for ten years or more, and still does, so far as I can tell. Left her husband for him, traveled with him everywhere. Her husband’s dead now—I met a fellow officer who knew her before she left him. A fiery dark-eyed woman—quite beautiful in her day—he thought she’d died. People say she still has Grandcourt under her thumb. It’s amazing he didn’t marry her. There’s a fine boy, and I understand Grandcourt can do exactly as he pleases with the estate. Lush told me as much.”

“What right did he have to marry this girl?” Deronda asked, voice tight with distaste.

Vandernoodt adjusted his cigar, shrugged, and pursed his lips.

“She couldn’t possibly know,” Deronda insisted, almost too quickly. But instantly, the thought struck him: *Could she have known?*

“It’s rather a fascinating picture,” Vandernoodt mused. “Grandcourt caught between two passionate women. And believe me, this fair-haired one has fire in her. I saw it at Leubronn. It’s like Medea and Creüsa—only with a modern twist. Imagine them meeting! Grandcourt’s a new kind of Jason. I wonder what role he’ll play. At best, it’s a dog’s part. I can almost hear Ristori now, shouting, ‘Jasone! Jasone!’ These strong women always end up with a stick.”

“Grandcourt can bite,” said Deronda. “He’s no passive thing.”

“No, no—Jason, I meant. I can’t quite make sense of Grandcourt. But he’s sharp enough—well-built, too. If he inherits all this property, the estates could be divided. This girl, whose family sank into poverty, may feel lucky to have him. I don’t want to judge harshly—men get tangled in these things. But he could be more pleasant. Last night I told him a brilliant story, and he got up and walked off halfway through. I nearly kicked him. Do you think it was indifference—or arrogance?”

“A mixture,” Deronda said. “He follows the rules, but he doesn’t really listen.”

After a pause, he added, “I suspect there’s some exaggeration in what you’ve heard about this woman at Gadsmere.”

“None at all,” Vandernoodt insisted. “It’s been quiet for years. People forgot. But the nest is still there—the birds are in it. And I know Grandcourt goes there. I’ve solid proof. Still, it’s none of our business. The affair has sunk beneath the surface.”

“I’m surprised you learned so much,” Deronda remarked dryly.

“Oh, plenty knew. But stories like this are tucked away like old letters. They interest me. I like knowing how people live now—contemporary gossip, not ancient legends. Those academic types build reputations by digging up scandals about Semiramis or Nitocris, then we get a thousand poems from every amateur poet. But I don’t care about the missteps of mummies. You do, though—you’re one of the historical sort. More interested in a lady when she’s got wrinkles and bony toes showing. Does that flatter your imagination?”

“Well, if she suffered in love, at least we know she’s free of it now,” Deronda replied.

“Ah,” said Vandernoodt, “you’re thinking of Medea.”

Deronda changed the subject, pointing to a cluster of towering oaks, bare and stark against the twilight sky. He found himself drawn to the tale—yet sensed Vandernoodt had said all he intended.

Since his earliest attempts to unravel the mystery of his own origins, Deronda had never been so absorbed in piecing together another’s hidden life as he was now, trying to understand Gwendolen’s marriage. This secret relationship of Grandcourt’s—had she known about it? Was it the reason she hesitated, only to yield under pressure of need? He replayed their conversations in his mind, noticing nuances in her words—moments where she seemed to acknowledge guilt, to carry the weight of having caused harm. His own pain gave him insight into the suffering such a situation might bring upon the children and their mother. Could Mrs. Grandcourt, beneath her composed facade, be consumed by grief—by shame, disappointment, jealousy? He dwelled especially on signs of remorse, feeling inclined to forgive, to pity. He believed he had found a key to understanding her better: how crushing must it be for a young woman to bind her hopes to a man burdened by secrets? He now understood why Sir Hugo had never mentioned this affair to him. And instantly, the image of Mrs. Glasher merged with his own buried past.

Gwendolen knowing of that woman and her children, marrying Grandcourt, and appearing satisfied—such a woman would have repulsed him. But Gwendolen tasting regret, mourning the harm she may have caused—she drew close to his heart. If true, she had reached a shared depth of understanding about life’s hidden struggles—a realm most women rarely grasp with fairness or generosity. Traditionally, Gwendolen might have seen her marriage as Grandcourt’s redemption, while Mrs. Glasher represented his abandoned sin. But Deronda felt a quiet anger on behalf of those cast aside—like Hagar and Ishmael.

Undeniably, Deronda’s growing concern for Gwendolen stemmed largely from the way she treated him. And few, man or woman, would benefit from total indifference to such appeal. One sign his feelings had deepened was that he dismissed any fear she might be a coquette setting traps. He resolved to meet her honestly, to speak freely whenever the chance arose.

He slipped away from Vandernoodt and wandered into a quiet corner, lost in thought. Half an hour passed swiftly, filled with speculation about Gwendolen’s state of mind. Then he remembered: she would likely be at tea with the ladies in the drawing-room.

His guess proved correct. Gwendolen, after deciding not to descend for four hours, began to feel restless after just one. Shutting herself away meant missing everything—seeing, hearing, connecting. Her visit would last only two more days. She straightened herself, restored her composure, and descended, determined to be agreeable.

Only women were present, and Lady Pentreath entertained them with tales of Regency drawing-rooms and the fashion of 1819—the year she was presented. As Deronda entered, she paused.

“Shall I be welcome?” he asked. “Perhaps I should return and find the others. They’re probably in the billiard-room.”

“No, no,” Lady Pentreath said. “They were tired of me. Let us hear what _you_ have to say.”

“That’s rather an awkward request,” Deronda replied, pulling up a chair beside Lady Mallinger. “But perhaps I should mention our songstress—unless you’ve already spoken of her.”

“Oh, the little Jewess?” Lady Mallinger said. “No, I haven’t. Never occurred to me that anyone here needed singing lessons.”

“All ladies know someone who does,” Deronda said. “I’ve found a truly exquisite singer”—he turned to Lady Pentreath—“living with friends of mine—the mother and sisters of a Cambridge chum. She performed in Vienna, but wants to leave the stage and support herself through teaching.”

“There are plenty of such people,” the older woman remarked. “Are her lessons cheap or expensive? Those are the only two hooks I know.”

“There’s a third,” Deronda said. “Her singing is exceptional. She has such excellent training—or rather, such natural talent combined with training—that you’d swear it came entirely by instinct.”

“Why did she leave the stage?” Lady Pentreath asked. “I can’t believe first-rate talent gives up first-rate opportunities.”

“Her voice was too weak,” Deronda said. “Perfect for a room. You who endure my Schubert would be enchanted by hers,” he added, looking at Mrs. Raymond. “She could sing at private gatherings or concerts. Her voice is fully capable of that.”

“I’ll have her in my drawing-room when we go to town,” Lady Mallinger said. “You shall hear her. I haven’t yet, but I trust Daniel’s judgment. My girls will take lessons from her.”

“Is it a charitable venture?” Lady Pentreath asked. “I detest charitable music.”

Lady Mallinger, uncomfortable and bound not to reveal Mirah’s story, smiled faintly and glanced at Deronda.

“It’s charity to those who want a model of true feminine singing,” Deronda said. “Everyone with ears would benefit from refining their taste. If you heard Miss Lapidoth”—he looked at Gwendolen—“perhaps you’d reconsider giving up singing.”

“I’d likely confirm my decision,” Gwendolen replied. “I don’t feel able to enjoy my own mediocrity.”

“For my part,” Deronda said, “those who do anything well inspire me to try. Not because I think I can match them—but because they make the pursuit seem worthwhile. I can accept my own music isn unlikely to matter much, but the world would be duller if I thought music itself wasn’t worth anything. Excellence lifts life—it shows the soul’s richness.”

“But if we can’t imitate it,” Gwendolen said, irritated, “it only makes our own lives seem smaller.”

“That depends on perspective,” Deronda replied. “We’d have a poor life if we relied solely on our own performances. A little imitation of excellence is like private devotion. Most of us should practice art not to perform, but to understand and appreciate what others achieve. I think Miss Lapidoth is one of those rare ones.”

“She must be very happy,” Gwendolen said, with a hint of sarcasm, turning toward Mrs. Raymond.

“I can’t say,” Mrs. Raymond answered. “I’d need to know more.”

“It must have been painful,” Juliet Fenn murmured, “that her voice failed her.”

“I suppose she’s past her prime,” Lady Pentreath said.

“On the contrary,” Deronda said. “She hasn’t reached it. She’s barely twenty.”

“And very pretty,” Lady Mallinger interjected, eager to help. “And well-mannered. I’m sorry she’s a devout Jewess—I wouldn’t wish it for anyone else, but it doesn’t matter in singing.”

“Well, since her voice won’t let her scream much,” Lady Pentreath said, “I’ll ask Lady Clementina to put her on my nine granddaughters. I hope she convinces eight that they lack voice enough to sing anywhere but church. My belief is that many girls today need lessons not to sing.”
