“I’ve had my share,” Gwendolen said, looking at Deronda. “See? Lady Pentreath is on my side.”

As she spoke, Sir Hugo entered with several gentlemen, including Grandcourt, and stood near the tea table.

“What imposition is Deronda putting on you, ladies—slipping in alone among you?”

“Wanting to pass off an obscurity as better than any celebrity,” Lady Pentreath retorted. “A Jewish singer who’s supposed to astonish these young people. You and I, who heard Catalani at her peak, aren’t easily amazed.”

Sir Hugo smiled, took a cup of tea from his wife, and said, “Well, a Liberal must believe there have been singers since Catalani.”

“Ah, you’re younger than I am. Perhaps you chased Alcharisi. But she married and left you all behind.”

“Yes, yes,” Sir Hugo said. “It’s cruel when great singers marry into silence before their voices crack. The husband becomes a public thief. Leroux once said, ‘A man might as well take down a fine peal of church bells and carry them off to the steppes.’” He set down his cup and moved away.

Deronda, who had shifted to make room, sat apart, sensing he was no longer wanted. Then he noticed Gwendolen had slipped away from Vandernoodt and stood at the piano, examining the sheet music.

Was she inviting him to join her? Perhaps she wished to make amends for her earlier resistance to his praise of Mirah—something he often noticed: her first impulse was to reject, then regret.

He approached.

“Looking for something to play or sing?”

“I’m not looking for anything,” she said softly, “but I *am* relenting.”

“May I know why?”

“I’d like to hear Miss Lapidoth—and take lessons from her, if you admire her so much. When we go to town, I mean. Lessons in rejoicing at her excellence and accepting my own shortcomings,” she added, offering him a warm, open smile.

“I’ll be glad you do,” Deronda said, returning her smile.

“Is she perfect in everything else?”

“I can’t vouch for that. I’ve seen little of her. But I’ve seen nothing I’d wish to change. She’s endured hardship since childhood, grown up amid pain. Yet I think you’ll find no advantages could have given her more grace or refinement.”

“What sort of troubles did she face?”

“I don’t know precisely. But I know she once stood on the edge of drowning herself in despair.”

“And what stopped her?” Gwendolen asked quickly, eyes fixed on him.

“A ray of light—something that made her feel she ought to live, that life was worth living,” Deronda said quietly. “She’s full of piety, and seems ready to submit to anything when it’s framed as duty.”

“Those people aren’t to be pitied,” Gwendolen said impatiently. “I have no sympathy for women who always do the right thing. I don’t believe in their great sufferings.” Her fingers fluttered over the edges of the music.

“It’s true,” Deronda said, “that knowing one has done wrong cuts deeper. We flawed creatures can never feel as much for the irreproachable as for those crushed by their own faults. It’s an ancient story—the lost sheep—but it repeats every day.”

“That’s just talk,” Gwendolen said bitterly. “It’s not real. You admire Miss Lapidoth because you think her flawless. And you’d despise a woman who’d done something you consider wrong.”

“That would depend entirely on her own view of her actions,” Deronda said.

“You’d be content if she were miserable,” she snapped.

“No,” he said gently. “Not content—full of sorrow for her. I didn’t mean to suggest the pure aren’t admirable. But those who might seem uninteresting can become worthy of compassion when they face deep remorse. Lives grow in different ways. Some never wake up unless shaken by the consequences of their choices. And when they suffer, we must care more for them than for the self-satisfied.”

Deronda forgot everything except the vision of Gwendolen’s possible torment. Compassion swelled in him, and his voice and eyes betrayed his concern.

Gwendolen had slipped onto the music stool and looked up at him, her long eyes filled with pain—like a wounded creature seeking help.

“Are you coaxing Mrs. Grandcourt to play for us, Dan?” Sir Hugo asked, stepping up and placing a hand on Deronda’s shoulder with a gentle, teasing squeeze.

“I cannot persuade myself,” Gwendolen said, rising.

Others followed Sir Hugo, ending any chance of intimacy for the evening.

But the next day was New Year’s Eve—and a grand ball was to be held in the gallery above the cloister, where numbers and movement could create pockets of privacy. As Gwendolen dressed, she longed to wear the old turquoise necklace—her only keepsake from Leubronn—but dared not offend Grandcourt by appearing shabby on a night he expected brilliance. Instead, she wound it thrice around her wrist, making it a bracelet—putting it on just before entering the ballroom.

The dance was always a breathtaking sight, preserved by family tradition as closely as possible. Red carpet covered the floor; tropical plants and evergreens formed bowers at each end and in every alcove; and generations of ancestral portraits lined the walls, silent spectators from centuries past. Neighboring gentry, high and low, were invited—making it a moment when the future master and mistress of Abbott’s and King’s Topping could glimpse their destiny: a picturesque provincial rule, embodied by prosperous tenants.

Sir Hugo hoped Grandcourt would feel flattered to be welcomed to the Abbey during this festival honoring the family estate. He also hoped his own vigor would remind his successor that the inheritance was distant—and that wisdom lay in accepting a substantial sum now, rather than waiting for a lesser title.

All present, from the humblest farmer’s daughter to the highest noble, knew they were seeing “young Grandcourt”—Sir Hugo’s nephew, presumptive heir and future baronet—now visiting with his bride after years away. Any tension between uncle and nephew had melted into cordial warmth.

The bride opening the ball with Sir Hugo became the center of attention. Less than a year ago, if a magic mirror had shown Gwendolen her fate, she would have imagined herself glowing with triumph, confident she could shape a brilliant future with her wit and spirit.

Now, she wondered why she felt so little joy in the exaltation she had dreamed of—so far from the petty, stifling world of her youth, with its endless sisters and lack of distinction. She longed to feel wildly elated, to forget everything but the moment’s glory. But her thoughts clung like persistent tormentors, refusing sleep.

Wandering through lines of admiring guests in the country dance that traditionally opened the ball, she was watched by women who envied her—this woman who had risen from nothing, without a penny, yet carried herself with astonishing grace. Even if she had been a princess, she could not have claimed the evening’s honors with greater ease.

Poor Gwendolen. Soon, it would become a skill—automatically practiced—to bear this final, great loss with perfect composure.

_Aspern._ Pardon, my lord—I speak for Sigismund.
_Fronsberg._ For him? Oh, ay—for him I always hold a pardon safe in bank, sure he will draw sooner or later on me. What his need? Mad project broken? Fine mechanic wings that would not fly? Durance, assault on watch, bill for Epernay, not a crust to eat?
_Aspern._ Oh, none of these, my lord; he has escaped from Circe’s herd, and seeks to win the love of your fair ward Cecilia: but would win first your consent. You frown.
_Fronsberg._ Distinguish words. I said I held a pardon, not consent.

Though Daniel Deronda longed to return to London—his concern for Mirah mingling with a growing curiosity about the mysterious Mordecai—he did not arrive before Sir Hugo, who had gone ahead of his family to prepare for the opening of Parliament on February sixth. Deronda settled into his rooms in Park Lane, knowing full well they were already occupied by Hans Meyrick. That was expected. But what followed was not.

We’ve all seen Retzsch’s drawing of destiny as Mephistopheles playing chess with man for his soul—a game where the devil feigns carelessness, luring the mortal into misplaced defenses while the true danger looms unseen. The fiend mocks preparation, convincing us to leave our waterproofs behind when the sky is clear, knowing full well it will rain. He counts on our folly to turn illusion into prejudice, rather than study the signs. It is a rare test of character when, after carefully preparing for one kind of trial, we discover our foresight was off-target—our best intentions no more than finely cut dovetails built upon a false foundation. We brace ourselves for one kind of failure, only to face an entirely different demand on our spirit. Something like this befell Deronda.

At first, he found nothing but delight in the transformation of his sitting room into a lively studio—scattered with sketches, two chests from Rome unpacked, windows half-shaded with baize, and Hans Meyrick presiding over the chaos like some whimsical muse. His hair longer, his face more deeply creased with expression, his voice still rising with excitement, Hans was as animated as ever. Their friendship, forged during their Cambridge days, had endured through letters, shared travels, and quiet companionship. The original balance—Deronda’s trust, Hans’s indulgence—had deepened into something richer, a mutual exchange of spirit, nurtured over time.

“I knew you’d want to see my casts and antiquities,” Hans said, after the usual greetings and inquiries. “So I didn’t hesitate to bring them here. But I’ve found two rooms in Chelsea—just a few hundred yards from my mother and sisters—and I’ll be moving soon. Just waiting for the walls to be scraped and new lights installed. But I’m not waiting to begin work. You can’t imagine how great I’m going to be. The seed of immortality has sprouted within me.”

“Only a fungoid growth, I dare say—a disease blooming in the lungs,” Deronda replied, used to teasing Hans like a brother. He walked toward the bookshelf, where five rapid sketches leaned against the edge—different angles of the same face. He stood back, silent. Hans, too, paused, picked up his palette, and began touching up the painting on his easel.

“What do you think?” Hans asked at last.

“The full face looks too massive,” Deronda said, colder than usual. “Otherwise, the likenesses are good.”

“No, it’s not too massive,” Hans insisted. “I’ve noted that. There’s always a little surprise when you go from profile to full view. But I’ll enlarge her scale for Berenice. I’m doing a whole series—look along there. And now I realize—you’re just the model I need for Agrippa.” Hans, pencil and palette still in hand, moved beside Deronda, then added quickly, as if catching himself, “No, no—I forgot. You don’t like posing, confound you! But I’ve found a wonderful Titus. Five figures in total. First: Berenice clinging to Gessius Florus’s knees, begging him to spare her people. That’s on the easel. Then, here: she stands on the Xystus with Agrippa, pleading with the crowd not to harm themselves in resistance.”

“Agrippa’s legs won’t work,” Deronda said.

“They’re fine realistically,” Hans replied, grinning. “Public men often wobble. ‘Their legs, the emblem of their various thought,’ as someone says in *The Rehearsal*.”

“But these are as absurd as Raphael’s Alcibiades,” Deronda objected.

“Then they’re ideal,” Hans countered. “Agrippa’s legs may have been bad. I idealize it—make them impossibly bad. Art must intensify. Don’t worry about the legs now. The third sketch: Berenice exulting at the news that Vespasian is Emperor and Titus his successor—her future as Empress of Rome.”

“You should put a scroll in her mouth. Otherwise, no one will understand.”

“That’ll make them feel their ignorance—an excellent aesthetic effect. The fourth: Titus sending Berenice away from Rome after ten years together—both reluctant, both sorrowful—*invitus invitam*, as Suetonius says. I’ve found a model for the Roman brute.”

“Will you make Berenice look fifty? She must have been.”

“No—just a few mature touches. Dark-eyed beauty ages well. Hers especially. Now, the fifth: Berenice seated alone on the ruins of Jerusalem. Pure imagination. What should have been—perhaps was. Here’s where I show a tragic absence. No one knows what became of her—that’s perfectly conveyed by ending the series without a sixth picture.” Hans struck a pose, eyes wide, breath caught, as if awaiting awe. “I break off in the Homeric style. The story is chipped off, jagged, fading into nothing—*le néant*. Can anything be more sublime? Especially in French?”

The vulgar would want to see her corpse, her burial, her will read aloud. But no—this silence is deeper. Come, see the painting on the easel. I’ve made progress.”

“That beseeching gesture is truly strong,” Deronda admitted after a moment. “You’ve been busy over Christmas. I suppose you started this since coming to London.” Neither had mentioned Mirah yet.

“No,” Hans said, adding brushstrokes. “I decided on the theme before. I take this lucky chance as an omen—I’m about to burst onto the world as a great painter. I saw a magnificent woman in Trastevere—the finest women there are half-Jewish—and she inspired me to find a grand scene for a Jewish woman in Rome. Like many learned men, I ended up taking what lay on the surface. I’ll show you a sketch of her head when I can find it.”

“She’d be a better model for Berenice,” Deronda said, unsure how to express his discomfort.

“Not at all. The model should be the most beautiful Jewess in the world—and I’ve found her.”

“Are you certain she wants to be painted in this role? I doubt any woman would find it appealing. Does she even know what you’re doing?”

“Of course. I got her to assume this very pose. Little mother sat for Gessius Florus, and Mirah clasped her knees.” Hans stepped back, studying the effect.

“I suspect she doesn’t know Berenice’s history,” Deronda said, feeling a surge of irritation he couldn’t fully justify.

“Oh, yes, she does—ladies’ edition. Berenice was a passionate patriot, but seduced by love and ambition into siding with her people’s arch-enemy. Hence, Nemesis. Mirah sees it as a tragic parable. She weeps thinking of the penitent Berenice returning to Jerusalem, sitting desolate among ruins. That was her exact phrase. I couldn’t bear to tell her I invented that part.”

“Show me your Trasteverina,” Deronda said, mostly to avoid saying something sharper.

“Mind turning over that folio?” Hans asked. “All my head studies are there. Though they’re in disarray. You might find her next to a crop-eared undergraduate.”

After flipping through a few pages, Deronda said, “These seem to be Cambridge faces and rural scenes. Perhaps I should start from the beginning.”

“No—she’s around the middle. I merged one folio into another.”

“Is this one of your undergraduates?” Deronda asked, holding up a sketch. “It’s a remarkably pleasant face.”

“That? Oh, that’s Rex Gascoigne. A fine fellow—his upper lip is particularly good. I coached him before his scholarship. He should have taken honors last Easter. But he fell ill and had to stay another year. I ought to look him up. Want to know how he’s doing.”

“There she is,” Deronda said, lifting a sketch of the Trasteverina.

“Ah,” Hans replied, glancing at it with mild disdain. “Too coarse. I was unregenerate then.”

Deronda closed the book, leaving the sketch exposed. He turned, gripping his coat collar, and said, “I know my scruples may seem excessive, Meyrick—but I must ask you to abandon this idea.”

Hans threw himself into a dramatic posture and cried, “What! My series—my immortal Berenice series? Think what you’re saying, man—destroying not a life but an immortality, as Milton said. Wait before you answer—let me lay down my tools and prepare to tear out my hair!”

He dropped his brush and palette, collapsed backward into a chair, draped himself limply over the arm, let his long hair fall across his face, raised his hooked fingers beside his head, and looked up with mock horror. Deronda had to smile as he said, “Paint as many Berenices as you like—but I wish you could feel what I do. Perhaps you will, on reflection—choose another model.”

“Why?” Hans asked, standing again, serious now.

“Because she might be recognized. Mrs. Meyrick and I want her known as a gifted singer. It’s right—and she wishes it—that she become independent. She has real chances. One introduction is secured. I’m speaking to Klesmer. Her face may become well-known—and it’s useless to explain unless you feel as I do. I believe that if Mirah understood the situation clearly, she’d strongly object to being portrayed this way—to being used as a model for such a heroine.”

As Hans listened, thumbs in his blouse belt, his face shifted from surprise to amusement—then nearly burst into laughter. But seeing Deronda’s offended look, he stopped himself. “Excuse my laughing, Deronda. You’ve never given me the upper hand before. If it weren’t about my own pictures, I’d swallow every word you said. And so you actually believe I’d get five paintings hung in public, studied closely by everyone? Zounds, man! Cider-cup and conceit never gave me such a dream. My pictures will likely remain as private as hypersensitivity desires.”

He returned to painting, filling awkward silences. Deronda stood still, realizing his concern about publicity was misplaced—but his unease remained. He wasn’t satisfied with himself or Hans, yet silence carried him through. Hans respected his friend so deeply that he felt shy when Deronda seemed wrong—but he didn’t give up easily, especially not on a whim. He continued, brushing paint:

“But suppose I *did* have a public rushing after my art like a railway series—nurses, babies, bonnet-boxes—I still don’t see justice in your objection. Every painter worth remembering has painted the face he admired most. It’s part of his soul, poured into his work. He channels his hatred into caricature, his love into sacred form. If a man could paint the woman he loves a thousand times as *Stella Maris*, inspiring courage in sailors on a thousand ships—how much greater honor to her! Isn’t that nobler than painting a vulgar gaze and calling it divine?”

“Every objection can be answered if you take broad enough ground, Hans,” Deronda said, firm now. “No moral question can be settled that way. I might accept your generalities—and still be right in saying you shouldn’t display Mirah’s face as Berenice. But I admit I was unreasonable about publicity.”

He hesitated. “Still—even privately—there may be good reasons to refrain. You must acknowledge her situation is delicate. Until she’s more independent, she should be treated as fragile as Venetian glass—protected from shocks that might shatter her fragile peace. Are you certain of your own discretion? Excuse me, Hans. Finding her has bound me to guard her. Do you understand?”

“Perfectly,” Hans said, smiling warmly. “You think I’m the kind who breaks everything I touch—and myself along with it. Fair enough. Since birth, everything I’ve loved has turned into trouble—for me or others. Everything I’ve embraced has somehow become a mess. Painting is my latest mess—and I’ll spend my life trying to escape it. You think I’ll cause trouble at home? No—I’m regenerated. You think I’m madly in love with Mirah? Quite right—I am. But you think I’ll scream and ruin everything. There you’re mistaken—excusably, but profoundly so. I’ve undergone baptism by immersion. Awe protects me. Ask the little mother.”

“You don’t count hopeless love among your troubles, then,” Deronda said, his voice deepening as Hans’s rose.

“I don’t mean to call mine hopeless,” Hans replied, coolly setting down his tools, hands in his belt, stepping back as if to assess his work.

“My dear fellow, you’re only setting yourself up for pain,” Deronda said, decisive. “She wouldn’t marry a Christian—even if she loved him. Have you heard her talk about her people, her faith?”

“That can’t last,” Hans said. “She’ll meet no tolerable Jew. Every man of that race is unbearable—‘insupportably advancing’—his nose.”

“She may return to her family. That’s what she longs for. Her mother and brother are probably strict Jews.”

“I’ll convert, if she wants,” Hans said, shrugging and laughing.

“Don’t joke, Hans. I thought you meant this seriously.”

“So I do. You think it’s desperate—but I don’t.”

“I know nothing. I can’t predict what will happen. But I can hardly imagine a greater surprise than that Mirah might have feelings for you worth building hopes on.” Deronda sensed his tone was too harsh.

“I don’t build romantic hopes on a woman’s feelings,” Hans said, amused by seriousness. “I turn to science and philosophy for romance. Nature designed Mirah to fall in love with me. The blending of races demands it—the softening of human ugliness demands it—the harmony of opposites assures it. I am the ultimate contrast—bleached Christian, incapable of singing two notes in tune. Who could stand against me?”

“I see now—it was all *persiflage*. You don’t mean a word, Meyrick,” Deronda said, placing a hand on Hans’s shoulder, relieved. “I was foolish to take you seriously.”

“Upon my honor, I do mean it,” Hans replied, turning sharply, laying his hand on Deronda’s shoulder, their eyes locked. “I’m confessing. I meant to tell you the moment you arrived. My mother says you’re Mirah’s guardian—and she feels responsible to you for every breath she takes under her roof. Well, I love her—I worship her—I won’t despair—I mean to deserve her.”

“My dear fellow, you can’t,” Deronda said quickly.

“I mean to try.”

“You won’t keep your resolve, Hans. You once promised your mother and sisters things—”

“You have a right to reproach me, old friend,” Hans said gently.

“Perhaps I’m ungenerous,” Deronda said, not apologizing. “Yet it can’t be ungenerous to warn you—you’re indulging mad, Quixotic dreams.”

“Who will be hurt but myself?” Hans said, pouting. “I won’t speak to her unless I’m sure of the answer. I won’t consult the oracles—I prefer cheerful uncertainty, as Sir Thomas Browne might say. I’d rather risk losing than be certain of winning elsewhere. And I won’t drink the poison of despair, even if you offer it. I’ve given up wine—so let me get drunk on hope and vanity.”

“With all my heart—if it helps,” Deronda said, easing Hans’s shoulder with a light push. He softened his tone, but his words were hollow. He was silent about his true feeling.

He felt that peculiar irritation that comes when others trust you as a guide—when they assume you’re untouched by desire, temptation, or weakness. We expect our mentors to be sinless—as if those who’ve recently stumbled aren’t often the wisest teachers. Throughout their friendship, Deronda had accepted Hans’s self-absorption, even enjoyed it. But now, he resented it—especially Hans’s assumption that Deronda was as far removed from rivalry for Mirah as an angel. It’s one thing to step aside willingly; another to endure others doing it for you. He’d expected Hans to cause trouble—but not trouble tinged with personal emotion. And he was ashamed that Hans’s hopes unsettled him, despite knowing they’d never come true. They stirred an image of Mirah changing—and though he denied it, the denial kept the image alive. Poor Hans seemed to be entering Deronda’s experience too deeply—going beyond the role of rescued prodigal, stirring something beyond compassion.

When Deronda visited Chelsea, Mrs. Meyrick’s relief at her son’s return was evident—but not enough to make him comfortable. Mirah seemed brighter than before, and for the first time, he saw her laugh. It happened when they spoke of Hans, naturally the mother’s favorite topic.

Mirah wanted to know if Deronda had seen Mr. Hans performing a sort of character piece without changing clothes.

“He shifts from one figure to another like a flame flickering between shapes you barely see,” Mirah said, glowing with the subject. “He’s so quick. I never liked comedy on stage—they dragged on too long. But in one minute, Mr. Hans becomes a blind bard, then Rienzi addressing the Romans, then an opera dancer, then a dejected young man—I pity them all, yet I laugh—all in one.” And then, she gave a little laugh that might have fit into a song.

“We never thought Mirah could laugh until Hans came,” Mrs. Meyrick said, noticing both she and Deronda were watching her.

“Hans seems in high spirits,” Deronda said, pleased. “I’m glad he brings you joy.”

“He’s been perfect since he returned,” Mrs. Meyrick said, keeping to herself the next thought—“if only it lasts.”

“It’s a great happiness,” Mirah said, “to see the son and brother return to this dear home. To hear them talk about their childhood—together. That sounds like heaven. To have a mother and brother who speak that way. I’ve never had it.”

“Nor I,” Deronda said, quietly.

CHAPTER XXXVIII. There are those who believe the truest tragedy lies not in Prometheus bound *after* he has stolen fire from the gods, but *before*—when he is already gripped by the invisible chains of circumstance, his soul aflame with purpose yet unable to act, trapped in a solitude where no one sees his suffering or hears his cry. Such men are not merely ignored; they are buried beneath the weight of poverty, illness, and silence, their dreams too fragile for the world’s notice. Yet there are others—few, but real—who do not merely dream, but *foresee*. Their minds shape visions so vivid they feel like prophecy: the deed they long to do appears whole and complete before it happens; the future they fear or desire rises up before them, rooted in countless impressions, growing like a seed in dark earth. These people are not less rational than others; often, they are more so—because their inner worlds are wider, more complex, like a city with many gates through which thoughts pour in from every direction. But as with all things, there are extremes: some such souls are diminished, broken, even grotesque—like a tiny creature that might fit into the palm of your hand. Still, what great idea or noble cause is free from its share of unworthy representatives? We dare not think how many claim the name “patriot” without honor, or how crowded heaven might be on Judgment Day with writers who brought their books—not just in hand, but on wagons.

This reflection is offered not as excuse, but as context—for Mordecai, whose presence had lodged itself in Daniel Deronda’s mind like a question waiting to be answered. But Deronda’s interest was only half-formed, a quiet curiosity. The man himself—thin, pale, with eyes that seemed too deep for his face—was a quiet craftsman, a scholar perhaps, living humbly like Spinoza once did. He did not match any image Deronda had held. Yet for Mordecai, the meeting was different. For years, as his body weakened and loneliness deepened, he had clung to one desperate hope: to find someone young, strong, and open-hearted enough to receive the life he could no longer live—to carry forward the wisdom, the passion, the very breath of his spirit. Not recovery—he had long abandoned that illusion—but transmission. His longing had grown into belief, then into faith—a faith sharpened, not dulled, by the certainty of his own dying.

He had spent years searching, not for a mirror of himself, but for someone *other*: a Jew, yes, but not like him—stronger, brighter, born into ease and grace. A man whose voice would flow freely, whose bearing would command respect, whose beauty would make the world see the greatness of their people. Mordecai had wandered through galleries, gazing at portraits of noble faces—those rare images of youth and strength, of sorrow that did not crumble into despair, but burned with quiet power. He had stood before one such painting, thin and worn in his fur-lined cap, unseen by others, perhaps mistaken for a beggar or a fool. But he knew the truth: that his ideas were mocked not only by prejudice, but by his own frailty—his voice, his body, his very appearance. And so he dreamed of another: not a second self, but a full life, vibrant and capable, ready to inherit all that was best in him.

His mind worked in images—so much so that his thoughts often resembled dreams: vivid, sudden, slipping from known to unknown. For years, he saw this imagined companion as a distant figure against a golden sky—dark and mysterious, moving toward him. Why golden? Because London, though grim, held moments of beauty. He loved the bridges at dawn, the river wide and calm, the city rising in dim grandeur, boats gliding into light. There, under the vast sky, his thoughts expanded, as if the air itself carried meaning. And so, when he pictured the one he waited for, the figure emerged against that luminous backdrop—no longer turned away, but advancing, face revealed: young, noble, Jewish, refined. A composite drawn from memory—faces seen in Holland, in Bohemia, from paintings that stirred his soul. It was not mere fantasy, but a spiritual need—like a child imagining love, but deeper, fiercer, shaped by loss and time.

And now, the urgency grew. Time was running out. Each breath was thinner. Was the moment near? Would the deliverer come? The one who would rescue his thoughts from oblivion, give them a place in the future of his people? Some might call it madness—this belief in a savior, this refusal to accept death as final. But Mordecai did not seek glory. He sought continuity. He wanted to be an agent, not just a witness. To love, not merely to observe. And while the sun still warms, there will always be those who say: *I am master of this moment—and I will fill it with my soul.*

Yet, despite his certainty, he did not wait passively. He tried, in small ways, to plant seeds. Two years earlier, he had moved into Ezra Cohen’s home—seen by the family as teacher, worker, oddity, saint, heretic. Little Jacob, now taller, quicker-witted, had grown close to him. Mordecai taught him English, arithmetic—yet something deeper passed between them. The boy, though unaware of it, became a vessel for Mordecai’s soul. At times, when work was done, Mordecai would invite Jacob to sit beside him, or let him jump onto his knees—small hands gripping worn limbs. Then, he would begin to recite a Hebrew poem, written long ago, pouring into it all the fire of his early dreams. “Repeat after me,” he’d say. “Let these words sink into you.”

Jacob, eager and clever, would mimic the sounds—though he didn’t understand a word. He’d roll his eyes, pull his cheeks, pinch noses—childish antics, but Mordecai endured them, breathing life into each syllable as if it were sacred. Once, the boy suddenly leapt away, landing on his hands, feet in the air, picking up a bright farthing from his pocket. Mordecai stopped mid-verse. A jolt went through him—as if the child had laughed at his prayer. “Child! Child!” he gasped, voice breaking. Jacob froze. Then, Mordecai leaned forward, seized the boy’s shoulders, whispering fiercely: “A curse upon your generation! They will dig up the mountain, tear down the golden wings, melt them into coins! They’ll break the solemn faces into earrings for wanton women! They’ll take a new name—but the angel of shame will know them, and their hearts will be tombs of dead desires!”

The words, spoken with such intensity, shook Jacob to his core. He stood trembling, convinced the world was ending. Then, as Mordecai relaxed, the fear turned to tears. Jacob wept openly, lifting his face like a little prophet in distress. Mordecai, softened by the child’s grief, drew him close, pressing his head to his chest. The danger passed. Jacob soon began to imitate the scene—turning it into play. The next day, he frightened Adelaide Rebekah in the same way, and succeeded well.

But Mordecai was shaken. He regretted the moment—the rawness, the madness of it. He was wise enough to know when he crossed the line. And so, he turned again to his hope—the coming friend, the one who would understand without needing proof. That was when he saw Daniel Deronda.

It was during his usual afternoon watch over the old bookshop. Deronda entered—tall, calm, with a face and frame that seemed to fulfill Mordecai’s vision. At first, the denial of Jewish birth struck him like a blow. But then, later, when Deronda sat at the Cohens’ table, the impression returned—stronger, confirmed by chance. Mordecai asked, “Do you know Hebrew?” He was already certain. But the answer—“No”—shattered everything. Again, he felt the weight of disappointment, like a shipwrecked man watching a distant sail vanish.

Yet the vision would not leave him. It came back—not as a ghost, but as a living presence. Deronda now appeared in his dreams, in his waking hours, against that golden sky—no longer distant, but near. The image had taken root. And though reason said no, emotion whispered yes. The longing had become a conviction. He felt the stirrings of something greater—something beyond words.

In January, the tension grew. He could no longer focus on teaching Jacob, or attending the weekly club. Something else was coming. He longed for the river—the wide sky, the bridges, the water that shimmered with life and sorrow. He needed the vastness, the breath of the world, to hold his spirit until the moment arrived.

CHAPTER XXXIX. “Before the wise, to stand—
Safe in every case!
If long you’ve suffered pain,
He knows where it lacks.
And praise you may expect;
For he sees where you hit.” —GOETHE: _West-östlicher Divan_

That very evening after Deronda’s visit to the little house in Chelsea—where the conversation had turned on Mirah’s public name—something momentous unfolded for him. But for those gathered there, the true consequence of that meeting came two days later.

At about four o’clock, wheels paused before the door, followed by a knock and ring so resonant it seemed to announce not just a visitor, but an event. In this quiet corner of London, where the most stirring sounds were often the muffin-man’s call, such a signal stirred the air with sudden importance. All the girls were at home, and the two rooms had been joined together to accommodate Kate’s latest drawing project—and a long stretch of embroidery, now taking center stage like a grand finale in their domestic artistry. It sprawled across the front room, a colorful tapestry of threads, with Mrs. Meyrick bent over one end, Mab in the middle, and Amy at the other. Mirah, whose sewing skills were more suited to patchwork than precision—her education in needlework having been neglected—sat on a camp-stool reading aloud to the group. She also served as Kate’s model for a vignette depicting a fair audience absorbed in the family tea-table’s literary offerings.

She was reciting Elia’s delightful essay, “The Praise of Chimney-Sweeps,” her voice clear and charming, drawing smiles from all at the mention of “innocent blackness,” when the knock and ring interrupted them. They looked up, startled.

“Dear me!” said Mrs. Meyrick. “Could it be Lady Mallinger? Is there a grand carriage, Amy?”

“No—only a hansom cab,” Amy replied. “It must be a gentleman.”

“The Prime Minister, I should think,” Kate said dryly. “Hans says even the greatest man in London can ride in a hansom.”

“Oh, oh, oh!” Mab cried. “Suppose it’s Lord Russell!”

Their faces lit with amusement when the old maid-servant rushed in, card in hand, and left the parlor door open. And there, bowing toward Mrs. Meyrick, stood a figure quite unlike any political leader—tall, commanding, even in his simple coat and kerseymere, with a massive face, fiery hair, and gold spectacles. From the card, Mrs. Meyrick read: _Julius Klesmer_.

Even if she’d been flustered, the “little mother” would have remained composed. But she quickly grasped the significance: the great musician had come to Mirah, not the other way around—a sign of genuine interest. Yet as he stepped inside, the small rooms seemed to shrink into closets. The cottage piano looked absurdly tiny, and the whole household felt suddenly insignificant, like mice in the Tuileries.

Klesmer’s presence alone evoked vast halls and crowded audiences. His glance swept the room as though he were surveying a concert hall, and indeed, he likely did—because we each carry our inner world within a familiar setting, and those who imagine larger stages often seem pompous to others. Klesmer was vain, yes—but no more so than many men of stature whose vanity bursts forth like a hidden blade. His gestures were natural, his bearing effortless. To appear diffident would have been the true affectation.

As he took in the scene, his sensitivity revealed itself—not just in observation, but in empathy. He remembered a modest home on the edge of Bohemia, and knew well the dignity found in humble lives. He addressed Mrs. Meyrick with deep respect.

“I hope I’m not overstepping,” he said. “Being nearby, I thought I’d save time by calling. Mr. Deronda mentioned that I might meet a young lady here—Miss Lapidoth.”

Though he’d seen Mirah the moment he entered, he glanced politely around at the three sisters as if uncertain which was the one in question.

“These are my daughters,” Mrs. Meyrick said, gesturing toward Mirah. “This is Miss Lapidoth.”

“Ah,” Klesmer replied, turning a radiant smile and deep bow toward her. Mirah, calm and unflustered, met his gaze with quiet joy. She liked him—felt sure he would scold her, like a great artist and a kind soul.

“You’ll not mind beginning our acquaintance with a song?” he asked, eager to skip the formalities.

“I’d be honored,” Mirah said. “It’s generous of you to listen.” She moved to the piano.

“Shall I accompany myself?” she asked.

“By all means,” Klesmer replied, seating himself where he could see her clearly. Mrs. Meyrick didn’t admit it, but she thought: *He’ll appreciate her singing more if he sees her.*

All hearts—except Mirah’s—beat fast. Klesmer sat with a stern, listening frown, and they dared only glance at him sideways. If he criticized, it would hurt them all. Still, they comforted themselves: Prince Camaralzaman, who’d heard the finest voices, had preferred Mirah’s. And she sang with such care—more at ease than usual.

She chose a powerful setting of words from Leopardi’s Ode to Italy:
“_O patria mia, vedo le mura e gli archi / E le colonne e i simulacri e l’erme / Torridi degli avi nostri._”
Recitative. Then: “_Ma la gloria—non vedo_”—a mournful melody, a rhythmic lament. Then, rising into triumph: “_Beatissimi voi / Che offriste il petto alle nemiche lance / Per amor di costei che al sol vi diede_”—a soft adoration. Then, bursting forth: “_Oh viva, oh viva: Beatissimi voi / Mentre nel mondo si favelli o scriva._”

When she finished, Klesmer sat silent for a moment. Then: “That is old Leo’s music.”

“Yes,” Mirah said softly. “He was my last teacher in Vienna—so fierce, yet so good.” She smiled wistfully. “He said my voice wasn’t meant for the stage. And he was right.”

“_Con_tinue,” Klesmer said, pursing his lips, shaking his fingers, muttering something unintelligible.

The three girls hated him—why say nothing? Mrs. Meyrick felt a pang. Mirah, focused only on obeying, assumed he wanted German next. She sang through Prince Radzivill’s settings of Gretchen’s songs from _Faust_, one after another, without pause.

When she finished, Klesmer rose, walked to the far end of the room, then returned to the piano. Mirah stood, hands crossed before her, waiting.

Then—suddenly—his brow softened, his eyes gleamed, and he reached out his hand. “Let us shake hands,” he said firmly. “You are a musician.”

Mab felt tears rise. The girls all fell in love with him.

Mrs. Meyrick exhaled deeply.

But instantly, the frown returned. He held out his hand again, palm up, and gently touched the back of Mirah’s with his fingertip. “Not for great tasks,” he said, lip protruding. “No high roofs. We are no skylarks. We must be modest.”

Mab’s admiration cooled. *As if Mirah had shown the least pride!*

Mirah waited in silence. Klesmer continued: “I wouldn’t advise or support your singing in any space larger than a private drawing-room. But there, you will do. And in London, that’s one of the best paths open. Lessons will follow. Will you sing at a private concert at my house on Wednesday?”

“Oh, I’d be grateful,” Mirah said, hands clasped devoutly. “I’d rather earn my bread that way than through anything more public. I’ll try to improve. What should I focus on?”

Klesmer muttered, fingers twitching, then spoke clearly: “I’ll introduce you to Astorga—he is the foster-father of good singing. He’ll guide you.”

Then, turning to Mrs. Meyrick: “Mrs. Klesmer will call before Wednesday, if you allow it.”

“We’ll consider it a great kindness,” Mrs. Meyrick said.

“You’ll sing for her,” Klesmer added, turning back to Mirah. “She is a true musician, and has more ears than most. Your singing will satisfy her. ‘Vor den Wissenden sich stellen.’ You know the rest?”

“‘Sicher ist’s in allen Fällen,’” Mirah replied promptly.

“Schön!” Klesmer said, extending his hand again. He had praised her in the most delicate way. The girls now admired him fully.

Then, fixing his gaze on Mab, he declared: “That young lady is musical, I see.”

She blushed, burned.

“Yes,” Mirah said, defending her. “And she has a touch.”

“Oh, please, Mirah—a scramble, not a touch,” Mab said, trembling. *What would he demand next?* This man—this terrible, knowing spirit—might make her sit at the piano. Her heart felt molten.

But instead, he smiled kindly. “Will she accompany Miss Lapidoth and hear the music on Wednesday?”

“There could hardly be a greater pleasure,” Mrs. Meyrick said. “She’ll be delighted.”

Klesmer bowed to the three sisters—more grandly than they’d ever been bowed to. The image was amusing: the small room, the immense sweep of his bow, the sisters like miniature figures, the Holbein portraits on the walls watching silently, as if witnessing a drama beyond their time.

Mrs. Meyrick slipped out with him, closing the door behind her.

He understood her. “She will do,” he said, nodding. “If she doesn’t overreach, and her voice holds, she can earn a living. I know that’s the point—Deronda told me. You’re looking after her. She seems a good girl.”

“She is an angel,” Mrs. Meyrick said warmly.

“No,” Klesmer replied, playfully. “She is a pretty Jewess. Let’s not give angels all the credit. But I think she’s found a guardian angel.” He bowed and left.

The four girls stared at each other in stunned silence until the door slammed. Then—explosion.

Mab clapped and danced wildly. Mrs. Meyrick kissed Mirah and blessed her. Amy exclaimed: “We can never get her a new dress before Wednesday!” Kate said: “Thank heaven my table isn’t knocked over!”

Mirah had quietly sat back on her stool, tears streaming down her cheeks.

“Now, now, Mab,” Mrs. Meyrick said. “Come sit and let’s talk.”

“Yes, let’s talk,” Mab agreed, returning to her seat, caressing her knees. “I feel large again. Hans said he’d come this afternoon. I wish he’d been here—though there’d have been no room for him. Mirah, why are you sad?”

“I’m too happy,” Mirah said. “Full of gratitude. And he was so kind.”

“Yes, at last,” Mab said sharply. “But he might have said something encouraging sooner. I thought him dreadfully ugly when he frowned and said only ‘_Con_tinue.’ I hated him—from the top of his head to the toe of his boot.”

“Nonsense,” Kate said. “He has a splendid profile.”

“_Now_, but not _then_,” Mab insisted. “I can’t bear people who bottle up their thoughts just to burst them out with a pop. It feels like they begrudge your happiness unless they make you miserable first. But I forgive him everything—though he invited me. Why did he pick me as the musical one? Was it because I have a bulging forehead and peep from under it like a newt under a stone?”

“It was how you listened,” Mrs. Meyrick said. “He sees everything through those magic spectacles.”

“What was that German quote, Mirah?” Amy asked. “You learned it so quickly.”

“Oh, that wasn’t learning,” Mirah said, smiling through her tears. “I repeated it for lessons. It means: it’s safer to perform—sing, or do anything—before those who truly understand.”

“That’s why you weren’t afraid,” Amy said.

“But now—we must talk about your dress for Wednesday.”

“I don’t need anything better than this black merino,” Mirah said, standing to show it. “Just white gloves and new _bottines.”_ She lifted her foot, still clad in the famous felt slipper.

“There comes Hans,” Mrs. Meyrick said. “Stand still—let’s hear what he thinks.”

Hans entered. Mirah turned toward him childlike. He knelt on a hassock to examine her.

“This would be a fine stage-dress for me,” she said pleadingly, “if I were playing a poor Jewess singing to fashionable Christians.”

“It would stand out,” Hans said thoughtfully. “Among the fashionable _chiffons_.”

“But you shouldn’t claim all the poverty,” Amy said. “There are poor Christians and rich Jews alike.”

“I didn’t mean harm,” Mirah said. “I’ve always thought about dresses for roles. I usually had plain ones.”

“That makes it questionable,” Hans said, suddenly conventional—just as he’d judged Deronda over the Berenice paintings. “It looks a bit theatrical. We mustn’t make you a _rôle_—not of a poor Jewess, nor of being a Jewess at all.”

“But it’s who I am,” Mirah said. “I’m not pretending. I’ll never be anything else. I always feel myself a Jewess.”

“But we don’t feel that,” Hans said, earnestly. “What does it matter whether a perfect woman is a Jewess?”

“That’s your kind way of praising me,” Mirah smiled. “I’ve never been praised so.”

“People don’t see me as British Christian,” Hans said, grinning. “They see me as an imperfectly handsome young man and an unpromising painter.”

“But the dress—the dress!” Amy insisted.

“Yes—is it settled?” Mirah asked, looking at Mrs. Meyrick, who glanced at her son.

“What do you think, Hans?”

“That dress won’t do,” Hans said firmly. “She’s not going to sit on ruins. Little mother, jump into a cab with her and go to Regent Street. Plenty of time to get a black silk dress—like ladies wear. She mustn’t be mistaken for charity. She has talents that make people owe her.”

“I think Mr. Deronda would want her to have a fine dress,” Mrs. Meyrick said.

“Of course he would,” Hans said sharply. “Take my word—any gentleman would.”

“I wish to do what Mr. Deronda would want,” Mirah said gravely.

Hans turned away, picked up one of Kate’s drawings.

“Shouldn’t you sketch Klesmer’s head, Hans?” Kate asked. “Have you seen him often?”

“Seen him!” Hans exclaimed, throwing back his head, seating himself at the piano, surveying the room as if it were an amphitheater, fingers poised above the keys.

Then, in a flash, he wheeled around, looked at Mirah, and said half-shyly: “Perhaps you don’t like this mimicry. Always stop me when you don’t.”

Mirah had been smiling—now she smiled more, but with something deeper. “Thank you. But you’ve never done anything I didn’t like. I hardly think he could, belonging to you,” she added, looking at Mrs. Meyrick.

And so Hans found nourishment for his hope. How could the rose resist when several bees, one after another, took its sweetness as proof of personal devotion?

“Within the soul a faculty abides,
That with interpositions, which would hide
And darken, so can deal, that they become
Contingencies of pomp; and serve to exalt
Her native brightness, as the ample moon,
In the deep stillness of a summer even,
Rising behind a thick and lofty grove,
Burns, like an unconsuming fire of light,
In the green trees; and, kindling on all sides
Their leafy umbrage, turns the dusky veil
Into a substance glorious as her own,
Yea, with her own incorporated, by power
Capacious and serene.” —WORDSWORTH: _Excursion_, B. IV.

Daniel Deronda stepped out of the narrow house in Chelsea, his mind restless, stirred by emotions he could not yet name—what he called the fumes of temper. He needed motion, something physical to clear the air within him. Heading toward London, he saw the Chelsea Stairs and the boats waiting there, and without hesitation, chose to row rather than ride in a cab. The act of pulling the oar felt like a release—each stroke grounding him in the present, pushing away the weight of thought.

His destination was Ram’s bookshop, where he had missed Mordecai the day before. He’d been told the man returned between five and six. Deronda wanted more than just a conversation—he sought understanding, connection. He didn’t want their meeting to end again as it had before, abruptly, like a drawbridge raised too soon. He hoped this time, they might speak longer, deeper, and perhaps finally unlock something real.

As he rowed, the rhythm of the water and the effort of the oar shifted his thoughts. He began to see himself not just as someone seeking help for Mirah, but as one who might be using others—like a recruiting sergeant who takes no interest in the lives of those he enlists. Was he doing the same? Was he treating Mordecai merely as a tool?

*If I get what I need from him,* Deronda mused, *would I care if he never spoke of himself again? If he had expectations I couldn’t meet? It’s possible we’ve passed each other like ships at sea—both carrying exiles who might have recognized one another, had they seen each other’s faces. But is there such a thing as missing someone you’ve never known? Or longing for absence in a crowded room? Still… I’ve made my choices. I’ve walked into this moment, and now I must face it.*

By the time the wherry neared Blackfriars Bridge, it was half-past four. The sky was dying in splendor—purple streaks fading into saffron gold, calm above, but alive below. On the river, reflections danced: ripples flared, sails glowed, barges slipped from shadow into color. It was a scene both still and stirring, like the quiet before a revelation.

Deronda, warmed now, handed the oar back and pulled his Inverness cape tighter. As he fastened the top button, he looked up—and froze.

There, silhouetted against the western light, was a face he knew well. Pale, thin, yet glowing with intensity—Mordecai stood on the bridge, gazing down. And then, as if drawn by some unseen force, he lifted a white hand and waved—not once, but again and again, urgently.

Deronda had signaled first, eager that Mordecai should recognize him. And now, the answer came swiftly. Mordecai raised his cap and waved back—his whole being alight with fulfillment. For him, this was prophecy fulfilled. Obstacles dissolved. Doubts vanished. This was not mere chance—it was destiny arriving. His joy was like that of a scientist watching the first sign of life in a long-expected experiment. The figure from his dreams had come, and had acknowledged him. The future now lay open.

Three minutes later, Deronda stepped ashore, paid the boatman, and approached Mordecai, who stood motionless, waiting.

“I was glad to see you here,” Deronda said. “I meant to go to the bookshop, but I’m glad you’re already waiting.”

“Yes,” Mordecai replied, voice low and steady. “That’s why I came to the bridge.”

The simplicity of the reply startled Deronda. Was this man truly unbalanced? Had Cohen been right in hinting at something strange?

“You didn’t know I was at Chelsea?” Deronda asked after a pause.

“No,” Mordecai said. “But I expected you to come down the river. I’ve been waiting for you—for five years.”

His eyes, deep-set and dark, held Deronda’s with an almost unbearable tenderness. There was a childlike dependence in them, mixed with solemnity. Deronda felt a pang—not of doubt, but of sorrow. He believed, even as he feared, that this man was living in illusion.

“If I can help you,” he said earnestly, “I will. Shall we take a cab? You’ve walked enough, I think—your breath is short.”

“Let us go to the shop,” Mordecai said quietly. “It will soon be time. But look upriver,” he added, turning his gaze back to the water, speaking in a hushed tone that carried the weight of emotion. “See how the sky fades. I’ve always loved this bridge. As a boy, I stood here. It’s a place where spiritual messengers meet. The Masters were right—the world has its angels, its full messages from afar. Here, I’ve listened to the voices of earth and sky. When I was strong, I watched for stars in the deep night. But sunset—this hour—was always my favorite. It faded slowly, like my own life. It paused. It waited. And now… it has brought me my new life—my new self—who will live when this breath is gone.”

Deronda said nothing. He felt deeply moved. The earlier suspicion—that Mordecai might be deluded, perhaps even mad—gave way to something quieter, more profound. He wasn’t ready to dismiss this man’s vision. He had always believed in depths beyond his own experience. And now, standing here, he felt a call—not to judge, but to listen.

After a moment, Mordecai turned. “Let us go.”

They rode in silence, save for the rattling of the cab. Deronda’s thoughts drifted to Mirah—but now, he sensed that Mordecai would shape the conversation, not he. He found himself wondering: what questions could he ask? What truths might he uncover?

*I suppose,* he thought, *I am in a state of superstition—waiting for fate to reveal itself. But isn’t faith, even when mistaken, more powerful than reason? Expectation, even when disappointed—hasn’t it shaped the world? Will I fulfill this man’s hope—or break it? If I can, I will not fail.*

Ten minutes later, they arrived at the bookshop. The small space was lit by gas, dim and intimate. They stood facing each other, both removing their hats instinctively—as if they wished to see each other clearly, fully.

Mordecai leaned against the counter. Deronda stood across from him, barely four feet away. Their faces were etched in contrast: Mordecai—pale, gaunt, with the sharp lines of a man worn by illness, yet burning with inner fire; Deronda—young, strong, composed, with a quiet dignity that seemed to hold space around him.

Imagine Mordecai: a Jewish face, marked by suffering and intellect, hair and beard still black, skin yellowed by disease, hands thin and fragile. Yet his eyes—bright, yearning, filled with the look of a mother seeing her son one last time. A sense of continuity, of legacy, radiated from him.

And opposite him—Deronda: not exotic, but striking in his natural grace, his presence calm and attentive. He did not look like a man of the world, nor a scholar, but someone who could hold the weight of another’s soul.

This moment tested Deronda’s deepest nature. He didn’t believe Mordecai’s words were true—but he felt the truth of his pain. He felt the cry from the depths of another human heart, and with it, the demand to receive, not reject.

Receptiveness—rare, like courage—had taken hold of him. His face softened, became still, gentle, powerful. And in that stillness, Mordecai found strength.

“I cannot tell you what led me to you,” Mordecai began. “You wonder?”

“I am not impatient,” Deronda said. “I am ready to listen.”

“You see some reasons why I need you,” Mordecai continued, voice quiet, conserving energy. “You see I am dying. I am like one behind bars, unheard, pitied. The day is closing. Soon, we would not have seen each other. But you have come in time.”

“I rejoice that I have come in time,” Deronda said, feeling the truth of it.

“But the deeper reasons begin far back,” Mordecai went on. “When I was young, studying abroad, I found ideas—beloved ideas—because I was a Jew. They were a trust, an inspiration. My life began then. I counted every breath, every meal, every waking hour—only as fuel for the divine flame.”

He pressed a hand to his chest, then stretched his fingers forward.

“But then—care, labor, disease—blocked my path. I was bound by iron that eats into the soul. I asked: How shall I save the life within me, when my breath is stifled?”

He paused, catching his breath. Deronda dared not speak. The silence hummed with awe.

“And yet,” Mordecai continued, “you may think I speak like a dreamer. But I know English—my mother tongue. I was born here. But my soul was fed in Holland, at my mother’s brother’s side—a rabbi of learning. Then Hamburg, Göttingen—I studied widely. I was free then. I had a craft. I said: Let my body dwell in poverty, but let my soul be a temple of memory—where knowledge enters, and hope remains.”

He smiled faintly. “They called me a visionary. I did not deny it. Visions create the world. I see the world as it is—and as it will be. You are not listening to madness.”

“I am not,” Deronda said softly. “I listen to understand.”

“A spiritual destiny embraced willingly—in youth?” Mordecai repeated, correcting gently. “No. It was born in me as a child. It brought its own world—a medieval world, where men revived the ancient language in new psalms of exile. They absorbed Gentile thought into Jewish faith, yet longed for a center for our people. One of their souls was reborn in me. I walked with them—through Spain, Provence. I debated with Aben-Ezra. I sailed with Jehuda ha-Levi. I heard the Crusaders roar, the cries of tortured Israel.”

He paused, then whispered, hoarse but fierce:

“While it is imprisoned in me, it will never learn another.”

“Have you written entirely in Hebrew?” Deronda asked, remembering his own ignorance of the language.

“Yes,” Mordecai said, voice heavy with sadness. “In youth, I wandered into solitude, but I did not feel alone. The great dead surrounded me. The martyrs listened. But soon, the living were deaf. I thought my life would stretch long. I believed in patience, in finding roots where others failed. But then—new messengers came. Family troubles called. I had to work. I was left alone again. And death—his shadow was near.”

He sighed. “I pleaded. I spoke to leaders, scholars, merchants. No one listened. Some mocked. One said: ‘The Book of Mormon would never work in Hebrew. If you want to teach our learned men, you’ll fail.’ He touched a truth.”

A dry irony lingered in his voice.

“But though you write in Hebrew,” Deronda said gently, “few use English better than you.”

Mordecai shook his head. “Too late. I cannot write now. My words would be like this breath—gasping. But the breath may awaken pity. The writing—no. If I wrote now in English, I’d be like one beating a board to summon those who only hear a bell. My soul hears the flaws in its own speech. New writing would be like this body”—he spread his arms—“within it, there might be Ruach-ha-kodesh—the breath of divine thought—but men would smile and say, ‘A poor Jew!’ And the worst smilers—would be of my own people.”

He let his hands fall. His spirit sank. Despair hovered.

“I feel with you,” Deronda said, his voice clear and warm. “But forgive me—if I speak hastily—your writings need not die. Publication is possible. Trust me. I can make it happen.”

“That is not enough,” Mordecai said quickly, lifting his head. “I need more than a hand. I need a soul. One who believes my belief, feels my hope, sees the glory I see! You must be my life—reborn. You must take the inheritance. It has gathered through ages. Generations crowd upon my narrow life—past and future meet here. The bridge is breaking. But I have found you. You have come in time. You will carry the sacred inheritance of the Jew.”

Deronda paled. Fear surged—not of failure, but of harm. He dreaded discouraging this man, yet also feared feeding an illusion. The grip on his arm was urgent, pleading. He could not refuse, not now.

With quiet instinct, he placed his palm gently over Mordecai’s hand—an act of peace, of solidarity. Then he spoke, slowly, carefully.

“Do you forget what I told you before? That I am not of your race?”

“It can’t be true,” Mordecai whispered, unshaken. The touch had fortified him.

A pause. Deronda could not answer. Mordecai pressed on, his voice rising with conviction.

“You are not sure of your own origin.”

“How do you know that?” Daniel asked, withdrawing his hands.

“I know it. I know it. What is my life else?” Mordecai said, almost pleading. “Tell me everything. Why do you deny?”

CHAPTER XLI. “This, too, is probable, according to that saying of Agathon: ‘It is a part of probability that many improbable things will happen.’” —ARISTOTLE: _Poetics_.

Imagine the turmoil within a mind like Deronda’s—deeply feeling, yet relentlessly questioning—on the evening after his encounter with Mordecai. For a man less sensitive, such an experience might have stirred curiosity or surprise; but for Deronda, it had struck at the very core of his being. The intensity of the moment left him unsettled, and as was his nature, he turned inward, dissecting his emotions with the precision of reason. He felt, with unease, how much of his reaction had been shaped by Mordecai’s fervor—the strength of his belief, the fire in his voice, the certainty that seemed to radiate from every word. And this awareness sparked a quiet alarm.

Deronda had always resisted what he saw as moral complacency—the tendency to dismiss what is close at hand simply because it lacks grand historical weight. If he had read of such a meeting centuries ago—between a restless youth and a dying visionary in ancient Rome or Jerusalem—it would have seemed perfectly natural that the young man would be moved. In his imagination, the scene would unfold with all the gravity of legend: robes, torchlight, prophetic gestures. Why, then, should he feel ashamed of his own stirring, merely because he wore a white tie and dined among people who would scoff at any serious reflection on life’s deeper currents? Was not that mockery—the pretense that emotional depth is mere vanity—a symptom of spiritual poverty?

He refused to yield to such cowardice. Yet he also feared yielding to impulse without thought. He could not allow himself to be swept along by reverence alone, nor let pity for another’s struggle blind him to the path ahead. What, truly, had occurred between them?

He heard clearly the voice of Sir Hugo, if not in words, then in tone: *“A dying Jew, consumed by a fanaticism sharpened by death and resistance, has chosen you as the living image of a dream—half hope, half despair. His belief is intense, yes, but it is not unique. Fanaticism may not be as common as financial ruin, but it flourishes in many forms. While one man waits on a bridge for divine fulfillment, another insists he has cracked the universe’s code, accusing physicists of conspiracy. Another claims to hold the metaphysical key, differing only by a hair’s breadth from truth. These men are scattered across the world, each convinced they must be heard. Some write books no one wants to print. This Mordecai is more poetic than most—more passionate, more compelling—but still, he belongs to the same breed. Kindness is due, but what chance is there that his ideas carry the weight he assigns them? A man of the world knows better.”*

Deronda heard these thoughts—not as others’ opinions, but as his own inner caution. He repeated them aloud in silence. It was not the first time he had faced this question: the strange kinship among those driven by passion—prophets, visionaries, reformers, inventors of impossible machines. The same hunger animates them all. The same human drama plays out again and again. Even error has its martyrs. Reduce the greatest minds to abstract qualities, and they begin to resemble fools. Say that Copernicus stood firm against ridicule—and so does the inventor of perpetual motion. We cannot judge spirits fairly by such tests. To avoid punishing the innocent, we must understand their subject matter, and share in the burden of human striving—both near and far.

So, could he say: “Let time decide?” But time begins now. And justice begins in individual hearts—separate yet linked. Even steam engines wouldn’t exist without that connection. They were born not just in Watt’s mind, but in the shared understanding of others.

This line of thought was familiar to Deronda. It kept him from dismissing Mordecai outright—even if their exchange had carried with it a claim that felt almost supernatural. That claim—*You must believe what I believe. Feel what I feel. See what I see.*—was, in rational terms, absurd. To accept it directly would be folly; to pretend to do so would be dishonesty. And yet, in the midst of his compassion, Deronda felt relief that he had not surrendered to false promises.

But the claim rested on a fragile assumption: that he, Deronda, was of Jewish blood. How hypothetical was that! Yet since the age of thirteen, he had lived with another hypothesis—equally unproven, yet deeply felt—that Sir Hugo was his father. That belief had shaped his emotions, fueled his struggles, given meaning to his love and resentment. He had long practiced living with possibility, even when it might be disproved. He had prepared himself for revelations that might alter his sense of duty.

To live in suspense, yet emotionally engaged, was not foreign to him. And now, suppose this imagined Jewish heritage—and Mordecai’s desperate call to discipleship—were not illusion, but the prelude to truth? Suppose Mordecai’s beliefs actually took root in him? Could it be that, as Mordecai found renewal through Deronda’s presence, so Deronda might find form in Mordecai’s vision? Might he discover, in that other’s mind, the complete shape of a duty that had long lain fragmented in his own thoughts—like sculptures waiting to be joined?

The idea was dreamlike. And yet, he began to defend it. If the influence had come from a celebrated scholar, a philosopher, a revered teacher—would receptiveness be mocked? Only by those who fear dependence on others’ ideas, who prefer to imagine they’ve always held the truth in silent superiority.

And what of Mordecai’s poverty? Was that enough to dismiss his spirit? There was a tale of Emperor Domitian, who, hearing of a Jewish family descended from David, summoned them in fear—only to release them upon seeing their work-worn hands. He judged them unworthy. But the Rabbi who waited at Rome’s gate believed the Messiah would come among the poor. Both were wrong. Outer signs mean nothing. Poverty does not prove inspiration—but it has often walked beside it. To reject discipleship because of it would be dullness, not wisdom.

A stronger objection: Mordecai’s mind was fevered, his visions overpowering. Did such passion allow for sound judgment? Could someone who sees the world through dreams be trusted to assess consequences? Perhaps not. But perhaps—just perhaps—Mordecai was one of those rare souls where passion and foresight are fused. Where belief shapes reality not through delusion, but through force of will.

History shows us that great discoveries have begun this way. Science itself depends on the courage to believe before proof arrives. Passion, in human affairs, is even more potent. Enthusiasm can be proof. One soul’s fire may become tomorrow’s light.

So, Mordecai’s excitement was not, in itself, a reason to dismiss him. Suppose he had presented himself as a cold logician. Would that make him free from error? Even the driest argument can fall into fantasy—concluding too hastily that it has finally grasped the universe. Men dream in proofs. They build illusory worlds from axioms and conclusions, ending with a neat Q.E.D. No formula protects us from error. The unemotional mind may wander into mathematical dreams where nothing exists except what never did. Perhaps the emotional mind, though, sees truths ahead—truths that will one day be real, as the artist sees patterns science later explains.

At any rate, assumptions must not be trusted. We must endure our imperfect thinking—whether in whole history or in single minds. Columbus held beliefs we call superstitions, used arguments we now scorn. Yet he also had accurate physical insights and the genius to persist. The world laughed at those who ignored him. “My contempt for them,” Deronda said to himself, “binds me to avoid repeating their mistake on a smaller scale—to refuse to deafen myself to the possibility that this man and I may be connected in ways I cannot yet see.”

What mattered was not how Mordecai came to believe, but what might come of their meeting. If he were truly of Jewish descent, and if he came to embrace Mordecai’s faith—then the encounter would be transformative. But if not? Then he would be the cause of deep sorrow. He would have to deliver a refusal that might crush Mordecai’s last hope. And though tenderness could soften the blow, it could not erase the pain.

Would it be worse to disappoint him—or to deny himself the chance of something greater? Here, Deronda hesitated.

Late in life, he had begun to feel differently about his possible Jewish roots. The old reluctance had softened. And yes—he was romantic. The same youthful energy that drives countless adventurers to seek hidden origins stirred in him. The idea that he might be walking a path of discovery—of identity, purpose, belonging—excited him. Not because he sought glory, but because it touched something deeper: the longing to know oneself.

Yet he could not call it anything more than a possibility. His belief that Sir Hugo was his father remained strong. To imagine otherwise felt like betrayal—not just of memory, but of love. He loved Sir Hugo fiercely, beyond anger or doubt. To entertain the thought that this bond was a lie was painful beyond words.

Still, he repeated to himself what he had told Mordecai: *I cannot hasten the truth without clearer grounds.* And now, he found himself tempted to keep uncertainty alive—not as evasion, but as protection. If future meetings revealed only illusion, then the lack of proof might spare Mordecai the worst wound—the denial of kinship. Perhaps even, it could be used gently to encourage friendship, to offer comfort while preserving dignity.

These were the thoughts that filled Deronda’s mind during the four days before he could keep his promise to visit Mordecai at Ezra Cohen’s. Sir Hugo’s demands often stretched late into the night, making any evening journey to Holborn impossible. But the silence of those days was thick with questions—questions not just of blood, but of belief, of duty, of the strange, trembling possibility that a man might find himself not in the mirror of society, but in the eyes of a stranger who saw him as a chosen one.

“If there are ranks in suffering, Israel stands at the top—if endurance and quiet strength elevate a people, then the Jews belong among the noblest of all nations—if literature is deemed rich for a few classic tragedies, what name can we give to a national tragedy lasting fifteen hundred years, written and lived by its own heroes?”

Daniel Deronda had recently read these words by Zunz, and they echoed in his mind as he walked toward the Cohen household—where sorrow, if it existed at all, wore no grand or solemn face. Ezra Cohen was not draped in the tragic dignity of martyrdom; instead, he thrived on commerce, his success in money-making a sharp contrast to the long history of Jewish hardship. This pawnbroker, this Jeshurun of trade, was no symbol of ancient sorrow. Yet could it not be said that within this ordinary home—a place of bustling prosperity and unreflective contentment—lived Mordecai, a fragile vessel carrying the weight of a nation’s memory, breathing with difficulty, yet still alive?

The Cohens greeted Deronda with open joy. Their faces shone with warmth when he arrived. Cohen himself remarked, half-jokingly, that while the diamond ring might have brought more profit if kept longer, he cared nothing for that now—not even a sixpence—compared to the pleasure it gave the women and children to see a young man whose first visit had been so delightful they’d “talked about it ever since.”

Young Mrs. Cohen apologized that the baby was asleep but was delighted that Adelaide was still awake. She urged Deronda to leave the shop and come into the parlor to meet “mother and the children.” He willingly followed, having brought small gifts: paper figures for Adelaide and an ivory cup-and-ball game for Jacob.

In the parlor, the grandmother sat with cards before her, making “plates” with the children—stacking them carefully until one fell apart. Just as Deronda entered, Jacob dashed forward.

“Stop!” he called. “Don’t step on my plate! Wait—let me throw it up again!”

Deronda paused, sharing a quiet smile with the grandmother as Jacob tossed the card stack high. It held together through several throws before finally breaking. Then, at last, the visitor was allowed to sit down.

He noticed that the door from which Mordecai had emerged during their earlier meeting was now closed. But Deronda wanted to show interest in the family before revealing his deeper concern for the man inside.

Only after settling Adelaide on his knee and setting the paper figures dancing on the table—while Jacob practiced catching the ball in the cup—did Deronda ask softly,

“Is Mordecai here?”

“Where is he, Addy?” Cohen called out, stepping in from a brief moment of business.

“In the workroom,” answered his wife, nodding toward the shut door.

“The truth is, sir,” Cohen said, “we don’t know what’s come over him lately. He’s always been a little… off, you understand”—he tapped his temple—“not quite as clear-headed as you or I. But usually he’s steady, hardworking, and takes real pride in the boy. But these past days, he’s been wandering like someone asleep, or sitting perfectly still, like a statue.”

“It’s the illness, poor dear,” murmured the grandmother gently. “I fear he won’t hold out much longer.”

“No,” said the younger Mrs. Cohen, “it’s something in his head. He’s been turning over papers constantly. When I speak to him, it takes ages for him to respond.”

“We may seem weak ourselves,” Cohen added, somewhat apologetically. “But my wife and mother wouldn’t let him go—even if he were worse than he is. We know how things stand, but it’s our principle. There are fools who lose money without knowing it—I’m not one of them.”

“Oh, Mordecai carries a blessing inside him,” the grandmother insisted.

“He has something wrong inside,” corrected Jacob, marching up with seriousness. “He told me he couldn’t talk to me, and he wouldn’t take any bread.”

“I don’t find your kindness surprising,” said Deronda. “In fact, I feel something of the same myself. I’ve spoken with him at Ram’s bookshop—and I promised to come here to fetch him. We’re going out together.”

“That’s it!” Cohen exclaimed, slapping his knee. “He’s been waiting for you. It’s taken hold of him. I suppose he talks about his learning to you. That’s very kind of you, sir. I doubt there’s much use in it—else he wouldn’t be where he is. But there’s the shop.” With that, he hurried back to his duties.

Jacob, who had been standing close to Deronda’s side, offered, “I’ll call Mordecai for you, if you like.”

“No, Jacob,” his mother said firmly. “Open the door quietly for the gentleman. Let him go in alone. Hush—don’t make a sound.”

Jacob obeyed, turning the handle with care. Deronda stepped behind him and paused at the threshold.

The room was dim—lit only by a dying fire and a single candle beneath a shade. On the board under the window lay scattered jewelry, and in the corner, a pile of books. Mordecai sat on a high chair, back to the door, hands folded on the board, a watch propped upright before him. He was in a state of painful expectation—like a prisoner listening for a delayed release.

Then Deronda spoke: “I’ve come for you. Are you ready?”

At once, Mordecai turned without a word. He grabbed his fur-lined cap and moved to join Deronda.

They returned to the sitting room just moments later. Jacob, noticing the change in Mordecai’s expression, seized his arm and shouted, “Look at my cup and ball!” He tossed the ball near Mordecai’s face—something cheerful to cheer a man recovering.

Mordecai smiled faintly. “Fine, fine.”

“You forgot your coat and blanket,” said young Mrs. Cohen. He returned to the workroom and retrieved them.

“He’s come back to life, see?” Cohen whispered, rejoining them. “I told you so. I’m mostly right.” Then, aloud: “Well, sir, we mustn’t keep you. But I hope this isn’t the last time we see you.”

“Will you come again?” Jacob asked, stepping forward. “See? I can catch the ball every time. I’ll bet I catch it without stopping—if you come back.”

“He has clever hands,” Deronda said, glancing at the grandmother.

“On my side,” Cohen replied promptly. “My wife’s family aren’t known for such things. But bless you—ours is a sort of cleverness like gutta percha—you can twist it any way. Some old gentlemen will do anything if you set them to it.” He winked at Jacob’s back.

But Jacob only let out a nasal whinny and began hopping around, singing, “Old gentlemen, old gentlemen,” in rhythm.

Deronda thought: *I’ll never truly know them until I ask Cohen plainly—did he lose a sister named Mirah when she was six?* But the moment felt too heavy to approach yet. Still, his initial discomfort at their plainness was softening. Though their speech and manner were rough, there was a quiet dignity in how they treated Mordecai—the sick, thoughtful man whom they saw not as a burden, but as a harmless dreamer, full of strange thoughts.

“The Cohens seem to care for you,” Deronda said as they stepped outside.

“And I for them,” Mordecai replied at once. “They carry the heart of Israel within them—even if they walk like horses and mules, blind to paths beyond their own.”

“I’m sorry I delayed,” said Deronda. “I meant to come yesterday, but couldn’t.”

“Yes—yes, I trusted you. But I’ve been uneasy. The spirit of my youth has stirred within me, and this body is too weak to bear it. I am like a man bound for years—suddenly freed, given voice, given motion. He weeps, he stumbles—joy threatens to break the house of flesh.”

“You shouldn’t speak so much in this evening air,” Deronda said, feeling each word like a thread binding him tighter. “Cover your mouth with the scarf. We’re going to the _Hand and Banner_, I suppose? Will it be private there?”

“No—that’s the trouble you didn’t come yesterday. Tonight is the club night. We may not have time alone until late, when others leave. Perhaps we should find another place. But I’m used to this one. In new places, the world presses in, and my inner vision narrows. And the people there know me.”

“I don’t mind the club,” Deronda said. “As long as I’m welcome. If we don’t have time, I’ll return. What kind of club is it?”

“It’s called ‘The Philosophers.’ Few of us—like the cedars of Lebanon—poor men who think. None poorer than I. Sometimes visitors of higher rank come. We may bring a friend interested in our talk. Each pays for a drink, and most smoke. I’ve gone when I could—there are others of my race who come, and sometimes I’ve spoken. I’ve found a faint likeness between these men and the ancient Masters—those who worked with their hands for scant bread, yet preserved the soul of Israel, passing down memory like a seed amid tombs. The heart finds comfort in such echoes.”

“I’d be glad to join you,” Deronda said, relieved at the prospect of delay before their private talk. “It sounds like a gathering I’d enjoy.”

Within minutes, they passed through the glazed door with its red curtain and entered a small parlor—barely fifteen feet square. Gaslight shimmered through a haze of smoke, illuminating a scene new and striking to Deronda. Half a dozen men of various ages, dressed simply, many with clay pipes in their mouths, sat in a tight semicircle around the fireplace. One man, broad-shouldered and blonde-haired, wearing pepper-and-salt clothes, had just finished quoting Shelley:

*“As thought by thought is piled, till some great truth
Is loosened, and the nations echo round.”*

The entrance of Deronda and Mordecai broke the silence. Seats shifted, glasses rearranged. This was a sober group—but sobriety did not mean abstention. Smoking and drink were essential tools for presence and conversation.

Mordecai was welcomed warmly, though with a quiet tone of sympathy. All eyes turned to Deronda.
