“I’ve brought a friend,” Mordecai announced. “One who has traveled and studied much.”

“Is the gentleman anonymous? Is he a Great Unknown?” asked the broad man, with a grin.

“My name is Daniel Deronda. I am unknown—but not great.”

A smile lit his face, warm and modest. The room responded with a low murmur—almost a cheer. The broad man nodded.

“You recommend the name, sir. Welcome. Come, Mordecai—sit beside me,” he added, offering the coziest spot.

Deronda settled across from them, easily observing the group. Mordecai stood out—still, intense, marked by a deep melancholy. Among the others, several bore unmistakable signs of Jewish descent.

Indeed, pure English blood was rare. Miller, the bookseller, had German ancestors—or perhaps distant Jewish ones denied. Buchan, the saddler, was Scottish. Pash, the watchmaker, was a dark, lively Jew. Gideon, the instrument maker, was a red-haired Jew who looked entirely English. Croop, the shoemaker, seemed more Celtic than he knew. Only three—Goodwin, Marrables, and Lily—looked unmistakably English.

A select company of the poor, drawn together not by wealth or status, but by a hunger for thought—rare even among the privileged. Not the sort of place a gentleman would seek for scandal or comedy. Deronda, already somber from what lay ahead, found no reason to mock. Instead, he watched with quiet respect. He ordered whisky and water, offered cigars from his case—always carried, rarely used. He didn’t smoke, but liked to offer. Perhaps he feared seeming rigid, afraid of becoming a mere rule rather than a living person.

Yet he made an immediate impression. The men relaxed, eager to resume their talk.

“This is one of our touch-and-go nights,” Miller said, addressing Deronda. “Sometimes we stay focused. But tonight, Pash raised the law of progress—we went to statistics. Then Lily said we already knew things would repeat themselves without counting—just as quantities remain constant, so do qualities. Numbers reflect qualities—the number of drunkards is a quality of society. So numbers tell us nothing, only prompt us to study causes of difference between societies. From there, we turned to social change—and when you came in, I was speaking of ideas—the main force of transformation.”

“I disagree, Miller,” said Goodwin, the wood-inlayer. “You mean too many things by ‘ideas’—like saying light is a cause. Or you mean a narrow kind—then I reject it. Look: every action born of thought is an idea—sowing seeds, building boats, baking clay. These ideas grow with life, but they depend on material. Wood and stone yield to the knife—so the idea of shaping arises. With enough material, shaping continues. Ideas mixed directly with life are powerful. The slower the mix, the weaker the idea. As for social change—ideas are like a parliament, but outside lies a commonwealth, working change without knowing what the parliament does.”

“But if mixing is the test,” said Pash, “some impractical ideas spread fastest. They enter language without being understood.”

“They may act by changing gas distribution,” Marrables suggested. “With finer instruments, we might measure the spread of a theory through atmospheric shifts—and nerve changes.”

“Yes,” Pash said, his face brightening playfully, “the idea of nationality—perhaps wild asses are sensing it, growing more social.”

“You don’t share that idea?” Deronda asked, noting the irony in Pash’s tone against his strong features.

“Rather, he doesn’t share that spirit,” Mordecai said, turning a sorrowful gaze on Pash. “Unless nationality is a feeling, what power can it have as an idea?”

“Granted,” Pash replied, good-naturedly. “And since the feeling of nationality is fading, I see the idea as a ghost—already walking to announce death.”

“A sentiment may seem dead and yet revive,” said Deronda. “Nations have risen again. We may see a great awakening among the Arabs, inspired by new zeal.”

“Amen, amen,” Mordecai whispered, looking at Deronda with sudden light. His posture straightened, his face less worn.

“That may hold for backward peoples,” Pash said, “but in Europe, nationality is doomed. It will linger where oppression lasts—but nowhere else. Progress flows against it.”

“Ay,” Buchan said, his thin Scotch voice cutting in like cool air. “You’ve brought us to the point. You all agree societies change—not always, not everywhere—but generally, over time. Now, with respect, we must ask: Is all change progress? If not, how do we know which is progress? And third—how can we shape change to help it, or divert it when harmful?”

But Buchan’s order failed. Lily interrupted.

“Change and progress merge into development. Laws of development are being discovered. Changes following those laws are necessarily progressive. Any notion of progress opposed to them is mistaken.”

“I can’t see how calling change ‘development’ gives you certainty,” Deronda said. “We still face degrees of inevitability—our will, our actions. We still risk mistaking resistance for fate—which seems as dangerous a superstition as any.”

“That is true,” Mordecai said. “Woe to those who see no place for resistance! I believe in growth—a passage, a new unfolding. The seed holds greater potential, charged with divine form. A people’s life grows—it knits and expands, in joy and sorrow, thought and action. It absorbs other nations’ ideas, returns them transformed. It is a force in the body of humanity. But there may come a halt—memories silenced, love weakened. Or memories shrink into relics—the soul of a people may seem to die. But who can say, ‘Their spring is dried? They shall cease forever?’ Who dares say it? Not he who feels his people’s life stir within him. Shall he say, ‘Events move this way—I will not resist?’ His soul is resistance itself—a spark that may ignite millions, blaze a new path.”

CHAPTER XLIII. “My spirit is too weak; mortality
Weighs heavily on me like unwilling sleep,
And each imagined pinnacle and steep
Of godlike hardship tells me I must die
Like a sick eagle looking at the sky.” —KEATS.

A quiet stillness settled around them, and Mordecai stirred, lifting his eyes to Deronda—not with surprise, but with a deep, peaceful content. Deronda rose quietly, drawing his chair closer, so that no word needed to be raised in effort. The gesture was gentle, like the hand of a nurse adjusting a pillow for a weary patient. Mordecai felt it, and his voice came low, as if he were speaking only to himself, not to another soul.

“In the teachings of the Cabbala,” he began, “the soul returns again and again to new bodies until it is perfected—until it sheds its earthly burden and joins with another soul, one that needs it, so they may complete their purpose together. Then they rise beyond this world, leaving room for new souls to emerge from the eternal source. It is the unfinished work of those already born—their lingering imperfections—that delays the coming of the Messianic age. So the mind shapes what is hidden, like a shadow of truth, speaking in parable yet saying something real. When my soul leaves this worn-out body, it will join yours. And together we shall finish what remains undone.”

He paused, and in that silence, Deronda felt a pull—a need to respond. He tried to speak plainly, honestly. But words could not carry the depth of what he felt. Still, he said only, “Everything I can do, in conscience, to make your life meaningful—I will do.”

“I know,” Mordecai replied, calm and certain, as though no further assurance was needed. “I heard you. You see what I see—you stand beside me on the mountain of vision, and you behold the paths others deny.”

He fell silent for a moment, then continued, lost in memory. “You will take up where my life broke off. I feel myself back in that morning—sunlight on the quay at Trieste. Men from every land wore garments that shone like jewels. Boats pushed away from the shore. A Greek ship would carry us to Beyrout in an hour. I was going as clerk and companion to a merchant. I thought: *I will see the lands of the East, and speak with fuller sight.* I breathed freely, as you do now—no struggle, only youth’s light step and endurance. I had chosen poverty, and loved it—for to me, poverty meant freedom. My heart soared, as if it were the heart of Moses ben Maimon, strong with sixty years and knowing the work that would fill them. It was my first time south. My soul remembered its old sun. Standing there, the ground beneath me seemed to glow, and shadows held an azure brilliance, like spirits made visible. I felt myself caught in a flood of glorious life, where my own short years dissolved into nothing. A great sob rose within me—too much joy, too much strength.”

He paused, gazing ahead as if seeing the past clearly. “I waited for my companion. And then he said: ‘Ezra, I’ve been to the post—there’s a letter for you.’”

“Ezra!” Deronda exclaimed, unable to hold back.

“Yes,” Mordecai repeated, softly, absorbed in recollection. “I had written often to my mother. That name—*Ezra*—was like a wand touching me, pulling me back from the vast ocean of existence, where I had drifted free of individual pain. I opened the letter. And the name came again—this time as a cry, piercing even heaven, calling me to sorrow: *‘Ezra, my son!’*”

Mordecai stopped, his eyes fixed on the distant past. Deronda sat breathless, waiting.

After a while, Mordecai looked down, lost in thought. Then he spoke again. “She was a mother worthy of praise—someone who might have been called, *Her children arise and call her blessed*. In her, I understood the Master who, hearing his mother’s footsteps, rose and said, *‘The Majesty of the Eternal cometh near!’* This letter was her cry from the depths—her anguish, her desolation. She had lost four children to death. Then came my sister, late, the child of her deepest love. And the letter—oh, it was a wound: *‘Ezra, my son, I am robbed of her. He has taken her away and left disgrace behind. They will never return.’*”

At that, Mordecai lifted his eyes suddenly, placed his hand on Deronda’s arm, and said, “Mine was Israel’s fate. For the sin of the father, my soul must wander. For the sin of the father, my work was broken, my day delayed. She who bore me was shattered—dishonored, alone. I turned back. At once. Her spirit, and the spirits of her ancestors—men of noble Jewish hearts—moved within me. God, who holds the universe, became my strength. I turned. I traveled through hardship, to save what little money she would need. I left the sun, and walked into freezing cold. On the last leg, I spent a night exposed to snow and wind. That night… it began this slow death.”

His gaze wandered again. He withdrew his hand.

Deronda held his questions back. Not now. While Mordecai was in this state, no question should be forced—only what came naturally. And even then, Deronda felt a kindred emotion, making him fear his own words might be too heavy.

“But I worked,” Mordecai continued. “We had nothing—everything seized. She was ill. Grief gripped her too tightly, and stirred some hidden sickness. Sometimes she could not stand—her heart beat wildly, and visions filled her mind: chambers of terror, where she saw my sister growing in evil. In the dead of night, I heard her cry out for her child. Then I rose. We stretched our arms together and prayed. We poured out our souls, begging that Mirah be saved from harm.”

“Mirah?” Deronda whispered, needing to confirm—had he truly heard?

“Yes,” Mordecai said. “That was her name. After we prayed, my mother would rest. It lasted barely four years. And in the moment before she died, we were praying the same prayer—I aloud, she silently. Then her soul flew away.”

“Have you never heard of her since?” Deronda asked, gently.

“Never,” Mordecai said. “I don’t know if she was delivered. I don’t know. Who can trace the pathways? The wicked’s will is poison. It poisoned my life—it is slowly choking this breath. Death freed my mother. I welcomed it, being alone in these long winters of suffering. But now—what are the winters?” He laid his hand on Deronda’s arm again, and looked at him with a fevered joy that pierced the heart. “There is nothing to mourn in this fading body. The work will be done better. Once I said, *This beginning is mine—I am born to do it.* Well, I shall do it. I shall live in you. I shall live in you.”

His grip tightened, almost painfully. Deronda, shaken more deeply than ever—knowing now that this man was Mirah’s brother, and feeling the weight of their connection—felt his heart race, his lips grow pale. He could not speak. He feared revealing anything about Mirah, lest it break Mordecai’s fragile peace. He feared any answer less than the height of hope—like a flicker of fire near death, which makes watchers dread to watch it fade.

So he did what he had done once before: he placed his firm, gentle hand over Mordecai’s. And as if guided by a force beyond will, Mordecai’s hand relaxed, turning upward under Deronda’s. Their palms met, pressed together. In that touch, Mordecai returned to himself.

“Let us go,” he said softly. “I cannot talk anymore.”

They parted at Cohen’s door without another word—only a final pressure of hands.

Deronda carried a weight within him—half joy, half sorrow. The joy of discovering in Mirah’s brother a nature greater than he could have imagined—worthy of her, and more. Yet the reunion was also the first step toward a final parting—like a farewell kiss that feels like greeting, or a last glance that becomes the sharpest pain.

And then there was the anxiety—how to tell Mirah, how to bring them together without pain, without jarring circumstances. He knew he would want to shield her from the harshness of the world, especially from the Cohens’ well-meaning but awkward ways. He wanted Mirah’s first meeting with her brother to be pure—free from the noise of charity, the discomfort of poverty. He would arrange it all carefully. For Mordecai, too—he would find a place not just healthier, but more fitting—where dignity and ease could meet.

In stories of heroes, such moments come without clutter. But this was not legend. These were lives still walking among shadows, not yet stars. And Deronda saw his task as delicate, demanding patience and care—especially in persuading Mordecai to leave his familiar, threadbare life.

But of Mirah’s feelings, he had no doubt. She would embrace her brother. She would understand his greatness.

Yes—greatness. That was the word Deronda chose now, deliberately. This man—consumptive, poor, clothed in rags, living on charity, speaking to listeners who took his words as lightly as Flemings take chimes above the market—yet he possessed greatness. A mind alive to the grand currents of human destiny, yet tender enough to turn from fame for the sake of a mother’s cry. Capable of choosing a lifelong mission, yet willing to sacrifice it for the near duty, like a bird feeding its young.

Deronda felt, tonight, that this dying flame was now his charge. He had seen, at the club, how Mordecai was ignored, tolerated—but never truly seen. And that stirred him. He had known the smallness of passion in ordinary minds, and it had made him cautious. Tolerance came easily to him—but so did the fear of standing out, of pushing too hard. Yet now, that caution seemed foolish. For the first time, he stood face-to-face with a soul burning in solitude—exiled by the rarity of his thoughts, unheard by most, spoken to only in whispers—unless, perhaps, when nearing death, signs of recognition finally pierce the loneliness.

Like Copernicus, whose fingers touched the printed pages of his own book when sight had failed—seeing only dimly through the darkening dusk.

Deronda had found one such exile. And in his nature, he could not help but feel bound to him—not merely as a friend, but as a companion in purpose. His own longing—once denied, now stronger—for a deeper bond, for a sense of belonging, echoed in Mordecai’s vision. He was ready to obey, even when it was difficult.

Thus, without hesitation, he began his new role. He would seek Mrs. Meyrick’s help—she would be his guide in bringing Mirah and her brother together. Happily, good lodgings for a consumptive were not far from Chelsea. His first task: to find Mordecai a home where air and light could aid his frail body.

Irony, indeed—heroes often lack carpets and teacups. Seen through a shopkeeper’s window, they might be offered fourpence for three hundred percent. Yet Deronda’s mind was already shaping a plan: he would dismantle his own rooms—take down his best books in vellum, move his favorite chair, place the bas-reliefs of Milton and Dante in a space meant for healing.

But would Mirah be there? What furniture could match the grace of her presence? What harmony of color could rival the sweetness of her voice?

Here, at least, was one blessing: Mordecai had chosen him—and in doing so, had reclaimed a sister whose love waits only for him.

Fairy folk a-listening
Hear the seed sprout in the spring.
And for music to their dance
Hear the hedgerows wake from trance,
Sap that trembles into buds
Sending little rhythmic floods
Of fairy sound in fairy ears.
Thus all beauty that appears
Has birth as sound to finer sense
And lighter-clad intelligence.

Gwendolen was thinking of Deronda more than he was thinking of her—often wondering what his thoughts were about life, about meaning, about the world beyond her own narrow orbit. It was like a lap-dog imagining the grand adventures of dogs everywhere: utterly impossible. She could not fathom that Deronda’s life might be shaped by something vast and ancient—the destiny of a people long forgotten, or a calling no one else could see. Nor could she picture him riding away on a golden horse into the sky, disappearing like a star. Her mind, though sharp and quick, had never reached such heights.

Yet even with the deep sense of inferiority that had settled over her since marriage, she still believed herself central in his thoughts. It is natural, perhaps, for young hearts to assume that others feel as they do—especially when loneliness makes every glance, every word, seem charged with significance. Gwendolen, isolated and yearning, clung to the idea that Deronda saw her differently. That he valued her. That he noticed her—not just as Mrs. Grandcourt, but as someone whose soul stirred beneath the surface.

She often asked herself: *What would he tell me to do?* He had said she must care more for others, learn more, seek out the best things in life. But how? Where should she begin? She imagined him recommending books—great minds, towering intellects—and smiled at the thought of asking him if those unreadable volumes were really “medicine for the mind,” as some called them. Then, ashamed of her teasing, she would quietly gather a selection—Descartes, Bacon, Locke, Butler, Burke, Guizot—names she knew were respected, authors she assumed he had read. She hoped, by dipping into them quickly, she might catch a glimpse of the world through his eyes.

But time slipped away. The demands of being Mrs. Grandcourt left little room for quiet reflection. Every moment was watched, measured, judged. Grandcourt, ever watchful, had found new reasons to tighten his grip—making sure the marriage served his purposes, and serving them completely. And Gwendolen, though rebellious within, could not afford to fail in her performance. She could not bear the thought of giving any sign—any look, any word—that would betray her inner turmoil. Not yet. Not while the world still looked on her with admiration.

Around Diplow, during those early weeks of the year, she was seen as triumphant. Dressed in fine silks, riding with confidence, receiving guests with grace, she wore her title like a crown. “She hides it under an air of indifference,” Mrs. Arrowpoint remarked. “A stranger might think she has condescended rather than risen. I’ve always sensed that duality in her.”

To her mother, above all, Gwendolen wished to appear content. Poor Mrs. Davilow, deceived by the distance now between them, took it as a sign of changed affection—though Grandcourt had done nothing but provide for her. Visits were brief: lunch, dinner, a quick call with the husband waiting nearby. No real intimacy remained.

The truth was, when Gwendolen had once suggested inviting her mother with the Gascoignes, Grandcourt had hesitated, then drawled, “We can’t be having _those people_ always. Gascoigne talks too much. Country clergy are always bores—with their confounded fuss about everything.” The words chilled her. To have her family dismissed so casually—her mother included—was a wound. Yet she could not explain. She could not say, *He wants nothing to do with you. And I don’t want you to see how unhappy I am.*

So she kept silent, brushing aside hints gently. When her mother mentioned possibly moving near Ryelands, Gwendolen replied, “It wouldn’t be as nice as staying near the rectory, mamma. We’ll likely be away from Ryelands much. You’d miss Aunt and Uncle.” All the while, her heart swelled with a secret longing—for her uncle’s warmth, for her family’s presence, for anything that felt like home.

And perhaps, without knowing it, she was growing. Not through books, which demanded silence and focus she couldn’t give, but through memory, through love, through the ache of absence. She began to see her uncle not as a distant figure, but as someone who had cared, who had tried—even when he was wrong. It was a kind of quiet strength she hadn’t known before.

One day, the unexpected joy came: Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne were at Offendene, and Gwendolen rode up alone—without Grandcourt, only her groom beside her. The whole household, including the four girls and Miss Merry, saw her approach. Even the older ones felt a spark of romance, as if the beautiful sister on the proud chestnut horse had stepped out of a storybook—Harriet Byron reborn, or Miss Wardour returning from happiness ever after.

Her uncle met her at the door. She leapt down with a lightness that seemed to promise joy. Today, she was determined to comfort her mother—to show her there was still peace, still love. The seven kisses were no longer a burden. “Mr. Grandcourt is out,” she said, laying down her hat and sitting beside her mother. “So I decided to come visit.” Then, with a playful scolding tone, “You’re punished for not wearing better lace. You didn’t think I’d come and catch you—so careless-about-yourself, mamma!” She touched her mother’s hair tenderly.

“Scold me, dear,” Mrs. Davilow whispered, her face flushing with pleasure.

“I wish there was something you could eat after your ride,” she added. “Not just these scraps. Let Jocosa make you chocolate—your old way. You used to like that.”

Miss Merry rose immediately, though Gwendolen waved her off. “No, just bread or a biscuit. I’m not hungry. I’m here to say good-bye.”

“What! Going back to Ryelands?” asked Mr. Gascoigne.

“No,” said Gwendolen, breaking bread but not eating. “We’re going to town.”

“Early to go to town,” said Mrs. Gascoigne. “And Mr. Grandcourt isn’t in Parliament.”

“Oh, there’s just one more day of hunting, and Henleigh has business with lawyers, I think,” Gwendolen said. “I’m glad. I’ll enjoy being in London.”

“You’ll see your house in Grosvenor Square,” said Mrs. Davilow, her eyes shining.

“Yes,” Gwendolen agreed. “And there’s so much to see and do.”

Then Mr. Gascoigne spoke, with the weight of advice: “I wish you’d use your influence with Mr. Grandcourt to encourage him to enter Parliament. A man of his standing should make his voice heard. The ministry may need to appeal to the country soon. And he’s at the age when public duty becomes essential. A wife has great influence over her husband. Use yours, my dear.”

To Gwendolen, the speech tasted like bitter irony. If she had been laughing, she might have burst out. Her uncle had just admitted he didn’t know Grandcourt’s political views. And *influence*? Once, she had believed in her power to shape things. Now, she knew better. But she answered carefully: “I’d be glad, uncle. But I think Mr. Grandcourt wouldn’t want the trouble of an election—unless it could be without speeches. Don’t candidates always speak?”

“Not necessarily,” said Mr. Gascoigne. “A man of position needs few words. Tell him I say so.”

“Here comes Jocosa with my chocolate,” Gwendolen said, relieved to escape further conversation. The rector, feeling he had fulfilled his duty, sat back with the air of a wise magistrate offering counsel.

Mrs. Gascoigne, less composed, hinted at discomfort: “Your uncle and Anna will likely be in town by Easter. Rex hopes to come out with honors—perhaps a fellowship. Lord Brackenshaw has been very kind since returning to the Castle. He may invite them.”

“I hope my uncle brings Ann to stay in Grosvenor Square,” Gwendolen said, risking a small hope for the moment—though in truth, she dreaded bringing anyone near Grandcourt again.

“We mustn’t rejoice too soon,” said the rector, pleased with the topic. “But I’ve spoken with impartial judges—they hold high hopes for Rex. He’s clear-headed, principled. He’ll be a great lawyer someday.”

“How very nice,” Gwendolen murmured, her voice flat with sarcasm. Lawyers, she thought, were rarely *nice*.

“Lord Brackenshaw has been so kind,” Mrs. Davilow said. “He invited me to stay here until I find another house. And now one has come up—Mr. Jodson’s place. Small, but pleasant. Just a mile from the Rectory. You remember the low white house hidden by trees?”

“Yes,” said Gwendolen, her voice softening. “But you have no furniture, poor mamma.”

“Oh, I’m saving,” Mrs. Davilow said, touching her daughter’s hand. “You know who made me rich, dear. And Jocosa manages so well—it’s wonderful.”

“Oh, please let me go upstairs and fix my hat,” Gwendolen said suddenly, raising a hand to her hair. Her heart was heavy. She had to hide the truth: her mother would have been worse off without Grandcourt’s money. But the thought of losing this place, this family, this quiet warmth—made her nearly weep.

“I suppose I’ll never see this again,” she whispered, entering the black-and-yellow bedroom. She sank into a chair before the mirror, groaning softly, as if drained. She refused to cry. Her face turned pale.

“You’re not well, dear?” Mrs. Davilow asked.

“No,” Gwendolen said, lifting her hand. “That chocolate made me sick.”

“I’d come to you if you were ill,” her mother said, pressing her hand to her chest.

“I’ll be fine,” Gwendolen said, leaning her head against her mother. “I’m strong. Don’t fret. Make yourself happy with the girls. They’re better children than I’ve been.”

She smiled up at her mother. “You’ve always been good to me.”

“Why, what did I ever do that was good?” Gwendolen asked, rising with a sudden playfulness. “Except marry Mr. Grandcourt? And I wouldn’t have done that unless it pleased me.”

She tossed her chin, picked up her hat. “God forbid, child! I wouldn’t have had you marry for my sake. Your happiness is half mine.”

“Very well,” Gwendolen said, arranging her hat with care. “Then you’ll please consider yourself half happy—which is more than I’m used to seeing.”

She turned, smiling as before. “Now I’m ready. Oh, mamma—Mr. Grandcourt gives me money, expects me to spend it. But I can’t. I hate charity cases. Here—thirty pounds. Give it to the girls. Let them buy little things for themselves when you move.”

She placed the notes in her mother’s hands and hurried toward the door.

“God bless you, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said.

“It will please them so much,” Gwendolen said, turning back with a nod. “They’re troublesome—but they don’t trouble me now.”

She didn’t understand why she gave the money, but she didn’t want it taken seriously. She was glad to leave the room without showing more emotion. As she rode away, she thought sarcastically: *I think I’m making a very good Mrs. Grandcourt.*

She believed Grandcourt had gone to Gadsmere that day—had guessed it, as she had long guessed who lived in the “dog-hutch” he described. And yet, the thought of him going there—of him caring for those people—sent a fire through her. Why should it hurt her that he showed concern for those whose suffering she herself regretted?

Hadn’t she promised before marriage to act on their behalf? And wasn’t it good that he was making arrangements for his will, keeping conscience alive?

Yet now, as a wife, the knowledge that he was at Gadsmere burned like a brand. She had brought this upon herself—the humiliation of silence, the terror of being discovered. She had married him knowing the truth, and now she must endure it. As she told Deronda: *I must go on.*

After moments of fierce hatred, the old pressure returned—the spiritual force that made submission inevitable. She dared nothing except impulsive acts. Not even the faintest plan for freedom. For any attempt would bring deeper shame.

Still, the worst result of her marriage was not the pain, but the spectacle. She feared being seen as broken. And her humiliation was eased by believing only Mrs. Glasher knew the truth—the secret of the Whispering Stones, the letter, the threat. She had never told anyone Lush was involved. In her mind, Mrs. Glasher held the secret—and the fear that she too would dread exposure.

And there was another secret—her own suppressed rebellion. One she dreaded more than any other. Grandcourt could not truly imagine how she felt. He saw only what suited his will. But he sensed her resistance, magnified it—even as it deepened his satisfaction in controlling it.

And so, Gwendolen rode away, a queen in silk and sorrow, carrying her secrets like stones in her heart.

CHAPTER XLV. Behold my lady’s carriage stop the way. With powdered lacquey and with charming bay; She sweeps the matting, treads the crimson stair. Her arduous function solely “to be there.” Like Sirius rising o’er the silent sea. She hides her heart in lustre loftily.

The Grandcourts arrived at Grosvenor Square just in time to receive an invitation to Lady Mallinger’s musical gathering—a social event Sir Hugo had anticipated with some interest, knowing full well that his estranged nephew, Daniel Deronda, would be among the guests. It was only the third evening since their arrival in London, and though Gwendolen had been introduced to the grandeur of her new home—its white walls, gold trimmings, and pale crimson draperies—her mind remained elsewhere. She wandered through the rooms half-absent, her thoughts fixed not on the opulence around her, but on the prospect of seeing Deronda again, and of meeting Mirah Lapidoth, the young Jewish woman whose story had stirred him so deeply.

Gwendolen remembered every word Deronda had spoken about Mirah, especially the phrase: “capable of submitting to anything in the form of duty.” That sentence echoed in her mind now, a quiet reproach. She could not help comparing it to her own life—her submission, she knew, was not born of duty, but of compulsion, of shame, of a yoke laid upon her by choices she regretted. If anyone had told her she lacked true sacrifice, she might have argued fiercely—but deep down, she understood the truth: her obedience was not noble, but self-serving, driven by fear and pride.

The drawing-rooms of Park Lane were tastefully arranged and lightly filled when the Grandcourts entered. Soft instrumental music had just ended, giving way to murmured conversation and gentle movement. Klesmer was already seated at the piano, preparing for Mirah’s performance. He had chosen Leo’s *O patria mia* for her—a piece he believed best revealed her voice’s depth and character. As Mirah stood beside the instrument, poised and still, Gwendolen entered, resplendent in pale green velvet and diamonds that seemed almost too bright for such a somber night. She was ushered to a seat near the front, where she could see both Mirah and Deronda clearly.

With her sharp eyes and practiced composure, Gwendolen quickly scanned the room. She caught sight of Mirah, then nodded politely to Klesmer as she passed. The brief exchange sparked a sudden memory—the morning when she had dreamed of standing where Mirah now stood, admired not for beauty or birth, but for talent. Now, she was merely another ornament in silk and jewels, one of many who came to applaud or critique. A flicker of bitterness crossed her mind: *He thinks I am in the right road now.*

She did not see Deronda at first. While making polite conversation with Sir Hugo, she glanced around carefully, afraid that any obvious search for him might seem vulgar or unseemly. But even the most discreet glance can lead to unexpected encounters. Among those who met her gaze was Mr. Lush—the man once banished from her thoughts, now standing close to her husband. Sir Hugo turned away slightly, pretending to listen to Lord Pentreath. Yet Gwendolen felt a jolt—an icy sensation—like a secret reawakening. For the first time, it occurred to her: this man knew things about Sir Hugo’s life. Things that made her wretched. He had vanished from her world, hidden behind other concerns, but now he stood here, at her husband’s side, and suddenly, the past surged forward.

She forced herself to look away, pretending her attention was drawn to a painting on the wall. Then, finally, she saw Deronda—not far from the door, speaking quietly with Hans Meyrick. He looked anxious, perhaps even tense. They were both concerned for Mirah’s performance, worried that she might not be heard properly. Deronda, in particular, felt something stir within him—images of Mordecai, of the future, of the brother he would soon reveal to Mirah. He had just escaped Lady Pentreath, who had remarked, in her low, mocking tone, “Well, your Jewess is pretty—there’s no denying that. But where is her Jewish impudence? She looks as demure as a nun.”

That comment stung. Deronda felt, as he had once felt for himself, a fierce protectiveness. He resented the casual way Mirah was treated—as if she were a mere curiosity, a novelty for the fashionable crowd to examine and discard. And he knew Mordecai would feel the same pain at being labeled “Jewess” as if it were a mark of inferiority.

In that moment, as the Grandcourts entered, Deronda felt a fleeting return of his old repulsion toward Gwendolen. Not because of her beauty, but because of what she represented—wealth, indifference, a world that could not recognize the worth of someone like Mirah. It was a kind of class resentment, born of affection for what others failed to see.

Hans, admiring Gwendolen with his usual exaggeration, said, “That Vandyke duchess of a beauty!” Deronda replied, with a dryness that masked his discomfort, “I thought you could admire no style of woman but your Berenice.”
“Berenice is the style I worship,” Hans answered. “Other women I might make myself wicked for. But for Berenice, I could make myself—well, pretty good. Which is harder.”
“Hush,” said Deronda, as the music began.

He was not pleased with the reply, and was relieved when Hans moved closer to the piano.

Mirah sang *O patria mia*. Deronda listened intently. He knew Leopardi’s poem well—the image of Italy as a mother in chains, weeping in silence. The few words of the song carried the weight of the whole poem, breathing sorrow and defiance. To Deronda, Mirah’s voice seemed to echo Mordecai’s spirit—his courage, his loneliness, his love for a people he could never fight for. Certain lines not in the song rang in his mind: *Do none of thy children defend thee? Arms! bring me arms! alone I will fight, alone I will fall.* These were not words of futility, but of sacred sacrifice.

Mirah sang beautifully. When the applause rose, Klesmer leaned over and whispered, “Good, good—the crescendo better than before.” But Mirah cared less for praise than for Deronda’s approval. She looked toward him, hoping he saw her. He did, but stayed where he was, watching as admirers gathered around her. Then, the crowd parted—and Gwendolen approached, escorted by Mrs. Klesmer.

Now, standing near Mirah, Gwendolen felt a strange shift in her feelings. She no longer saw Mirah as a rival, but as someone fragile, gifted, and strangely vulnerable. She offered her thanks: “You must let me say how much I am obliged to you. I had heard from Mr. Deronda that I should have a great treat in your singing, but I was too ignorant to imagine how great.”

Mirah, absorbed in Gwendolen’s presence, replied simply: “You are very good to say so.” She found herself fascinated by the grand lady—her jewels, her bearing, the aura of a world beyond her own. It felt like a dream.

“We shall all want to learn from you—I, at least,” said Gwendolen. “I sing very badly, as Herr Klesmer will tell you,” she added, glancing up at him with a playful archness. “But I’ve been rebuked for not liking to be middling, since I can be nothing more. Is that a different doctrine from yours?”

Klesmer smiled. “Not if it means you’d study further—and Miss Lapidoth would enjoy teaching you.” Then he stepped away.

Mirah, sincere and earnest, said, “If you think I could teach you, I shall be very glad. I am anxious to teach, but I have only just begun. If I do it well, it must be by remembering how my master taught me.”

Gwendolen, taken aback by the simplicity, tried to change the subject. “You haven’t been long in London, I think? Were you introduced to Mr. Deronda abroad?”

“No,” Mirah said softly. “I never saw him before I came to England.”

“But he has seen you often, hasn’t he?” Gwendolen pressed, eager to hear more about Deronda. “He spoke of you with the highest praise. He seemed to know you quite well.”

“Oh,” Mirah said, her voice softening with gratitude. “I was poor and needed help. Mr. Deronda gave me the best friends in the world. That is how he came to know me—because he was sorry for me. I had no friends when I came. I was in distress. I owe everything to him.”

Gwendolen, who had once dreamed of struggling artists and creative freedom, couldn’t help feeling a pang. The way Mirah spoke—so humble, so grateful—was a contrast to the pride she herself had once worn. Yet she also sensed that Mirah was protecting Deronda, refusing to let anyone assume they were equals. There was grace in that.

As the music resumed, Gwendolen chose to sit farther from the piano, placing herself on a settee with only one neighbor—closer to Deronda than before. It was natural, then, that he should come over to shake hands before the next piece began. After a moment, he sat beside her, as if the music itself had drawn them together.

But when the music ended, and Gwendolen hoped to speak freely, she noticed Mr. Lush nearby, leaning against the wall. Anger flared in her cheeks, but she forced calm. “Miss Lapidoth is everything you described her to be,” she said.

“You’ve been quick to discover that,” Deronda replied, with irony.

“I haven’t found all your excellencies,” she said quickly. “But I think her singing is charming, and she is lovely—such a complete little person. I think she’ll be a great success.”

Deronda frowned. He didn’t answer. She knew he was displeased. The presence of Lush made her restless. She wanted to say something meaningful, but feared it would be overheard. So she stayed silent.

The silence stretched. Neither looked at the other until Lush finally moved away.

Then Gwendolen said, “You despise me for talking artificially.”

“No,” Deronda said, looking at her calmly. “That’s excusable sometimes. But I didn’t think what you just said was entirely artificial.”

“There was something that displeased you,” she said. “What was it?”

“It’s impossible to explain,” he said. “One cannot communicate nuances of feeling about words and manner.”

“You think I’m shut out from understanding them,” she said, her voice trembling slightly. “Have I shown myself so dense to everything you’ve said?”

Her eyes held his—glistening with unshed tears.

“Not at all,” he said, his voice softening. “But experience differs. We don’t all wince at the same things. I’ve had proof you’re not dense.”

He smiled gently.

“But one may feel things and still not know how to act,” she said, not returning the smile. “I begin to think we can only grow better by having people around us who raise good feelings. You must not be surprised at anything in me. I think it’s too late for me to change. I don’t know how to become wise, as you told me to be.”

“I seldom find I do any good by preaching,” Deronda said, thinking sadly of the necklace incident, and wondering if his efforts had only hardened her further. “I might as well have kept from meddling.”

“Don’t say that,” she said urgently. “If you despair of me, I shall despair. Your telling me not to go on being selfish and ignorant has given me strength. If you say you wish you hadn

“If anyone were to ask me why I loved him, I could only answer, ‘Because it was he, because it was I.’ There is something beyond words—an inexplicable force that brought us together.”—MONTAIGNE: _On Friendship_.

The moment had come to prepare Mordecai for the revelation of his long-lost sister and for the change in living arrangements that would allow Mirah to meet her brother under peaceful and dignified circumstances. Mrs. Meyrick, to whom Deronda had confided everything except Mordecai’s deeper connection to himself, had been actively involved in finding a suitable home in Brompton, just a short walk from her own house. This way, the siblings would remain within reach of her protective care. Her unique blend of Scottish warmth and Gaelic liveliness had helped her keep the secret tightly guarded—from the girls, from Hans, and even from Mirah herself. Any slip might have reached her, stirring fears or doubts that could disrupt the delicate beginning of the work meant to secure her independence—a word we use loosely, though it speaks to one of life’s most meaningful forms of self-reliance.

Both Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda had reasons—too deep to articulate—for wanting Mirah to stand on her own. Perhaps the little mother found it easier to stay silent because she wasn’t entirely sure how to feel about the remarkable man described to her as Mirah’s brother. If any joy stirred in her, it came not from personal admiration, but from trust in Deronda’s judgment. The news of Mordecai’s illness touched her deeply; yet she struggled with the idea of an enthusiasm she could only see as Jewish zealotry—something foreign, perhaps even awkward, among their circle. To her, he sounded less like a modern man and more like one of Scott’s Covenanters—earnest, intense, but out of step with the world.

Her mind was far from dull. She shared Mab’s delight in the romantic tale of Mirah’s journey and her life with them. But romance, when it steps into real life, needs adjustment. We read late into the night about Sakya-Mouni, St. Francis, Oliver Cromwell—but would we welcome such figures at our door? Or worse, discover they were family?

Mrs. Meyrick had hoped, as her children did, that Mirah’s passion for Judaism would slowly soften, blending into the growing affection between her and their new family. In truth, her favorite version of this story had never included a lost brother at all—but something far more hopeful, something that aligned with the future she saw for Hans. And now—here was a brother who would plunge Mirah back into the deepest currents of Jewish feeling. She couldn’t help but say to Deronda,

“I’m as glad as you are that the pawnbroker isn’t her brother. There are Ezras and Ezras in this world—and really, it’s a relief to think not all Jews are like those shopkeepers who won’t let you leave their stores. And besides, what he said about his mother and sister makes me bless him. I know he’s good. But I’ve never cared much for fanaticism. I suppose I heard too much preaching in my youth—it spoiled my taste for it.”

Deronda smiled gently. “I don’t think you’ll find Mordecai preachy,” he said. “He’s not what I’d call fanatical. I reserve that word for someone whose passion is narrow, blind, who loses sense of balance and becomes unjust to others outside his path. Mordecai is an enthusiast—yes, but I mean that as a compliment. He cares deeply for great human causes, for the welfare of mankind. He’s not strictly orthodox. He makes allowances for others, often adapting his ways not out of compromise, but out of compassion for fellow Jews who live differently. The people he lives with adore him—they just can’t understand his thoughts.”

“Oh, well,” Mrs. Meyrick replied, “I can rise to the level of the pawnbroker’s mother. I can appreciate what’s good in him—and where I don’t see it, I’ll take your word. By your definition, one could be fanatical about common sense, couldn’t they? My poor husband used to say the world would be a dull place if there were nothing but common sense in it. Still, Mirah’s brother will have good bedding—I’ve made sure of that. And I’ve pasted up this extra window to stop drafts.” (They were standing in the new lodging.) “It’s comforting to know these people aren’t strangers to me—no false pretenders. And once the children know, we’ll make the rooms much more cheerful.”

“The next step,” Deronda said, “is to tell Mordecai everything and get him to move. That may be harder than expected.”

“And will you tell Mirah before I speak to the children?” asked Mrs. Meyrick.

Deronda hesitated.

“No,” she continued, persuasively, “let me tell Hans and the girls the evening before. Then they’ll be away the next morning.”

“Yes, that’s best,” he agreed. “But please—give Mordecai justice. Or Ezra, as Mirah will likely want to call him. Don’t feed their imaginations by comparing him to Habakkuk Mucklewrath.” He smiled—Mrs. Meyrick herself having once drawn that comparison.

“Trust me,” she said warmly. “I’ll have to work so hard to make them happy about it, I’ll end up believing it myself. When I’m afraid, it helps to have someone to be angry with for not being brave. It warms the blood.”

Deronda might have spent more time explaining Mordecai’s character—if he hadn’t been consumed by the task ahead: revealing the truth without hurting the Cohens. Mordecai had already shown, in one unforgettable reply, how deeply he felt for others’ feelings. During the days leading up to this moment, Deronda had met Mordecai at the *Hand and Banner*, but now, after careful thought, he wrote to him, asking to meet privately in his own home that evening. He requested only an hour in Mordecai’s workroom, if the Cohens wouldn’t mind the intrusion. If not, he’d accompany Mordecai elsewhere. He hoped this would create a quiet anticipation, preparing Mordecai’s heart.

When Deronda arrived, he was greeted with the usual warmth—some extra care in the women’s clothing, the children slightly awed, and the elders holding back a curious silence. Their understanding of Deronda’s dealings with Mordecai remained a mystery, one they respected without questioning. Jacob, having overheard bits of conversation, stepped forward and asked, “What do you want to talk to Mordecai about?”

“Something very important to him,” Deronda said, pinching the boy’s ear. “But you wouldn’t understand.”

Jacob immediately launched into a string of Hebrew verses he’d memorized, speaking with a mix of guttural and nasal tones, nodding solemnly as if offering irrefutable proof.

“No,” Deronda said seriously, “I can’t say anything like that.”

“I knew it,” Jacob declared, doing a little dance of triumph with his red legs. He pulled things from his knickerbocker pockets and tucked them back in, hinting at his reserves. Then he ran to the workroom door, flung it open, leaned against it, and called out, “Mordecai, here’s the young swell”—a mimicry of his father’s phrase, which he thought perfect for the moment.

He was pulled back by hushes from mother and grandmother. Deronda entered, closed the door behind him, and saw that a carpet had been laid, a chair placed, and the fire and lights tended to—all signs of the Cohens’ respect. As Mordecai rose to greet him, Deronda noticed the solemn expectancy in his face—just as if he’d known Deronda was about to reveal something profound.

Neither spoke until Deronda, with gentle kindness, moved the empty chair across the hearth and sat near Mordecai. Then Mordecai said, voice trembling with certainty:

“You’re here to tell me something my soul has longed for.”

“It’s true,” Deronda replied, cautious. “I have something weighty to share—something I hope you’ll rejoice in.”

“It’s all revealed,” Mordecai breathed, leaning forward, hands clasped. “You are like my brother who drank from my mother’s breast—the heritage is yours. There is no doubt between us.”

“I’ve learned nothing new about myself,” Deronda said gently.

The disappointment was clear. Better not prolong the mistaken hope.

“Except,” he continued, “that some time ago, I discovered another kind of hidden bond between us—beyond what you’ve spoken of.”

Mordecai didn’t open his eyes, but his eyelids fluttered.

“I’ve met someone you care deeply for.”

“One closely tied to your mother,” Deronda added, trying to ease the revelation. Seeing Mordecai shrink, he went on: “Someone she and you cherished above all others.”

Mordecai suddenly grasped Deronda’s wrist—his grip tight, his body rigid with terror. Deronda understood instantly.

“What was prayed for has come to pass,” he whispered. “Mirah has been delivered from evil.”

Mordecai’s hold loosened slightly, but he panted, breathless, tears unshed.

Deronda continued: “Your sister is worthy of the mother you honored.”

He waited. Mordecai sank back, closed his eyes, and began to speak in Hebrew—softly, almost inaudibly—for several minutes. Then he fell into a quiet stillness, his face glowing with peace. Deronda watched—saw something new in Mordecai’s expression, a sweetness like that of the dearly departed. For the first time, he sensed a resemblance to Mirah.

When Mordecai was ready, Deronda told the rest. He softened the account of Mirah’s flight—vague about her father’s behavior, emphasizing instead her longing to come to England, where she believed she might find her mother. He omitted the detail of her attempt to drown herself and his role in saving her. Instead, he described how she’d found shelter with friends of his, whose kindness and efforts he had supported. What he focused on was Mirah’s devotion to her mother and brother—every detail, every emotion.

“It was searching for them,” Deronda said, smiling, “that led me to this house. The name Ezra Cohen was then the most meaningful name in the world to me. I confess I was afraid for a long time. Perhaps you’ll forgive me now for asking about the elder Mrs. Cohen’s daughter. I cared deeply about who Mirah’s friends were. But I found a brother worthy of her when I learned that her Ezra was hiding under the name Mordecai.”

“Mordecai is my true name—Ezra Mordecai Cohen.”

“Is there any kinship between your family and mine?” Deronda asked.

“Only the kinship of Israel,” Mordecai replied. “My soul clings to these people who have sheltered me, who gave me succor through their love—like sweet fragrance rising from things long buried. It is good for me to endure their ignorance, to bind myself to them in gratitude. It reminds me of the spiritual poverty of the Jewish people, and keeps me from replacing loving wisdom with impatient knowledge.”

“But now,” Deronda said, fearing resistance, “with a closer tie drawing you, do you feel bound to stay? It seems right—don’t you think—that you should live with your sister? I’ve prepared a home near her friends, so she can join you. Please grant me this wish. It will let me be with you often when Mirah must leave. That’s my selfish reason. But the real reason is that Mirah will want to care for you—and you ought to let her guard you as a brother. You’ll have books. I’ll learn from you. I’ll take you to see rivers and trees. You’ll have the rest you need—nay, the rest I need for you. This is my claim upon you now that we’ve found each other.”

Deronda spoke with earnest tenderness, as if pleading with a revered elder brother. Mordecai stared at him, listening deeply. After a pause, he said, almost reproachfully:

“And you would have me doubt whether you were born a Jew? Have we not touched each other with invisible threads? Have we not quivered together, like leaves from one root? I know what I am outwardly—I am one among the poor, stricken, dying. But our souls know each other. They looked silently, as if long parted and now reunited. When they found voice, they were certain—every word, understanding. The life of Israel flows in your veins.”

Deronda sat still, his face tingling. He could neither deny nor affirm. He waited, hoping Mordecai would offer more clarity.

After a moment, Mordecai said firmly:

“What you ask of me, I will do. And our mother—may the Eternal bless her in our hearts—would have wished it too. I accept your kindness. Mirah’s home shall be mine.” He paused, then added softly, “But I shall grieve to leave these parents and children. You must tell them—my heart would fail me.”

“I knew you’d want me to,” Deronda said, relieved. “Shall we go now?”

“Yes,” Mordecai said, rising with the dignity of one facing a painful duty. “Let us not delay. It must be done.” Then, after a moment, “But do not dwell on my sister more than necessary.”

They entered the parlor. Mordecai turned to Jacob: “Ask your father to come. Tell Sarah to watch the shop. My friend has something to say.” He addressed the elder Mrs. Cohen. It was part of Mordecai’s oddness that he called this man his friend. The two women tried to show respect by insisting Deronda sit in the best seat.

Cohen entered, pen behind his ear, rubbing his hands. “Well, sir!” he exclaimed. “I’m glad you’re joining our family again. We’re quite comfortable, I think.”

He looked around, shining with pleasure. Once seated, the scene was touching: Baby rocked under a scarlet blanket in the corner, Adelaide Rebekah on her grandmother’s knee, Jacob nestled between his father’s legs. In the center stood Deronda and Mordecai—Mordecai slightly withdrawn, trying to hide his emotional vulnerability. The fire cast rich shadows, turning dark eyes into jewels, and lending speech to the quiet looks exchanged.

“I’ve just told Mordecai of an event that changes his life,” Deronda began. “But I hope you’ll agree it’s joyful. Since he sees you as his dearest friends, he wants me to tell you at once.”

“Relations with money, sir?” Cohen interrupted, feeling a sudden insight.

“No,” Deronda smiled. “But a precious relation—his sister. A young woman, good and lovely, who will care for him in every way.”

“Married?”

“No.”

“But with a maintenance?”

“She has talents that will ensure her livelihood. A home has already been arranged for Mordecai.”

Silence. Then the grandmother sighed:

“Well, well… and so you’re leaving us, Mordecai?”

“And no children—no Jacob, no Adelaide, no Eugenie!” she wailed.

“Ay, ay,” Cohen said, firm. “Jacob’s learning will wear out. He must go to school. It’ll be hard times for Jacob.”

To Jacob, this sounded like doom. His face filled with sorrow. At the mention of “hard times,” he burst into loud sobs. Adelaide Rebekah followed suit, howling abruptly. Baby woke and screamed. Someone had to carry her out. Hushing filled the room. Mordecai, pierced by the cries, reached for Jacob, who, amid tears, turned his head to see the reaction. His father, saying, “Never mind, old man—you’ll go to the riders,” released him. Jacob ran to Mordecai, who held him close, resting his cheek on the child’s black hair—silent.

Then Cohen, sensing the need for a speech, addressed Deronda with elevated tone, elbows squared, hand on each knee:

“We’re not the sort to begrudge anyone’s luck, sir. I’m not envious. If someone offered to set Mordecai up in a shop two doors down, I wouldn’t make faces. I’m not one to think low of myself or fear others’ chances. If I’m offal, let a wise man tell me—I haven’t heard it yet. In business, I’m not a fragile thing. If they roll me, I curl up like a caterpillar and find my feet when left alone. Though you’re taking some of our good works—property bearing interest—I can afford it. My wife and mother gave their all to Mordecai. A Jew must not act like a servant paid only for reward—though I see no harm in reward if I can earn it. As for schooling—neither poor nor greedy. I wouldn’t hang myself for sixpence. But the truth is—women and children love Mordecai. You can see it, sir. A Jewish man thanks God daily for not being born a woman. But a woman thanks God for being made as He willed. And we know what He made her—a child-bearing, tender-hearted creature. Our children are strong, as Addy’s are. She’s not soft, but her heart is tender. So forgive us, sir, for not being glad at once. And as for this young lady—by what you say, ‘young lady’ is the proper term—we’ll be glad for Mordecai’s sake, when we count our blessings and see where we stand.”

Before Deronda could respond, Mordecai broke in:

“Friends! For food, clothing, shelter—I could not have asked for better than you’ve given. You’ve sweetened the morsel with love. I thought my joy would be to teach the lad till my last breath. But now—I am like one who dressed himself in a shroud, accustomed to making the grave his bed—when the divine voice called, ‘Arise, go forth; the night is not yet come.’ I would not have left your kindness for any lesser cause. But we’ve been taught—‘The reward of one duty is the power to fulfill another,’ said Ben Azai. You’ve made your duty to a poor brother a joy for both of us. Your reward will be that you cannot rest without joy in similar deeds. And may Jacob come to visit me?”

He turned to Deronda, who nodded.

“Of course. It’s no farther than Brompton.”

Jacob, calmed by the unfolding scene, began to see light. “Visit” meant cakes, freedom, grandpa’s knife shop. He danced away from Mordecai, taking a central post on the hearth, hands in pockets.

“Well,” the grandmother sighed, “I hope you’ll get kosher meat, Mordecai. You’ll have to trust your new household.”

“That’s all right,” Cohen said quickly, eager to avoid questions. “So, sir,” he added, turning to Deronda with amused clarity, “it was better than learning you had to talk to Mordecai about! I wondered then. I felt there was something.”

“Mordecai may explain how I came to seek him,” Deronda said, rising. “I should go now.”

CHAPTER XLVII. “And you must love him ere to you He will seem worthy of your love.” —WORDSWORTH.

There was something almost tender in the way Daniel Deronda imagined Mordecai dressed in his new clothes—gray flannel shirts, a dressing-gown like a Franciscan’s brown robe, with only his head and neck visible above it. It was as if he were painting a portrait in his mind, not just of the man, but of the moment Mirah would see him. Half of Deronda’s delight came from seeing Mordecai through her eyes—knowing how deeply she would rejoice, untouched by any sense of discomfort or judgment. But after all was arranged, doubt crept in. Was he perhaps mistaken? Was he replacing the deeper truth with mere appearances? Could Mirah not feel the same reverence for her brother precisely because of the poverty that marked him—the quiet dignity of a life lived apart, shaped by hardship and memory?

Yet there were others to consider. The Meyricks, kind-hearted though they were, might find Mordecai too foreign, too unlike their own world. And Deronda found himself quietly shielding Mordecai from anything that might provoke their prejudice—anything that could stir up that instinctive aversion felt by those who lack the gift of seeing beyond surface, who cannot bathe even the humblest thing in the golden light of compassion and awe. That ability, born of love and reverence, was rare—and it was exactly what Deronda saw in Mirah.

That evening, long after Mirah had gone to bed, the household gathered around Mrs. Meyrick’s fire. Hans, now settled in his Chelsea rooms, lingered on. With the flames dancing, Mrs. Meyrick said, “Now, Kate, put out your candle, and come close. Hans, dear, stop laughing at those poems—just once, please—and join us. I have something wonderful to tell.”

“Oh, I know,” Kate replied, smiling. “I’ve seen it in your eyes for days. You’ve been pretending to run errands when you really wanted to wait until we were all together.”

The girls drew near, feet warming on the fender. Hans pushed his chair closer, sitting astride it, chin resting on his fists. “Well then,” said Mrs. Meyrick, speaking clearly, “if you’re so clever, maybe you already know that Mirah’s brother has been found.”

“Confound it!” Hans burst out, nearly jumping up.

“Hans, that’s cruel,” Mab scolded. “Suppose we’d lost you?”

“I can’t help feeling sorry,” Kate admitted softly. “But where is her mother?”

“She’s dead,” Mrs. Meyrick said gently.

“And the brother?” Amy asked. “Is he a nice man?”

“Not some flashy fellow covered in jewelry—a Crystal Palace Assyrian with a hat,” Hans muttered, annoyed.

“You unfeeling children!” Mrs. Meyrick sighed. “You don’t think of Mirah’s joy at this news.”

“But Mirah hardly remembers him,” Kate reminded her.

“People lost for twelve years should never come back,” Hans declared. “They always get in the way.”

“Hans!” she reproached him. “If I’d been missing twenty years, you’d feel differently.”

“I said twelve,” Hans interrupted sharply. “Twelve years—that’s the time when lost relatives ought to stay away.”

“Well, finding people is still nice,” Mab said, clasping her knees. “It means there’s a story to tell.”

“Did Prince Camaralzaman find him?” she asked.

Then Mrs. Meyrick told them everything she knew—how Mr. Deronda had helped bring Mordecai into their lives, how he admired him deeply, and how he believed Mirah was the sister who could truly understand him.

“Deronda is getting completely absurd about Jews,” Hans declared, rising abruptly and slamming his chair back. “He wants to encourage Mirah in every one of her strange beliefs.”

“Oh, shame on you, Hans!” Mab protested. “To speak like that of Mr. Deronda!”

Mrs. Meyrick’s face flickered with an emotion she quickly hid—something between pride and sorrow.

“And now,” Hans continued, pacing with hands in his pockets, “we’ll never be all together again. We’ll have to invite this prophet Elijah for tea, and Mirah will spend all her time dreaming of Jerusalem’s ruins. She’ll become narrow-minded—like a nun. Everything will change—our home, our peace. I’ll start drinking.”

“Oh, Hans,” Kate said, exasperated. “Men are the most selfish creatures alive. They must have everything their way, or they become unbearable.”

“Oh, oh, oh—it’s dreadful!” Mab cried. “I feel as if ancient Nineveh has returned.”

“If you went to university and learned so much,” Amy said, “you’d think you could be more mature. You should learn to accept the people Providence sends us. _We_ will have to accept him.”

“I hope you all enjoy the new Lamentations of Jeremiah—‘to be continued in our next,’” Hans said, grabbing his wide-awake hat. “It’s no use being educated if you have to endure men who stare at you with fixed ideas, demanding every word you say be a footnote to their sermon. If you’re under a petrifying wall, better be an old boot. I don’t feel like an old boot.”

Then, suddenly, “Good night, little mother,” he whispered, bending to kiss her brow quickly—almost desperately. On his way out, he added, “Good night, girls.”

“Suppose Mirah knew how you’re behaving,” Kate called after him.

His reply was the slam of the door.

“I wish I could see Mirah when Mr. Deronda tells her,” Kate said, turning to her mother. “She’ll look so beautiful.”

But Deronda, reconsidering, had written a letter the previous night—asking Mrs. Meyrick to break the news herself, rather than waiting for him. He didn’t reveal the real reason: that he feared repeating a tale in which he sounded too grand, too self-sacrificing. Instead, he said he wished to stay with Mordecai while Mrs. Meyrick brought Mirah, so they could meet privately before any sudden changes in their living arrangements.

Still, Deronda wondered—how would Mordecai react? After years of isolation, consumed by thought and weakened by illness, would he allow himself to feel warmth for his sister beyond duty? Deronda had noticed Mordecai’s affection for the Cohens, especially little Jacob—proof that his heart still held room for love. But these were familiar faces, part of his daily life. Now, Mirah was different. Mordecai asked no questions about her. He remained unusually silent, accepting the changes without comment. He wore the new clothes obediently, but later smiled faintly and said, “I’ll keep my old garments—just for memory.”

When they sat waiting, Mordecai kept his eyes closed, yet his face twitched with tension. His hands moved restlessly. He was experiencing a deep inner disturbance—one known only to minds long fixed in a single current, suddenly forced into a new channel. For those whose strength lies in long-held habits, such a revival of the past can feel like a threat—like an illness. Joy may come, but joy too can be terrifying.

Deronda felt the tremor of excitement. When the doorbell rang, he stepped out—not knowing why—so he could greet Mirah first.

He was startled to see her wearing the very hat and cloak he had first seen her in—the one that had once been soaked and used as a shroud. She had come down in that dress deliberately. When Mrs. Meyrick asked, “You like to go in that dress, dear?” Mirah answered quietly, “My brother is poor. I want to look like him. Otherwise, he might feel distant.”

Deronda could not respond. He felt a quiet shame for his own careful preparations.

They shook hands silently. Mirah looked pale, overwhelmed. When Deronda opened the door, Mordecai rose slowly, his eyes fixed on the entrance.

Mirah took only a few steps—then stopped.

They stood motionless, not quite seeing each other, but meeting in the echo of memory. Their presence was less important than the past they carried.

Mirah spoke first, in the same gentle tone she had used when telling of her mother’s call: “Ezra.”

Mordecai moved forward swiftly, placing his hand on her shoulders. He was taller, looking down at her with tenderness. “That was our mother’s voice,” he said softly. “Do you remember her calling me?”

“Yes,” Mirah whispered. “And how you answered—‘Mother!’—and I knew you loved her.”

She threw her arms around his neck, clasped her hands behind his head, and pulled him down, kissing his face with innocent, joyful abandon. Her hat fell backward, revealing her curls.

“Ah, the dear head, the dear head,” Mordecai murmured, touching her hair gently.

“You’re very ill, Ezra,” Mirah said, her eyes full of concern.

“Yes, dear child,” he replied calmly. “I shall not be long with you in the body.”

“Oh, I’ll love you,” she said, her voice sweet and steady. “We’ll talk. I’ll tell you everything. And you’ll teach me—how to be a good Jewess. What Mother would have wanted me to be. I’ll be with you whenever I’m not working. I work now. I’ll earn money to keep us. Oh, I’ve had such good friends.”

Until then, Mirah had forgotten they were not alone. Now she turned, keeping one hand on her brother’s arm, and looked toward Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda. The little mother’s heart swelled with happiness at the sight—this reunion, this fragile bond restored. To her, Mordeci seemed more dignified, more refined than she had dared imagine, based only on Deronda’s descriptions.

“See this dear lady,” Mirah said. “I was a stranger, a poor wanderer. She believed in me, treated me like a daughter. Please give my brother your hand,” she added, taking Mrs. Meyrick’s hand and placing it in Mordecai’s, then pressing both hands to her lips.

“The Eternal Goodness has been with you,” Mordecai said softly. “You have helped fulfill our mother’s prayer.”

“Let’s go now,” Deronda said gently, laying a hand on Mrs. Meyrick’s arm. “We’ll return later.”

She nodded at once. Deronda did not want to risk mentioning anything about himself that he had withheld from Mordecai. And now, he felt no unease at leaving them alone—together at last.

It is a bitter and unrewarding task to plan every contingency with the sole aim of securing one’s own safety—so Machiavelli observed when speaking of Cæsar Borgia, who had foreseen every possible danger after his father’s death, except one: he never imagined that his own life would end so swiftly upon the very moment of his father’s passing.

Grandcourt’s influence in society was not forged by action or ambition, but by inheritance. His importance lay in land, not in ideas. The shifting currents of politics, social reform, or even financial crises passed him by like distant thunder, touching only the surface of his life through the rise and fall of his rents. He skimmed the leading columns of newspapers, yet his opinions on such matters were as narrow as they were dismissive—he lumped all Germans, all businessmen, and all voters who used the wrong kind of soap into one scornful category: “brutes.” He did not act on these views; instead, he met any mention of them with a cold glance beneath lowered lids and an icy silence that unsettled those who dared to speak. But within the sphere of his own desires, Grandcourt displayed traits akin to the most ruthless continental diplomats—the kind who command without needing to explain.

He watched Gwendolen’s every movement around Deronda with sharp attention. He would have denied being jealous, for jealousy implied uncertainty, and Grandcourt believed himself entirely in control. It did not trouble him that his wife might prefer another man’s company; what mattered was that she knew, as clearly as if her hand were cuffed shut, that no inclination could ever override his will. There had been hesitation in his decision to marry—but none in how he interpreted the marriage bond. He did not regret it. In fact, it had given his life direction, new objects to dominate. And he did not regret choosing Gwendolen. Her beauty, her bearing, the shape of her nails, the delicacy of her ears—all met his exacting taste. He wanted a woman elevated by him, admired by others, intelligent enough to spar with wit, yet prone to flare up when provoked—because that made her submission more satisfying. He took pride in knowing she married him not out of love, but from pressure, expectation, and perhaps a measure of fear. For men who value dominion over affection, why should the mind change its nature at the altar?

Grandcourt did not believe he had chosen poorly. Once he had assumed the role of husband, he refused to be mocked or seen as weak. This was not jealousy—it was something colder, sharper. Yet his behavior often mirrored jealousy as closely as yellow resembles yellow: different causes, same effect.

He had come to London earlier than usual, not merely for legal business—arranging his will, transferring mortgages, finalizing the deal with his uncle over Diplow, secured by the quiet lure of ready cash—but also to display his bride. Though he claimed not to care about admiration, such indifference required an audience to admire or envy. Like someone who enjoys staring stonily at smiling faces, he needed the smiles to exist. Grandcourt paraded Gwendolen through London’s grandest circles—attending lavish receptions, riding in elegant carriages, appearing beside her at every notable event. He wanted her sought after, desired, spoken of. He enjoyed seeing other men eager to talk to her, escort her—within his sight. Even a touch of haughty coquetry on her part pleased him. But he despised her interactions with Deronda.

After the musical gathering at Lady Mallinger’s, where Grandcourt had observed the exchange between Gwendolen and Deronda with the same keenness as Hans, he invited Deronda to the next gathering—alongside the Mallingers—without a hint of concern. He avoided suggesting that Deronda’s presence mattered to him. Nor did he directly confront Gwendolen about her behavior that evening. To do so might have revealed too much disgust, and pride forbade it.

A few days later, however, he remarked casually, without regard for context:

“Nothing makes a woman look more awkward than watching people and showing temper in public. A woman ought to have good manners. Otherwise, it’s unbearable to appear with her.”

Gwendolen understood the implication—and felt a pang of alarm. She, too, disliked things deeply, and preferred that her distaste included admirers. But now, the shadow of disapproval only heightened her longing for any chance encounter with Deronda. Her city life, though exhausting, was exhilarating—a whirlwind like foreign travel. Inside, she might feel despondent, but there was always a schedule, a performance, a distraction. Yet whenever she met Deronda—even briefly—the moment expanded in her mind. Each word between them grew in significance, each glance seemed charged with meaning. How could Deronda avoid this? He did not shun her. On the contrary, he gently reassured her that her trust in him had not been misplaced—that it had not diminished his respect. And he liked being near her. How could he not? She was not just a puzzle; she was a radiant woman, whose mind and fate stirred his care. Though he saw their paths diverging, he could not ignore the weight of her need—the way she turned to him, beseeching, persistent, as if he had once saved her from harm, and now she expected him to save her again.

One morning, during breakfast, Gwendolen, seized by a sudden resolve, toyed with her prawns and said, “I think I’ll take singing lessons while we’re in town. I want to become accomplished.”

“Why?” Grandcourt asked, languidly.

“Why?” she echoed, playing at defiance. “Because I can’t eat pâté de foie gras to make me sleepy, I can’t smoke, and I can’t go to the club to enjoy leaving. I need variety in my boredom. What time would suit you best—when you’re busy with your lawyers—to have lessons from that little Jewess whose singing is all the rage?”

“Whenever you like,” he replied, pushing away his plate and leaning back, his eyes fixed on her with their usual reptilian stillness as he idly played with the tiny spaniel on his lap. Gwendolen detested the dogs—they fawned on him too eagerly.

Then, with the same bored tone, he added, “I don’t see why a lady should sing. Amateurs make fools of themselves. A woman can’t risk herself like that in society. And no one wants to hear squalling in private.”

“I like frankness,” Gwendolen said, lifting her chin slightly, turning her gaze away. “That’s a husband’s charm. But”—she paused, swallowing her humiliation—“I suppose you don’t object to Miss Lapidoth singing at our party on the fourth? I thought of hiring her. Lady Brackenshaw had her. The Raymonds, who are very particular about music, did too. And Mr. Deronda, who’s a musician and a first-rate judge, says no one sings with such taste for a drawing-room. I think his opinion counts.”

She meant to throw a small stone.

“It’s indecent of Deronda to praise that girl so openly,” Grandcourt said, voice flat.

“Indecent?” Gwendolen exclaimed, flushing, startled. “To praise?”

“Yes. Especially since she’s under Lady Mallinger’s patronage. He should keep quiet. Men can see what his connection with her must be.”

“Men who judge others by themselves,” she shot back, her face whitening, instantly struck by the danger of her words.

“Of course,” he said, relishing the pinch. “And a woman should follow their judgment—or she’ll run headlong into the wrong place. I suppose you consider Deronda a saint.”

“Oh dear no,” she said, summoning her strength, her voice high and brittle. “Only a little less of a monster.”

She rose, pushed her chair back slowly, and walked out as if afraid of revealing she’d drunk too much wine. She locked her dressing room door and sat down, pale and still. Even after reading the poisonous letter, she had never felt such agony. Emotion had reached a point where it blurred into physical pain. Deronda—no longer the noble figure she believed him to be—had become a grotesque apparition. She couldn’t process whether it was truth or fiction. Worse, she realized how fragile her faith in him had been—how little she truly knew of his life. His severity, his rebukes, even his poetry—all now seemed monstrous. His grave face, once inspiring, now appeared like a mask worn by ordinary men.

This storm passed through her like a fever dream. Then, suddenly, sunlight pierced the gray morning, flooding the room. She moved gently, looked around—there was a world outside this nightmare. The dream meant nothing. She stood, arms raised, hands clasped in her familiar gesture of relief, walking through the beams.

“It’s not true! What does it matter if *he* believes it?” she whispered—not faith restored, but a desperate cry against suffocation.

How could she endure the day? With sudden impulse, her mind raced toward actions that would prove her belief: she would question Lady Mallinger, write to Deronda, pour out her fury. No—she would go to Mirah. That was feasible. She had the excuse of asking her to sing at the party. What else would she say? She didn’t know—she couldn’t wait to find out. If that idea were alive, she would strangle it without thinking.

She rang for her maid, asked if Grandcourt had left—confirmed he had—and ordered the carriage. She began dressing, then wandered the drawing room like a trapped creature, not recognizing herself in the mirrors, oblivious to the gilded prison around her. Her husband would discover her absence—and punish her. But she cared for nothing now but certainty.

She had Mirah’s address. Soon, she was on her way, surrounded by fine equipage, her heart pounding, waiting for answers she didn’t know how to ask. She was unaware of the journey until she reached Mirah’s house and heard Deronda’s voice behind a folding door. She knew it instantly—by instinct, by dread. She trembled, unbuttoning and rebuttoning her gloves, biting her lips, pretending difficulty, while the door opened and Mirah stepped forward with calm grace and a gentle smile.

Relief washed over Gwendolen. She smiled back, extended her hand silently. As she sat, hearing Deronda’s voice still, she felt a surge of energy—some vague sense that the truth could not be what she feared.

Mirah drew her chair close, as if to muffle their conversation, and looked at her with quiet anticipation.

“Perhaps you wonder to see me,” Gwendolen began softly, almost shyly. “I should have written—but I wanted to make a request.”

“I’m glad you came instead,” Mirah said, noticing the changed expression of the “Vandyke duchess” as Hans had called her. Her own face was warm and composed, a stark contrast to Gwendolen’s pale, agitated beauty beneath the plumed hat.

“I hoped,” Gwendolen continued, “you wouldn’t mind singing at our house on the fourth—like Lady Brackenshaw’s party. I’d be so grateful.”

“I’d be honored,” Mirah said. “At ten?”

“At ten,” Gwendolen replied, then paused, realizing she had nothing else to say. She couldn’t leave. She had to speak. The words burst out:

“Mr. Deronda is in the next room.”

“Yes,” Mirah answered calmly. “He’s reading Hebrew with my brother.”

“You have a brother?” Gwendolen asked—she’d heard it before, but hadn’t paid attention.

“Yes,” Mirah said, her voice softening. “A dear brother, ill—consumptive. Mr. Deronda has been the best friend to both of us. He found me when I was about to drown myself—so poor, so forsaken. You would have thought me a beggar. But he treated me as if I were a queen’s daughter. He brought me to the best woman. He found my brother. And he honors him—though he too was poor, nearly destitute. And my brother honors him. That’s no small thing,” Mirah said, her voice deepening, shaking her head. “My brother is wise and great-minded. And Mr. Deronda says there are few men like him.”
