A flash of Jewish pride flared in her gratitude—and anger, directed at Gwendolen for doubting him.

But Gwendolen was like a thirsty soul drinking pure water. She didn’t notice Mirah’s anger. All she felt was the overwhelming certainty that Deronda’s life was nothing like Grandcourt’s slander. Even Mirah’s words dissolved into the flood of relief. She pressed Mirah’s hand, whispering, “Thank you, thank you,” then rose, murmuring, “I must go—I’ll see you on the fourth—I’m so grateful,” and left, moving mechanically, while Mirah watched, puzzled by the sudden retreat into cold dignity.

Gwendolen had no warmth left for Mirah. The desperate need to refute Grandcourt’s lies had been satisfied—now she only wanted to escape. She felt out of place, afraid of Deronda seeing her. Back in the carriage, she faced the return home.

When she arrived at Grosvenor Square, Grandcourt was arriving with a cigar in his fingers. He tossed it aside, helped her out, and followed her upstairs. She slipped into the drawing room, avoiding further contact. She sat, weary, removing her gloves, rubbing her forehead, making her presence seem as insignificant as possible. But he sat too—close, directly across from her.

“May I ask where you’ve been at this hour?” he said.

“Oh, yes,” she replied, placing her gloves on the table. “I went to Miss Lapidoth’s to ask her to sing for us.”

“And to inquire about her relationship with Deronda?” he asked, voice low, sneer colder than ice.

For the first time since their marriage, Gwendolen lashed out—without hesitation.

“Yes,” she said sharply. “And what you said is false—a vile, wicked lie.”

“She told you so?” he returned, the sneer distilled to poison.

Gwendolen was silent. Her anger curdled into mute fury. What proof could she offer? Her inner certainty crumbled under his breath. There was no evidence—only her feeling, which he would call folly. She turned away, glaring at the far wall. She tried to rise—but he blocked her path.

Grandcourt saw his victory. “It doesn’t matter about the singing,” he drawled. “You may have her if you wish.” Then, after a pause, in his lowest, most commanding tone: “But you will not go near that house again. As my wife, you must accept my judgment on what is proper. When you became Mrs. Grandcourt, you agreed not to make a fool of yourself. You did today. And if you continue, you’ll soon be talked about at the clubs in ways you won’t like. What do *you* know of the world? You married *me*. You must follow my judgment.”

Each sentence cut deeper than the last. If a doctor had delivered a fatal diagnosis, she could not have been more helpless. But she was allowed to move now. And Grandcourt never mentioned it again. He knew the power of his words. Had this man with the rigid profile been sent to govern a rebellious colony, he might have earned fame. He understood that it was safer to destroy than to placate. And he would not hesitate to do it.

Ever in his soul, that larger justice which makes gratitude triumph over resentment—this was the mark of noble natures, those whose lives are wide and full, capable of deeper joy than mere intellect can grasp. Not the clever minds who magnify goodness until it crumbles into dust, unworthy even of a “thank you”—those are the devil’s friars, vowed to poverty in love and trust, yet forced to beg from a world that still holds some human worth.

Daniel Deronda had not told Gwendolen, as he left her, that he would not see her again for a long time—that he was going away. He chose his words carefully, lest Grandcourt misinterpret the significance of such a parting, and understand that it mattered deeply to her. But Daniel was indeed leaving under circumstances so profound they stirred the deepest currents of his being. When he set out to keep his promise of visiting her, he was already wrapped in a solemn emotion, one that called back the most sacred moments of his life.

Sir Hugo had summoned him urgently to his chambers with a note: “Come immediately. Something has happened.” The warning had brought a strange relief when Daniel entered the baronet’s study and found not distress, but quiet affection awaiting him.

“It is nothing to grieve you, sir?” Daniel asked, his voice steady with renewed confidence rather than inquiry, as he took the hand offered to him.

There was something unusual in Sir Hugo’s gaze, a softness beneath the gravity, and his voice trembled slightly as he replied, “No, Dan, no. Sit down. I have something to say.”

Daniel obeyed, though a shadow of预感 hovered over him. It was rare indeed for Sir Hugo to show such depth of feeling.

“Not to grieve you, my boy,” the baronet continued, “no. At least, not more than it may grieve you too. But I never thought this—this very thing—would ever come to pass. There were reasons why I never prepared you for it. Reasons why I never spoke of your parentage. Yet I have always done what I could to shield you from harm.”

He paused, and Daniel could not speak. He could not say, “I’ve never felt it as harm.” Even if that were true, his voice failed him. More than either man knew rested on this moment—the breaking of silence, the unveiling of truth.

Sir Hugo had never seen Daniel’s face so pale, his lips pressed together in silent pain. With growing tenderness, he went on, as if afraid now of hurting him further. “I acted at your mother’s request. She wished secrecy. But now she wishes it ended. She wants to see you. I will give you this letter—read it when you’re ready. It tells you only what she asks, and where to find her.”

He handed Daniel a letter written on foreign paper. Without hesitation, Daniel slipped it into his breast pocket—a small comfort, to be spared the immediate burden of reading.

The emotion on Daniel’s face moved the baronet deeply, visibly unsettling his composure. Sir Hugo struggled to continue.

And Daniel—his entire soul consumed by a question too heavy to voice—could not wait. This was a sacred moment. If he let it pass, he would lose forever the courage to ask and the grace to hear the answer.

For a while, both men sat in silence, their thoughts hanging unspoken between them. Then Daniel lifted his eyes and spoke, his voice trembling with reverence, careful not to hint at the years of unexpressed longing, the ache of withheld affection:

“Is my father still alive?”

The reply came swiftly, low and firm: “No.”

In the storm of emotions that followed, joy and sorrow mingled beyond distinction. For Sir Hugo too, new light had broken through the past. After a silence in which Daniel felt like a man whose old beliefs had vanished before he’d fully embraced another, the baronet said, almost in confession:

“Perhaps I was wrong, Dan, to take on this role. Perhaps I grew too fond of it—of having you all to myself. If I caused you pain that I might have eased, I ask your forgiveness.”

“The forgiveness has been there long ago,” Daniel answered softly. “The greatest pain has always been for someone else—someone I never knew—someone I am now about to meet. But it has never dimmed the love I’ve held for you. You have shaped much of the life I remember.”

And in that moment, without word or plan, the two men clasped hands—bound not by duty, but by truth, by shared grief, and by love.

CHAPTER L. “If some mortal, born too soon,
Were laid away in some great trance—the ages
Coming and going all the while—till dawned
His true time’s advent; and could then record
The words they spoke who kept watch by his bed,
Then I might tell more of the breath so light
Upon my eyelids, and the fingers warm
Among my hair. Youth is confused; yet never
So dull was I but, when that spirit passed,
I turned to him, scarce consciously, as turns
A water-snake when fairies cross his sleep.”
—BROWNING: _Paracelsus_.

It was a letter from an unknown hand that Sir Hugo placed into Daniel Deronda’s hands—a letter that carried no flourish of emotion, only quiet commands wrapped in silence.

My good friend and yours, Sir Hugo Mallinger, will have told you that I wish to see you. My health is frail, and I do not wish to delay what I have long withheld. Be at the _Albergo dell’ Italia_ in Genoa by the fourteenth of this month. Wait for me there. I cannot say when I shall arrive from Spezia, where I remain. It depends upon things beyond my control. Wait for me—the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Bring with you the diamond ring Sir Hugo gave you. I would like to see it again.

—Your unknown mother,

The words were plain, almost cold. They offered no clue to what lay ahead—only an invitation to meet a woman who had lived apart from him, unseen and unheard, for all his life. Yet Deronda did not hesitate. He accepted Sir Hugo’s silence as a boundary, a promise not to guess, not to shape the truth before it came. The realization that his lifelong assumptions about his origins were wrong stunned him—not with anger, but with a strange stillness. His mind raced through possibilities, but he refused to let any one take root. To fix on a hope or fear now would be to betray himself, to give weight to a dream before it became fact. He must wait. And in waiting, he must prepare—not with longing, but with courage.

He could not speak of this journey to anyone—not even to Mordecai, whose heart would beat with the same urgency, though in different measure. If he said, “I am going to learn the truth about my birth,” Mordecai’s hopes would rise like fire, dangerous and bright. So instead, Deronda said only that Sir Hugo wished him to travel, that it might be brief, and that he was uncertain how long he’d stay. He tried to sound indifferent, though his own pulse betrayed him.

“I will ask to have the child Jacob stay with me,” Mordecai said softly, as if seeking comfort in small things. “I will go round and ask Mrs. Cohen to let him come.”

“Her grandmother will deny you nothing,” Deronda replied gently. “I’m glad you were a little wrong as well as I,” he added, smiling. “You thought old Mrs. Cohen wouldn’t bear to see Mirah.”

“I undervalued her heart,” Mordecai murmured. “She can rejoice in another’s bloom, even when her own has faded.”

“Oh, they are dear good people,” Mirah said, her voice warm with affection. “I feel as if we all belong to each other.”

“What would you have felt if Ezra had been your brother?” Deronda teased, a little irritated that she welcomed someone who had caused him so much unease.

Mirah looked at him, startled, then lowered her eyes. “He is not a bad man,” she whispered. “I think he would never abandon anyone.”

And then she blushed deeply, glancing shyly at Mordecai before turning away. Her father was in her thoughts—her secret sorrow, their shared pain. “If he should come and find us!”—that thought haunted her, turning daylight into shadow, every street corner into a hiding place for ghosts.

Deronda understood. He saw the flicker of fear in her face, the echo of a past too heavy to speak. And suddenly, he recognized something in himself—the same ache, the same dread of revelation. For his mother’s letter, though it brought her closer, also pushed her farther away. The tender image he had built of her—the woman who might have suffered, who deserved love and care—crumbled under the weight of her real words. Her handwriting, her tone—cold, detached—had stripped away the fantasy. She was not the mother he had imagined. She was a stranger, veiled, enigmatic, and now, somehow, less real than the ghost he had conjured.

Yet even as he mourned that loss, he found himself thinking not of her, but of Mordecai and Mirah. Their lives, their hopes, their fragile bond—these filled his mind more than the mystery of his blood. When he set out for Genoa, it was not just to meet his mother, but to carry the weight of their dreams with him.

“God bless you, Dan,” Sir Hugo said, gripping his hand firmly. “Whatever changes, I’ll always be the oldest friend you’ve known—the one who’s loved you most. I couldn’t have loved you better if you’d been my own son—though I’d have preferred seeing you master of the Abbey, not just my fine nephew. Then you’d have taken a political path, perhaps. But such things are not ours to choose.”

It was a gentle defense, a way of softening the sharpness of unspoken truths. Sir Hugo knew, and yet he left room for silence.

When Deronda arrived at the _Albergo dell’ Italia_, the princess was not there. On the second day, a letter came—written in careful French—saying she might arrive within a week, or not for two weeks more. Circumstances made it impossible to know. She asked him to wait patiently.

And so he waited.

With nothing but time and uncertainty, Deronda turned to the city. He wandered its streets, watched the sea from a boat, studied the quay where centuries ago Spanish Jews had fled—exhausted, dying, their children at their breasts, their eyes hollow with hunger. He saw history not as distant memory, but as a living wound. And in those images, he could not help wondering—was he one of them? Was his blood part of that suffering? He wanted to shut out such thoughts, but they rose like tides. He had never admitted to himself that he *wished* Mordecai’s belief to be true—that he was descended from a line of exiled Jews, bound by faith and fate. But he could not deny that the idea stirred him. Still, wishing was weakness. It was disowning the truth before it came. He had no choice. He must accept whatever was revealed.

And yet—what if Mordecai was wrong? What if he, Daniel Deronda, was not tied to any such destiny? Then he would not say, “I wish.” But he could not help feeling where the cost lay.

Over these thoughts, another presence loomed—Gwendolen. Not with passion, not yet, but with a quiet ache that echoed deeper than desire. There was a kind of love that wasn’t possessive, not entirely romantic, yet not mere friendship either. It was the love of what might have been—“I should have loved her, if…”—the “if” carrying both lost chances and unspoken rules. In Gwendolen, he had seen beauty, strength, a soul that trembled beneath its pride. And she had spoken words that stayed with him: “You must remain near me—must not forsake me.” Those words returned to him like music, like arrows dipped in pity—“Lamenti saettaron me diversi / Che di pietà ferrati avean gli strali.”

He remembered her as she stood at the edge of her world, caught between duty and desire. He remembered the look in her eyes—fear, yearning, defiance. And now, as he waited in Genoa, her fate seemed entangled with his own. Hers was personal, immediate. His was broader—shaped by secrets, by ancestry, by a purpose he could not yet name. And yet, in his heart, he sensed that her life mattered as much as his, even if she did not understand it.

Days passed. The air hummed with news of war—Austria challenged, armies moved, the world poised for battle at Sadowa. But in Genoa, life went on. The sun grew hotter, the dust thicker, the oleanders drooped like weary travelers. Evenings came with music—mule bells, stringed instruments, laughter drifting through the lanes. The hills above, crowned with forts and gardens, seemed to wake from sleep, gazing down with full beauty as moonlight softened the stone and shadows danced across cathedral steps.

Deronda watched the days turn, as if time itself were a clock of ancient figures marching in warning. He listened for a knock that would bring the moment he had dreaded and desired. He read books, but they blurred. He walked, but the streets held no peace. Nights were spent staring into the dark sea and sky, wrestling with doubt—was his life merely a dream? Or was it possible that he could serve something greater, even if it meant sacrifice?

Sometimes, he felt despair—his future reduced to a whisper, a fleeting change in the pattern of existence. Other times, a surge of hope lifted him—he might find a love that matched his duty, a life where personal devotion and moral purpose flowed together. And sometimes, rebellion flared—why must things be this way? Why hide, why delay? Why must he suffer for truths others buried?

But at last, the moment came.

On the morning of his third week, a new knock echoed through the hall. A servant in Chasseurs livery entered, bowing slightly, and delivered a message in French:

“The Princess Halm-Eberstein has arrived. She will rest during the day. She would be grateful if Monsieur would dine early, so as to be free at seven. At that hour, she will receive him.”

She held the spindle as she sat, Errina with the thick-coiled mat
Of raven hair and deepest agate eyes,
Gazing with a sad surprise
At surging visions of her destiny—
To spin the byssus drearily
In insect-labor, while the throng
Of gods and men wrought deeds that poets wrought in song.

When Deronda arrived at his mother’s apartment in the *Italia*, a familiar warmth stirred within him—the echo of childhood, tinged with early longing. The two servants in the antechamber glanced at him with quiet curiosity, surprised that the doctor their lady had summoned was this striking young man, whose presence lent even the strict lines of evening dress an air of grace. But Deronda barely noticed them. Only when the second door opened did he find himself face to face with the woman who had shaped his dreams from afar.

She stood at the far end of the spacious room, draped in black lace that fell loosely from her silvery hair to the long train trailing behind her. Her arms, bare to the elbow and adorned only with rich bracelets, were folded across her chest. The noble tilt of her head gave her features a dignity beyond mere beauty. Yet before Deronda could fully take her in, he was already before her, holding the hand she extended, then lifting it gently to his lips.

She kept her hand in his, studying him with a gaze both piercing and unreadable. He felt no time to think—only the awareness that her eyes shifted like living shadows, her expression changing with each breath, as if she were not just a woman but a force shaping the moment. He stood still, uncertain how to respond, his cheeks warming despite himself. He was shaken—not by joy, nor sorrow, but by something deeper: the realization that every imagined reunion had been more vivid than this one. He couldn’t even guess what language she would speak.

Then, suddenly, she released his hand and placed both palms on his shoulders. A flash of admiration lit her face—every line softened, every trace of age erased for a fleeting instant, as though youth had returned in defiance of time.

“You are a beautiful creature,” she said, her voice low and melodic, with a rhythm that hinted at foreign lands yet carried a charm all its own. “I knew you would be.” Then she kissed him once on each cheek, and he returned the gesture. It was not love, but recognition—like the meeting of two sovereigns.

She paused, letting the lines return to her face, and then spoke again, colder now. “I am your mother. But you can have no love for me.”

“I have thought of you more than any other being,” Deronda replied, his voice trembling slightly. “More than anyone.”

“I am not what you imagined,” she said firmly, withdrawing her hands and folding her arms once more, her eyes fixed on him as if inviting him to judge her. He had always pictured her face as resembling his own—he saw echoes now, yes, but they were drowned in differences too profound to ignore. She was extraordinary. What made him feel such distance? Her beauty was worn, yes, but also alien—as though she belonged not to this world, but to some half-remembered myth, a Melusina caught between realms.

“I used to think you might be suffering,” Deronda said, careful not to wound her. “I wished I could bring you comfort.”

“I _am_ suffering,” she answered, her voice harder now. “But not the kind you can ease.” She moved toward a sofa where cushions had been arranged with care. “Sit down.” She gestured to a chair beside her, then softened, noticing the distress in his face. “I am not suffering now. I am at peace. I can talk.”

Deronda obeyed, sitting quietly, waiting. He felt as though he stood before fate itself, not the mother he had dreamed of. A strange distance had opened between them—one born not of absence, but of revelation.

“No,” she began. “I did not call you here to be comforted. I didn’t know how you’d feel about me. I don’t know now. I don’t expect you to love me simply because I am your mother—when you’ve never known me. But I believed I chose better for you than keeping you with me. I didn’t believe I took anything worth having from you.”

“You cannot mean to tell me your affection wouldn’t have been worth having,” Deronda said, sensing she expected a reply.

“I’m not speaking ill of myself,” she said sharply, pride flaring. “But I had little affection to give. I didn’t want it. I was smothered by it. I wanted to live the life inside me, unburdened by others’ lives. You wonder who I was. I wasn’t a princess then.”

She rose abruptly, standing tall as before. Deronda rose with her, breathless.

“No princess in this quiet life I lead now. I was a great singer. I acted as well as I sang. All others paled beside me. Men followed me across borders. I lived a thousand lives in one. I did not want a child.”

There was fire in her voice—defensive, raw. Precedent meant nothing to her. She had cast it aside. There was no excuse, only truth, spoken with desperate intensity. She seemed to hurl those words into the air, as if warding off some unseen judgment. And Deronda stood there, clutching his coat collar, feeling as though he watched a sacred ritual—something holy and terrible, where crime wore a veil of reverence.

What else would she reveal?

She continued, her face pale yet luminous with inner light.

“I did not want to marry. I was forced into marrying your father—forced by my father’s demands. And even then, it was my way to gain freedom. I could rule my husband. Not my father. I had a right to be free. I had a right to break free from a bondage I despised.”

She sat again, her eyes narrowing slightly, lips pressed tight—as though she held back more words. Deronda remained standing, watching.

“And the bondage I hated for myself, I wanted to spare you,” she said, softer now, almost pleading. “What more could any mother do? I freed you from the burden of being born a Jew.”

“Then I *am* a Jew?” Deronda burst out, voice deep and urgent, making her recoil slightly against her cushions. “My father was a Jew… and you are a Jewess?”

“Yes,” she said, watching him closely, her expression shifting. “Your father was my cousin.”

“I am glad,” Deronda said, impulsively, his voice low but fierce. He hadn’t expected to say it. He hadn’t allowed himself to admit it before. Yet here it was—spilled forth in opposition to her, born of anger and confusion. He was shaken—by rage, yes, but also by grief. This mother had borne him unwillingly, abandoned him willingly, and now revealed herself reluctantly. Was that why she spoke so harshly?

The mother, too, trembled—but differently. Her body shook visibly, her eyes wide with pallid excitement.

“Why are you glad?” she demanded, voice rising. “You are an English gentleman. I ensured that.”

“You didn’t know what you ensured,” Deronda replied, throwing himself sideways into his chair, leaning his arm over the back. “How could you choose my birthright for me?”

He was aflame with a sense of injustice—foreign, yet undeniable. But he fought to silence himself. A horror crept in: that he might say something irreversible in this moment of transformation.

Silence stretched between them.

Then she spoke again, her voice firmer now, layered with resistance:

“I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself. How could I know you’d carry your father’s spirit? How could I know you’d love what I despised—if you truly love being a Jew?” The last words cut through the air with bitterness. Any listener might have thought hatred had taken root between them.

But Deronda had regained his composure. He recalled the life she had lived—the years lost, the pain etched in her frame. He remembered his own tendency to shamefully accept things as final, as if they were solely his burden. Now, looking at her, he found a new clarity. His face softened, regaining its calm depth. Yet it unsettled her—her eyes locked onto his, fascinated, yet untouched by maternal joy.

“Forgive me if I speak hastily,” he said, voice grave and humble. “Why have you chosen now to reveal this—to me, after keeping it hidden? Why—since you seem angry that I am glad?”

“Oh, the reasons for our actions!” she said, a bitter edge in her tone. “When you’re as old as I am, you’ll see it’s not so simple to ask, ‘Why did you do this?’ People speak of motives as if they were neatly tied. Every woman is supposed to feel the same—otherwise she’s a monster. I am not a monster. But I did not feel what others claim to feel—or pretend to, so they won’t be seen as different. When you blame me for sending you away, you mean I should have said I loved you as mothers say they love their children. I did not. I was relieved to be rid of you. But I did what was best for you. I gave you your father’s fortune. Do I now seem to be undoing everything? No—there are reasons. I feel things I cannot explain. A fatal illness has grown in me for a year. I may not live another. I will not deny what I’ve done. I will not feign love where none exists. But shadows gather. Sickness brings them. If I wronged the dead—I have little time left to make amends.”

Her speech flowed with a mastery of tone and emotion—like a performance by the finest actress. Yet it was real. She was not merely acting; she *was* her emotions. For her, feeling became drama instantly. Pain, joy, regret—they all transformed into spectacle. In lesser people, this might be common; in her, it was artistry perfected. She felt deeply, yes—but through a lens of self-awareness, where every ache bore a halo of spiritual intoxication, both elevating and numbing.

Deronda did not reflect on this. He listened only to the meaning—her confession, her suffering, the weight of her past. He longed to understand the strange conflict that had brought him into the world. Compassion swelled within him, drawn to her pain, her honesty. He dared not question further when she fell silent, brow furrowed, head turned slightly away, eyes fixed on something invisible.

She broke the silence abruptly, turning to him with sudden intensity.

“Sir Hugo has written much about you. He says you have a remarkable mind—you comprehend everything—you are wiser than he, at sixty. You say you’re glad to be born a Jew. I won’t pretend I’ve changed my mind about that. Your feelings oppose mine. You don’t thank me. Will you understand your mother—or only condemn her?”

“There is not a fiber in me that does not wish to understand her,” Deronda replied, meeting her gaze steadily. “It is a cruel reversal of my desire to blame. For fifteen years, I’ve tried to understand those who differ from me.”

“Then you are unlike your grandfather in that,” she said. “Though you resemble him in face. He never understood me. Or if he did, he only sought to bind me to obedience. I was to be the ‘Jewish woman’ under threat of his curse. I was to feel what I didn’t feel, believe what I didn’t believe. I was to revere the parchment in the *mezuza*; dread butter touching meat; admire men binding *tephillin* while women were forbidden. I was to adore laws that seemed absurd to me. To love the prayers in the ugly synagogue, the chanting, the fasting, the endless feasts, my father’s endless lectures on our people—words like thunder without meaning. I was to care forever about Israel’s past. I cared for nothing of it. I cared for the wide world, for all I could become in it. I hated living under my father’s rigid shadow. Teaching, teaching—‘this you must be,’ ‘that you must not’—it pressed on me like a cage tightening with every year. I wanted to live freely, to move with the current, not be bound to care. Ah!”—her voice sharpened—“you’re glad to be born a Jew. You say so. That’s because you weren’t raised as one. That separation seems sweet to you because I saved you from it.”

“When you decided that, you meant I should never know my origins?” Deronda asked, unable to contain himself. “You’ve changed in your feelings about that.”

“Yes—that was my intent. That is what I upheld. It’s not true I’ve changed. Things have changed around me. I remain Leonora”—she touched her chest—“here, the same desire, the same will, the same choice. But”—she spread her hands, palms up, lips compressed—“events come upon us like curses. Thoughts, feelings, visions in the dark—these are events, are they not? I do not consent. We only consent to what we love. I obey something tyrannical”—she gestured again—“I am forced to wither, to suffer, to die slowly. Do I love that? Well, I’ve been forced to obey my dead father. I’ve been forced to tell you you are a Jew, to deliver what he commanded.”

“I beg you—tell me what drove you, when you were young, to take this path,” Deronda said, seeking refuge in the past, away from the agony of her present suffering. “I understand your struggle. Though my experience is different, I feel your pain. I can imagine the cruelty of enforced renunciation.”

“No,” she said, shaking her head, arms folded decisively. “You are not a woman. You may try—but you cannot imagine what it is to possess a man’s genius, yet endure the slavery of being a girl. To have a mold imposed—‘this is the Jewish woman; this is what you must be.’ A woman’s heart must be small—no larger, or it must be crushed like Chinese feet. Her happiness is made like cakes, by fixed recipe. That was what my father wanted. He wished I had been a son. He saw me as a substitute. His heart was set on Judaism. He hated the idea that Jewish women would be seen as fit for public singing, for acting. As if we weren’t more enviable for that! That’s a chance to escape bondage.”

“Was my grandfather learned?” Deronda asked, eager for details.

She waved her hand impatiently. “Oh, yes—clever, a physician, good. I don’t deny he was admirable. A man worthy of a play—grand, with iron will. Like Foscari before he pardons. But such men turn wives and daughters into slaves. They’d rule the world if they could; failing that, they crush women’s souls beneath their will. But nature sometimes resists. My father had no other child but me—and I was like him.”

She folded her arms again, facing whatever came next.

“Your father was different. Gentle, loving. I knew I could rule him. Before marriage, I made him promise secretly that he would never hinder my art. My father lay dying when we wed. From the start, he intended me to marry my cousin Ephraim. And when a woman’s will matches a man’s, half her strength lies in concealment. I meant to have my way—but only by pretending obedience. I feared my father—always. I hated that fear. I wished I could defy him openly—but I never could. I couldn’t imagine it. I couldn’t act it out in my mind: defying him and succeeding. And I would never risk failure.”

She paused, as if the words unlocked a flood of memories.

Deronda listened, emotions swirling—repulsion at her coldness, indignation at her choices, but now also a growing awe. The initial clash of expectations had faded. He saw not just a mother, but a woman of immense power, whose mistakes walked on high paths. If she had not been his mother, he might have admired her as a stranger. Still, he could not be neutral. He feared what she might say next. He dreaded the coercion she seemed to endure in revealing herself. He almost wished to say, “Only tell me what’s necessary.” Yet he was captivated—drawn to her words, her face, her soul.

He tried to pull her back to facts.

“Where was my grandfather’s home?”

“In Genoa—where I married. His family lived here for generations. But my father traveled widely.”

“You must have lived in England?”

“My mother was English—a Jewess of Portuguese descent. My father married her in England. Certain circumstances of that marriage changed everything. Through it, my father thwarted his own plans. My mother’s sister was a singer—later she married the English partner of a merchant house here. They lived here eleven years. My mother died when I was eight. My father let me stay with Aunt Leonora, taught under her eye—as if he didn’t mind the danger of her encouraging my dream to sing, as she had. But this was it—I saw it again and again: he didn’t guard against consequences, because he believed he could stop them whenever he liked. Before my aunt left Genoa, I had enough training to awaken the singer and actress within me. My father didn’t know everything—but he knew I was learning music, that I loved it. That meant nothing to him. He expected obedience. And he insisted I marry my cousin Ephraim—the only surviving member of his family he knew. I didn longer to marry. I planned ways to resist. But finally, I realized I could control him. So I agreed. My father died three weeks after our wedding—and then I had my way!”

She said it with a surge of triumph—but then her face hardened.

“It hasn’t lasted. My father is winning now.”

She looked at her son again, more contemplatively.

“You are like him—but milder. There’s something of your father in you. He devoted his life to me. He gave up banking, money-changing—lived only to serve me. He went against his conscience for me. As I loved my art, so he loved me. Let me see your hand again—the one with the ring.”

Deronda drew closer and offered his hand. We know the hand—strong, noble. Hers, smaller, was of the same type. As she touched it, as he saw her face—his own, aged not by time but by passion—he felt a surge of reverence.

“Mother,” he said, voice fervent, “take us all into your heart—the living and the dead. Forgive everything that wounds you. Accept my love.”

She looked at him—admiring, not loving. Then kissed him gently on the brow.

“I reject nothing,” she whispered, “but I have nothing to give.”

And with that, she released his hand and sank back into her cushions.

Deronda turned pale—the pain of rejected tenderness, a sensation deeper than emotion. She noticed, and said, softly, melodiously,

“You are strong. But I am weak. And weakness cannot answer strength.”

“La même fermeté qui sert à résister à l'amour sert aussi à le rendre violent et durable; et les personnes faibles qui sont toujours agitées des passions n’en sont presque jamais véritablement remplies.” —LA ROCHEFOUCAULD.

Among the letters Deronda received the next morning was one from Hans Meyrick, stretching across four quarto pages in the delicate, elegant script that ran through generations of the Meyrick family.

MY DEAR DERONDA,—In return for your thoughtful sketch of Italian politics and your wide-ranging view of global affairs, I can only offer a homegrown observation: here, the most sensible opinion on how current events will unfold is simply, “time will tell.” As for explaining past outcomes through present causes—well, it’s now widely acknowledged that the recent telegraph frauds were responsible for last year’s cattle plague. A fine refutation of so-called philosophy, and a clear justification for compensating the farmers. My own theory—that soon a murrain will strike the commercial class, with its roots revealed in the sudden sale of rejected paintings—has been dismissed as an unsound analogy. But let us not forget: there are minds willing to rob even the neglected artist of his final solace. To me, there is poetic beauty in imagining that some misguided judge might pay a fortune for my *Berenice* series, while the men of finance have already been punished by my undeserved luck.

Meanwhile, I console myself for your absence by stepping into the light you’ve left behind—shining like Hesperus after Hyperion has set. I’ve taken to sitting with our Hebrew prophet during the hours he once spent with you, studying the contours of his face, earning his respect as a learned young Gentile who, had circumstances allowed, would have gladly become a Jew. We agree on a broad principle: whatever is best must, by definition, be Jewish. I’ve never claimed to be a rigorous logician, but I can see that if everything best is A, and B happens to be best, then B must be A—even if we never expected it. On this basis, I once read a pamphlet arguing that all true art must be Protestant. I didn’t believe it, but I respected the reasoning. Still, our prophet makes a far more compelling model than Rembrandt ever did with his Rabbi—he’s alive with meaning, every visit revealing something new. One constant wonder: how, despite his fierce devotion to his people and their traditions, he remains anything but rigid. He doesn’t spit at the word *Christian*, nor does he revel in visions of the Gentile mouth drooling uselessly over roasted Leviathan while Israel feasts endlessly, *ad libitum*. (You’ll understand—I’ve learned what to expect from orthodox Jews.) I confess I once found your portrayal of Mordecai somewhat defensive, a gesture toward antiquity, as if you feared doing injustice to the megatherium by not honoring it fully. But now, having heard him speak in person, I find him a man of profound philosophical depth, wrapped in allegory and mysticism, yet sharp enough to silence any noisy debater with a single pointed question. The blend may be a Jewish prerogative, for all I know. His mind feels so vast that my own opinions fit comfortably within it—how they align with the rest is his problem, not mine. I leave the foundation to him. After all, every foundation is just an elephant supporting the world, more or less powerful and costly to maintain. I can’t afford a private elephant. So I retreat into mystery—it’s cheaper, longer-lasting, a sort of gas generated by the slow decay of such elephants. And if an idea pleases me, I treat it kindly, without suspicion. I’ve grown fond of Mordecai’s belief that a whole Christian is three-fourths a Jew, and that since Alexandrian times, the most expansive minds have been Jewish. I’d love to show Mirah this thought—apart from Arabic and other life details, there’s really little difference between me and Maimonides.

But lately, I’ve discovered that it’s the shallow lover who can’t help declaring himself. If Mirah weren’t so distracting, if being near her weren’t like standing in heaven, I’d have long ago thrown myself at her feet and begged her to tell me—plainly—whether she wanted me to end my life. I have a gift for hope, which is almost as valuable as an estate in reversion—if only I could resist turning it into certainty, which might ruin everything. My Hope wanders among blossoming orchards, feels warm snow fall through sunlight, and doubts nothing. But when it catches sight of Certainty in the distance, it sees a grim Janus-faced god, winking doubtfully, and quickly turns away. You, with your calm reasonableness and readiness to accept the worst—you don’t understand Hope. That immortal, delicious maiden, forever pursued, forever kind, whom fools call deceitful—yet it is not Hope who carries the cup of disappointment, but her deadly rival, Certainty, whom she escapes only by transformation. (You notice my new turn to allegory?) Seriously, though, I must insist that truth will prevail, prejudice will fade, diversity accompanied by merit will become irresistible, and no noble aspiration will go unfulfilled—all doctrines taught in schools, and they imply that the Jewess I adore will choose me. Any fool can quote generalities, but the wise recognize the particular truths they conceal.

I’m less certain that my presence compensates Mordecai for your absence, but Jacob Cohen occasionally steps in as a substitute. It’s worth seeing Mordecai’s expression when he has this remarkable young Israelite on his lap, pouring out Semitic wisdom with a look of solemn patience and deep reverence. Sometimes Jacob covers his ears with his palms, pretending to hear Hebrew through a muffling veil, imitating the sounds he hears. When Mordecai gently pulls down his fists and holds them fast, Jacob’s face becomes animated—like a child walking through a zoo, trying to mimic every animal, most convincingly the owl and the peccary. I imagine you’ve seen such scenes before. He treats me with easy familiarity, viewing me as a second-hand Christian, likely to depreciate in value. He speaks frankly about my shortcomings, as if considering future purchase. Yet when Mirah enters, he transforms instantly—his age, usually seeming like those ancient Israelite garments worn in the desert, perhaps near forty but appearing freshly made—becomes childlike. With Mirah, he reminds me of dogs raised by women, obedient only to them. Still, he loves Mordecai too, bringing sugar plums to share, stuffing his own mouth embarrassingly full, and watching carefully how Mordecai manages with less. Judging from this modern Jacob at six, I’m amazed his people haven’t bought us all up long ago, holding our weaker generations as stock and scrip—slave property in disguise. There is one Jewess I wouldn’t mind serving. But I fear I imagine Mirah growing quieter, trying hard to hide it. Naturally, given that she watches her brother slowly die, someone she worships with such tender devotion that I often wish I were in his place.

Otherwise, things are slightly livelier than usual. Rex Gascoigne—you remember the head I sketched, the fellow with the strong upper lip, studying law—has taken rooms nearby, and for the past fortnight, his neat sister (also blessed with a good upper lip) has been staying with him. I introduced them to my mother and sisters, who discovered from Miss Gascoigne that she’s cousin to your Vandyke duchess!!! I added the exclamation marks to mark the initial shock to my simple mind. Upon reflection, I realized there was no reason to be surprised—unless I’d assumed no one could be anyone’s cousin without my knowing. This kind of surprise, I think, stems from a lively spine and a brain that’s perpetually null. I once knew a man in Rome who exploded with amazement at the simplest facts. Tell him anything—say, you liked easy boots—and he’d always respond, “No! Are you?” with the same intense wonder. The very man Browne described:

“A wretch so empty that if e’er there be
In nature found the least vacuity
’Twill be in him.”

I’ve solved it—he had a lively spine.

Still, this cousinship came up by chance one day when Mirah was visiting and they discussed the Mallingers. _Apropos_, I’m becoming important enough to receive rival invitations. Gascoigne wants me to join him at his father’s rectory in August, to see the countryside. But I suspect self-interest will lead me to Topping Abbey—Sir Hugo has invited me, and proposed—God bless him for his boldness—that I paint his three daughters seated on a bank, in the Gainsborough style. He visited my studio recently and advised me to focus on portraiture. Of course, I know what he meant: “My dear fellow, your efforts at history and poetry are pitiable. Your brush is suited for portraits—truth mixed with ease in falsehood. Idealism won’t serve gods and goddesses, but flattery might fetch a high price. Fate, my friend, has made you the rear wheel—_rota posterior curras, et in axe secundo_—run behind, because you can’t help it.” What effort it takes our friends to deliver such honest advice! I’ve known a man who called specifically to tell me I’d irretrievably exposed my poor judgment in handling my subject, and that if I’d asked, he’d have lent me his own. Such was my ingratitude and my talent for composition that, even as he spoke, I mentally sketched a Last Judgment with his face on the left. But none of this matters compared to Sir Hugo, whose way of implying one’s talents aren’t supreme is so kind and comforting that I begin to feel it’s an advantage not to be at the very top. And his kindness tastes sweeter because it comes from his affection for you, old friend. His conversation is delightfully amusing. By the way, he mentioned your Vandyke duchess has gone yachting with her husband to the Mediterranean. I wonder—could one land from a yacht, or be taken aboard from the shore? Might you, by chance, get the opportunity to continue your theological debates with the fair Supralapsarian—wasn’t that her creed? Is Duke Alphonso also theological? Perhaps an Arian who resents the Trinity. (Stage direction: While D. reads, a deep scorn gathers on his face until, at the final word, he throws down the letter, grips his coat collar in a statue-like pose, and remains motionless, radiating awe-inspiring intensity throughout the following soliloquy: “O night, O blackness, etc., etc.”)

Forgive the brevity. You’re used to my bare facts, without comment. One fact I omitted—the Klesmers, on the eve of departure, behaved magnificently, shining as expected when genius and fortune align. Mirah is rich with their Oriental gifts.

What luck if you return and appear at the Abbey while I’m there! I’ll behave with perfect discretion and earn golden opinions. But I’ll sneak into town now and then, just to peek into Gad Eden. See how far I’ve advanced in Hebrew lore—up to Lord Bolingbroke, who knew no Hebrew but “understood that sort of learning and what is writ about it.” If Mirah commanded it, I’d descend below the tri-literal roots. Already, I don’t care whether the vowels are marked. But while her brother lives, I suspect she wouldn’t listen to a lover—even one whose “hair is like a flock of goats on Mount Gilead”—and I flatter myself that few heads could endure that comparison better than mine. So I remain with my hope among the orchard blossoms.—Your devoted,

Some months earlier, such a letter from Hans would have stirred Deronda’s thoughts with irritation—his romantic musings about Mirah would have felt intrusive, edged with a lack of empathy for his friend’s likely heartbreak. But much had changed since March. Mirah was no longer in such a precarious position with the Meyricks, and Deronda’s own life had transformed, culminating in the revelation of his origins. Though he refused to fixate on visions of the future, this new horizon inevitably reshaped his perception of both past and present. Now, Hans’s so-called “hope” no longer seemed a reckless fantasy provoking anger, but rather a persistent, whimsical dance of imagination—one he could almost pity if he believed it might cause real pain.

Yet part of the busy thoughts filling that long day—during which he waited anxiously for a message from his mother—revolved around a deeper assessment: Hans’s nature wasn’t one where love could take root deeply enough to transform disappointment into sorrow. Too restless, too easily excited by novelty, too quick to turn emotion into artifice, wearing grief like a costume. “He’s already playing at love,” Deronda thought. “He treats the whole thing as a comedy. He knows perfectly well there’s no chance for him. Just like him—never seeing any obstacle I might have to his declarations about Mirah. Poor old Hans! If we were caught in a hailstorm together, he’d scream like a Greek, and if I didn’t scream too, he’d never realize I was suffering as much. And yet he’s tender-hearted, affectionate in intent, and keen to imagine what others feel—but always shaping it to suit his own desires.”

With this touch of irony, Deronda dismissed the warmth stirred by Hans’s heartfelt excess. The mention of Gwendolen, casually noting her departure with her husband on a Mediterranean cruise, merely sparked a troubling echo of his own strange farewell. But one sentence stood out—Hans’s quiet suspicion that Mirah carried a hidden sadness. This struck Deronda sharply. Not because it aligned with his wishes, but because it contradicted them. Instead of doubting Hans’s observation, he began to search for its source. Was it something that had happened during his absence? Or simply a growing dread of what might come? Could it stem from her new circumstances? Or—had Mordecai, breaking his lifelong silence, shared his secret hopes about Deronda with her? Had her sensitive soul been wounded by the realization that her brother’s will, or his unwavering vision, had subtly shaped their friendship? Had she sensed that Deronda’s regard for her was tinged with pity, or condescension, rather than equal affection?

Deronda’s guess was not entirely wrong in identifying the core of Mirah’s nature—a quiet rebellion against the treatment she had endured all her life until meeting him. The gratitude that compelled her to insist on her debt to him carried half its intensity from the contrast with how others had treated her. Deronda’s emotional kinship allowed him to glimpse such depths. But he was mistaken in believing Mordecai had broken his silence. To no one but Deronda himself had Mordecai spoken of their bond, or of his trust in Deronda’s origin. These were matters too sacred for casual speech—not only because they required weighty reason, but because Mordecai had perceived Deronda’s discomfort at any mention of his birth. The same strict reserve that prevented Mordecai from answering questions about private matters in the Cohen family applied even more strongly here.

“Ezra, how is it?” Mirah once asked him softly. “I keep finding myself speaking to Mr. Deronda as if he were a Jew?”

He smiled gently. “Perhaps because he treats us as if we were brothers. But he dislikes dwelling on differences of birth.”

“He has never lived with his parents, Mr. Hans says,” Mirah continued, eager to learn about everyone she cared for.

“Do not seek to know such things from Mr. Hans,” Mordecai replied gravely, laying a hand on her curls as he often did. “What Daniel Deronda chooses to reveal about himself is for him alone to decide.”

Mirah felt rebuked—as Deronda had once done. But to be corrected by Mordecai in this way filled her with quiet pride.

“I see no one greater than my brother,” she told Mrs. Meyrick one day, visiting the Chelsea house on her way home and, as she hoped, finding the little mother alone. “It’s hard to believe he belongs to the same world as those people I used to live among. I once told you they made life seem like a madhouse. But when I’m with Ezra, he makes me feel his life is a great good, though he has suffered so much—unlike me, who once wished to die after a little pain, lasting only a short time. His soul is so full, he cannot desire death as I did. I feel the same way when I was tired yesterday, returning through the park after sweet rain and sunshine touched the grass and flowers. Everything looked so pure and beautiful that weariness and trouble seemed small parts of existence, and I grew more patient, more hopeful.”

A soft note of melancholy in her voice caused Mrs. Meyrick to study her closely. After removing her hat and smoothing her curls with an air of fatigue, Mirah sat opposite her friend, hands and feet crossed—distantly resembling a serene painted statue. But Mrs. Meyrick saw a new look of suppressed sorrow, matching the hint that patience and hope required extra strength.

“Is there fresh trouble on your mind, my dear?” she asked, setting aside her needlework to focus fully.

Mirah hesitated. “I’m too ready to speak of troubles. It seems unkind to burden others unless I’m sure it might prevent worse. And perhaps I’m too hasty, too fearful.”

“Oh, my dear,” Mrs. Meyrick said, “mothers are made to bear pain for their children. Is it because singing lessons are rare, and may dwindle as the season ends? Success doesn’t come all at once.”

She didn’t believe she’d hit the mark, but a guess could open the door.

“No,” Mirah said, shaking her head. “I’ve been disappointed—many ladies said they wanted me to teach them or their daughters, but then I never heard from them again. But perhaps after the holidays I can teach in schools. Besides, you know, I’m as rich as a princess. I haven’t touched the hundred pounds Mrs. Klesmer gave me. And I’d never fear for Ezra’s needs, because there’s Mr. Deronda. He said, ‘It is the chief honor of my life that your brother will share anything with me.’ Oh, no! Ezra and I need fear nothing about food or clothing.”

“But there’s another fear,” Mrs. Meyrick said, sensing more. “A fear that might disturb your peace. Don’t forecast evil unless you can guard against it. Anxiety is useless if it can’t become defense. But not all evils can be guarded against. Have you more reason to worry now than a month ago?”

“Yes,” Mirah whispered. “I’ve kept it from Ezra. I couldn’t bring myself to tell him. Please forgive me—I can’t help telling you. I _have_ more reason to fear. Five days ago… I’m certain I saw my father.”

Mrs. Meyrick shrank back, arms crossed tightly, leaning forward as if to stop herself from hurling insults at that man.

“The years have changed him,” Mirah went on. “He was already altered, worn down before I left. You remember I said he sometimes wept. He was always agitated. I’ve told Ezra everything I told you. He says my father took to gambling—making people distressed one moment, exalted the next. And now—only a moment—I saw him. His face was more hollow, his clothes shabby. He was with a much worse-looking man, carrying something, and they were rushing after an omnibus.”

“Well, child,” Mrs. Meyrick said, “he didn’t see you, I hope?”

CHAPTER LIII. “My desolation does begin to make A better life.” —SHAKESPEARE: _Antony and Cleopatra._

Before Daniel Deronda was summoned for a second meeting with his mother, a day had passed in silence—only a message sent, saying she was not yet well enough to receive him. But on the third morning, a note arrived: “I leave today. Come at once.”

He was led into the same room as before, though now it was draped in shadows—blinds drawn, curtains pulled tight, dimming the light like the veil of memory. The Princess was not there when he entered, but soon she appeared, wrapped in a loose silk robe the color of sunset fire, her head adorned with black lace that floated around her like smoke. Her arms emerged bare from beneath wide sleeves, and her face, illuminated by the low light, seemed more striking than ever—the eyes larger, the lines sharper, as if carved by time and defiance alike. One might have thought her a woman who had once ruled over youth and beauty, but now chose to stand apart from them, having tasted too much.

She stepped forward at once, placing her arms gently on his shoulders and kissing his cheeks—one after the other—then sat down among her cushions with an air of quiet authority, far removed from the restless energy of their first encounter. She gestured for him to sit beside her. He obeyed, murmuring, “You are feeling better now, I hope?”

“Yes,” she replied, calm and composed. “I am at ease again. Is there anything else you wish to ask me?”

“Can I find the house in Genoa where you lived with my grandfather?” Deronda asked.

“No,” she said, lifting her hand slightly in dismissal. “It’s gone—demolished. Not to be found. But everything about our family—where your father lived, what he did—you’ll find it all in the chest, written down. Better than I could tell you. My father was a physician. My mother, a Morteira. I heard those things often—but never truly listened. You’ll find the truth there. I was born into them without choice. I left them behind as soon as I could.”

Deronda felt a pang, but held back. “Then,” he said softly, “is there nothing else you would want to share—something that might bring peace to both of us?”

“I think I’ve told you all that needed to be said,” she answered, her voice cool, almost distant. It was as if she had already given up the struggle. In truth, she had decided: *I’ve confessed. I’ve done all I can. I will not reopen this wound.* And so she stood firm in her resolve.

But for Deronda, the moment was unbearable. His longing—for connection, for belonging—had brought him here, only to meet silence. The shrine of his mother’s identity had been stripped bare; no relics remained. And now, as he looked at her, he saw not just a woman, but someone who had chosen exile even from love itself.

“Then,” he whispered, his voice trembling, “are we to part forever? Will I never mean anything to you?”

“It is better this way,” she said, softer now, almost tender. “There could be no true bond between us—not even if you were truly mine. You would not love me. Don’t deny it,” she added sharply, raising her hand. “I know what lies within you. You disapprove of what I did. You’re angry. You believe I stole something from you. You side with your grandfather—and you’ll always carry judgment in your heart.”

Deronda felt himself silenced. He rose from his seat, choosing to stand rather than sit under her command, as if standing meant refusing to surrender to emotion. Yet she looked up at him then, her gaze softening with something new—admiration.

“You’re wrong to be angry,” she said. “You are stronger because of what I did.”

After a pause, she asked, “And now—what will you do?”

“Do you mean right now?” he asked. “Or about your future?”

“The future,” she said, more sharply. “What difference does it make that I’ve told you of your birth?”

“A very great difference,” he replied firmly. “Perhaps the greatest thing that could happen to me.”

“And what will you do?” she pressed.

“Become like my grandfather? Be what he wished? Turn myself into a Jew as he wanted?”

“That is impossible,” Deronda said, his voice growing stronger. “The education I’ve received cannot be undone. The Christian world shaped me. But I feel it is my duty—my deepest impulse—to connect with my people. If I can find a purpose for them, one I can give my soul and hands to—I will choose it.”

She stared at him, her eyes searching his face as if reading a language only she could understand. He met her gaze with steady courage, holding fast to who he was.

Then she leaned toward him, speaking with sudden certainty: “You are in love with a Jewess.”

Deronda flushed. “My reasons are not dependent on that.”

“I know better,” she said, decisive. “I’ve seen how men are. Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who refuses anyone but a Jew. There are a few such,” she added, with a flicker of disdain.

He hesitated. He didn’t wish to lie, nor to confirm what might hurt her further. So he stayed silent.

She waited, then said, “You love her as your father loved me. She draws you as I drew him.”

The words struck him deeply. A tenderness flickered in his eyes—and she mistook it for agreement.

Her voice rose, charged with passion: “But I was pulling him in another direction. And now your grandfather is avenging himself.”

“Mother,” Deronda said gently, “don’t see it that way. I admit your choices may have brought benefit. I prefer gratitude to resentment. I believe I should have known my heritage from the start. But you gave me something else—broadness, depth, understanding. And now—events have restored more than you could have imagined. You’ve saved my people from losing me, and me from losing my duty. Can’t you let your heart open to this?”

He paused, sensing her attention. She listened, her expression unreadable, yet slowly shook her head.

He tried again, more urgently: “You said you sought what you believed best for me. Now, open your heart to your grandfather—he sought what he believed best for you.”

“No,” she said, shaking her head firmly, folding her arms tightly. “Not for me. He never thought of me as a daughter—only as a tool. Because I had desires outside his plan, I was to be confined, twisted. If that is the law of the world, I refuse to accept it. If God demands I surrender what I kept—punish me for deceiving my father, for not warning him I would betray his trust—well, I’ve told everything. I’ve done all I could. And your soul agrees. That is enough. I was, after all, the instrument he wanted.”

As she spoke the last lines, she narrowed her eyes, nodded slowly, and lowered her voice, speaking as if quoting something sacred—yet forced:
*“I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped a Deliverer might spring from it.”*

“Were those your grandfather’s words?” Deronda asked.

“Yes,” she said, almost fiercely. “Yes. And you’ll find them written. I wanted to thwart him.” Then, quieter: “You would have me love what I’ve hated since I was this high”—she held her hand a foot above the floor—“That can never be. But what does it matter? His yoke was upon me—whether I liked it or not. You are the grandson he wanted. You speak like wise men—like you know the meaning of it all. What does it mean?”

Her tone was sharp, dismissive.

Deronda, caught between sorrow and reverence, clung to the memory of their bond, afraid his words might wound.

“Mother,” he said, voice deep and pleading, “don’t say I feel myself wise. We are caught in hard truths. I see no way to clarity except through honesty—through speaking what must be said. No wonder such truths reveal themselves, despite silence. Generations prepare consequences that outlive any single will. Your will was strong—but your father’s trust, which you accepted and failed to fulfill—what you call his yoke—is rooted deeper, bound into the sacred foundations of humanity. You renounced me—you still reject me—as a son.” His voice faltered. “But that greater force has determined I shall be exactly the grandson you wished to destroy.”

She watched him, still. Then admiration returned to her face.

After a long silence, she said, softly, “Sit down again.”

He obeyed, settling beside her. She placed her hand on his shoulder.

“You rebuke me,” she said. “Well—then I am the loser. And you are angry because I banish you. What could you do for me but wear yourself out? I am broken. My life is little more than memory—except when pain comes. You accuse me of letting go of you. But I had joy without you. Now you return—and I cannot give you joy. Do you carry the curse of the Jew in you? Can you not forgive me? Will you be glad knowing I am punished for not being a Jewish mother to you?”

“How can you ask that?” Deronda replied, distressed. “I’ve begged to be your son—even now. My sorrow is that you say I cannot comfort you. I’d give up much—everything dear—to ease your suffering.”

“You shall give nothing,” she said, agitated. “You shall be happy. Let me believe you are happy. I’ve done you no harm. You have no reason to curse me. Think of me as they think of the dead—pray for me. Long that I be freed from pain, from punishment. And I shall see you—instead of always seeing your grandfather. Will any harm come to me for breaking his trust after he was gone? I don’t know. But if you believe _Kaddish_ helps—say it. Say it. You will come between me and the dead. When I am in your thoughts, you will look at me—as you do now—like a tender son. As if I had been a tender mother.”

She fought to keep control, but her hand trembled on his shoulder.

A wave of compassion overwhelmed Deronda. With a face full of sorrow, he put his arm around her and gently pressed her head against his chest.

They sat like that for moments—silent, heavy with unspoken grief.

Then she lifted her head, sighed deeply, as if releasing a lifetime of burden.

Deronda sensed the farewell drawing near.

But suddenly, her mood shifted. “Is she beautiful?” she asked abruptly.

“Who?” he said, turning red.

“The woman you love.”

He couldn’t delay. “Yes.”

“Not ambitious?”

“No. I don’t think so.”

“Doesn’t demand her own path?”

“I think her nature is not made for claiming greatness.”

“She is not like that?” she said, pulling a miniature from her purse—a portrait of herself in youth, radiant with fire and life. She held it up to him.

Deronda looked at it with quiet sadness.

“Did I not have a right to be more than just a daughter, a mother?” she asked. “My voice, my spirit matched my face. Whatever else was wrong—acknowledge that I had a right to be an artist, even if my father forbade it. My nature gave me a charter.”

“I do acknowledge it,” Deronda said, looking from the portrait to her face—still powerful, still alive, even in weariness.

“Will you take it?” she asked gently. “If she is kind, teach her to remember me kindly.”

“I’ll be grateful,” he said, “but—I must confess, I have no assurance she loves me. I’ve said nothing.”

“Who is she?” she demanded.

It was not a question—it was a command.

“She was raised to be a singer,” Deronda said, reluctant. “Her father took her from her mother early. Her life has been unhappy. She’s young—only twenty. Her father wanted her to reject her Jewish roots, but she clings to her mother’s memory and her people’s fellowship.”

“Ah, like you,” the Princess said, cutting in. “She’s attached to Judaism she knows nothing of. Poetry—fit for an opera night. Does she enjoy her art? Is her singing good?”

“Exquisite,” he said. “But her voice isn’t suited for the stage. I think the artist’s life repels her.”

“Then she is made for you,” she said. “Sir Hugo said you despised being a singer. And I see—you would never have allowed yourself to be lost in a wife, as your father was.”

“I repeat,” Deronda said firmly, “I have no promise of her love. No certainty we can be together. Painful choices may lie ahead. I’ve always prepared myself to renounce such hopes. Perhaps I feel that way about happiness in general. Whether it comes or not, one must learn to live without it.”

“Do you feel that way?” she asked, placing her hands on his shoulders, studying his face, speaking slowly, pausing between words.

“Poor boy,” she murmured. “I wonder—what if I had kept you? Would you have turned to the old ways against me? Would we have quarreled? Would your grandfather have lived in you? Would you have weighed down my life with your growth from the old root?”

“I think my affection could have endured,” Deronda said, growing sadder. “And it wouldn’t have hindered you—surely it would have enriched your life.”

“Not then,” she said, voice thick with regret. “Not then. I didn’t want it. But now—perhaps I might have welcomed it—if I could welcome anything.”

“But you love your other children,” he asked, anxious. “And they love you?”

“Oh, yes,” she said, as if it were obvious. Then, lower: “But I am not a loving woman. That’s the truth. Love is a gift I lack. Others have loved me—and I’ve played their love. I know what love does—it makes one subject to another, absorbing the self into another’s.” She touched her chest. “I was never willing to be subject. Men were subject to me.”

“Perhaps the man who was subject was the happier,” Deronda said, not smiling, but with a quiet sorrow for her loneliness.

“Perhaps,” she said. “But I was happy—for a time. If I hadn’t feared failure, I might have continued. I miscalculated. And now—nothing remains. Another life! People talk of ‘another life’ as if it begins beyond death. But I’ve already entered it.”

With those words, she raised her arms until they were bare to the elbow, her brow furrowed, her eyes closed, her voice stifled. In her flame-colored dress, she seemed a vision—something from beyond the world of the living.

Deronda felt himself unraveling. A sob escaped him.

She opened her eyes, let her hands rest once more on his shoulders, and said, “Good-bye, my son. Good-bye. We shall hear no more of each other. Kiss me.”

He embraced her, kissed her, and then stepped back.

He did not remember how he left the room.

When he reached the hall, he felt older—no longer a boy, but a man who had walked through fire. All his childhood dreams of his mother were gone. He had faced a truth that would shape the rest of his life. And in its shadow, he understood—love, duty, and destiny were not gifts to be claimed, but burdens to be carried with grace.

“The unwilling brain
Feigns often what it would not; and we trust
Imagination with such phantasies
As the tongue dares not fashion into words;
Which have no words, their horror makes them dim
To the mind’s eye.” —SHELLEY.

Madonna Pia, abandoned by her husband to a desolate life in the marshlands of the Maremma, lingers in Dante’s *Purgatory* as a figure of sorrowful redemption—her sins confessed, her soul seeking mercy. We know little of the quarrel that led to her exile, but we can guess that her husband was neither gentle nor kind, and that the bleakness of his chosen prison only magnified his harshness. In punishing her, he found himself punished too: for in banishing her from society, he also severed her from the world’s compassion—and thus made his own cruelty a shared burden. Yet though her fate stirs pity, it is not so deep as the ache felt by Gwendolen, whose suffering unfolds not in the quiet afterlife, but in the cruel present—trapped in a marriage that has become a cage, her heart entangled in webs of regret, desire, and dread that grow tighter with every breath she takes.

Grandcourt had no intention of abandoning Gwendolen when he took her aboard the yacht. On the contrary, he meant to claim her fully—body, mind, and spirit—as his own. The sea journey was not an escape, but a declaration: here, on this floating island of silk and cedar, he would be sovereign, and she, his subject. He loved yachting—the slow drift, the absence of duty, the freedom from the tedious demands of society. It suited his nature perfectly. And unlike the bleak swamp of the Maremma, this voyage offered beauty and ease. But beneath its surface lay something else: a calculated removal. He suspected Gwendolen’s growing resistance, especially her lingering affection for Deronda. What others might call jealousy, Grandcourt called resolve—a firm decision to end any foolishness that threatened his authority. He saw no flaw in taking her away, far from friends, family, and distractions. After all, marriage was a contract. She had agreed to it, and he had delivered everything promised: rank, wealth, comfort. He had fulfilled his part. Why should she not fulfill hers?

And yet, Gwendolen knew better than to pretend innocence. She had married him not for love, but for security—for the life he could give her. She had accepted his terms, knowing full well they came at a cost. But she was not blind. She carried within her a sense of right and wrong, a conscience that stirred even in silence. Her purgatory had already begun—not in some distant heaven, but here, on earth, in the stillness of a cabin where every glance, every pause, spoke of power and surrender.

Now, imagine her: alone with her husband on a tiny vessel, drifting across the sapphire waters of the Mediterranean. The world behind them—London, Ryelands, the familiar faces of society—was gone. The yacht was elegant, sumptuously furnished, perfumed with cedar and silk, manned by a crew who seemed more like actors in a play than sailors. Mr. Lush had returned to England, leaving only the two of them in this fragile bubble of isolation. And Gwendolen? She liked the sea. It did not make her ill. She found pleasure in watching the sails, predicting the shifts of wind, imagining herself in command—though she knew she was never truly in charge. The weather was fair, the coast southward painted in gold and purple, the sky endless. There was beauty everywhere.

But beauty cannot fill a hollow heart.

What good are jewels, soft cushions, and perfect skies when the soul is poisoned by fear and shame? What peace can come from a life lived under watchful eyes, where every word is measured, every movement scrutinized? For Gwendolen, the luxury of the yacht was a gilded prison. The sea, instead of freeing her, only mirrored her inner turmoil. While she sat in silence, wrapped in velvet and dreams, another woman somewhere was counting eggs, hoping for footsteps that would bring joy. A couple huddled over a scrap of cloth, dreaming of holidays among wild heather. Their lives were small, perhaps, but real—filled with choices, with hope. Hers was suspended, adrift between memory and dread.

Did Grandcourt understand any of this? He knew she didn’t love him—but what did that matter? He held her in his grasp, and he had no need for affection. He believed himself desirable, admired, superior. He saw other men as grotesque—clumsy, smirking, vulgar. And he understood Gwendolen’s dislike of Lush, because he recognized the absurdity of such men. But he could not comprehend why she should loathe *him*. How could she? He was polished, powerful, refined. To him, repulsion was a physical thing—something you could see or smell. Moral repulsion? That was beyond his reach. He could not fathom how a woman might come to hate not just his actions, but his very presence—how beauty could become unbearable, how strength could turn into terror.

So he remained unaware.

Their interactions were polite, almost formal. No laughter, no arguments, no warmth. He offered her a shawl when needed, handed her a glass of wine. She accepted without complaint. They moved through days like figures in a silent film—each aware of the other, yet separated by an invisible wall. At dusk, he would point out sugar-cane plantations or distant sails. She would nod, feigning interest. Then he would sit, gaze at her with those narrow, unblinking eyes, as if she were part of the ship itself—something to be observed, not touched. And she, aware of being watched, would fix her gaze on the horizon, pretending not to notice.

At dinner, he would remark on stale fruit or suggest a change of wine. She would respond with proper grace. But to argue with him? Impossible. It was like speaking to a serpent coiled in her cabin—dangerous, inevitable, without escape. And what argument could she make on a yacht, miles from help, surrounded by men who served him?

For Grandcourt, this life was a triumph. To control her in such intimacy, to dominate her in a world stripped bare of distraction—that was power. It gave their existence a royal quality, a theatrical dignity. Every gesture, every moment, was performed under his rule. And the silence, kept strictly private, only heightened the illusion of absolute control.

But for Gwendolen, it was a descent. Even before marriage, she had known little of heroism or greatness. Now, her world was reduced to one man—and her relation to him. Those closest to us often become our interpreters of life. A dull husband, a cold wife—they may seem trivial, but in truth, they shape the soul’s vision. Like a warped mirror, they distort reality, turning beauty into ugliness, meaning into emptiness. Gwendolen saw the world through the lens of Grandcourt’s tyranny. His petty judgments, his bored indifference, his cold superiority—they turned her own life into a procession of grotesque idols.

She had no wish to become a mother. Not because she disliked children, but because to desire one would be to accept the completion of her betrayal. She feared pregnancy not as a miracle, but as a sentence. The thought of a child brought not joy, but dread—of another soul trapped in the same web of shame and powerlessness. Instead, she clung to faint hopes: accidents, escapes, sudden breaks in the pattern. To imagine something unexpected—something outside her control—was her only refuge from despair.

Hatred, like love, grows in secret. It feeds on silence, on fear, on the inability to speak. The deepest hatred is born of terror—when rage cannot erupt, it turns inward, becoming a kind of self-destruction. Gwendolen’s mind became a temple of hidden rites—rituals of vengeance, fantasies of escape, visions of destruction. She imagined herself committing terrible acts—murder, flight, madness—only to wake in horror, realizing the consequences. She saw death not as release, but as a new kind of imprisonment. A white face, motionless, staring back at her—her own reflection in the abyss.

And then there was Deronda.

His words echoed in her thoughts: “Turn your fear into a safeguard. Keep your dread fixed on the idea of increasing your remorse. Take your fear as a safeguard. It is like quickness of hearing. It may make consequences passionately present to you.”

Yes. That was it. Fear was not weakness—it was warning. It showed her the path she must avoid. And Deronda—though absent—was her guide. She could not hide from him. She could not lie to him. He represented truth, purity, a moral clarity she longed for. In him, she saw not only salvation, but judgment. Whatever relief she might find, it would be shaped by his perception. And so she clung to him—not as a lover, but as a witness.

Inarticulate prayers rose from her lips—no words, only sighs, whispered into the night. Sometimes, after a dream of violence, she would wake trembling, tears streaming down her cheeks, whispering, “I will not mind if I can keep from getting wicked.” It was a prayer, a vow, a cry for help.

Days passed. They sailed past the Balearic Isles, toward Sardinia, then slowly north again toward Corsica. The sea was calm, the air sweet. But for Gwendolen, it was no longer peaceful. It was a nightmare, dressed in sunlight.

“How long are we to be yachting?” she asked one day, after touching at Ajaccio. The mere act of going ashore had lifted her spirits briefly, as if breaking free from the rigging of her thoughts.

“What else should we do?” Grandcourt replied. “I’m not tired of it. Why shouldn’t we stay out as long as we like? There’s less to bore one here. And where would you go? I’m sick of foreign places. We’ll have enough of Ryelands. Would you rather be there?”

“Oh, no,” she said, voice flat. “I only wondered how long you’d like it.”

“I like yachting better than anything,” he said. “And I had none last year. I suppose you’re beginning to tire of it. Women are so confoundedly whimsical. They expect everything to give way to them.”

“Oh, dear, no!” she retorted, voice sharp as a flute. “I never expect you to give way.”

“Why should I?” he murmured, looking at her, then reaching for an orange.

She resolved to endure whatever came. But the next day, after a squall left her pale and ill, he came down to her cabin.

“There’s been hell’s own work overnight,” he said. “The skipper says we’ll have to stay in Genoa for a week while repairs are made.”

“Do you mind that?” she asked, lying amid white drapes, her face drawn.

“I should think so. Who wants to broil in Genoa?”

“It will be a change,” she said, her voice softer, caught off guard by fatigue.

“I don’t want any change,” he snapped. “Besides, the place is intolerable. One can’t move along the roads. I’ll go out in a boat, as I used to. I’ll manage it myself. I can get a few hours each day instead of struggling in a damnable hotel.”

A spark ignited in Gwendolen. Hours alone—without him. The thought filled her with wild, conflicting visions: running away, escaping, vanishing. She had always known it was madness—but now, in the grip of desperation, it seemed possible. Like a prisoner dreaming the wind might blow down the walls, she allowed herself to believe in miracles. Perhaps the sea would carry her. Perhaps the stars would guide her.

Hope revived her. She smiled, laughed, moved with a lightness she hadn’t felt in months. She watched the moon sink into the sea, no longer feeling the weight of loneliness. Could it be? Was rescue possible? Why not? The weather had favored her once. Maybe it would again.

She woke the next morning to the anchor dropping in Genoa’s harbor—woken from a dream in which she fled over Mont Cenis, warmer even in the snow, until she met Deronda, who told her to return.

And then, in the grand staircase of the *Italia*, she saw him.

Deronda stood before her, hat in hand, eyes wide with surprise. He hesitated—then bowed, passing by. The moment was fleeting, charged with unspoken things. Their last meeting had been tense, uncertain. Now, here he was—on the same soil, in the same city, at the same time.

Grandcourt saw it too. His face darkened. How had Deronda arrived? Was it coincidence? Or conspiracy? He scowled, searching for clues. Had Gwendolen sent word?
