Had she written from Marseille or Barcelona? The idea seemed absurd—yet impossible to dismiss. He refused to believe it was mere chance. It was a disgrace. And she—she was pleased. He could see it in her eyes, in the slight lift of her shoulders, the glow in her skin. She was expecting him.

He watched her take coffee, her hands—adorned with heavy jewels—resting gently at her neck. A small gesture, but one that spoke volumes. She was alive. She was hopeful. She was no longer just his wife—she was someone else’s.

And Grandcourt, ever perceptive in his own way, read it all—misreading it entirely. He saw not longing, but defiance. Not hope, but treachery. And in that moment, he felt the full weight of betrayal—not just from her, but from the universe itself.

CHAPTER LV. “Return to your science,
That which is more perfect feels the good the more,
And thus the sorrow.” —DANTE.

When Daniel Deronda met Gwendolen and Grandcourt on the hotel staircase, his thoughts were deeply entwined with emotion and uncertainty. He had just returned from a second meeting with his mother—a conversation that had left him shaken, not by words spoken, but by what they revealed: the Princess Halm-Eberstein had already departed Genoa. In theory, he could have set off immediately for Mainz—his mission clear: deliver Joseph Kalonymos’s letter and claim the family chest that held secrets of his lineage. Yet something within him refused to move.

Two hours after parting from his mother, he remained rooted in place, caught between past and future, memory and duty. The weight of what he had learned pressed upon him like a storm at sea—unseen, yet powerful. He relived every moment of their encounter, not as history, but as living sensation, each detail sharpened by the pain of recognition. He mourned for her life—not only because it was near to his own, but because it was so utterly apart. He saw the world anew, transformed by the knowledge of bonds long hidden, ties that shifted the balance of hope and fear within him. It felt as though, in darkness, he had wandered into a foreign camp, only to awaken at dawn and find the true tents of his kin standing far away, faintly visible across the plain.

He imagined his grandfather—the man whose name was now linked to his own—not merely as an ancestor, but as a kindred spirit, stirred awake within him. The old man had lived with passion and purpose; perhaps those same fires were now stirring in Daniel’s soul. And through all this inner turmoil, Mordecai and Mirah stood beside him, silent companions in thought, their presence a quiet strength, a reminder that he was not alone in seeking truth.

Deronda was made of such sensitive fiber—so finely tuned to feeling—that even beneath his calm exterior, his heart beat with deep sensitivity. His early years had taught him reserve, but they had also forged a soul capable of profound empathy. When the bell rang again, marking the hour for departure, he reached for *Bradshaw*, the railway guide, intending to plan his journey. But his hand fell still. He did not pack. He did not summon the porter.

His mind drifted instead—to Mainz, where treasures awaited; to London, where friends and responsibilities called. But another longing held him fast: the desire to stay one night longer, to attend the evening service at the synagogue, where the ancient prayers might echo the voice of his ancestors. And then there was Gwendolen—her image rose unbidden, haunting him with a mixture of regret and concern. He had parted from her abruptly, sharply, and now, in this quiet space of time, he found himself wondering if it would be cruel to leave without offering some sign of care, some gesture of enduring kindness—despite Grandcourt’s likely disapproval.

So he stayed. He ate dinner, but tasted nothing. He rose quickly, drawn toward the synagogue, and paused only to ask the porter if Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt were still at the hotel. The porter gave him their room number—but added, with a glance over his shoulder, that they had gone boating.

The news unsettled him. Not because he feared danger, but because it stirred a premonition—an unease that whispered of finality. As he stepped into the synagogue, the air thick with the scent of candle wax and old wood, he heard the Spanish-Hebrew liturgy rise in solemn waves. The worshippers, dark-faced and sparse in number, moved through prayer with a strange ease, as if sacred words were familiar as breath. Their reverence felt both alien and intimate—like a thread connecting generations across continents. Daniel thought of his grandfather, whom the Princess had described as a man of rare depth and conviction. Was he, too, an exception among Jews—as Mordecai was? Or were all great souls exceptions—men who dreamed beyond their times, whose visions shaped the world while others merely endured?

A melancholy impatience grew within him, mingling with his worry for Gwendolen. He left the synagogue earlier than intended, choosing instead to walk along the quay—a place he often visited, but now imbued with new meaning. Perhaps, he thought, he might catch sight of the Grandcourts returning. If so, he would greet them boldly, regardless of any coldness Grandcourt might show.

The sun had slipped behind clouds, casting a soft yellow glow over the restless sea. A breeze stirred the water, sending ripples across the surface like whispers. Deronda walked slowly, watching the shore. Then he saw it—a small sailing boat approaching rapidly, rowed by two men. A crowd gathered, voices rising in confusion. Some pointed with telescopes; others shouted. One insisted the boat carried a drowned man—the *milord*. Another swore it was the lady, lying prostrate. A Frenchman joked that perhaps the husband had taken his wife out to drown her, "according to national practice"—a remark met with laughter and swift dismissal by an English sailor, who declared plainly: “It’s a woman.”

Daniel’s heart pounded. Every stroke of the oars seemed to split his mind into visions—some plausible, others terrible. What had happened? Had Grandcourt fallen? Had she followed him into the water? Was this the end of everything?

Then the boat neared the shore. And there, beneath layers of tarpaulin and heavy coats, Daniel saw her—Gwendolen. She raised herself slightly on her hands, pale as death, her hair drenched and clinging to her face. Her eyes were wide, filled with shock, as if she had awakened in a world turned upside down, where judgment loomed and strangers closed in around her. The sailors helped her ashore, their rough hands gentle in their urgency. Her clothes clung to her limbs, soaked and heavy, making each step a struggle.

Then—she saw him. Her gaze locked onto his, and instantly, as if she had been waiting for him all along, she reached out her arms—only to be held back. In a voice barely above a whisper, broken and trembling, she said: “It is come, it is come! He is dead!”

“Hush,” Daniel said, stepping forward, his tone firm, calming. “Quiet yourself.”

To the men around her, he spoke clearly: “I am connected to this lady’s husband. Please bring her aboard the *Italia* at once—I will handle everything else.”

He waited until the last sailor confirmed the truth: Grandcourt had gone down—gone forever. His boat floated empty, abandoned. The two men had heard a cry, rushed to the scene, and seen Gwendolen leap into the water after him. They had pulled her out just in time.

With that, Daniel hurried back to the hotel. He ensured medical help would arrive swiftly. Then he sent telegrams—first to Sir Hugo, urging him to come without delay, then to Mr. Gascoigne at the rectory, knowing he was the closest link to Gwendolen’s mother.

As he wrote, certain words from her past echoed in his mind—words spoken in moments of vulnerability, when she had confessed her fears, her loneliness. She had once said her mother might help—if only she could reach her.

Now, Daniel understood. The moment had come. And he would not let her face it alone.

“The pang, the curse with which they died,
Had never passed away: I could not draw my eyes from theirs,
Nor lift them up to pray.” —COLERIDGE.

That night, Deronda did not undress. Gwendolen, after insisting on seeing him one last time before she would allow herself to be left alone, had been quiet—too quiet. Her voice, when she spoke, was barely a whisper, trembling with an urgency she could not hide. She asked only that he promise to come to her when she sent for him in the morning. Yet even as he stood there, watching her, he felt a deep unease. The possibility of a sudden change, the fear of fever or delirium, and the lingering dread that something in the catastrophe had stirred a hidden turmoil within her—all weighed heavily upon his mind. He told her attendant he would remain close by, ready at any moment if her condition worsened. He made it clear to all present that he was in touch with her family in England and that he bore responsibility for her care—a role he could assume easily enough, since he was known to Grandcourt’s valet, the only old servant who had accompanied them on the voyage.

But when exhaustion finally overtook him, Deronda slept—not peacefully, but tangled in dreams that mirrored the chaos of the day. They came like a storm of fragmented images: the sea, the boat, the cry, the silence. And then, waking suddenly, he saw it—the face of Grandcourt, dead and staring, imprinted in his mind by the weight of her unspoken guilt.

Morning came without summons. A small comfort. As he dressed, he allowed himself a moment of hope. It was still early—no message yet. Later, he learned that she had not slept well, but had remained calm, no violent signs of distress. She had finally drifted into rest. He marveled at the strength within her—how she could endure such terror and still keep her composure. He sensed, too, that beneath the physical shock, she was holding something back, concealing a deeper wound. For himself, he felt strangely detached. His own recent encounter with his mother had worn down his emotions; he seemed now to act out of duty rather than passion. His heart, once so open to suffering, felt numb. He was living through another kind of sorrow, and her pain, though sharp, felt like a memory he had not yet fully returned to.

Still, he went about his duties. He secured a formal statement from the fishermen who had rescued Gwendolen. Details were scarce. The boat Grandcourt had taken was found adrift, sail loose. The men believed he had been thrown overboard by the flapping canvas while turning the vessel, and that he could not swim. But their attention had first been drawn by a man’s cry—then, swiftly, by a woman’s scream. They saw her leap into the water.

Back at the hotel, he was told Gwendolen had risen and wished to see him. He was led into a dim room, curtains drawn tight, where she sat wrapped in a white shawl, her long hair carefully coiled, her earrings still in place—those blue stars glinting faintly in the shadow. When he entered, she rose abruptly, her face pale as snow, except for the purple shadows beneath her eyes, her lips parted in a look of helpless accusation. She seemed like a ghost of the proud, defiant Gwendolen Harleth he had once known—the woman who walked away from her losses at the gaming table with cold dignity. Now, she was broken, and the sight pierced him with a grief he could not name.

“Please,” he said softly, stepping forward, “rest. Do not stand.”

She obeyed, sinking back into the chair.

“Will you sit near me?” she whispered. “I must speak very quietly.”

He drew a small chair beside her. The gesture seemed to stir her deeply. Turning her pale face toward his, she leaned in, her voice barely audible:

“You know I am a guilty woman?”

Deronda turned paler. “I know nothing,” he replied, unable to say more.

“He is dead,” she murmured, with a quiet firmness.

“Yes,” he answered, voice thick with sorrow, reluctant to speak further.

“His face will never rise above the water again,” she continued, hands clenched tightly. “Not for anyone else—only for me. A dead face. I shall never escape it.”

Her words came from a place of desperate repression. She stared past him, into the distance, as if reliving every second. Was she delirious? Was she trying to conceal even from herself what she had done? Deronda’s mind flickered with these questions—half-hope, half-dread. But he could not speak. She was confessing. And he feared what she might reveal. He wanted to turn away, to let her bear her burden alone. But he was not a priest. He could not simply accept this soul laid bare upon him. Still, he could not deny her.

“You will not say I should tell the world?” she pressed, looking up at him. “You will not say I should be disgraced? I cannot. I could not bear it. Not my mother. Never. I must tell you—but you will not make me tell anyone else.”

“I can say nothing,” Deronda replied, voice low and heavy, “except that I wish to help you.”

“I told you from the start,” she whispered. “I told you I was afraid of myself.” There was a pleading ache in her tone, almost childlike. “I felt a hatred inside me—like a spirit of evil—always plotting. Everything I could do to free myself came to mind. And it grew worse. That’s why I asked you to come to me in town. I thought I would tell you everything. I tried. But I couldn’t. Then—he came in.”

She paused. A shudder ran through her.

“I will tell you everything now. Do you think a woman who wept, who prayed, who fought to save herself from herself—could be a murderer?”

“God!” Deronda breathed, voice shaking. “Don’t torture me. You didn’t kill him. You jumped into the water to save him. That’s what happened. This was an accident. One you could not prevent.”

“Don’t be impatient,” she said, trembling. The plea in her voice forced him to meet her eyes. “You used to say—you cared more for those who had done wrong and suffered. You said they might be redeemed, scourged into better things. If you hadn’t said that—if I hadn’t remembered your words—everything would have been worse. I *did* remember. It came to me at the end—that’s why I—But now, if you cannot bear to hear me—if you turn away from me—what shall I do? Am I worse than when you first found me? All the wrong I’ve done was already in me—more than I knew. If you had not come, if you had not been patient—would I be worse? And now—will you forsake me?”

Her hands, clenched moments before, now lay limp and trembling on the armrest. Her lips parted, breathless. Deronda could not answer. He looked away. Then, slowly, he took one of her hands—soft, cold—and clasped it as if they were two children walking together. It was the only way he could say, “I will not leave you.” And all the while, he felt as if he were signing a blank contract, one that might be filled with horror.

This touch was new to Gwendolen. No man had ever offered her tenderness she truly needed. And in that moment, she read his silence as a vow of endless patience, of unwavering constancy. Strength surged through her. She could go on.

“All sorts of thoughts,” she began, voice drifting, “contrivances—so hard. I fought against them. I was terrified. I saw his face—dead.” Her voice dropped to a whisper, almost touching his ear. “Long ago, I saw it. I wished him dead. But it frightened me. I was two people. I wanted to kill—like thirst. And then—immediately—I felt I had done something terrible, irreversible. Something that would make me a monster. And it came. It came.”

She fell silent, lost in memory.

“It was all in my mind when I first spoke to you—at the Abbey. I had done something then. I couldn’t tell you. It was the only thing I did toward carrying out my thoughts. They haunted everything—but only one became real. I did one thing. And I never undone it. It remains. Back when we were at Ryelands. In my boudoir—among the toys—something small and sharp, like a willow leaf in silver. I locked it in my dressing-case drawer. I kept thinking of it. I imagined putting it under my pillow. But I never did. I never opened the drawer again. I dared not. It had its own key. And not long ago, on the yacht—I dropped the key into the deep water. I meant to free myself. After that, I thought how to open it without the key. When we came to Genoa, I planned to have it opened secretly at the hotel. But then—on the stairs—I met you. I thought I’d talk to you—tell you everything I couldn’t in town. And then I was forced into the boat.”

A sob escaped her. She sank back, overwhelmed by the memory of that moment.

“And it has all stayed in my imagination,” Deronda said gently, not looking at her. “Only in your mind. To the very end, you resisted the temptation?”

Silence. Tears traced paths down her cheeks. She pressed her handkerchief to her face, straightening her posture. She was gathering herself again. Then, leaning toward him, she whispered:

“No. I will tell you the truth—exactly as God knows it. I will not lie. What else could I do? I used to think I could never be wicked. Wickedness seemed far away. But I have been. I have felt it. And everything has punished me. The things I once desired—now they burn. Even daylight feels like punishment. Because—you know—I should not have married. That was the beginning. I wronged someone. I broke my word. I sought pleasure for myself—and it turned to misery. I wanted to profit from another’s loss—you remember? Like roulette. And the money burned into me. I couldn’t complain. It was as if I had prayed for others to lose and me to win. And I won. I knew it. I knew I was guilty. On the sea, lying awake in the cabin, I sometimes felt everything I had done was revealed—no excuses—nothing hidden. How could anything be known only to me? It wasn’t mine—it was God’s knowledge entering me. Even the silence punished me. Everything but you. I always thought you wouldn’t want me punished. You would try to help me become better. Only thinking of that helped me. Will you change now? Will you want to punish me?”

Another sob.

“God forbid,” Deronda groaned. But he sat still.

The confession unfolded in fragments, each memory a wound reopened. She skipped over some details, blurred others, confused the line between thought and deed. But her voice, though low, carried the weight of truth.

“That’s why it was so hard when I was forced into the boat. Because when I saw you, it was joy—unexpected. I thought I could tell you everything—the locked drawer, what I hadn’t said before. If I had told you, if it were in your mind, it would have less power over me. I hoped in that. But after all my struggles, my tears, my rage—the hatred, the longing, the thirst—came back. And that disappointment—being shut out, driven into the boat—brought it all back. As if I were locked in prison with it, no escape. Oh, it seems so long ago now—when I stepped into that boat! I would have given everything to have lightning strike him dead.”

There was a fierce edge in her voice, born of memory.

“If he were here now—what would I do? I cannot wish him here. Yet I cannot bear his dead face. I was a coward. I should have borne scorn. I should have fled—wandered like a beggar—rather than stay and feel like a fiend. But wherever I turned, there was something I couldn’t bear. Sometimes I thought he would kill me if I resisted. But now—his dead face is there. And I cannot bear it.”

Suddenly, she pulled her hand from his, stood up, arms raised high, and moaned:

“I have been cruel! What can I do but cry for help? I am sinking. Die—die—you are forsaken. Go down, go down into darkness. Forsaken—no pity—*I* shall be forsaken.”

She collapsed into the chair, sobbing uncontrollably. Even Deronda was gone from her awareness. He was shattered. He had expected his recent trials to dull his feelings—but instead, the agony of this young woman’s fall from innocence into remorse struck him with unbearable force. Her swift journey from bright girlhood to this torment, witnessed by him in helpless silence, pierced him deeper because it followed so closely on the heels of his own spiritual revelation. He found himself standing in one of those rare moments when pity becomes so intense it makes one choose to renounce joy, to live only for the suffering.

He had risen from his seat during her outburst—her confession spoken in whispers, even in her madness, as if to a secret God. At last, he turned his back and walked away.

Then, silence. She realized he was gone. When he turned back, he saw her face tilted toward him—eyes wide, lips parted. She was a picture of timid, desperate appeal—too afraid to speak, too afraid to ask. Was he leaving her? Was she abandoned?

But his eyes met hers—full, sorrowful, unflinching. And in that gaze, she read: *I know. But I will not abandon you.*

He sat beside her again—without speaking, without touching her.

Gwendolen felt it again—the same compunction that had moved her at the Abbey. A love deeper than guilt. She said, softly, with regret:

“I make you very unhappy.”

Deronda gave a faint “Oh,” shrinking slightly. Then, gathering himself, he said clearly: “It does not matter whether I am happy or not. What matters is what will help you most. Tell me everything you need to say.”

His devotion was noble—but it created distance. She felt him beyond reach, compassion wrapped in a halo of superiority. She longed to kneel—to humble herself completely. But her mind held back. She remained silent, motionless, caught between conflicting desires.

At last, he said, “Perhaps you are too tired. Shall I go? I will return whenever you wish.”

“No,” she whispered, the fear of his departure restoring her voice. “I must tell you—what happened in the boat. I was full of rage—forced to go. I could do nothing but sit like a slave. Then we left the harbor—into the deep. Everything was still. We didn’t look at each other. He gave orders. And the light—it seemed to hold me prisoner. I remembered as a child imagining sailing away to a world where no one was forced to live with whom they didn’t like. I didn’t want my father-in-law to come home. And now—just the opposite. I had stepped into a boat. My life was sailing—gliding—away—alone with *him*, from salvation. And because I felt helpless, my thoughts turned darker. I longed for worse things. I had cruel wishes. I imagined impossible ways—yet I didn’t want to die. I feared drowning us both. If it had helped, I would have prayed—for something to happen to him. That he would sink and leave me alone. I knew no way to kill him there—but I *did* kill him in my thoughts.”

She paused, submerged.

“But still, I felt I was growing more wicked. And what you once said came back—about fearing to increase your sin and remorse—then you would hope for nothing. It was like fire written inside me. Becoming wicked was misery—being cut off forever from knowing what you—what better lives were. That had always returned to me—but now with despair. Evil wishes were too strong. I remember letting go the tiller and saying, ‘God help me!’ But then I had to take it again. And the evil prayers came back—blotting everything—until, in the midst of them—I don’t know how—he was turning the sail. A gust. He was struck. I know nothing. I only know—I saw my wish outside me.”

Her voice quickened, dropping lower.

“I saw him sink. My heart leapt—as if it were leaving my body. I think I didn’t move. I kept my hands tight. Long enough to be glad—and yet know it was no use—he would come up again. And he *was* up—farther off. The boat had moved. It was lightning. ‘The rope!’ he called—not his voice. I hear it now. I bent for the rope. I felt I must. I was sure he could swim. I dreaded him coming back. But he sank again. I had the rope in my hand—no, there he was—face above water—calling again—and I held my hand—and my heart said, ‘Die!’—and he sank. And I felt, ‘It is done. I am wicked. I am lost!’—and I had the rope in my hand—I don’t know what I thought—I was leaping away from myself. I would have saved him then. I was fleeing from my crime—and there it was—close to me as I fell—there was the dead face—dead, dead. It can never be changed. That is what happened. That is what I did. You know it all. It can never be altered.”

CHAPTER LVII. “The unripe grape, the ripe, and the dried. All things are changes, not into nothing, but into that which is not at present.”—MARCUS AURELIUS.

Deeds are the pulse of time, its living rhythm—righteous or unrighteous, once done, they echo through the ages, vibrating in the silence until time itself grows still, and the world breathes no more upon a mirror.

That evening, she called for him again. It was nearly the hour when she had been brought in from the sea the night before, and the light was soft, dimmed by drawn blinds and open windows. She sat gazing fixedly at the ocean, her cheek resting on her hand, quieter now than when he had last left her, yet shadowed with a deep sorrow that shifted into anxious hesitation as he approached. She did not reach out to him, but said softly, “How long ago it seems.”

Then, almost pleading: “Will you sit near me just a little while?”

He took his place beside her, just as he had before. He saw the look in her eyes—the quiet urgency, the unspoken words trembling on her lips—and waited. But she turned again toward the window, silent, then back to him, the same longing expression returning, yet no words came. There was something holding her back. To ease her fear, Deronda gently turned his face away.

After a moment, she whispered, broken and desperate: “You won’t tell anyone else? You’ll never let another know?”

“Never,” he answered firmly. “There is no action to be taken. No wrong can be undone this way. No justice can be served by revealing what happened. It would only deepen the wound.”

She was so still, so still, that it seemed she held her breath before speaking again. “But if I hadn’t hesitated—if I hadn’t let that cruel thought take hold—if I’d thrown the rope at once… might it have saved him?”

“No,” Deronda said slowly. “I don’t think so. If he could swim, he must have suffered cramp. Even with your swiftest effort, it would have been impossible. That fleeting wish—to see him die—could not have changed fate. Its true consequence lies within you. In our hearts, evil desires matter deeply. They may twist us into harm, or awaken shame that drives us toward better things. And that shame, Gwendolen—your horror of yourself—it may be the beginning of healing.”

“I’ve spared others,” she murmured. “Others will have everything they need. They’ll have what they deserve. I knew that before I left town. You don’t think I wanted anything for myself?”

Her voice faltered. “I didn’t even consider that,” Deronda replied. “I was thinking only of what you’ve endured.”

“Perhaps you don’t know where it all began,” she said slowly, as though forcing herself forward. “There was someone else—he should have married her. I knew it. I told her I wouldn’t stand in the way. And I left—that was when you first saw me. But then we lost everything. Poverty came suddenly, and I was overwhelmed. I felt tempted. I thought, *I’ll do as I please—I’ll make it right.* I convinced myself. But it wasn’t right. It was terrible. Then came hatred, dark thoughts. That’s how it started. I told you I feared myself. And I tried to turn that fear into protection. I imagined what would happen if I gave in—how I’d dread the morning, wish it were always night, yet see death in every shadow. If you hadn’t known how much I suffered, you might have judged me differently—but now it’s too late. It’s all been for nothing. All I care about now is protecting them—my mother, who has never known joy.”

Silence fell. Then, choked by sobs: “You can’t bear to look at me anymore. You think I’m beyond saving. You don’t believe I can become worthy—of anything—of love—of being good. I’ll always be too wicked…”

Her voice broke, helpless.

Deronda’s heart ached. He looked at her face—so frail, so full of anguish—and said, “I believe you can become more worthy than you’ve ever been—worthy to live a life that brings peace to others. No sin condemns us forever unless we cling to it, desire it, refuse to break free. You *have* fought it. You will keep fighting.”

“But you were the one who made me want to change,” she whispered, pressing her hands together on the armrest, her eyes fixed on him. Her face bore the traces of a life shattered in twenty-four hours—a new existence born from a deed that fulfilled a forbidden desire. “I’ll endure any penance. I’ll live any life you choose. But don’t leave me. Stay close. If you had been here—if I could have told you everything—I might have been different. Please, don’t abandon me. Will you stay?”

“It would never occur to me to leave you,” Deronda said quickly, his voice steady, warm. His tone—like his eyes—had an unintended depth, making his kindness seem more personal than it truly was. In that moment, he felt a flicker of unease. His compassion for her pain stirred images of future burdens, of promises that might one day feel unkept. He met her gaze, but beneath it, he sensed the weight of what he was saying—an indefinite vow tied to uncertain hope.

Anxieties crowded his mind—both near and far. After a pause, he said, “Sir Hugo Mallinger should arrive by tomorrow night. And I hope Mrs. Davilow will follow soon. Her presence will mean the most to you. It will give you reason to spare her unnecessary grief.”

“Yes,” she breathed. “Yes, I’ll try. And you won’t go?”

“Not until after Sir Hugo arrives.”

“And then—we’ll all return to England?”

“As soon as possible,” Deronda replied, avoiding further details.

She turned again toward the window, her expression changing—as if awakening from a daze. The twilight deepened, but Deronda noticed the subtle shift in her eyes, in her hands, like the slow return of awareness after shock.

“You’ll always be with Sir Hugo now?” she asked, looking at him. “You’ll live at the Abbey—or Diplow?”

“I don’t know where I’ll live,” he said, his cheeks flushing slightly.

She saw the change in him and fell silent, realizing she had spoken too boldly.

After a while, she began again, her gaze drifting away. “I can’t imagine how my life will go on. Now I think it would be better to be poor, to work.”

“New paths will open as days pass,” Deronda said gently. “When you’re among your friends again, you’ll find new duties. For now, make it your task to recover—to grow calm, to regain yourself—before—”

He hesitated.

“Before my mother comes,” she finished.

“Oh,” she added, turning to him, “I haven’t looked at myself. Would you have recognized me—if you’d seen me now? Would you have known me, the woman you met in Leubronn?”

“Yes,” he said quietly. “I would have known you. The outward change isn’t great. I would have seen at once that it was you—and that you had suffered terribly.”

“Don’t wish you’d never seen me,” she begged, tears welling. “Don’t wish that.”

“I couldn’t despise myself for wishing it,” he said. “How could I know what I wished? We must accept our duties in what happens—not in what might have been. If I allowed myself such foolish dreams, I wouldn’t wish I’d never seen you. I’d wish I’d been able to save you from this.”

“You’ve saved me from worse,” she whispered, sobbing. “I’d have been worse without you. If you hadn’t been kind, I’d have been more wicked than I am.”

“I should go now,” Deronda said, weary from the emotional strain. “Remember what we spoke of—your task. Be strong, be calm. Prepare yourself before others arrive.”

He rose. She offered her hand, obedient, subdued.

But once he was gone, she collapsed to her knees, consumed by uncontrollable weeping. The distance between them was too vast. She was a soul cast out—gazing at a life she had forfeited through sin. Found there, crushed on the floor, her grief seemed natural enough for a woman whose husband had drowned before her eyes.

CHAPTER LVIII. “Much adoe there was, God wot; He wold love and she wold not.” —NICHOLAS BRETON.

Time moves in many ways—sometimes with the quiet persistence of a slow tide, sometimes with the sudden force of a storm. A man may journey across continents, discover new truths, endure great trials, and return to find his world unchanged: the same gossip in the village, the same familiar faces, the same old habits. Yet within him, everything has shifted. The mind can travel farther than any map, and the soul may be transformed without a single footstep taken. This is how life works—not by measure of miles or years, but by depth of feeling, by the weight of experience that settles into being.

Such a shift had marked the year since Gwendolen Harleth stood radiant at the Archery Meeting, confident and bright, her future seemingly written in gold. Now, she was humbled, broken by a truth she could no longer deny. She had come to see herself as unworthy—her pride shattered, her heart laid bare. And yet, around her, life continued much as before. Her family at Pennicote remained much the same: the rectory still warm and peaceful, its gardens blooming with peonies and hollyhocks, the rector still cheerful and dutiful, tending his parish with quiet devotion. He wrote articles on church matters—unsigned, so they were credited to others—and took pleasure in knowing that even if no one read them, they pleased those who mattered most. His peace was simple, untroubled by ambition or envy. It was a life lived in harmony with nature, far from the noise of fame or criticism.

And then there was Rex—his son, now returned for a summer visit, bringing with him Anna, whose presence lit up the house like morning sun. Rex had changed. Once full of restless energy, he now moved with purpose, rising early, studying late, focused on the law as though it were a calling rather than a career. When his father asked if he regretted choosing such a path, Rex replied firmly: “There is no profession I would choose before it. I want to be a judge, to help shape the laws. Give me something to do with making the rules, and let others make the songs.”

The rector smiled. “You’ll have to digest a lot of rubbish,” he said.

“Not worse than what people swallow in literature,” Rex answered. “At least law teaches you how to think. And beyond that—it’s history, philosophy, the story of human justice. Yes, it’s hard work. But greatness never comes easily.”

His father nodded. “That’s the best sign of success—when a man believes his work is the finest in the world.”

And indeed, Rex seemed destined for distinction. The rector spoke of him with quiet pride, comparing him to Paley, whose father once declared with certainty that his son would achieve greatness. Mrs. Gascoigne, hearing this, asked, half-jokingly, whether Paley had been an old bachelor. The rector didn’t remember, and only chuckled. But the moment passed—no need to dwell on irrelevancies.

At Jodson’s, the Davilows’ modest home just outside Pennicote, time too had passed gently. Mrs. Davilow looked a little more worn, her face softened by sorrow, her hair touched with silver. The girls had grown—bloomed, really—freer now that the shadow of their former life at Offendene had lifted. Jocosa, ever practical, remained steady, content with her place, untouched by the glitter of society. Their drawing-room, narrow but cozy, opened wide on a July afternoon to the scent of roses and the distant sound of wheels, creating a scene both intimate and lively—especially with Rex and Anna added to the usual circle.

Anna, always beloved by her cousins, shared stories of London—of grand houses, fashionable acquaintances, and the dazzling world of the wealthy. Naturally, Gwendolen came up. What had she said? How had she lived? Had Anna seen her after the news of the yacht?

“No,” Anna said simply.

And so the imagination took over. The yacht—Marseilles—the elegant cabins—Gwendolen’s letters filled with *dittos*—it all became legend. The newspaper had mentioned it. Gwendolen was living a life of adventure, elegance, mystery. Isabel, ever the dreamer, imagined pirates, storms, daring escapes. But when Rex entered the room, the topic vanished. He was not to be reminded of anything connected to Gwendolen.

Instead, they talked of the Meyricks and their extraordinary Jewish friends—Mirah and her brother. To these country minds, Jews were a marvel—like creatures from ancient lore, real but strange, different enough to seem almost another species. Bertha wondered what they believed. Miss Merry thought Mirah could never be reasoned with. Alice confessed she couldn’t bear them. Mrs. Davilow corrected her gently: “The great families among them are quite proper—just like anyone else in London or Paris.” But she admitted the common ones were less appealing. Isabel asked whether Mirah sounded like a Christian, or if you might sit beside her and never know.

Rex, amused, played along, exaggerating each remark for laughter—until a messenger arrived from the rectory, breathless, bearing a letter.

Mrs. Davilow opened it, read it twice, then stared into space. The room grew still. All eyes turned to her. Then, with a sob that held relief and grief, she whispered, “My dears… Mr. Grandcourt is drowned.”

Rex leapt up as if struck. Anna looked at him, startled. But he quickly regained himself. “Can I do anything, aunt?” he asked. “Shall I tell my father?”

“Yes, dear. Tell him I’ll be ready—he’s very kind. He says he’ll go with me to Genoa. He’ll be here by half-past six. Jocosa, Alice—help me pack. Gwendolen is safe—but she must be ill. I’m sure she is. Rex—Anna—go tell your father I’ll be ready. I wouldn’t miss a night. Bless him for being so quick.”

They left together, walking through the golden sunlight now thick with silence. No words passed between them. Anna worried about Rex’s wound—had the shock reopened it? Rex, meanwhile, was lost in a storm of thoughts. Not grief—for Grandcourt was a stranger to him—but a turmoil of selfish imaginings, rising like ghosts from the past.

Had Gwendolen been free? Was she now unbound? Could she ever love him?

He knew better. He knew the shame of such thoughts—how they mocked his own resolve. For nearly a year, he had built a calm life, letting go of longing, accepting his fate. But three words—“Mr. Grandcourt is drowned”—had shattered it all. The hope he’d buried came rushing back, not with joy, but with guilt.

She would never love him. Not now. Not ever. She was rich, exalted, surrounded by admiration. He was nothing—only a young man with a quiet ambition, a future still uncertain. And yet—why did it hurt so much?

He walked into the shrubbery, trying to still the chaos. He told himself plainly: *She would never have you. You would never ask. Thinking of it now is no better than looting a battlefield. There’s nothing to gain. Nothing.*

But the voice of desire refused to be silenced.

He paced, angry with himself, until he finally forced himself back into the house. Into the study, where his father was packing a traveling desk.

“Can I help, sir?” Rex asked, forcing calm into his voice.

“Yes, my boy. When I’m gone, look after my letters. Answer what needs answering. Send me word of everything. Dymock will manage the parish. You’ll stay with your mother—or go back and forth—until I return.”

“You won’t be long, I suppose,” Rex said, rolling a railway rug. “Will you bring my cousin back?”

He said it quietly. The rector looked up, surprised—then pleased. “That depends,” he said. “Perhaps her mother will stay with her. Or maybe I’ll return soon. The telegram doesn’t say. But I expect Gwendolen will be well provided for—splendidly, even.”

“She must have been shocked,” Rex said, gathering strength. “Was he a devoted husband?”

“Without doubt,” the rector said firmly. “Few men in his position would have stepped forward as he did.”

Rex had never met Grandcourt. Never heard of his pursuit of Gwendolen. He only knew that Grandcourt had loved her deeply, offered marriage when she was poor, and helped her family generously. That was noble—what any decent man might do. Grandcourt had been lucky. He had found happiness. And now, he was gone.

Yet Rex couldn’t shake the question: Had Gwendolen truly loved him? Or had she simply endured his affection, unable to tell him how she felt?

The answer remained hidden—buried beneath grief, secrets, and the quiet ache of a love that could never be.

CHAPTER LIX. “I count myself in nothing else so happy / As in a soul remembering my good friends.” —SHAKESPEARE.

Sir Hugo Mallinger did not set out for Genoa as quickly as Mr. Gascoigne had, and Daniel Deronda, for reasons both personal and profound, waited until he could meet the baronet in person. Grandcourt’s death was still fresh—shattering, irrevocable—and then there was the deeper upheaval within Deronda himself, the revelation of his origins, which he had not dared to unfold fully in writing. The truth about his mother—her fleeting presence, her sorrowful departure—had left him with questions too tender for letters. So he stayed, waiting at the station on the fifth evening, as promised by telegram, to greet Sir Hugo, whose arrival was expected between eight and nine.

As he stood beneath the pale twilight, anticipation stirred in him. The sight of that familiar face—the kind, steady features etched into the earliest memories of his life—would bring comfort. And yet, even now, amidst grief and uncertainty, a quiet smile touched his lips. He imagined Sir Hugo’s joy at finally being master of his own estate, free to pass it on to his daughters, or perhaps—according to a vision recently awakened in Deronda’s mind—to accept a future heir through a daughter’s child. Such thoughts were not without warmth, though they carried their own weight of irony.

We are not meant to be entirely hard-hearted, after all. To feel no pleasure in another’s happiness unless it aligns perfectly with our own ideals would make us strangers to kindness. Our faces might grow sour, our eyes cold, while our own lives remained imperfectly aligned with what we believed should be. But human connection thrives not on perfection, but on understanding. It is possible—even necessary—to be grateful for love, even when it is misdirected, when its intentions are pure, however flawed the means.

Deronda knew this well. He could not condone Sir Hugo’s role in concealing his true parentage. That was not merely silence—it was a lie, a veil drawn over the truth of his birth. Yet he also understood that Sir Hugo had likely never known the full depth of the mother’s betrayal. With his easygoing nature, the baronet may have simply believed it reasonable—indeed generous—that a woman who refused to part from her son should see him raised as an English gentleman, despite her own unorthodox ways. She had chosen secrecy, and Sir Hugo had honored it, not out of malice, but out of affection and duty.

And so, though Deronda’s judgment was firm, his heart remained tender. He wished to forgive rather than condemn. There is a grace in holding fast to principle while softening the edge of blame—like seeing a crooked frame hung askew and feeling pity for the hand that tried to hang it straight. Perhaps, after the past ten days—days that had stripped away illusions and revealed hidden truths—Deronda was more inclined than ever to resist the urge to punish, to resent, to condemn. He had seen too much suffering, too many lives shaped by unseen forces, to let anger rule him now.

When Sir Hugo stepped down from the train, the old bond surged forward—stronger than memory, deeper than words. The years melted away in the grip of their hands, the look in their eyes speaking volumes. No greeting was needed. They walked slowly toward the hotel, letting the evening air carry them, the stars beginning to glimmer above the sea.

“I’ve taken my journey easily,” said Sir Hugo, “and I’m in excellent spirits.”

Deronda nodded. “I’m glad.”

“I didn’t rush off,” continued Sir Hugo, “because I wanted to look into things a little. I saw your letter to Lady Mallinger before I left. How is the widow?”

“Getting calmer,” Deronda replied. “She’s recovering from the shock and the physical strain. Her uncle and mother arrived two days ago, and she’s well cared for.”

“And any chance of an heir?” Sir Hugo asked, his tone light but probing.

“From what Mr. Gascoigne told me,” Deronda said carefully, “I don’t think so. He seemed to suggest it’s uncertain whether she’ll inherit the estates at all.”

Sir Hugo gave a short laugh. “Then it won’t be much of a blow to her affections, I suppose, losing her husband?”

Deronda hesitated. “The suddenness of his death has been a great blow,” he said quietly, avoiding the question.

“Did Grandcourt give her any hint about the terms of his will?” Sir Hugo pressed.

Deronda looked at him. “Do you know what they are, sir?”

“Yes,” Sir Hugo answered swiftly. “Gad! If there’s no legitimate heir, he’s left everything to a boy he had with Mrs. Glasher—a woman who lived with him for years, practically a wife. Three older daughters, and now this boy, who’s already called Henleigh. He’ll take the name Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt. The Mallinger part? Bah—I’m glad that won’t matter to him. He’ll have more than enough with fourteen years’ minority. No need for me to have wasted fifty thousand on Diplow, which he had no right to claim. Meanwhile, the young widow gets two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere. A nice little exile, if she chooses to shut herself up there—which I doubt she will. Mrs. Glasher has been living there lately. I’m utterly disgusted with Grandcourt. I don’t feel obliged to respect him just because he’s dead. Though, frankly, nothing in his life suited him like leaving it.”

Deronda paused. “In my view, he was wrong to marry her—not in leaving the estate to the other son,” he said, “but in not providing for her properly. She was penniless, raised to a rank far beyond her background. She deserved a decent income—four or five thousand a year, and the London house for life. That’s what I would have done.”

Sir Hugo nodded. “I agree. Even a wise man lets folly slip into his will. My father did. And if a man has bitterness in him, he often hides it in such documents. Clearly, Grandcourt intended his death to end his wife’s hopes—if she bore no heir.”

“And if she had?” Deronda asked, his voice edged with scorn.

“Precisely,” Sir Hugo said. “Gadsmere and two thousand pounds. Odd, isn’t it? One nuisance is that he made me executor. But since he was my brother’s only son, I can’t refuse. Still, I’ll do what I can to help the widow. Lush thinks she knew about the family secrets—about the will, too. He hints there wasn’t much harmony between them. But I imagine you’re the one who truly knows what she felt—or didn’t feel—eh, Dan?”

His tone was serious now, not playful. Deronda sensed the gravity behind the question. He could not dodge it.

“She was not happy,” he said. “They were ill-matched. But regarding the estate… based on what I’ve seen of her, I believe she will accept whatever comes.”

Sir Hugo shrugged slightly. “Then she’s not like most women. But she must be something extraordinary—there must be some strange connection between your fate and hers. When the telegram came, Lady Mallinger said, ‘How very strange that it should be Daniel who sends it!’ And I’ve had something similar in my own life. Once, abroad, I met a woman left destitute by her husband. I helped her—only to discover she was an old flame, foolish enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and a short temper. But it was my own business that brought me there—not knight-errantry. Just as your coming here wasn’t about the Grandcourts.”

They walked in silence for a moment. The port slipped behind them, the sea calm under the stars. Sir Hugo had begun with the easier topic—the Grandcourts—but both men knew the real conversation lay ahead.

Deronda had written to Sir Hugo after meeting his mother, but the letter had only deepened the distance between them. He felt it was better to wait for the baronet to lead.

Then, just as they turned toward the *Italia*, Sir Hugo stopped, gazing back at the harbor. He turned, his voice softer now.

“And about your reason for coming to Genoa, Dan? Has anything you’ve learned deeply wounded you? Is there anything that changes your place in life? You know—whatever happens to you, it matters to me.”

Deronda took a breath. “I want to repay your kindness with complete honesty, sir. But I cannot answer those questions with yes or no. Much of what I’ve learned has hurt me. Meeting and saying farewell to my mother—seeing her in pain—was difficult. But knowing my parentage… that is not a wound. It is a relief. A clearing of doubt. And as for my position—my gratitude to you remains unchanged. Your care, your love, have always been a father’s to me. But knowing I am Jewish… that may shape my life in ways I cannot yet explain.”

He spoke with quiet resolve, though fear lingered beneath the words. He knew their worlds were different, and soon, inevitably, those differences would become harder to hide.

Sir Hugo looked at him sharply, then turned away. After a pause, he said, “I’ve always expected something remarkable from you, Dan. But for God’s sake—don’t go chasing eccentricities! I tolerate anyone’s beliefs, but not melodrama. At this stage of the world, if you want to be taken seriously, keep clear of theatrics. Don’t misunderstand me—I don’t think you’re mad. But you’re drawn to the persecuted, Dan. I feel for them too. But choosing company based on suffering… that’s risky ground. Still, I won’t pressure you. When you decide on a path that needs money, I’ve got sixteen thousand pounds saved for you—above what you’ve already received. And now that I’m here… do you want to return to England soon?”

“I must go first to Mainz,” Deronda said. “To retrieve a chest belonging to my grandfather. And perhaps visit a friend of his. Though it’s been twenty years, I feel a strange urgency to get it myself—like something might happen to it now. And I lingered after my mother left, instead of departing immediately. But I don’t regret it. If I hadn’t been here, Mrs. Grandcourt would have had only servants to assist her.”

Sir Hugo smiled wryly. “Yes, yes. I hope you’re not going to elevate a dead Jew above a living Christian.”

Deronda flushed, biting back a retort.

They stepped into the *Italia*, the night quiet around them, the sea whispering beneath the stars.

“But I shall say no more of this at this time; for this is to be felt and not to be talked of; and they who never touched it with their fingers may secretly perhaps laugh at it in their hearts and be never the wiser.”—JEREMY TAYLOR.

There was once a legend that the Roman Emperor, seeking vengeance for Joseph’s betrayal by his brothers, ordered ten learned Israelites put to death. And through history, men like him have flourished—those whose piety lies not in mercy, but in punishing what they see as injustice, yet blind to the deeper truth of gratitude. For how can one ever convince a heart ruled by pride that love has its own reason? Or make a soul untouched by affection believe that there is meaning in devotion? We learn this from language itself: *love* comes before *lovable*. The feeling precedes the justification.

When Daniel Deronda arrived at the banking house on Schuster Strasse in Mainz, he carried a letter of introduction and a quiet hope. He asked for Joseph Kalonymos, and was promptly shown into a private room where an elderly man sat sorting letters at a table. It was the same white-bearded figure Deronda had glimpsed the year before in the synagogue at Frankfurt. The man wore his old felt hat still, and beside him stood a packed portmanteau, wrapped coat and shawl draped over it—like a traveler preparing to leave.

At Deronda’s entrance, Kalonymos rose slowly, but did not step forward or offer his hand. His eyes—small, sharp, glittering like dark stones beneath a pale yellow skin—fixed upon the young man. In German, he said, “Good. Now you come to me.”

“Yes,” replied Deronda, “I come to thank you—for your kindness, for your care, for helping me find my way back to what was mine.” His voice was calm, fluent, shaped by years of listening to many tongues, yet rooted in English clarity.

Kalonymos then extended his hand, and his tone softened. “So you are no longer angry at being more than just an Englishman?”

Deronda smiled. “On the contrary. I am grateful. You helped me uncover a truth I had been kept from—my blood, my name, my grandfather’s legacy. And you preserved the chest he left behind, entrusted to you.”

“Sit down,” Kalonymos said, motioning to a chair, his voice low and urgent. He set aside his hat, revealing a head thick with white hair, and began to stroke his beard as he studied Deronda’s face. The moment stirred something deep within the young man—an almost electric connection to a past he had never known, yet now felt as if it were part of his breath. Here was a man who had walked beside his grandfather, who had loved him fiercely, who had guarded his memory. To stand before such a witness, to feel the weight of ancestral bonds rekindled, was like stepping into a sacred space where the dead still spoke.

It was a sensation beyond logic—something only those with open hearts could truly feel. Others might dismiss it as sentimentality, but Deronda knew it was real. And in that awareness, his expression softened, brightened—something Kalonymos seemed to notice, and approve.

Then, speaking in Hebrew, Kalonymos quoted a line from one of the ancient liturgical hymns:
“As thy goodness has been great to the former generations, even so may it be to the latter.”

He paused, then continued. “Young man, I am glad I have not yet departed. That you have come now, when I am still here, allows me to see the likeness of my dear friend—his youth reborn in you. No longer estranged from your people. No longer turning away in anger from one who would claim you as a Jew. You come not with resentment, but with thanks. You come willingly to say, ‘I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.’ Is this true?”

“It is,” Deronda answered firmly.

“And let me say,” he added gently, “that I would never have treated a Jew poorly simply because he was a Jew. But I understand why I hesitated to speak of my mother—to strangers, I mean. I could not tell them I knew nothing of her.”

“A sin,” Kalonymos said sharply, raising a hand and closing his eyes as if in pain. “A sin against our people—just as when our children were raised for Rome. But it is broken. I broke it. When Daniel Charisi—may God protect him—was young, and I was barely taller than his shoulder, we made a vow. We swore to be friends always. He said, ‘Let us bind ourselves with duty, as if we were sons of the same mother.’ That was his nature—always binding himself with purpose. He believed, ‘Let us bind love with duty; for duty is the love of law, and law is the nature of the Eternal.’ So we bound ourselves. Though life took us apart, the bond held. When he died, others sought to steal from him—but they could not steal me. I rescued what he had saved for his heir. And now I return it to him.”

Kalonymos stepped out briefly, returning with a clerk who bore a heavy chest. They placed it on the floor, removed a leather cover, and left. The chest was not large, but sturdy—adorned with gilt iron fittings, its wood engraved with Arabic script. It looked both ancient and precious.

“So,” Kalonymos said, resuming his seat. “And here is the key.” He pulled it from a small leather pouch. “Keep it carefully. I trust you are careful, and wise.”

He gave Deronda a look—sharp, cautious, the kind elders give to youth when entrusting something sacred. Deronda smiled. “I will guard this more closely than any other possession. It is not just an object—it is a symbol of hope, of effort, of love passed down. And I will never forget that you were part of that effort.”

“Have you time to hear more of your grandfather?” Kalonymos asked. “Or must you go?”

“I would stay,” Deronda said, “but I have duties calling me home. Friends in England need me. I’ve been away too long. Yet knowing more about you—and your family—would be reason enough to return.”

“Good,” Kalonymos said. “But you will not find me easily. I am beyond seventy, and I wander. I carry my shroud with me. But my sons and their children live here in comfort and unity. Times have changed since Karl the Great brought our ancestors from Italy to teach knowledge to the Germans. We fought for every inch of progress. Our youth suffered, but we won—wealth, safety, influence. Germany’s learning thrives on Jewish minds—even if not always on Jewish hearts.”

“Were you left entirely ignorant of your people’s life?” Kalonymos asked.

“No,” Deronda replied. “Even before I suspected my roots, I studied their history—more deeply than anything else. It turns out I was preparing myself to understand my grandfather without even knowing it.”

Kalonymos nodded, pleased. “Ah. Then you might have become like him—if your upbringing had not blocked the way. You resemble him in face—but not quite. He had strength in his features—a will like iron. He already had a deep furrow in his brow when young. I see none of that in you. Still, you are his grandson. Daniel Charisi once said, ‘Better a wrong will than a wavering one; better a steadfast enemy than an uncertain friend; better a false belief than no belief at all.’ What he despised most was indifference.”

“And yet,” Deronda ventured, “he was not narrow-minded?”

“Narrow? Never,” Kalonymos said, shaking his head with gentle amusement. “From childhood, he drank in knowledge as a plant drinks water. He studied medicine, philosophy, the mysteries of life and health. He traveled far, spent much, to see and know. He believed humanity’s strength lay in balance—between separation and connection. He warned against our people losing themselves among the Gentiles. ‘It’s no better,’ he said, ‘than grain blending into sameness.’ He gathered wisdom from all sides—like the Arab scholars of the golden age. We studied together, but he went further. Though we were close, he was different from me—like the inside and outside of a bowl. I held firm to two things: faith and travel. I accepted his words as I accepted the shape of trees—true, but not to be questioned. We both remained faithful Jews—thankful not to be Gentiles. I have lived as I do now—wandering, observing, loving life, unbothered by hardship. But Charisi thought constantly of our future—of our people’s destiny. That was his passion. Not mine. So while I found freedom in movement, he found purpose in vision.”

Kalonymos paused. “When I am in the East, I lie on deck and watch the stars. Their sight fills me. I know them by their rise, and I do not hunger for more. But Charisi—no sight satisfied him. He filled each glimpse with memory and prophecy. Yet we loved each other. As he said, we bound our love with duty. We pledged to help and defend one another to the end. And I have kept that pledge.”

He rose, and Deronda followed.

“And in keeping it,” Deronda said, “you have done justice to me. I would have been robbed—not just of a chest, but of my heritage, of my identity. I thank you with all my heart.”

“Be worthy of him,” Kalonymos said. “What is your vocation?”

The question caught Deronda off guard. He hesitated. He could not honestly call his law studies a calling—though he had begun them. He chose his words carefully. “I cannot say I have one yet.”

“Get one,” Kalonymos insisted. “The Jew must be diligent. Will you call yourself a Jew? Will you profess your fathers’ faith?”

He placed a hand on Deronda’s shoulder, his gaze piercing.

“I will call myself a Jew,” Deronda said, his voice steady though his face grew slightly pale under the scrutiny. “But I will not claim to believe exactly as they did. Our ancestors changed their beliefs—they learned from others, adapted. Yet I believe in my grandfather’s idea: separateness with communication. My first duty is to my people. If there is work to be done—restoring, improving their life—I will make that my purpose.”

In that moment, as he spoke, something settled within him. The need to answer revealed the truth he had not yet named. He had found his path.

“Ah,” Kalonymos said softly, “you argue and you look ahead—you are indeed Daniel Charisi’s grandson.” He blessed him in Hebrew.

They parted soon after. And not long after Deronda reached London, Kalonymos was once again aboard ship, gazing at the stars above the sea, content with what he saw, needing no more.

CHAPTER LXI. “Within the gentle heart Love shelters him, As birds within the green shade of the grove. Before the gentle heart, in Nature’s scheme, Love was not, nor the gentle heart ere Love.” —GUIDO GUINICELLI (_Rossetti’s Translation_).

In a quiet house near Pennicote, far from the white mansion where Rex Gascoigne lived, another heart stirred with sudden pain and restraint when news of Grandcourt’s death reached it. Hans Meyrick, ever punctual with the morning *Times*, slipped into the parlor as usual on a Wednesday, his latch-key turning softly in the lock. It was a day he always chose to deliver the paper himself—just as Mirah was finishing her weekly lesson with Mab, who was attempting to sing _Lascia ch’io pianga_, her voice wavering between imitation and hope.

But this time, Hans did not enter quietly. He burst into the room with a sharp crackle of paper, holding the newspaper aloft like a herald of fate. The music stopped instantly. Mirah, still at the piano, jerked upright, startled by the noise—something thunderous after the fragile silence of song. Mab frowned. “O-o-o, Hans! Why do you bring a more horrible noise than my singing?”

Mrs. Meyrick, sitting nearby, looked up sharply. “What is it? Any news from Italy? Have the Austrians given up Venice?”

“Not about Italy,” Hans replied, his tone oddly strained. “But something from there.”

The air thickened. Mrs. Meyrick leaned forward. “Nothing bad?” she asked, her voice trembling slightly—Deronda had been on her mind.

“Not bad for anyone we care about,” Hans said quickly, though his eyes flickered with something deeper. “Rather lucky, I’d say. I’ve never known anyone die so conveniently before. Given how much of a dear gazelle I am, I’m constantly amazed I’m still alive.”

Mab rolled her eyes. “If you must talk about yourself, do it behind your own back. What *is* it?”

Hans placed the paper before his mother, finger pointing to a paragraph. “Duke Alfonso is drowned. The Duchess is alive. That’s all.”

He paused, then added, “And here’s the best part—Deronda was in Genoa, staying at the same hotel. He saw her brought ashore by fishermen who pulled her from the water just in time. She jumped in after her husband. A bit rash, I must say. But Deronda was there to look after her. Lucky fellow.”

Mirah sank back onto the music stool, hands clasped tightly, eyelids lowered. Her breath came shallow. Mrs. Meyrick took the paper and handed it to Mab. “Poor thing,” she murmured. “She must have loved him dearly to leap in after him.”

Hans grinned, crinkling his face. “An oversight—a momentary lapse. Who could love a jealous baritone with icy glances, always singing aside? That was the husband’s role, no doubt. Nothing neater than his drowning. Now the Duchess is free to marry someone with warm eyes and good hair. And I’ll be invited to the wedding.”

At that, Mirah rose abruptly. Her gaze locked onto Hans, fierce and bright. “Mr. Hans,” she said, voice shaking, “you ought not speak so. Mr. Deronda would not like it. How can you call it luck? Life is not the same for one person as it is for another. You don’t know what it might mean to him—if he loved her. It could be a great sorrow. She would take him away from my brother. I know she would. Mr. Deronda would not call that lucky—would not call it joy to wound my brother’s heart.”

The room froze.

Hans sat transfixed, blushing deeply, as if struck. “I’m a fool,” he whispered, voice cracking. “A brute. I withdraw every word. I’ll go hang myself like Judas—if it’s allowed to mention him.”

Even in his remorse, his words held their old wit. But Mirah’s anger did not ease. It was too deep. She had spoken without thought, as a wounded creature bites its own flesh to endure pain. She said nothing more, only returned to the piano, pressing down on the sheet of music as if to begin again.

Mab broke the silence. “Mirah’s right, Hans. You always take Mr. Deronda’s name lightly. And joking about him marrying Mrs. Grandcourt—it’s awful. Men’s minds must be very dark,” she added, scornful.

“Quite true,” Hans muttered, rising and walking toward the back window, avoiding his mother’s glance.

“We should continue,” Mirah said, voice firmer now. “You haven’t finished your lesson, Mab. Shall I sing it again—or shall I sing it to you?”

“Oh, please sing it to me,” Mab said, eager to forget.

And Mirah sang—_Lascia ch’io pianga_—with such depth and power that even Hans stopped moving, leaning against the mantel, eyes averted. When the final note faded, she stood up. “I must go home. Ezra expects me.”

She offered her hand silently to Mrs. Meyrick, hesitating before kissing her cheek as she usually did. Instead, she lingered, ashamed. But the little woman drew her close, whispering, “God bless you, my dear.”

Mirah felt the weight of her outburst—the offense, the pride, the ungratefulness. She had hurt her friend, and now regret gnawed at her. Yet she could not speak.

Hans grabbed his hat. “Come on,” he said, opening the door.

“No,” Mab said, softening. “You’re not going to walk with her. I’m sure she’d rather not. You’re dreadfully disagreeable today.”

“I’ll go to see her safe,” Hans said, stepping outside.

Mirah said nothing. He opened the door, stepped beside her, and walked with her, uninvited but not forbidden.

They walked in silence. Mirah couldn’t bring herself to speak—not because she didn’t want to, but because she feared her words would be too harsh, too raw. She carried a storm inside: grief, jealousy, a terrible certainty. And yet, beneath it, a new strength had risen—one shaped by years of endurance. She had learned to hide, to bear, to act through pain.

Hans, meanwhile, was equally lost in thought. Mirah’s fury had pierced him. For the first time, he saw her not just as a kind girl, but as a woman whose heart beat fiercely for someone else. And that someone was Deronda.

He had long suspected a bond between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt. He had pieced together fragments—overheard conversations, subtle glances, Anna Gascoigne’s hints. He believed Deronda cared for her, though he masked it well. But he also believed Deronda would never choose Mirah—because Deronda was not Jewish, and Mirah had sworn she would never marry outside her faith. So he assumed Deronda’s affections were fixed elsewhere.

Now, Mirah’s anger had revealed something deeper. Not just concern—for her brother—but a passionate, personal wound. And that revelation made Hans melancholy—not just for her, but for himself. He had hoped, however faintly, that Deronda might return to her. But now he saw it differently: perhaps it was better that Deronda loved someone else. At least then Mirah wasn’t entirely alone in her sorrow.

It was a strange comfort. To see the beloved object suffer because her rival loved another—this was easier to bear than seeing her fully happy with him.

Still, Hans could not express it. He longed to show her tenderness, to make his presence speak of regret and care. But he said nothing. They walked side by side, two souls vibrating in silence, each carrying wounds they could not name.

When they reached Mirah’s door, Hans said, “Good-bye,” hand outstretched, eyes pleading.

She met his gaze—soft, sorrowful—and said, “Will you not come in and see my brother?”

He understood. It was forgiveness.

They entered. Mordecai sat by the fire, holding a letter, his face alight with quiet triumph. He looked almost radiant, as if he had conquered death itself.

After greetings, Mirah slipped into her room—not to cry, not to rage, but to gather herself. She removed her hat, sat down, pressed her temples as if her head ached. Then she rose, splashed cold water on her face and hair until her curls sparkled with droplets, like dew on a morning flower. She sighed deeply, put on her slippers, and sat still for a moment—long enough to feel the future press upon her.

Then she went downstairs to make tea.

Life had returned, in its old rhythm. She remembered rehearsals, performances, the need to hide her feelings. Pain had taught her endurance. And today, the storm had passed into a steady resolve.

But beneath the calm, a truth burned: she was no longer indifferent to Deronda. She had loved him—without knowing it. And now, she saw him slipping away, drawn toward a woman who belonged to another world.

She had once believed love was pure gratitude, reverence. But now it was something darker—jealousy, longing, a fierce, unwanted possession.

“What I have read, sung, seen—this is happening to me,” she whispered to her pillow. “This feeling is the love that makes jealousy.”

And she knew—no one else would understand. No one would see it. Like her early devotion to her mother, this pain was hers alone. But unlike that devotion, this was a flaw—something shameful, selfish, born of a nature that had grown too tender.

She had never truly believed Deronda could love her. He was from another world—bright, distant, belonging to a life of light and wealth, while hers was shadow and silence. But now, the image of Mrs. Grandcourt beside him was clear—sharp as pincers on her flesh.

Her soul, once peaceful, now burned with a passion she could not explain. She had been used to repugnance—toward certain people, certain lives. Now, that repugnance focused on Mrs. Grandcourt. She imagined her as cruel, false, dangerous—though she knew nothing of her.

“I could bear everything before,” Mirah whispered. “But this is worse. This is worse. I used not to have such feelings.”

Strange, that she should pray against a feeling connected to Deronda.

Later, Mordecai spoke to her, exalted by the news of Deronda’s return. “Seest thou, Mirah,” he said, “the _Shemah_—our confession of divine Unity—is the heart of our faith. It teaches that all humanity is one. In unity, the part holds the whole, and the whole holds the part. And so, human life moves toward the image of the Supreme Unity. When the soul grows spiritual, possession becomes universal, independent of the body. In this moment, I hold the joy of another’s future within me—joy I will not see, but which I love so deeply I could lay down my life upon its altar and say: ‘Burn, burn indiscernibly into that which shall be, which is my love and not me.’”

“Do you understand, Mirah?”

“A little,” she said faintly. “But my mind is too poor to feel it.”

“And yet,” Mordecai insisted, “women are made for the love that finds possession in renunciation. There is a story in the _Midrash_—of a Jewish maiden who loved a Gentile king. She entered prison, changed clothes with his beloved, and died in her place, so the king might live in happiness. That is the highest love—losing self in the object of love.”

“No, Ezra,” Mirah said, voice low but firm. “That was not it. She wanted the king to know what she had done. She wanted to be greater than the other. It was her pride, her desire to conquer—that made her die.”

Mordecai fell silent.

“If she had believed the king would never know,” he said slowly.

“You can imagine that,” Mirah replied, “because you are great, and like to think the greatest things possible. But I think it was not so. The girl had jealousy. She wanted to be first in his heart. That is why she died.”

“My sister,” Mordecai said gently, “you have read too many plays. Writers show passions as demons, without mercy or holiness. You judge by stories, not by your own heart—which is like our mother’s.”

Mirah said nothing.

And in that silence, the truth settled—like a stone dropped into still water. She had not chosen to feel this way. But now, she could not deny it. And the future, once bright, now seemed shadowed.

CHAPTER LXII. “Das Glück ist eine leichte Dirne, Und weilt nicht gern am selben Ort; Sie streicht das Haar dir von der Stirne Und küsst dich rasch und flattert fort Frau Unglück hat im Gegentheile Dich liebefest an’s Herz gedrückt; Sie sagt, sie habe keine Eile, Setzt sich zu dir an’s Bett und strickt.” —HEINE.

Mirah had been waiting—almost dreading—for the moment that now arrived: not with the suddenness of a storm, but with the quiet persistence of sorrow returning home. After singing at a morning charity concert in a grand house near Knightsbridge—invited by Klesmer, whose name still carried weight among those who valued music—she stepped out into the London street, wrapped in a simple black dress and a dust cloak, her thoughts heavy with the day’s performance and the memory of her father’s absence. Outside, a crowd lingered to watch the performers depart, but Mirah walked on, alone, her steps measured, her eyes fixed ahead.

Then she felt it—the presence behind her. Not a threat, not yet, but a rhythm matching her own. A shadow kept pace. Her breath caught. It wasn’t the kind of attention that drew from strangers’ stares, nor was it the sort that made her fear for her safety. No—this was deeper. This was recognition. And with it came a flood of dread, as if she were being hunted not by men, but by memory itself.

She did not turn. To look would be to surrender to the emotions she had long buried. If it was him—if it was her father—then he would force her to face him. She could not flee, not truly. But neither could she meet him unprepared. There was Ezra, her brother, whom she had promised Mrs. Meyrick she would protect from sudden shocks. That vow now pressed upon her like a weight. She must not let him see this man—not yet.

So she resolved to turn before reaching her door, to confront him on her own terms. She had nearly reached the entrance of the quiet square where they lived when the footsteps quickened. Then—there was a hand on her wrist, warm and insistent. A voice, familiar and strained, whispered, “Mirah!”

She stopped. Not with shock, but with a kind of resignation. The voice was his. The face—when she turned—was worn, lined, once handsome but now dulled by time and want. His eyes held a mixture of charm and desperation, as though he meant to beg while pretending to command. He wore shabby clothes, his hair grizzled and curly, his frame slight but active. He looked older than fifty-seven, though he was not. The man who had once been her father stood before her now, stripped of dignity, clinging to the last vestiges of suavity.

“Why did you run away from me, child?” he asked, his voice rising in false tenderness, hands gesturing as if to soothe. “What were you afraid of? I never forced you. I broke your engagement in Dresden because I saw it wouldn’t suit you. I had arranged something better for you at the theater—I didn’t tell you, so I could surprise you. But you left me stranded. I had to vanish, contract broken, reputation ruined. And after all I gave up—your education, your future—after everything I sacrificed—what did you do? You abandoned me.”

He paused, then suddenly sobbed—a woman’s sob—and pulled out a yellowed silk handkerchief, wiping his eyes with exaggerated emotion. “I loved you more than any father ever loved a daughter,” he said, voice cracking. “And you repaid me by leaving me alone. I might have died in a ditch, and you wouldn’t have cared.”

Mirah listened, her heart pounding. She knew the truth beneath his words—the lies woven into grief. Yet she did not flinch. For the first time, she spoke plainly.

“You know why I left you, father,” she said, voice trembling but firm. “I trusted no one. I believed you deceived my mother. If I could have trusted you, I would have stayed. But I couldn’t.”

Lapidoth blinked. The handkerchief slipped back into his pocket. “I never meant to deceive her,” he insisted, voice thick. “I wanted to bring you back. But circumstances changed. Then came news—she was dead. I stayed. It was best. Your brother could fend for himself. Only you mattered to me. I sent money to cover the costs. There’s one chance—maybe someone lied to me just to get the money.”

Mirah said nothing. She could not say what she truly thought—*I don not believe you*. Instead, she turned slightly, urging him forward. They walked together, side by side, a strange pair: her poised and elegant in English modesty, he restless and foreign, his gestures too eager, his presence too loud.

“You’ve done well for yourself,” he said, studying her. “You’re not poor.”

“I’ve had good friends,” she replied, keeping her voice low. “They helped me find work. I give lessons. I sing privately. I just performed at a concert.”

She hesitated, then added, “And I have people who know everything about me.”

A flicker of unease crossed his face. “And you’d be ashamed to show them your father like this?” he asked, almost pleading. “No wonder. I came here hoping to find you. A mad hope, perhaps—but a father’s heart is drawn by instinct. I could have done well abroad. Without you, I could have settled anywhere. But loneliness breaks a man. I thought you’d come to regret leaving me.”

He sighed. “I’ve struggled. I don’t know what comes next. My talents mean nothing here. People won’t hire a man who looks like he’s fallen apart. I’ve sunk low for a shilling already.”

Mirah’s mind raced. She could not let him sink further. But before she could speak, he asked, “Where do you live?”

“In this square,” she said. “We’re close.”

“In lodgings?”

“Yes.”

“Anyone to care for you?”

“Yes,” she said, meeting his gaze directly. “My brother.”

His eyelids fluttered. A brief tremor passed through his shoulders. Then, after a pause, he asked, “Ezra? How did you find him?”

“It would take time to explain,” she said. “Here we are.”

They stood at the doorway. Mirah stepped onto the threshold, turning toward him. Her heart beat fast. The thought of Ezra seeing this man—her father—filled her with shame and pity. And yet, in this moment, she felt a deep, painful reverence. She had always obeyed him. Now, she was choosing to defy him—not with anger, but with love.

“Stay a minute, *Liebchen*,” Lapidoth said softly. “What sort of man is Ezra?”

“A good man,” Mirah said slowly. “A wonderful man. He was poor when my friends found him—just a workman. Twelve years ago, he was strong and full of hope, going eastward, which he loved. But my mother called him back—because she had lost me. He came, cared for her through great suffering, worked until she died—died in sorrow. And Ezra… he lost his strength too. The cold took him when he returned. For years, he’s grown weaker—always poor, always working—but wise, thoughtful, noble. Everyone who knows him honors him. To stand before him is like standing before a prophet. Falsehoods are useless.”

She lowered her eyes, unable to bear the expression forming on his face—the frustration, the emptiness. But Lapidoth moved quickly. “Mirah,” he said, softening again, “wouldn’t it be better if I looked more respectable before your brother sees me? If I had a little money, I could buy new clothes. I could present myself properly. With a decent shirt and coat, I’d be welcome somewhere. I could even offer myself as a courier. I’d like to be with my children. To forget. To forgive.”

He paused. “But you’ve never seen me like this before. If you had ten pounds—just ten pounds—I could fix myself up by tomorrow.”

Mirah hesitated. She knew the temptation. She also remembered her promise—to Mrs. Meyrick, to Ezra, to herself. She looked at him, her voice quiet but firm.
