“I don’t like to refuse you, father,” she said. “But I promised not to help you secretly. It’s hard to see you like this. But we can endure it. Soon, you’ll have new clothes. We’ll pay for them.”

Lapidoth’s smile faded. “You’re a strict young lady now,” he sneered. “You’ve learned useful virtues—keeping promises when your father needs a pound or two. Your father who gave up his life for you, who made you an idol.”

“It seems cruel,” Mirah whispered, her lips pale. “I know it does. But breaking promises breaks hearts. It broke Mother’s. It has broken Ezra’s. We must bear this. Bear it now. Come in. Let us care for you as you are.”

“Tomorrow,” Lapidoth said, turning away sharply—then stopping, hands digging into his pockets. “I’m a little shaken, Mirah. I’ll feel better by tomorrow. If you’ve got a bit of money in your pocket—just a trifle—it wouldn’t break your promise to lend me enough for a cigar.”

Mirah didn’t question. She reached into her pocket, her fingers numb, and handed him her purse.

He took it swiftly, pressing her hand gently. “Good-bye, my little girl,” he said. “Tomorrow.”

He walked away, only a few steps, then stopped. He opened the purse carefully, counted the coins—two half-sovereigns, some silver—and then noticed a small piece of paper tucked inside. On it, in beautiful Hebrew script, was written the name of her mother, the dates of her birth, marriage, and death, and a prayer: *May Mirah be delivered from evil.*

It was a gift Ezra had given her. A sacred thing.

Lapidoth read it. In that instant, he saw his youth—the bright-eyed groom, the beloved husband, the man who once dreamed of writing, of teaching, of love. But the memory was flat, tasteless—like food without flavor. He had traveled far from that man. And now, the past was just a shape without feeling.

He closed the purse, considering its value—not just the coins, but the object itself. Could he sell it? Could he return for more? He knew the risk: the son watching, the daughter guarded, the world now against him.

Meanwhile, Mirah entered the house, her composure shattered. She found Ezra seated at a table, quietly sorting through ancient manuscripts—texts he intended to pass on to Deronda. At the sight of her, he rose, alarmed.

She fell to her knees before him, clutching his legs, sobbing. “Ezra! Ezra!”

He said nothing. His concern was immediate, profound. He understood her pain without needing words.

After a moment, she raised her hand, still weeping. “Ezra… my father. Our father. He followed me. I wanted him to come in. I told him you would let him. But he said no—not now. Tomorrow. And he asked for money. I gave him my purse. And he went.”

Her voice broke. She had said it all.

Ezra waited until she calmed, then gently removed her hat, laid his hands on her head. “Wait,” he said softly. “When you are calm, tell me everything.”

She did. And when she finished, he said quietly, “He will not come tomorrow.”

Neither spoke the truth they both knew: that he might follow her again, beg again, try again.

Then, after a silence, Ezra said, “Seest thou, Mirah? Our lot is the lot of Israel. Grief and glory are mingled—as smoke and flame. Because we inherit the good, we feel the evil. These things are wedded to us, as our father was wedded to our mother.”

The words hung in the air. Though they were in Brompton, the tone could have come from a rabbi reciting ancient wisdom in Babylon. For the Rabbi had said, “The Omnipresent is occupied in making marriages.” And by marriages, he meant all the wondrous, tangled unions of fate—those that bring joy and those that bring sorrow—each shaping our lives, each weaving the threads of our destiny.

“Moses, though he opposed art, was himself a great artist, possessing the true spirit of creation. Yet his artistic vision—like that of his Egyptian kin—was directed only toward the colossal and enduring. But unlike the Egyptians, who shaped their monuments from brick and granite, he built human pyramids, carved human obelisks. He took a poor shepherd tribe and forged it into a people meant to defy the centuries… He created Israel!”—HEINE: _Geständnisse_.

Imagine Daniel Deronda’s inner world when he left England and when he returned. Then, he had set out for Genoa uncertain whether the desires stirring within him would be nurtured—or whether the truths revealed to him might lead him down paths far removed from the quiet direction his thoughts had lately followed, a path he had half-willingly embraced, yet one whose uncertainty made his longing dangerous. Now, he returned with something like a discovered birthright, a confirmation of the ambition that had begun to stir in his soul. He came back not merely free—but bound by a duty that felt more natural than freedom itself, a bond his experience had prepared him to accept willingly, even without the promise of fulfilling a secret yearning long too fragile to name. But now, at last, he could confess it: his heart had chosen.

Since that moment when he left the Chelsea house in silence, moved deeply by Mirah’s farewell glance and words—those quiet, pleading expressions that stirred in him a deep care for womanhood, awakened as it was when his own lips were still soft as a girl’s—she had held a place in his soul so quietly powerful that it had helped him remain steadfast in word and deed through every trial we know of. There seemed no chance he could ever court her—the woman who had grown dear amidst circumstances that forbade such pursuit. Yet she had become a beloved ideal, dimming the appeal of others, reshaping his affections until absence felt like a lack. That influence had only deepened over time.

Gwendolen’s fate had drawn Deronda closer to her not with love, but with a passionate pity that felt like self-sacrifice. His tenderness, less constrained, flowed freely toward another image—an inner presence entirely unlike Gwendolen. And then there was Mordecai. Their growing connection brought Mirah closer still, not because their relationship changed outwardly, but because she became entwined in all the thoughts that made him hesitate before actions that might wound her brother. This transformation had not passed unnoticed by Deronda. He felt it keenly—like an unspoken desire that he knew better to keep hidden than to confess, even to himself. But when Hans’s attentions flared, the jealousy ignited. When his mother accused him of loving a Jewess, any denial suddenly felt like betrayal. She forced him into a declaration, just as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled him to state his resolve. This clarity, once achieved, transformed him. A surge of energy broke free—the suppression, the hesitation, the endless caution—all released. He laughed at his own impatience as he approached England from Mainz, feeling each mile a burden. It was as if he had found not just ancestry, but a new soul—his judgment no longer drifting in impartiality, but choosing with purpose, embracing kinship as strength, exchanging cold detachment for warm fellowship. He wanted to return to Mordecai—to speak openly, to argue, to agree, to feel again the closeness he’d been denying. And above all, to see Mirah—not as a distant hope, but as someone whose presence he could meet without fear, interpreting her eyes and voice from a new beginning. He wasn’t afraid of Hans’s advances, but he sensed Mirah’s feelings had always flowed along a different course—one unlikely to turn into love. The thought of surprising her by becoming her lover, when she saw him only as a nobleman or guardian, chilled him. He longed for gentler transitions.

No wonder, then, that Deronda went straight from the London station to the small square in Brompton. Every reason urged haste. He had promised Lady Mallinger he’d visit her at the Abbey the next day, and sunset was already falling. He wished to deliver the precious chest to Mordecai—its contents waiting to be studied, both alone and together—and this visit would please Mordecai deeply. So it pleased Deronda too. The currents of his nature were converging: his deep affection for those near him, his need for meaning beyond daily acts, his hunger for connection. In this grand, historic moment—uncovering his heritage—he wore the ordinary clothes of his age, like any hero of old. He didn’t consider how well they suited him; he rarely gave such matters thought. But his own inner glow, the warmth of his being, shone brighter now, visible in his eyes, in the fleeting flush on his skin, as he entered the house wondering what awaited him. He stepped in as quietly as he could.

It was the evening of the same afternoon Mirah had spoken with her father. Mordecai, overwhelmed by her sorrow and the memories it stirred, had abandoned his work. Papers lay scattered across the floor, forgotten. Neither he nor Mirah had bothered to tidy them. They sat motionless, lost in silence, while the clock ticked on the mantel and twilight crept in. Mirah, unable to eat, had not moved since she’d thrown off her cloak and taken her seat beside him, hand clasped in his. He leaned back, eyes closed, breathing labored—Mirah thought he looked as if his soul could no longer endure its frail prison. The idea of his death haunted her whenever she saw him like this, drained of life. Now, to her grief, came the regret that she hadn’t controlled her outburst, which had shaken him so deeply. She watched him—her cheeks pale, her eyes bright with recent tears, her curls tousled like a child’s—watching that thin face as if a veil had fallen forever, as if her joy had died, leaving her strong enough only to mourn. Life stretched before her not just with sorrow, but with double loss—one living, one dead.

Then the door opened. A familiar voice called softly: “Daniel Deronda—may he come in?”

“Come! come!” Mordecai said, rising instantly, his face alight, eyes wide—as if he’d expected this visit all along. Mirah started up, blushing, caught between surprise and alarm.

When Deronda entered, the room seemed to clear after rain. No storm could darken the light of that moment. He reached out to Mirah, standing near her brother’s side, and placed his other hand gently on Mordecai’s shoulder, holding both men at once. He said nothing, reading their faces, then asked anxiously, “Has anything happened? Any trouble?”

“Speak not of trouble,” Mordecai said, shielding her. “There is joy in your face—let it be ours.”

Mirah thought, *It must be something he can’t tell us.* But they sat down, Deronda drawing a chair close to Mordecai.

“That is true,” he said, firmly. “I have a joy that will endure even in the worst sorrow. I did not tell you why I went abroad, Mordecai—because… never mind. I went to learn my origins. And you were right. I am a Jew.”

The two men clasped hands—a gesture born of Mordecai’s sudden spark, passing through Mirah like lightning. But Deronda continued without pause, speaking as much from Mordecai’s mind as his own:

“We share the same blood. Our souls are called to the same purpose. We shall not be divided by life or death.”

Mordecai whispered in Hebrew, barely audible, using the sacred words of covenant: “Our God and the God of our fathers.”

The weight of emotion silenced his usual fluent speech.

Mirah knelt beside her brother, gazing at his face—once so deathly, now radiant. It was an involuntary act, a release from despair into a joy so solemn it felt like a ritual. For a moment, she thought only of her brother’s happiness.

“And it is not only that I am a Jew,” Deronda went on, relishing this rare moment when desire and deed were one, when reality matched his deepest dream. “I come from a line that has fiercely preserved our people’s unity—a family of Spanish Jews, scholars and leaders. And I possess something that will bind us to them. My grandfather, Daniel Charisi, kept manuscripts and records, hoping they would reach his grandson. That hope is fulfilled—despite attempts to hide my parentage. I carry the chest containing them, his writings, and it rests below in this house. I mean to leave it with you, Mordecai, so we may study them together. Some I can read—Spanish, Italian. Others are in Hebrew, Arabic, perhaps Latin translations. I only glanced at them in Mainz. We’ll read them side by side.”

He ended with a smile—bright, sudden, breaking through his usual gravity, like sunlight after dusk. But when it turned to Mirah, it struck her too sharply. She rose, returning to her usual posture—hands folded, feet crossed—trying to appear calm. She had knelt instinctively, without thought of herself. But now, under Deronda’s gaze, she remembered Mrs. Grandcourt, the social expectations, and flushed. Deronda, equally sensitive, imagined his own feeling had shown too plainly, and frightened her. He feared any boldness might ruin her regard for him—and thus endanger the bond he shared with brother and sister. If Mirah could not love him, then his declarations might only bring her pain, given how closely they would remain tied.

While these thoughts raced through both minds, Mordecai saw only blessing in Deronda’s presence.

“Daniel,” he said, his voice swelling with meaning, “from the first, I told you—we do not know all the pathways. Has there not been a meeting among them, as in one soul, where a thought is born and draws all things to it, fed and glowing? All things are bound in that Omnipresence which holds the world, and events are like glass through which we see some of the paths. And if it seems that human error and unloving wills have prepared you—as Moses was prepared—to serve your people better, that depends on a higher order than the laws guiding our steps. For man’s evil will does not make a people’s good except by awakening righteous will. Beneath all clouds around the Eternal, this is clear: a people can be blessed only by wise counselors and many whose hearts obey justice and love. See now—it was your loving will that formed the chief pathway, resisting evil. By caring for my sister, seeking her brother, your soul was ready to receive the message: ‘Behold, the multitude of your brethren.’”

“It is true,” Deronda replied. “You and Mirah have been my teachers. Had this truth come before I knew you, I might have resisted it. Perhaps I would have said, ‘If I could choose, I would not be a Jew.’ But now—I feel my whole being consent to it. It is your mind, gradually shaping mine, that has brought this full acceptance.”

At that moment, the first evening in the bookshop flashed vividly in his memory—the distance he’d once felt from Mordecai’s prophetic certainty. He loved satisfying the eager soul before him, like a watcher who finally sees the signal flame on the mountain. He spoke with greater fervor:

“It is through your inspiration that I see my life’s purpose. You have given shape to what I believe was an inherited longing—echoes of passionate thoughts in generations past, especially in my grandfather. Imagine a stolen child of a mountain tribe raised in a city, or a painter born blind—yet the ancestral life remains within, a dim yearning for unknown things, a body like a silent instrument, trembling with latent music. That, I think, has been my experience. Since I learned to read, I’ve longed for an ideal task—where I might be the heart and mind of a people, a leader not sought for glory, but called by duty. You have shown me such a task: to unite our race despite division. You said, ‘Our religion united us before it divided us—it made us a people before Rabbanites and Karaites.’ I will try to build that union. I will work in your spirit. Failure would not be dishonor—but not to try would be.”

“Even as my brother who nursed at my mother’s breast,” Mordecai said, sinking back in his chair, exultant, as if after a finished labor.

To grasp the power of Deronda’s words, one must remember his former restraint, his careful avoidance of premature promises. This pledge carried a sacramental weight—for him, for Mordecai. For Mirah, the effect was profound but different: surprise at the sudden intimacy, a sense of dawn breaking through darkness. But after a pause, Mordecai spoke again:

“It has begun—the marriage of our souls. Only the flesh must pass, and then the betrothed shall unite in a deeper bond. Call nothing mine that I have written, Daniel. Though our masters taught that all should be quoted in the speaker’s name—good rule—that does not exclude the willing union of souls, where thought grows fuller, like waters mingling, inseparable in depth and clarity. I have judged my writings. I wish the body of my thought to die as this flesh will. Let it be reborn from our fuller soul—yours.”

“You cannot ask me to promise that,” Deronda smiled. “I must first see reason in the writings themselves. I am still a slow learner. That blending must happen without choice. But what we cannot stop should not dictate what we ought to do. Our duty is faithful tradition. And you would insist on that for anyone but yourself. Do not ask me to deny my spiritual roots when I find my life’s clue in recognizing my natural ones.”

“I ask no promise until you see the reason,” Mordecai said. “You speak truly—I would follow the Master’s law for another. But for years, my hope, my confidence, has been not that my imperfect words live, but that my vision and passion enter yours—yes, yours. For the one I longed for afar was not another, but you, whom I recognized as mine when you came near. Still, you shall judge. For my soul is satisfied.” He paused, then shifted tone, recalling Deronda’s revelation: “What drove your parents——?” He stopped himself. “No, I ask not about others unless you wish.”

“Some time—gradually—you will know all,” Deronda said. “But now, tell me of yourselves. How has the time passed? I know there’s been trouble. Mirah has suffered.”

He looked at Mirah, but she turned to her brother, pleading silently for him to answer. She hoped he wouldn’t reveal the truth about her father tonight—when Deronda had just drawn so close, identified with her brother. To hear of their disgrace now, as if it were his too, wounded her. To escape, she rose to fetch her hat and cloak, thinking she’d go to her room. Perhaps they’d speak more freely without her. But Mordecai said:

“Today, we faced a grief. A duty long buried has returned, bringing no joy—only dread. But for now, we are free from visible chains. Let us delay speaking of it, as if this evening, deepening around us, were the start of a festival where we offer our first fruits of joy—no mourning mixed in.”

Deronda understood the hint, and said nothing. As Mirah rose, he stood too, saying, “Are you going? I must leave soon—after I and Mrs. Adam lift the chest, and I give the key to Mordecai—no, Ezra—may I call him Ezra now? I’ve come to think of him as Ezra, hearing you say it.”

“Please call him Ezra,” Mirah whispered, faintly, touched by his nearness, his gaze. Was he truly changed? Or was it only her own heart? The emotions of the past hours had drained her. She was weak, hungry, trembling. Deronda saw her pallor, longed to comfort her—but dared not. She reached out, trying to smile. He opened the door for her. That was all.

A man of refined pride avoids courting a woman whose wealth or rank might make his advances seem presumptuous. But Deronda faced a subtler difficulty—one where appearances suggested the opposite, yet love demanded humility. To a reverent heart, the beloved always possesses a kind of nobility. And here, Mirah’s gratitude bound her to him, making every wish of his feel like a claim. Her inability to fulfill it would haunt her, especially in their shared care for Ezra. Thus, Deronda’s fears were not only of pride, but of tender compassion. To be seen as a benefactor—this very role—seemed an insurmountable barrier to declaring love, unless somehow Mirah’s heart had already accepted him. And the agitation within him was immense.

Even a man trained in romance, hardened by practice, may one day be seized by the awe of love—tremble, stutter, show signs of raw feeling beyond his control. How much more so a man whose inner life had guarded his sensibilities, keeping the language of passion alive, fresh as spring leaves on the hillside!

And so, Deronda stood at the threshold, not knowing what lay ahead—only certain that everything had changed.

CHAPTER LXIV. “Questa montagna è tale, Che sempre al cominciar di sotto è grave. E quanto uom più va su e men fa male.” —DANTE: _Il Purgatorio_.

It had not been long since her mother arrived that Gwendolen agreed to stay in Genoa. The thought of leaving behind the glittering sea and its dazzling beauty stirred something deep within her—a quiet strength she hadn’t known she possessed. For even the most idyllic place can become a prison when the heart is burdened; no matter how lovely the landscape, if our inner world is aflame, we walk through paradise like exiles.

“I’ll never look at the Mediterranean again,” Gwendolen said softly to her mother, whose eyes held a gentle understanding—though she never spoke of the husband who was no more. Mrs. Davilow, though she outwardly mourned the tragedy of her daughter’s life, found herself quietly rejoicing in this unexpected return. Her child was back—not just with love, but with a newfound tenderness toward her presence, as if she’d nearly lost her forever and now clung to every moment.

“Are you there, mamma?” Gwendolen called out one night, her voice small and trembling, just as she might have done as a child afraid in the dark. A bed had been made for her mother beside hers, and their closeness felt sacred.

“Yes, dear,” came the reply. “Can I do anything?”

“No,” Gwendolen whispered, “just knowing you’re here makes it real. Do you mind if I woke you?”

The question was new—unlike the girl she once was.
“I wasn’t asleep, darling.”

“It felt unreal,” Gwendolen murmured. “I needed to hear your voice. I can bear anything if you’re near. But don’t lie awake worrying about me. You must be happy now. Let me make you happy—at last. Or what will I do?”

“God bless you, my dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, her voice thick with emotion. “That’s the greatest happiness I could ever know—when you care for me so deeply.”

But the next night, hearing her daughter restless and sighing, Mrs. Davilow offered gently, “Let me give you your sleeping draught, Gwendolen.”

“No, mamma,” she replied firmly. “I don’t want to sleep.”

“It would help you, my love.”

“Don’t tell me what’s good for me,” Gwendolen snapped, her voice sharp with sudden frustration. “You don know what’s good for me. Uncle and you both keep trying to decide things for me, but I feel it differently. Don’t interfere.”

Mrs. Davilow said nothing. She didn’t wonder at her daughter’s mood. Then, after a pause, Gwendolen spoke again, her voice breaking: “I was always naughty to you, mamma.”

“No, dear,” she answered softly.

“Yes, I was,” Gwendolen insisted, tears rising. “It’s because I was always wicked that I’m suffering now.” And then she wept, her sobs echoing in the stillness of the room.

Her silence about the past—the marriage, the end—had only pushed her emotions into these sudden bursts of pain, half-expressed, half-hidden. Yet slowly, dimly, the truth began to dawn on her mother, carried by fragments of news from Sir Hugo to Mr. Gascoigne, and then, in part, to her. Sir Hugo, while handling the formalities around Grandcourt’s death and the possibility of his body washing ashore, decided it was kinder to speak to the rector—his old friend—about the contents of the will, before the man returned home under illusions.

He broke the news carefully, first suggesting only that the widow might not be as well provided for as expected. Only later, after hints about debts and prior obligations, did he reveal that Grandcourt had left everything to a son born outside marriage—one who now stood to inherit all, since no legitimate heir existed.

The rector was deeply wounded. He remembered now how cold and proud Grandcourt had been—how dismissive of him. He also recalled vague warnings about the young man’s reckless ways, pleasures indulged in secret, things buried beneath a polished surface. Now, those secrets were revealed: not as relics, but as living truths, crawling over the lives of decent people like insects on fresh meat.

Yet he kept his thoughts private. To Sir Hugo, he said calmly, “When a man makes a will in health, he expects to live long. Perhaps Mr. Grandcourt never imagined this would come to pass.”

After a moment, he added, “Still, it’s painful in many ways. Giving such prominence to illegitimate children may harm women’s reputations.”

“Well,” Sir Hugo replied with his usual ease, “since the boy exists, it was really the best option. Grandcourt had no closer relative. It’s chilling to think you leave life only to benefit a cousin. Men enjoy making wills when they’re thinking of their own families—but it’s a nuisance when you’re giving it all to someone you barely care for. That’s worse than having only a life interest. No—I forgive Grandcourt for that part. But what I can’t forgive is how poorly he treated his wife—our niece. Not much better than a doctor’s widow. Nothing angers me more than a man showing posthumous stinginess toward his wife. A man should take pride in providing for her. I would. I see it as a test of character—whether a man feels at peace with death knowing his wife and daughters are secure. I’ve heard of soldiers in the Crimea willing to drown if their widows were cared for.”

“It has surprised me,” the rector admitted, “especially since I, as her uncle, had trusted in Grandcourt’s generosity. I made no claims for her beforehand. That seemed right.”

“Not blamable,” Sir Hugo said. “I respect trust. But take my advice—if you marry another niece, even the Archbishop himself, bind him down. Your niece can’t remarry twice. If he’s a good man, he’ll welcome it. As for Mrs. Grandcourt… I feel closer to her now, knowing she’s been ill-used. I hope you’ll urge her to rely on me as a friend.”

And so the noble-hearted baronet expressed his outrage at the fate of the young, beautiful widow—left with two thousand pounds a year and a house in a coal-mining region.

To the rector, that income seemed less dire. Still, he felt the deeper humiliation—the public exposure of Grandcourt’s relationship with Mrs. Glasher. He saw how it would wound not only Gwendolen, but all who loved her. And like any good husband and father, he felt the shame through the eyes of women.

So when he finally told Mrs. Davilow, the discomfort was profound. He anticipated the pain in Gwendolen’s heart—and dreaded speaking of it.

But Mrs. Davilow had already begun to suspect. She now believed her daughter had known about the other woman, the children, perhaps even before the marriage. She hoped to learn more during their journey home, to prepare her gently. But Gwendolen spared her.

“I hope you don’t expect me to be rich or grand, mamma,” she said soon after the rector’s talk. “Perhaps I’ll have nothing at all.”

She sat dressed, still, lost in thought.

Mrs. Davilow was startled, but replied after a moment, “Oh, dear, you’ll have something. Sir Hugo knows the will.”

“That won’t decide,” Gwendolen said sharply. “Sir Hugo says I get two thousand a year and Gadsmere.”

“What you receive depends on what you accept,” she said. “You and Uncle mustn’t try to pressure me. I’ll do everything to make you happy—but in matters concerning my husband, I must be left alone. Is eight hundred a year enough for you?”

“More than enough,” Mrs. Davilow said quickly. “Don’t think of giving me so much.”

She paused, then asked, “Do you know who inherits the estate and the rest?”

“Yes,” Gwendolen said, waving her hand. “I know everything. It’s all right. I never want to speak of it again.”

Mrs. Davilow looked away, flushed slightly. She rose to fetch a fan-screen, unable to meet her daughter’s gaze. What agony had her child endured—what secrets remained locked inside? She could only imagine.

Gwendolen watched her closely, now sensing her mother’s distress. With softening resolve, she said, “Come sit nearer, mamma. Don’t be unhappy.”

Mrs. Davilow obeyed, biting her lips to hold back tears.

Gwendolen leaned forward, tenderly. “I mean to be wise. Truly. And good—so good to you, dear, sweet, old mamma. You won’t recognize me. Just don’t cry.”

In her heart, Gwendolen had made a decision: she would ask Deronda whether she should accept any of her husband’s money—whether she could take enough to support her mother. She felt strong enough to do anything that would raise her in his eyes. Anything.

Sir Hugo had invited them to stay at Park Lane, where he lived. He urged them to make it their home until mourning duties were settled. Town, he insisted, was quieter than rural retreats now. He would personally arrange for Gwendolen’s belongings to be removed from Grosvenor Square.

This plan suited her perfectly. At Park Lane, she could meet Deronda—if only she could find a way to send him a message.

During the journey, Sir Hugo, aware she knew the terms of the will, spoke openly about her future. He mentioned possible arrangements, suggested mild prospects, and spread a cheerful air around her widowhood. He believed it was more graceful for a woman to recover her spirits after discovering her husband hadn’t treated her generously. It was the testator’s fault if grief turned into sorrow over poverty.

Moved by kindness and sympathy, the baronet became almost paternal. He called her “my dear,” discussed Gadsmere as if it were “we” who would manage it, and praised its comfort—despite the soot.

“I wouldn’t mind the dirt myself,” he declared. “Nothing’s healthier. If your work is there, Gadsmere is a paradise. It’s even featured in Scrogg’s history of the county—with its little tower and lake. The prettiest print in the book.”

“A more important place than Offendene?” asked Mr. Gascoigne.

“Much,” Sir Hugo replied firmly. “I was there with my brother—over twenty-five years ago, but I remember it well. The rooms may not be larger, but the grounds are on a different scale.”

“Our poor dear Offendene is empty now,” Mrs. Davilow sighed. “Mr. Haynes refused it, and no one’s taken it since. I should have accepted Lord Brackenshaw’s offer to stay rent-free—just to keep it aired and warmed.”

“I hope you’ve something snug instead,” Sir Hugo said.

“A little too snug,” Mr. Gascoigne smiled, glancing at his sister-in-law.

Gwendolen turned at her mother’s words, her eyes changing. Outside, the train passed through sun-drenched fields, hedges gone, poplars marking the horizon. The conversation drifted—idle talk about coal pits, telegraph cables, church rates.

But Gwendolen’s mind wandered far beyond. She saw Offendene—the downs, the cattle, the shaded lanes, the neat hedges along the road from the parsonage. She saw the avenue, the hall door opening, her mother or one of her sisters stepping out to greet her.

A quiet home she once fled as dull now appeared as sanctuary—a place of morning breath and birdsong, after wandering through a nightmare masquerade. She had entered with intoxicating belief in illusion, and now, terrified, she saw the end: the masks falling, serpents hissing around her, and the fear that she herself had become one of them.

Offendene became a refuge in her memory. She dwelled on it, yet gave no sign. She listened without reacting to discussions of cable lines or political bills.

How wide our minds roam during a long journey! Even strangers exchange stories from China to Peru, sharing so much they risk feeling poorer in future meetings. But Sir Hugo and Mr. Gascoigne had an even stronger reason to talk—forced together in a mourning coach, they lightened the mood with endless chatter.

“I don’t mind telling you,” Sir Hugo said, confiding in the rector. And the rector, without saying so, shared details of his sons’ struggles.

Through discussing everyone near Diplow, Sir Hugo grew fond of the place again. He decided to bring his family there for a few weeks before autumn. Mr. Gascoigne welcomed the idea.

The journey ended with mutual affection between the men.

Meanwhile, Gwendolen sat apart—like one who had walked among spirits, full of a silent experience that made ordinary talk seem dreamlike. Mrs. Davilow, meanwhile, wondered what her daughter felt, and what lay behind her hesitation about accepting the inheritance.

Gwendolen had a single purpose ahead: to see Deronda. She needed his judgment—to ensure she did nothing he would disapprove of. Without him, might she have forgiven herself? Would secrecy have brought absolution?

But it was Deronda who had become her conscience—the one whose opinion shaped her joy and dread. His presence had pierced her soul, revealing truths she couldn’t face alone. He had judged her at the gaming table, and she had felt it. He could have ruined that influence—but he hadn’t.

She had forgotten to ask for his address. Her only hope was Sir Hugo.

She was not blind to what others might think—of her dependence on Deronda, of seeking him more than he sought her. Grandcourt’s scorn had taught her pride. But her nature clung to this connection. She would not let go, not for appearances, not for fear.

At Park Lane, when she learned Sir Hugo would visit the Abbey briefly, she turned to him—calmly, without hesitation, her mother present.

“Sir Hugo,” she said, “I wish to see Mr. Deronda again—soon. I don’t know his address. Could you tell me, or send him a message?”

A flicker crossed Sir Hugo’s face—but he responded smoothly, “Honestly, I don’t know if he’s at his chambers or the Abbey. But I’ll make sure. I’ll send a note now to his chambers. If he’s at the Abbey, I’ll deliver your message and send him up at once. I’m certain he’ll want to obey your wish.”

He spoke with quiet kindness—as if such a request were natural, even proper.

But inwardly, Sir Hugo was convinced Gwendolen had fallen passionately in love with Deronda—a bond formed long ago. His old suspicion returned, stronger than ever: that her feelings might lead her into imprudence. And so, despite his concern, he meant to protect her.

To him, it was a perfect romance—this brilliant woman and his favorite Dan, destined for each other. And Grandcourt? Well, he had died at just the right time.

Yet Sir Hugo was troubled by a new doubt: had Dan grown too lofty, too distant? Was there some plan in his head—some greater purpose—that would make him reject this lovely widow, even when she reached out?

It was ironic—Sir Hugo, who once warned Deronda against being too tender, now felt irritated that he might not be in love enough.

Of course, all this was premature—only a fortnight after Grandcourt’s death. But minds often rush ahead or lag behind.

Still, he sent the note. And it found Deronda at his chambers.

CHAPTER LXV. “O, welcome, pure-eyed Faith, white-handed Hope, Thou hovering angel, girt with golden wings!” —MILTON.

Deronda hesitated before answering Gwendolen’s summons, not from pride, but from a deep sensitivity to the weight of what she might ask of him. He felt keenly how much her heart had come to depend on him—more than he could bear without pain. It was no longer a question for debate; it was a truth that lived in his bones: Gwendolen’s soul clung to him with an ache so fierce, it seemed to threaten her very survival. We do not argue when we hear anger or scorn in another’s voice—we feel it, and there is no room for doubt. So Deronda knew, with a quiet certainty, that her fate hung by a thread, and he was the one who held it.

Had this moment come just over a year earlier, he would have acted without hesitation. The impulse then would have been clear: to save her, to shield her from sorrow, to fulfill the promise he had begun when he returned her necklace—a gesture that had become more than a mere act of kindness. Then, love would have dictated his course. But now, other bonds bound him—love, duty, purpose—each pulling him in directions beyond her reach. Yet even as he stood apart, he carried within him a quiet longing, a trembling at the thought of seeing her eyes again, hearing her voice, feeling the silent plea that never left her lips.

He waited in the back drawing-room—the same space where they had once sat together at the musical gathering, where she had first confessed that her life depended on his staying near her, and where her words had melted into Mirah’s song: *Per pietà non dirmi addio*. But the melody had come from Mirah’s voice, not his own heart.

The room, familiar to him for years, now felt transformed. Every object—the gentle smile of Lady Mallinger’s portrait, the dignified lions flanking the fireplace—seemed to belong to a past life, one he was visiting only in memory. His world had changed. The house, once a home, now felt like a stage upon which a new drama unfolded—one in which he was both actor and witness. And here he stood, hat in hand, awaiting the woman whose life was unraveling before him, caught in a transformation too painful to name.

When Gwendolen entered, she looked altered—not only in mourning dress, but in spirit. There was a stillness about her, a quiet resolve that hadn’t been there in Genoa. She was satisfied, simply because he was there. Yet no smile passed between them. They met, hands clasped, each lost in memory, each burdened by what lay ahead.

“It was kind of you to come,” she said softly, seating herself. “Let us sit down.”

He took the chair opposite, silence settling between them like a veil.

“I asked you here because I need to know what I should do,” she began at once. “Don’t hold back if it’s hard. I’ve made up my mind to do whatever you think right. Once I feared poverty—I couldn’t bear being dependent on others. That’s why I married. But I’ve endured worse. Now I think I could endure poverty—if you say I must. Do you know about my husband’s will?”

“Yes,” Deronda replied, already sensing her question. “Sir Hugo told me.”

“Then you understand what I’m asking,” she said, her voice growing urgent. “I want to know if I ought to accept anything he left me. I’ve thought about this endlessly. You may not realize how much I considered my mother when I married. I was selfish—but I loved her. I cared about her suffering. When I was unhappy, the only comfort was knowing she was better off because of me. Now, the hardest thing I can imagine is seeing her poor again. I’ve been thinking—what if I take only enough to provide for her? Nothing for myself. Would that be wrong? After all, I was precious to her—he took me from her—and if she had known—”

She stopped, breath catching. She had prepared for this conversation for days, rehearsing her thoughts in solitude, but now the emotions surged forward, too strong to contain. Her words faltered, her hands—bare except for her wedding ring—resting limply in her lap.

“Do not hurt yourself by speaking of that,” Deronda said gently. “There’s no need. The matter is simple. You’ve come to me because I am the only one who knows the deepest part of your pain—and I understand your scruples.”

He paused, giving her time to gather herself. The silence stretched, filled with unspoken tenderness.

“You feel something terrible,” he said finally. “A guilt toward someone who is gone. You believe you’ve lost your claim as a wife. You shrink from taking what was his. You wish to punish yourself—for disobeying the better part of you, the part that resisted temptation. I’ve known that feeling too. Am I right?”

“Yes,” she whispered. “I want to be good—not like I’ve been. I’ll bear whatever you think I must. I’ve told you everything. What should I do?”

“If only you were the one involved,” Deronda said slowly, “I wouldn’t dare oppose any remorseful instinct. But I see your concern for Mrs. Davilow—and that changes things. I don’t believe your husband’s rights are canceled by your actions. He chose to enter your life, and shaped its path in the most profound way. Even aside from that, it was his duty, in his position, to care for your mother. He understood that if this will took effect, she would share in what he provided for you.”

“She has had eight hundred a year,” Gwendolen said. “I thought—maybe I should take only that, and leave the rest.”

Her mind had clung to this idea for so long it was hard to let go.

“But that would be a mistake,” Deronda said quietly. “You’d be creating a painful mystery for your mother—an income from which you exclude yourself would only cause her bitterness. And your own path would grow harder. We agreed in Genoa that your burden is one no one else should know. Your future kindness will be best served by keeping this secret from all. In my view, you should follow your husband’s will exactly. Let your remorse guide how you use your independence—not whether you accept it.”

As he spoke, he picked up his hat, unconsciously, from the floor beside him.

Gwendolen felt her heart leap, as though it sensed his intention to leave. In that instant, she rose—without thinking—unable to stop herself. Deronda, of course, stood too, stepping forward slightly.

“I’ll do what you say,” she murmured, hurriedly. “But what else should I do?”

That was all she could manage. Even these few words brought her to the edge. Her pride, long guarded, had crumbled. The childlike simplicity of her speech struck her like a mirror, revealing her helplessness. A sob rose, and great tears filled her eyes.

Deronda felt a deep pang, but he pressed on. Duty called louder than emotion.

“You’ll likely be going to the country with Mrs. Davilow soon,” he said gently.

“Yes—in a week or ten days.”

She paused, staring out the window, as if searching for a distant horizon.

“I want to be kind to them all—they can be happier than I can. Is that the best I can do?”

“It is,” Deronda said firmly. “It’s a duty beyond question.” He paused, each word weighed with care. “Other duties will follow. To see your life as a debt may seem bleak from afar—but it won’t truly be so. What makes life bleak is the absence of purpose. But once you begin acting with this penitent, loving intent, unexpected joys will arise—new needs will appear, guiding you day by day. Your life will grow—like a plant.”

She turned to him, her eyes wide with thirst, as if listening to the sound of water in a dry land.

Deronda felt her gaze as if she reached across a vast distance, arms outstretched from a lonely shore. His voice softened, pleading.

“This sorrow—so deep, so sudden—has come while you’re still young. Don’t see it as a ruin. See it as preparation. Let it be a preparation—”

Any listener would have thought he was begging for his own happiness.

“Look! You’ve been spared the worst horrors of a marriage you knew was wrong. You’ve seen what selfishness can become—what degradation lies ahead. Imagine a stern guardian, watching you walk a dangerous path, seizing your wrist and showing you the abyss. And it came to you in your youth. Think of it as preparation. You can—will—become one of the best women, the kind who make others glad they were born.”

The words touched her like a miracle. Emotions swirled through her—hope, shame, strength—mingling into something new, powerful, alive. The divine hope of renewal stirred within her, fueling a quiet energy she had never known. And yet, this rebirth felt inseparable from Deronda. His presence was the breath of her new life.

She did not speak. But a flush rose across her cheek, brow, neck—deepened for a moment, then faded slowly.

Deronda stepped forward, holding out his hand. “I mustn’t weary you.”

She startled—realizing he meant to leave—and placed her hand in his, silent still.

“You look unwell,” he said softly, holding her hand. “Unlike yourself.”

“I can’t sleep,” she whispered, her old weariness returning. “Things keep repeating. They come back… they’ll all come back,” she finished, shuddering, fear creeping into her bones.

“They’ll grow quieter with time,” he said.

He could not let go. Not yet.

“Sir Hugo says he’ll come to stay at Diplow,” Gwendolen said suddenly, grasping at words that had slipped away. “You’ll come too.”

“Probably,” he answered—and then, sensing the coldness of the word, corrected himself. “Yes, I shall come.” Then, with a final, gentle pressure, he released her hand.

“And not again here, before I leave town?” she asked, pale, timid.

What could he say?

“If I can help—if you wish it—I will,” he replied.

“I must wish it,” she said fiercely. “You know I must. What strength do I have? Who else is there?”

Another sob threatened.

Deronda’s face darkened with pain. He looked distressed as he said, “I will certainly come.”

She saw the change in him—yet relief flooded her. The thought of his return gave her courage. The fragile hope returned, glowing in her eyes.

“Don’t be sad for me,” she said, her voice warm with affection. “I’ll remember every word you said. I’ll remember what you believe about me. I’ll try.”

She looked at him steadily, reaching out again as if forgetting all that had passed since those words.

But no smile touched her lips. She had not smiled since her husband’s death.

Standing still, silent, she resembled a melancholy statue—once full of laughter, now carved in sorrow.

Only by understanding the depth of her anguish can we grasp her behavior—her openness, her desperate appeal. Thoughts that would haunt others didn’t touch her. She was consumed by fire, and had thrown herself into his arms, clinging as if he alone could carry her to safety.

She saw in him not just a friend, but the force of her rebirth. He had saved her, not with words, but with action. And so she believed he would remain—because he was the only one who could.

And yet, he could not stay.

He visited Park Lane again before she left—twice—but always in Mrs. Davilow’s presence. Their meetings were less intense, more guarded.

Since deciding to accept her inheritance, Gwendolen had formed a plan she spoke of often: to bring her mother and sisters back to Offendene, to rebuild their lives as if time had turned backward. She wanted to piece her life back together—to return to the days when joy was real, though she hadn’t known it then.

She had spoken to Sir Hugo, who would work to lease Gadsmere at a rent that would cover Offendene’s cost.

Deronda listened willingly. Any subject that gave her peace was welcome.

He said nothing of his own concerns. She asked nothing of his life.

Her mind was fixed on his coming to Diplow before autumn ended. The Lapidoths—the little Jewish girl and her brother—meant nothing to her. Nor did the shifting tides of politics or society. Her world had narrowed to the path of conscience, the struggle to rise from guilt.

For Gwendolen, memory had been shattered. Everything outside her inner turmoil lay in dim silence.

CHAPTER LXVI. “One day still fierce ’mid many a day struck calm.” —BROWNING: _The King and the Book_.

While Gwendolen’s thoughts remained fixed on Deronda, untouched by the quiet drama unfolding elsewhere, Ezra and Mirah found their lives increasingly entwined with his presence—though not in the way she might have imagined. Their bond deepened, not through choice, but through fate’s unrelenting hand.

Lapidoth had left his daughter at the threshold of her new life, driven not by love or duty, but by the insistent pull of chance—a game he could neither resist nor master. The moment he held Mirah’s purse, the weight of hunger seemed to vanish, replaced by the intoxicating hope of gain. Yet even as he walked away from her door, his mind already turned to what came next: whether to pawn the purse for more stakes, or return it with a lie that would spare him shame. He was not without pride, and he believed, with the stubbornness of a man who has lived too long on illusions, that he had rights over his children’s possessions—even if they were earned through sacrifice and silence.

He had spent the morning at a lowly gambling den marked only by a faded sign reading “Pyramids,” where thirty shillings had vanished into thin air. Now, with the purse empty and his pockets bare, he wandered toward Brompton Square, drawn by the faint possibility that Mirah might be out, or returning. If he could catch sight of her, perhaps he could slip inside without confrontation. But evening had fallen, and after waiting in the cold street, weariness made him decide to ring the bell.

As he approached, however, he heard music—her voice, clear and pure, rising from the piano. Mirah sat there, lost in song, singing “_Herz, mein Herz_” with all the emotion of one who carries sorrow within her soul. Ezra sat beside her, eyes closed, listening—not merely to the melody, but to the memory it stirred. Then the door opened, and Mrs. Adam stood there, hesitant. “A gentleman below says he is your father, miss.”

Mirah rose immediately, her hands clasping together as though to steady herself. She looked at Ezra, pleading silently. But before she could speak, he said, “No, Mirah. Not so. Let him come up, Mrs. Adam.”

She stood frozen, heart pounding, watching her brother rise. His face was transformed—brow furrowed, lips tight, eyes sharp with something she had never seen before: not anger, but a terrible clarity. When the door opened again, Lapidoth stepped in, his expression carefully arranged into one of sorrow, though his body trembled beneath the mask.

Mrs. Adam glanced between them—the younger man, gaunt and grave; the elder, worn and hollow—and whispered to herself, “Father, sure enough.” The resemblance was unmistakable in outline, yet the depth of character had been shaped by years of divergence. One was a man of quiet strength, forged in suffering; the other, a shadow of what he once was.

“Ezra,” Lapidoth began, voice cracking, “my boy… you hardly know me after so many years.”

“I know you—too well—father,” Ezra replied, each word deliberate, heavy with meaning. “You have not changed.”

“Oh, I see you’re angry,” Lapidoth said quickly, regaining some of his old charm. “I don’t blame you. Things have gone wrong. A man in trouble can’t always do right. I’ve suffered, I tell you—I’ve known hardship.” He turned to Mirah, holding out her purse. “Here’s your little purse, my dear. I emptied it to pay for food and lodging. I knew you’d want me to clear myself. And now I stand here—penniless—before you, my children. You can turn me out if you wish. Say the word, Mirah. Say, ‘Father, I’ve had enough of you.’ Say it, and I’ll go like a spark into the night. I won no longer spoil your peace.”

Tears glistened in his eyes—habitual, perhaps, but real enough in their effect.

“You know I could never say it, father,” Mirah answered softly, her voice thick with pain. “But I feel the truth behind your words—only not the part about leaving.”

“Mirah, my sister,” Ezra interrupted, his voice firm, “leave us.”

She hesitated, her eyes full of fear and longing. She moved close to him, took his hand, and whispered, “Remember, Ezra—you said our mother would not have shut him out.”

“Trust me,” Ezra said, and she withdrew, climbing the stairs slowly, her heart racing. If he drove him away—if he sent him into the cold world again—what then?

Lapidoth watched his son, sensing the storm gathering. He had expected rebuke, perhaps even rejection—but not this. Not the voice of a man speaking from the depths of grief and justice, shaped by years of silent endurance. Ezra spoke now, not as a son, but as a judge.

“This home,” he began, “is built on kindness and labor. My friend supports me. My sister earns her own living. We have nothing to give freely, but we will not cast you out. You are our father. Though you broke every bond, we honor ours. But I will never trust you.”

He paused, breath catching.

“You fled with money, leaving debts unpaid. You abandoned my mother. You stole her child and broke her heart. You became a gambler—where shame and conscience should have been, there is only craving. You tried to sell Mirah—you had sold her, but the price was denied. Such a man cannot expect trust again.”

His voice faltered, but did not break.

“We will feed you. We will give you shelter, clothing, a bed. Because you are our father. But you will never be trusted. You are an evil man. You caused our mother’s suffering. That such a man is our father is a wound upon our flesh—one that will never heal. But God has laid this burden upon us. Even if justice demanded your punishment, even if your body failed under public scorn—we would still say, ‘This is our father. Make way, that we may carry him out of your sight.’”

Lapidoth had braced himself for condemnation, but not for this—this raw, honest fire from the soul of his son. It struck him like lightning, not from outside, but from within. The tears came then—uncontrolled, violent. He collapsed into a chair, burying his face in his hands, sobbing as if his heart would break.

Yet even in that moment, he knew it was not just grief—it was strategy. A reflex honed over years: the ability to use emotion as armor. As a young man, he had once used such vulnerability to disarm criticism. Now, it served him again.

Ezra sat back, exhausted. His hands shook. He felt as though he had spoken words that could not be unsaid, as though he had stepped across a threshold from which there was no return. He did not speak. He did not look at his father. He simply sat, drained.

But Mirah—her ears tuned to every sound—heard the sobs. She could not stay away any longer. She opened the door, her first thought not for her father, but for her brother. She rushed to Ezra, taking his trembling hand. He gripped hers tightly, finding strength in her touch, though he said nothing.

Lapidoth lifted his head, wiped his eyes with a handkerchief, and reached out to Mirah. “Good-bye, Mir5196284378a05d1e8f1c4c87788b918d

It was a disconcerting surprise for Deronda when he returned from the Abbey to discover that Mirah’s father had taken up residence in their lodgings at Brompton. Mirah had felt it necessary to speak of Deronda to her father—though not without hesitation. She had carefully explained how her friendship with Ezra began, and how a deep mutual sympathy had grown between them. But she spoke lightly of what Deronda had done for her, omitting entirely the dramatic rescue from drowning. Instead, she framed her stay with Mrs. Meyrick’s family as something arranged through those connections, allowing her father to believe that Deronda had come into her life indirectly, rather than through any direct involvement.

She could not bring herself to reveal more. To let her father’s breath touch the truth of her bond with Deronda felt like an unbearable violation. And Lapidoth, for his part, did not press her on the circumstances of her escape or arrival in England. Yet he took keen interest in the idea that his children had found a benefactor—someone of standing in society, seemingly devoted to their well-being. It was Ezra who broke the news to Deronda: “I have become calm in seeing him now,” he said. “And I try to believe that my sister’s kindness, and the peace of daily life, may yet turn him away from temptation. I have commanded her not to give him money—and she has promised. I’ve made her understand that accepting such gifts would be to buy his own ruin.”

Deronda first visited three days after Lapidoth’s arrival. His new clothes were not ready, and wanting to make a good impression, he chose not to appear in his old garments. From the window, Lapido5th watched Deronda leave, surprised by his youthfulness—something Mirah had never mentioned. He had imagined Deronda as someone older, perhaps grave and scholarly, like Ezra himself. Now, seeing this young man, Lapidoth began to suspect that love might be the true motive behind his attentions. And if so, all the better—because a connection to Mirah offered more hope than one to Ezra. Lapidoth saw a chance to win favor with Deronda, to soften any prejudice he might carry.

He behaved with quiet charm, making efforts to settle into domestic life—joining Mirah at the piano, showing willingness to abstain from smoking in Mrs. Adam’s parlor, even walking in the square with his German pipe and tobacco supplied by Mirah. He refrained from challenging her refusal to give him money, knowing it was a solemn promise made to Ezra. He would wait. Patience, he thought, was the key.

When Deronda came again, Lapidoth was dressed in his new clothes, satisfied with his appearance, and present in the room with Ezra, who endured his father’s presence as part of a painful duty. Deronda arrived cold and distant—the mere sight of this man, who had ruined his wife and children, stirred in him a deep repulsion, almost physical. But Lapidoth remained undeterred. He asked permission to stay, listened to Ezra read documents from the old chest, and even proved useful by helping decipher a difficult German manuscript.

This led him to suggest transcribing the papers—a task he offered to take on, along with copying any documents written in Latin or Greek characters. Though Ezra’s eyes were growing weak, Lapidoth’s still held strength. Deronda accepted the offer, noting a glimmer of grace in Lapidoth’s willingness to contribute meaningfully. And he saw a quiet satisfaction in Ezra’s face—though it faded quickly. “Let all writing be done here,” Ezra said firmly. “I cannot trust these papers out of my sight. There must be no risk of fire—or worse.”

Poor Ezra felt as though he were guarding a prisoner on parole. Unless he saw his father working, he couldn’t believe the work was being done in good faith. This arrangement bound Ezra to endure his father’s presence—not just because of long-standing pain, but because Lapidoth’s restless nature grew more pronounced as he settled in. Once freed from awe of his son, he became increasingly uncontrolled: moving about, talking too much, gesturing wildly, even falling back into old habits when Mirah was near—gossiping about past lives, retelling jokes, quoting lines from plays he once adapted, convinced he could summon the spark of his younger self at will.

To Ezra, it was torture. When Mirah was home, she tried to ease his burden by taking her father down to the parlor, where she could keep watch over him.

What does duty mean when it rests on a single, hard-won resolve? The real challenge lies not in the decision itself, but in enduring its consequences each day—waking to the prospect of irritation to suppress, shame to bear. These were the burdens they carried, like many others born to unworthy fathers—burdens that stretched, for Mirah at least, into the core of their lives.

Meanwhile, Lapidoth’s presence had created an invisible wall between Deronda and Mirah. Each feared what the other might think, misreading the other’s silence as coldness, their reserve as rejection. But soon, light began to break through.

As soon as he returned from the Abbey, Deronda called on Hans Meyrick, feeling it was only right that his friend know the reasons for his journey and the changes it had brought. Hans wasn’t there—he’d gone to the country for a few days. Deronda left a note and waited a week, expecting a reply. When none came, worried about Hans’s volatile temperament—especially since his planned retreat to the Abbey had been delayed—he went again.

He was admitted into the painting room, where Hans stood before his easel, palette in hand, wearing a light coat and no waistcoat, his long hair damp from a bath. His face looked haggard, far from refreshed. He had picked up his brushes only moments before Deronda entered.

They shook hands. “You don’t look much like you’ve been in the country,” Deronda said. “Cambridge?”
“No,” Hans replied curtly, dropping his palette as if caught in a lie. Then he pulled out a chair for Deronda and sank into another, leaning back with his hands behind his head. “I’ve been to I-don’t-know-where—No Man’s Land. A terribly unpleasant place.”

“You don’t mean you’ve been drinking?” Deronda asked, concerned.
“Nothing so good,” Hans said. “I’ve been smoking opium. Always meant to try it—see how much bliss one could get. Found myself low on other kinds lately, so I thought it wise to seize the moment. But I swear I’ll never touch it again. It doesn’t agree with me.”

“What happened?” Deronda asked gently. “You seemed cheerful when you wrote.”
“Oh, nothing special,” Hans said, forcing a smile. “The world started looking seedy—a cabbage garden with all the cabbages cut. A malady of genius, no doubt. And honestly, I was tired of being virtuous without reward—especially in this sweltering London heat.”

“Nothing else? No real trouble?”
Hans shook his head. “I came to tell you about my own affairs—but I can’t do it well if you’re hiding yours.”
“I haven’t any,” Hans said flippantly. “Just a quarrel with a bric-a-brac dealer. Besides, this is the first time you’ve ever spoken to me about your life. You’re only beginning to repay a long debt.”

Deronda sensed Hans was acting—performing—but he believed honest talk would restore their old closeness. “You laughed at the mystery of my trip to Italy,” he began. “But it was about something that touched my happiness at its roots. I knew nothing of my parents. I went to Genoa to meet my mother. My father died when I was a baby. My mother was the daughter of a prominent Jew; my father was her cousin. I’d suspected this all along. So when I found out, I was glad—glad to discover I’m Jewish.”

“You needn’t look surprised,” Hans said, shifting his posture and examining the heel of his slipper. “You knew?”
“My mother told me. She came to the house the morning after you were there—both brother and sister told her. You can imagine we don’t rejoice as they do. But whatever makes you happy, I’ll come around to it eventually—when exactly, I can’t say.” He spoke softly, as he often did when discontented, but unwilling to make a fuss.

“I understand you can’t share my joy,” Deronda said. “But I couldn’t let this stand between us. It changes everything for me. I’ve embraced some of Mordecai’s ideas—and I mean to live them, as far as one man can. I may go east someday and be away for years.”

Hans said nothing. He rose, grabbed his palette, and began brushing it idly, his back turned to Deronda, who felt suddenly awkward, as if the ground beneath him had shifted.

After a pause, Hans spoke again, voice low and without turning. “Excuse the question—but does Mrs. Grandcourt know all this?”

“No,” Deronda said, stiffening. “And I beg you, Hans, stop joking about that. Your assumptions are completely wrong—entirely the opposite of the truth.”

“I’m not joking,” Hans said flatly. “But I’m not sure you know what my thoughts really are.”

“Perhaps not,” Deronda said. “But let me say this once: in relation to Mrs. Grandcourt, I’ve never been—and never will be—her lover. If you’ve ever interpreted anything I’ve done that way, you’re profoundly mistaken.”

Silence followed—thick, uncomfortable.

“Perhaps I’ve been wrong in another interpretation too,” Hans said finally.

“What?”
“That you had no desire to love another woman—one who is neither married nor widowed.”

Deronda understood immediately. “You mean Mirah.”

“I can’t pretend I don’t understand,” he said. “It’s painful that our desires clash. But if you truly believe I have reason to envy you, then you’re mistaken.”

“Not the slightest,” Hans said bitterly. “You’ve measured my pride and know it towers above all your advantages.”

“I’m a nuisance to you, Meyrick,” Deronda said, rising. “I’m sorry. But I can’t help it. After what passed between us, I needed this talk. And I don’t see how my feelings have changed things for you. They won’t change things for me either, under these circumstances. Now—did you know her father is there?”

“Yes,” Hans said. “If he weren’t a Jew, I’d damn him—with faint praise, I mean.” He didn’t smile.

“She and I meet under greater constraint than ever,” Deronda continued. “We could go two years without understanding her heart. That’s the situation, Hans. Neither of us has hurt the other. We must accept this rivalry—hopeful, but likely to end in nothing. Our friendship can bear this strain, surely.”

“No,” Hans said sharply, throwing down his tools, hands in pockets, turning to face him. “Our friendship—my friendship—can’t bear the strain of pretending I’m not a thankless coward, begrudging you your happiness. Because you *are* the happiest man alive. If Mirah loves anyone more than her brother—it’s you.”

He turned abruptly and sat, staring up at Deronda with an expression utterly devoid of tenderness. A shock ran through Deronda. After a moment, he said, “That’s just a kind fiction of yours, Hans.”

“I’m not in a kind mood,” Hans replied. “I found it disagreeable when I realized it—especially because I thought you were pledged to the duchess. But now—confound you!—you’re in love where it matters. A Jew. Everything perfect.”

“Tell me what convinced you,” Deronda said, half doubting the joy he felt.

“Don’t ask,” Hans said. “Mother saw it. The result is—Mirah is jealous of the duchess. The sooner you confess, the better. There! I’ve paid off a score or two. Now I may curse you for getting what you deserve—the best luck I know.”

“God bless you, Hans,” Deronda said, reaching out his hand. Hans took it, and they gripped each other silently—two friends, bound by honesty, pain, and a love that had just begun to shine through the shadows.

*“All thoughts, all passions, all delights,
Whatever stirs this mortal frame,
All are but ministers of Love,
And feed his sacred flame.”* —COLERIDGE.

Deronda’s heart had never burned with such intensity as it did now—fanned by Hans’s words that Mirah was suffering from jealousy. The knowledge stirred him deeply, and when he returned to Ezra’s home the next day, he carried with him a quiet determination: he would not let this moment pass. He would seek out Mirah, speak plainly, and ask her to be his wife. If she accepted, he felt certain he could face any consequence. And if they were betrothed, he might also offer her a kind of protection—a shield against the turmoil that might come from her father.

He had noticed no outward change in Lapidoth. The man still seemed attentive, dutiful even. But Deronda sensed beneath the surface a growing unrest, an unease that could erupt into something terrible. He didn’t yet know how close the storm was, nor how fragile the peace they’d built truly was.

Lapidoth, meanwhile, was not at ease. To Ezra, his distraction was merely troubling; to Lapidoth, it was a prison. Every moment spent in the house felt like a delay—like being held back from freedom. He had once planned to wait for a grand opportunity, perhaps to extract a large sum from Deronda. But while he waited, he watched—curiously, relentlessly. Where did Mirah keep her money? Her keys? He searched for weaknesses, for moments when he could act without suspicion.

Yet Mirah was careful. She had entrusted all her savings to Mrs. Meyrick, keeping only what she needed. For Lapidoth, this was like being locked in a room where every door was bolted—safe, yes, but infuriating. He could not steal, not openly. Yet the thought lingered: *What if I took something small? Something that belonged to someone who would give me more anyway?*

That morning, Deronda arrived radiant—his eyes bright, his voice warm. He had been thinking of Mirah, of the future, and the joy of confessing his love. His presence stirred Lapidoth’s mind, not with kindness, but with new possibilities. Deronda’s ease made Lapidoth feel the chains tighter. He longed to escape—not just from the house, but from himself.

As Deronda read aloud from a rabbinical text, Lapidoth sat quietly in the background, his gaze drifting. Then—his eyes landed on the ring. It lay on the dark wooden table, gleaming under the light. Deronda had removed it earlier, placing it beside his cravat. It had been there all along, unnoticed, unguarded.

In that instant, Lapidoth saw not just a piece of jewelry—but a key. A way out. A sum of money that could buy passage abroad, freedom from restraint, silence. It wasn’t much—less than what he dreamed of—but it was real. It was within reach. And it belonged to a man who had already shown generosity.

He didn’t consider stealing. Not really. Theft was a crime. But this—this was different. Deronda was not a stranger. He was almost family. And if Deronda had given him more, wouldn’t he have taken it willingly? So why not take this?

The idea came swiftly, like a breath catching fire. He rose, stepped toward the window, then passed the table—his fingers brushing the ring. It slipped into his pocket without sound. He put on his hat and left.

No one noticed. Not Deronda. Not Ezra.

Then Mirah entered, breathless, her hat still on. “I just came to make sure Ezra had his medicine,” she said, looking flustered. “I must go to Mrs. Meyrick’s right away.”

“Let me walk with you,” Deronda said at once. “I can’t stay here—I’m too hot. I want to go to Mrs. Meyrick’s too. May I?”

“Oh, yes,” Mirah whispered, blushing. She turned to pour Ezra’s draught, her hands trembling slightly.

Deronda paused, suddenly remembering—he had left his cravat and ring behind. He went to the table, picked up the cravat, and froze.

“Good heavens,” he murmured. “Where is my ring?”

He began searching—the floor, under the furniture, between the carpet fibers. Ezra looked up from his books, puzzled. Mirah rushed to his side.

“Did you leave it here?” she asked.

“Yes,” he said, still scanning the floor. “It must have fallen.”

But Mirah knew. In that moment, her face drained of color. She moved quickly to Ezra’s ear and whispered, “Was my father here?”

Ezra nodded slowly, meeting her eyes with grim understanding.

She darted back to Deronda, her voice urgent. “You haven’t found it?”

“No,” he said, pretending to check his pockets. “Maybe I put it there.”

She stared at him, her voice sharp, clear: “It’s not there. You left it on the table.”

Then she ran—out of the room, down the stairs, into the bedroom, checking everywhere. She opened the door where his hat usually hung. Nothing. She stood at the window, hands clasped, lips pale, staring into the distance as if seeking a sign.

Deronda followed her silently. When she finally turned, her eyes met his—full of shame, grief, and something deeper: a silent surrender.

He stepped forward, took her hands gently in both of his. “Mirah,” he said, his voice low and reverent, “let me think of him as my father too. Let us share everything—joy and sorrow, honor and disgrace. I would rather carry your pain than feel the happiest joy of another woman. Say you will not reject me. Say you will let me be yours. Say you will be my wife. Say it now—say it always. I’ve waited so long. I’ve hidden my love so long. Say yes.”

Mirah had not known what to expect. At first, she heard only comfort—kindness offered because of Ezra. But as his words sank in, a warmth spread through her. Not just comfort—but hope. A future. A love that could rise above shame, above loss.

Her face glowed under his gaze. Yet she remained still, grave, as if listening to a sacred vow. When he finished, she could not speak. But she lifted her lips—and kissed his softly, as if that single touch was the simplest, most perfect “yes” imaginable.

They stood together, hands clasped, hearts full. No words were needed. They had found each other—through sorrow, through silence, through love.

Then, softly, Mirah whispered, “Let us go and comfort Ezra.”

“The human nature unto which I felt
That I belonged, and reverenced with love,
Was not a punctual presence, but a spirit
Diffused through time and space, with aid derived
Of evidence from monuments, erect,
Prostrate, or leaning toward their common rest
In earth, the widely scattered wreck sublime
Of vanished nations.” —WORDSWORTH: _The Prelude_.

Sir Hugo carried out his plan to spend part of the autumn at Diplow, and by early October, his presence had brought a gentle warmth to the surrounding countryside—from the grand estate of Brackenshaw and Quetcham down to the modest parlors of Wanchester’s shopkeepers. Sir Hugo was a man who enjoyed being seen, liked to be kind, and believed in reform without fearing it would unsettle the soul of English life—especially its deep-rooted admiration for tradition and rank. Thus, he made Diplow a welcoming home, inviting both seasoned solicitors and young curates alike, yet always with care. He knew how to mix guests so that no one felt overlooked or humbled. For instance, while Lord Brackenshaw might happily meet Robinson the attorney, Robinson himself would take offense at being placed among those he considered his equals. Sir Hugo understood these nuances well, and in doing so won affection and respect—two things that suited his generous nature perfectly.

Now, the rector of Pennicote found himself welcomed at Diplow in a way he’d never known under Grandcourt’s rule. It wasn’t because Sir Hugo admired Mr. Gascoigne, but because he wished to maintain a warm, visible friendship with him—for Mrs. Grandcourt. His chivalry toward her had grown steadily, though he could not fully explain why—even to Lady Mallinger. He refused to speak of what he believed was another woman’s secret, even if that woman was his own wife. This silence revealed a rare depth of honor in his devotion.

Deronda, after becoming engaged to Mirah, decided it was right to tell Sir Hugo everything—his future plans, his new purpose in life. He chose to write a letter. He sensed that his dear friend might be hurt or disappointed by this turn of fate, and he feared a face-to-face conversation might spark an impulsive reaction he would regret. In writing, he could spare them both the pain of immediate emotion.

Sir Hugo, though not entirely surprised, was deeply troubled. His first instinct was to share the letter with Lady Mallinger, knowing she would react with shock—giving him the chance to argue against her alarm, thus softening his own distress. And indeed, when she expressed dismay that Daniel’s brilliance and companionship should end in such an “absurd” attachment to the Jews, Sir Hugo could reply with calm assurance:

“Oh, nonsense, my dear! Dan will not make a fool of himself. He has strong ideas about Judaism—political ones you can’t grasp. But don’t worry—he’ll keep his head.”

Yet when it came to the marriage itself, Lady Mallinger offered no comfort. Gently, without bitterness, she admitted she’d never imagined such a thing when Mirah sang at her party or taught Amabel. After a pause, she confessed it had once crossed her mind—after Daniel’s sudden appearance in Genoa—that he might marry Gwendolen. It seemed too strange a coincidence. Though she didn’t like widows, she thought such a match would have been better than Daniel drifting into Jewish life altogether.

But Sir Hugo agreed with her so completely that he couldn’t dismiss her thoughts as mere feminine fancy. His anger over Gwendolen’s rejection remained unexpressed, unrelieved. He told Lady Mallinger not to speak of it—yet kept thinking: *If this is cruel to the poor girl, the longer she stays ignorant, the better. She’ll learn from Dan himself.*

His imagination had already reconstructed the whole story, piece by piece.

Meanwhile, Sir Hugo’s stay at Diplow allowed him to continue his fatherly attentions to Gwendolen—a role Lady Mallinger, despite her disapproval of widows, was glad to support.

Gwendolen had moved to Offendene, and there she settled with a quietness beyond her mother’s hopes. She was beginning to feel a peaceful melancholy born of surrender—of letting go of self-demand, of accepting kindness as a gift rather than a right. Like one rescued from darkness, she now savored the light, the air, the small joys of daily life. She saw each morning and evening not as routine, but as deliverance. Even the faint glow of compassion from others, or a dog’s loyalty, felt like grace. Those who know themselves deeply—like Hamlet—can understand this constant sense of rescue. And Gwendolen lived it daily, reliving her past struggles: from the false comforts of pride that led her away from conscience, to the final, desperate pull of hatred that threatened to consume her—while she wept and prayed for the very conscience she had abandoned.

She now treasured every word Deronda had spoken about her salvation, every sentence that helped her resist despair.

Yet she was also lifted by the hope of seeing him again. She did not imagine him distant—her need for him blurred the boundaries between their lives. Her world was shaped by his presence, just as ours often are shaped by our deepest attachments—not only to people, but to God. The future she faced was one where she would grow into the image he held before her. Hadn’t he first appeared to her as a corrective force? One she resented at first, then loved completely. She could not imagine ending that reliance—it had become, in her mind, as firm as the earth beneath her feet.

And soon, Deronda came to Diplow, closer to London than the Abbey. He had planned to take Ezra and Mirah to a mild coastal spot while preparing a new home for Mirah as his bride—a place where they could care for her brother. But Ezra begged not to be moved unless it was to join them in the East. The outside world had become a burden; yet the idea of that journey stirred a dreamlike joy within him.

Deronda, meanwhile, wanted to discuss his finances and future with Sir Hugo—especially since he hoped to marry Mirah within months. That gave him reason to visit Diplow. But there was another reason: his promise to Gwendolen. Though he felt blessed in his own life, a quiet ache remained. It may seem paradoxical—the beloved lover called happy, yet happiness often hides sorrow. But human feeling rarely fits neat labels. His joy for Mirah did not exclude care for Gwendolen. True love, after all, is not just joy—it is the embrace of countless burdens, greater than any pleasure outside it.

He visited Diplow twice, saw Gwendolen twice—and left without telling her anything about his new life. He blamed himself. Yet we all delay painful truths, waiting for the right moment, the right mood. The first time, Gwendolen was full of questions—about her own life, her ignorance, how to be kinder, how to make amends. Deronda could not interrupt her with news of his own changes. The second time, she was overwhelmed by dark memories, crying uncontrollably, convinced he would always despise her. All he could do was soothe her. When her tears subsided, leaving her eyes glistening with childlike trust, he could not add another weight.

Still, time passed. He knew he must tell her. Gwendolen had never questioned his affairs—had never wondered why he was in Genoa. Her innocence made the truth harder. If she learned it from someone else, she would feel betrayed. He couldn’t write—his tenderness forbade imagining her reading his farewell alone, perhaps sensing cold joy in his words. So he returned to Diplow, determined to face the risk.

On this third visit, Hans Meyrick was painting the three daughters on a bank—"in the Gainsborough style"—and sketching village children at Pennicote. Hans seemed cheerful, but Deronda sensed a forced brightness, like a lady’s rouge too evenly applied. Though grateful to Deronda, Hans could not help feeling a sting of irritation. He had poured his heart out about Mirah—only to discover Deronda had hidden a rivalry that now defined his life. Poor Hans had always trusted Deronda completely, never expected to be excluded. Now, he realized Deronda was more than he’d known—and that knowledge hurt. Still, Hans had done nothing wrong. He’d stood by his friend. As he told himself: “His metal rang truer than I ever thought possible.” Hans had long said he was a *dilettante* in virtue—loving it in others, failing at it himself. Perhaps that was why he now spoke freely.
